Genesis — Saints Bible cross-references
Completed: Genesis 1:1–50:26. Book initial curated pass complete. Overall next: Deuteronomy 17:1.
1531 completed source verses; 3341 completed cross-reference pairs.
Completed means the initial curated cross-reference pass: the source verse and every listed target passage were read in the pinned Saints-Translation English records, and each retained pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean every possible biblical connection has been exhausted, or independent scholarly approval.
1 target candidate(s) await source evidence or identity review and are excluded from completed pair totals; see source-checks.json.
Reference edition: Bespoke Saints English Translation. Greek-based source numbering is retained.

Genesis 1:1
In the beginning God made heaven and earth.
  John 1:1-3 — Thematic connection: John places the Word at the beginning and says that all things came into being through Him; Genesis opens with God's making heaven and earth.
  Hebrews 11:3 — Thematic connection: Hebrews describes the worlds as formed by God's word, explaining the creation by divine speech narrated in Genesis.
  2 Maccabees 7:28 — Thematic connection: The mother appeals to God's making heaven, earth and humanity as the ground for trust in His creative power.
  Psalms 32:6-9 — Thematic connection: The psalm attributes the heavens and their host to the Lord's word and command, corresponding to creation by divine speech.

Genesis 1:2
Now the earth was unseen and unformed, and darkness was over the deep; and the Spirit of God was moving over the water.
  Wisdom of Solomon 11:17 — Shared imagery: Wisdom describes God's powerful hand creating the world out of unformed matter, corresponding to the unformed earth in Genesis.
  Psalms 103:30 — Thematic connection: The psalm associates God's sending His spirit with the creation of living beings and renewal of the earth; it illuminates the life-giving role of the Spirit.
  Genesis 8:1 — Narrative parallel: God sends a wind over the flooded earth and the waters subside; both scenes place divine action over the waters before habitable land appears, without identifying wind and Spirit.

Genesis 1:3
And God said, “Let there be light.” And there was light.
  2 Corinthians 4:6 — Explicit reuse: Paul recalls God's command for light to shine out of darkness and applies that creative act to illumination in the heart.
  Psalms 32:9 — Thematic connection: The psalm's statement that God spoke and things came to be matches the effective command that produces light.
  Hebrews 11:3 — Thematic connection: The worlds' formation by God's word corresponds to the effective divine speech that brings light into being.

Genesis 1:4
And God saw that the light was good, and God divided the light from the darkness.
  Isaiah 45:7 — Shared imagery: Isaiah pairs God's forming light with making darkness, matching the created distinction between light and darkness.
  Job 38:19-20 — Shared imagery: God questions Job about the dwelling places and boundaries of light and darkness, corresponding to their ordered distinction in Genesis.
  John 1:4-5 — Thematic connection: John uses the contrast of light and darkness for the life-giving light associated with the Word; this is a theological analogy, not an account of the first day.

Genesis 1:5
And God called the light Day, and the darkness He called Night. And there was evening, and there was morning: one day.
  Psalms 73:16-17 — Thematic connection: The psalm assigns day and night to God and speaks of His establishing their lights and the earth's seasons.
  Genesis 8:22 — Thematic connection: The promise that day and night will continue preserves the ordered cycle first named in Genesis 1.
  Genesis 1:13 — Literary parallel: The third-day conclusion repeats the evening-and-morning structure introduced at the close of the first day.

Genesis 1:6
And God said, “Let there be a firmament in the midst of the water, and let it divide water from water.” And it was so.
  Psalms 103:2-3 — Shared imagery: The psalm pictures God spreading heaven and placing His upper chambers in the waters, corresponding to the heaven-and-water ordering in Genesis.
  Psalms 148:4 — Shared imagery: The call to waters above the heavens corresponds to the upper waters distinguished by the firmament.
  Genesis 1:7 — Narrative continuation: The next verse records the making of the firmament and the separation commanded in this verse.

Genesis 1:7
And God made the firmament, and God divided the water beneath the firmament from the water above the firmament.
  Psalms 148:4 — Shared imagery: The psalm explicitly names waters above the heavens, corresponding to the upper waters separated in Genesis.
  Psalms 103:3 — Shared imagery: God's upper chambers in the waters provide a poetic counterpart to Genesis's arrangement of heaven and the upper waters.
  Proverbs 8:27-28 — Shared imagery: Wisdom describes God's preparing heaven and establishing the clouds and deep, paralleling the ordering of heaven and waters.

Genesis 1:8
And God called the firmament Heaven. And God saw that it was good. And there was evening, and there was morning: a second day.
  Psalms 18:2 — Thematic connection: The psalm presents the heavens and firmament as declaring God's work, corresponding to the firmament named Heaven in Genesis.
  Genesis 2:1 — Narrative continuation: The later summary names heaven and earth and their ordered host as completed, including the heaven named here.
  Exodus 20:11 — Explicit reuse: The Sabbath command summarizes God's making heaven, earth and sea in six days, including the work of the second day.

Genesis 1:9
And God said, “Let the water beneath heaven be gathered into one place, and let the dry land appear.” And it was so. And the water beneath heaven was gathered into its gathering places, and the dry land appeared.
  Job 38:8-11 — Shared imagery: God describes bounding the sea and limiting its advance, paralleling the gathering of waters so that dry land appears.
  Psalms 32:7 — Shared imagery: The psalm speaks of God's gathering the sea's waters and storing the depths, corresponding to the gathering command in Genesis.
  Psalms 103:6-9 — Shared imagery: The psalm describes waters retreating from the mountains and receiving a boundary so that they do not cover the earth.

Genesis 1:10
And God called the dry land Earth, and the gathered waters He called Seas. And God saw that it was good.
  Psalms 94:5 — Thematic connection: The psalm assigns the sea and dry land to God's making, matching the two domains named Earth and Seas.
  Exodus 20:11 — Explicit reuse: The Sabbath command names earth and sea among the domains God made, corresponding to the named domains in Genesis.
  Psalms 103:24-25 — Thematic connection: The psalm praises God's abundant works on earth and in the great sea, the two created domains identified here.

Genesis 1:11
And God said, “Let the earth bring forth vegetation sowing seed according to its kind and likeness, and fruit-bearing trees upon the earth, each yielding fruit with its seed within it, according to its kind.” And it was so.
  Genesis 1:29 — Narrative continuation: God later assigns seed-bearing plants and fruit-bearing trees as human food, reusing the plant categories introduced here.
  Psalms 103:14 — Thematic connection: The psalm attributes growing grass and cultivated plants to God's provision, corresponding to the earth's producing vegetation.
  Mark 4:28 — Thematic connection: In Jesus' parable the earth produces successive stages of grain growth, drawing on the productive earth and seed-bearing vegetation; it is an analogy rather than a creation quotation.

Genesis 1:12
And the earth brought forth vegetation sowing seed according to its kind and likeness, and fruit-bearing trees upon the earth, each yielding fruit with its seed within it, according to its kind. And God saw that it was good.
  Genesis 1:11 — Narrative fulfilment: The earth's producing vegetation and fruit-bearing trees fulfils the command immediately preceding it.
  Genesis 2:9 — Thematic connection: The garden account describes God causing pleasing, food-bearing trees to grow from the earth, corresponding to the vegetation produced here.
  Psalms 103:14 — Thematic connection: God's making plants grow for human use corresponds to the successful production of vegetation in Genesis.

Genesis 1:13
And there was evening, and there was morning: a third day.
  Genesis 1:5 — Literary parallel: The third-day conclusion repeats the evening-and-morning pattern established for the first day.
  Genesis 1:19 — Literary parallel: The fourth-day conclusion continues the same evening-and-morning structure used for the third day.
  Exodus 20:11 — Explicit reuse: The six-day creation summary in the Sabbath command includes this third day within the sequence of God's work.

Genesis 1:14
And God said, “Let there be lights in the firmament of heaven to give light upon the earth and to divide the day from the night. Let them serve for signs and seasons, for days and years.
  Psalms 103:19 — Thematic connection: The psalm connects the moon with appointed times and the sun with its setting, matching the lights' time-marking function.
  Psalms 135:7-9 — Thematic connection: The psalm names the great lights and their rule over day and night, corresponding to the lights' appointed ordering function.
  Sirach 43:6-7 — Thematic connection: Sirach describes the moon's role in seasons and festival times, corresponding to the luminaries' service for signs and seasons.

Genesis 1:15
And let them be lights in the firmament of heaven, to shine upon the earth.” And it was so.
  Psalms 135:7-9 — Thematic connection: God's making the great lights, sun, moon and stars corresponds to the appointed lights in the firmament.
  Psalms 148:3 — Thematic connection: The summons to sun, moon and stars of light identifies the heavenly lights commanded to shine in Genesis.
  Genesis 1:17 — Narrative fulfilment: The later verse records God's placing the lights in heaven to shine on earth, fulfilling this command.

Genesis 1:16
And God made the two great lights: the greater light to rule the day, and the lesser light to rule the night; He made the stars also.
  Psalms 135:8-9 — Explicit reuse: The psalm repeats the sun's rule over the day and the moon and stars' rule over the night.
  Psalms 148:3 — Thematic connection: Sun, moon and stars are summoned to praise God, matching the three groups of heavenly lights made here.
  Sirach 43:1-8 — Thematic connection: Sirach praises the sun and moon and their ordered functions, expanding on the heavenly lights described in Genesis.

Genesis 1:17
And God set them in the firmament of heaven to shine upon the earth,
  Psalms 8:4 — Thematic connection: The psalm describes the heavens, moon and stars as God's established works, corresponding to His placing the lights in heaven.
  Psalms 148:3 — Thematic connection: The sun, moon and shining stars addressed by the psalm are the heavenly lights set in place in Genesis.
  Genesis 1:15 — Narrative fulfilment: The placing of the lights so that they shine on earth carries out the command given two verses earlier.

Genesis 1:18
to rule the day and the night, and to divide the light from the darkness. And God saw that it was good.
  Psalms 73:16 — Thematic connection: The psalm assigns both day and night and their lights to God, matching His ordering of those domains by the luminaries.
  Psalms 135:8-9 — Explicit reuse: The psalm repeats the distinct rule of the sun by day and the moon and stars by night.
  Genesis 1:4 — Literary parallel: The division of light from darkness recalls the first-day separation, now connected with the lights' appointed rule.

Genesis 1:19
And there was evening, and there was morning: a fourth day.
  Genesis 1:5 — Literary parallel: The fourth-day refrain repeats the evening-and-morning pattern first used for day one.
  Genesis 1:13 — Literary parallel: The fourth-day refrain follows the same literary pattern as the preceding third-day conclusion.
  Genesis 1:23 — Literary parallel: The next day's conclusion continues the evening-and-morning structure and advances the day count.

Genesis 1:20
And God said, “Let the waters bring forth living creatures that move, and birds that fly above the earth across the firmament of heaven.” And it was so.
  Psalms 103:24-26 — Thematic connection: The psalm praises God's teeming sea creatures and the sea's great creature, corresponding to the waters' living inhabitants.
  Psalms 148:7-10 — Thematic connection: The psalm includes sea creatures and winged birds among the created beings called to praise God.
  Genesis 2:19 — Thematic connection: The garden account names birds among the creatures God forms and brings to the human, corresponding to the birds introduced here.

Genesis 1:21
And God made the great sea creatures, and every living creature that moves, which the waters brought forth according to their kinds, and every winged bird according to its kind. And God saw that they were good.
  Psalms 103:25-26 — Thematic connection: The psalm describes the sea's many living creatures and the great sea creature God formed, corresponding to Genesis's sea life.
  Psalms 148:7 — Thematic connection: The call to great sea creatures and the depths corresponds to the great sea creatures made in Genesis.
  Genesis 6:20 — Narrative parallel: The ark instructions preserve birds and animals according to their kinds, continuing the creation categories used for living creatures.

Genesis 1:22
And God blessed them, saying, “Grow and multiply, and fill the waters in the seas; and let the birds multiply upon the earth.”
  Genesis 8:17 — Explicit reuse: At the release of the ark's creatures, God renews the growth and multiplication language on earth; the command in this reading is addressed to Noah.
  Genesis 9:1 — Literary parallel: God's blessing on Noah and his sons repeats the command to grow and multiply, now addressed to humanity.
  Genesis 1:28 — Literary parallel: The human blessing repeats the growth and multiplication language first addressed to sea creatures and birds.

Genesis 1:23
And there was evening, and there was morning: a fifth day.
  Genesis 1:5 — Literary parallel: The fifth-day conclusion repeats the evening-and-morning structure introduced on day one.
  Genesis 1:19 — Literary parallel: The fifth-day refrain follows the fourth-day refrain with the same structure and an advancing count.
  Genesis 1:31 — Literary parallel: The sixth-day conclusion continues the same evening-and-morning pattern used here.

Genesis 1:24
And God said, “Let the earth bring forth living creatures according to their kind: four-footed animals, creeping things, and wild beasts of the earth according to their kind.” And it was so.
  Genesis 2:19 — Thematic connection: The garden account describes God forming the land's animals, corresponding to the land creatures called forth in Genesis 1.
  Genesis 6:20 — Narrative parallel: The ark instructions preserve animals according to their kinds, carrying forward the categories used in the creation command.
  Psalms 148:10 — Thematic connection: The psalm names wild animals, cattle and creeping things, corresponding to the land-animal groups listed in Genesis.

Genesis 1:25
And God made the wild beasts of the earth according to their kind, and the cattle according to their kind, and all the creeping things of the earth according to their kinds. And God saw that they were good.
  Genesis 7:14 — Narrative parallel: The creatures entering the ark are listed by kind in groups corresponding to the land animals made in Genesis 1.
  Psalms 148:10 — Thematic connection: The psalm's wild animals, cattle and creeping things match the groups made in this verse.
  Genesis 1:24 — Narrative fulfilment: The making of land creatures according to kind fulfils the command of the preceding verse.

Genesis 1:26
And God said, “Let Us make man in Our image and likeness; and let them rule over the fish of the sea, the birds of heaven, the cattle, all the earth, and every creeping thing that creeps upon the earth.”
  Psalms 8:5-9 — Thematic connection: The psalm joins humanity's dignity with rule over animals, birds and fish, corresponding to the dominion entrusted in Genesis.
  Wisdom of Solomon 9:1-3 — Thematic connection: Wisdom recalls humanity's appointment to rule God's creatures and administer the world in holiness and justice.
  Sirach 17:1-4 — Explicit reuse: Sirach connects humanity's creation, God's image and authority over living creatures, bringing together the principal elements of this verse.

Genesis 1:27
And God made man; in the image of God He made him; male and female He made them.
  Genesis 5:1-2 — Explicit reuse: The genealogy's introduction restates creation in God's image and the making and blessing of male and female.
  Matthew 19:4 — Direct quotation: Jesus cites the Creator's making humanity male and female as part of His teaching on marriage.
  James 3:9 — Explicit reuse: James grounds respect for human beings in their being made according to God's likeness, recalling the image language of Genesis.

Genesis 1:28
And God blessed them, saying, “Grow and multiply, and fill the earth and subdue it. Rule over the fish of the sea, the birds of heaven, all the cattle, all the earth, and every creeping thing that creeps upon the earth.”
  Genesis 9:1-2 — Explicit reuse: God renews the multiplication blessing after the flood and describes humanity's relation to the earth's animals, birds and fish.
  Psalms 8:7-9 — Thematic connection: The psalm describes creatures being placed under human authority, corresponding to the dominion given here.
  Wisdom of Solomon 9:2 — Thematic connection: Wisdom identifies humanity's vocation to rule God's creatures, corresponding to the task assigned in Genesis.

Genesis 1:29
And God said, “Behold, I have given you every plant sowing seed upon all the earth, and every tree that bears fruit containing seed. These shall be yours for food.
  Genesis 2:16 — Thematic connection: God's permission to eat from the garden's trees corresponds to the gift of seed-bearing plants and fruit trees as human food.
  Genesis 9:3 — Contrast: The later permission to eat living creatures explicitly compares that gift with the earlier gift of green plants, marking an expansion of the food allowance.
  Psalms 103:14-15 — Thematic connection: The psalm describes vegetation, bread and other produce as God's provision for human nourishment.

Genesis 1:30
And to every wild beast of the earth, every bird of heaven, and every creeping thing upon the earth that has life within it, I have given every green plant for food.” And it was so.
  Psalms 103:14 — Thematic connection: God's growing grass for cattle corresponds to the provision of green plants as animal food.
  Psalms 103:27-28 — Thematic connection: The psalm describes living creatures depending on God for food, corresponding to His assigning their food in Genesis.
  Genesis 9:3 — Contrast: The later gift of living creatures as food to humanity differs from the plant provision assigned to living beings in Genesis 1.

Genesis 1:31
And God saw all that He had made, and behold, it was very good. And there was evening, and there was morning: a sixth day.
  Psalms 103:24 — Thematic connection: The psalm praises the wisdom and abundance of all God's works, corresponding to the completed creation's goodness.
  Sirach 39:16 — Thematic connection: Sirach declares all the Lord's works very good, echoing the approval of all creation in Genesis.
  Genesis 2:1-3 — Narrative continuation: The next passage summarizes the completion of heaven and earth and God's rest after the sixth day's finished work.

Genesis 2:1
Thus heaven and earth were finished, and all their ordered array.
  Genesis 1:31 — Narrative continuation: The declaration that heaven and earth are finished follows the sixth-day assessment of everything God had made as very good.
  Exodus 20:11 — Explicit reuse: The Sabbath command summarizes God's making heaven, earth, sea and their contents, corresponding to the completed ordered creation.
  Hebrews 4:3-4 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews places God's works at the world's foundation and immediately cites His rest from those works, joining completion with rest.

Genesis 2:2
And on the sixth day God completed the works He had made, and on the seventh day He rested from all the works He had made.
  Hebrews 4:4 — Direct quotation: Hebrews explicitly quotes God's seventh-day rest from all His works; this corresponds to the rest clause, without changing Genesis's sixth-day completion wording.
  Exodus 20:11 — Explicit reuse: The Sabbath command recounts six days of creation followed by God's seventh-day rest, the sequence stated in the supplied Genesis reading.
  Exodus 31:17 — Explicit reuse: The Sabbath sign is grounded in God's making heaven and earth in six days and ceasing and resting on the seventh day.

Genesis 2:3
And God blessed the seventh day and hallowed it, because on it He rested from all the works that God had begun to make.
  Exodus 20:8-11 — Explicit reuse: The command to keep the Sabbath holy culminates in God's blessing and sanctifying the seventh day because of His creation rest.
  Exodus 31:15-17 — Explicit reuse: The holy seventh-day rest and its covenant observance are grounded in God's six-day work and seventh-day cessation.
  Hebrews 4:9-10 — Thematic connection: Hebrews applies God's rest from His works to the Sabbath rest remaining for His people; it develops the rest theme rather than quoting the blessing clause.

Genesis 2:4
This is the book of the origin of heaven and earth, when they came to be, on the day when God made heaven and earth,
  Genesis 1:1 — Narrative summary: The origin notice recalls God's making heaven and earth, the event announced at the beginning of the book.
  Genesis 5:1 — Literary parallel: Both notices introduce a book of origins or generations, moving from heaven and earth here to mankind in the later genealogy.
  Hebrews 11:3 — Thematic connection: Hebrews attributes the formation of the ages to God's word, corresponding to Genesis's attribution of heaven and earth's origin to God.

Genesis 2:5
and every green plant of the field before it appeared upon the earth, and every plant of the field before it sprang up. For God had not sent rain upon the earth, and there was no man to till the ground.
  Genesis 1:11-12 — Thematic comparison: Both passages concern vegetation arising upon the earth; Genesis 2 frames its field plants in relation to rain and human cultivation, without requiring an imposed harmonized chronology.
  Genesis 2:15 — Narrative development: The absence of a human cultivator anticipates God's placing the man in the garden to tend and keep it.
  Genesis 3:23 — Narrative development: The later expulsion sends the man to till the earth, continuing the cultivation task whose absence is noted here.
  Psalms 103:13-14 — Thematic connection: The psalm joins God's watering the earth with growing plants for human service, corresponding to the moisture and cultivation conditions named in Genesis.

Genesis 2:6
But a spring rose from the earth and watered the whole face of the ground.
  Psalms 103:10 — Shared imagery: The psalm describes God sending springs through ravines, providing a poetic counterpart to the spring watering the ground here.
  Psalms 103:13 — Thematic connection: God's watering of mountains so that the earth is fruitful corresponds to the provision of water for the ground in Genesis.
  Genesis 2:10 — Narrative development: The garden river continues the chapter's account of water sustaining the land; the two water descriptions are linked without asserting identical courses.

Genesis 2:7
And God formed man from the dust of the earth, and breathed into his face the breath of life; and man became a living soul.
  Genesis 3:19 — Explicit reuse: The sentence of return to earth recalls the earth from which the human was taken, making mortality the reversal of this formation from dust.
  1 Corinthians 15:45-49 — Direct quotation and interpretation: Paul quotes the first Adam becoming a living soul and contrasts the earthly dust-made human with the life-giving last Adam.
  Job 33:4-6 — Thematic connection: Job's speaker says divine spirit made him and that he and his hearer were fashioned from clay. This parallels divine agency and earthly material; the supplied Job reading says the Almighty's breath teaches him, not that it gives him life.
  Sirach 17:1 — Explicit reuse: Sirach states that the Lord created a human from earth and returned him to it, recalling both this formation and its later mortal reversal.
  Psalms 103:29-30 — Thematic connection: The psalm connects the withdrawal of breath with return to dust and God's sending His Spirit with creation, corresponding to dependence on God's gift of life.
  Acts 17:24-26 — Thematic connection: Paul presents the Creator as giver of everyone's life and breath and origin of humanity, corresponding to God's forming and breathing life into the first human.

Genesis 2:8
And the Lord God planted a garden in Eden, toward the east, and there He placed the man whom He had formed.
  Genesis 2:15 — Narrative development: The later placement notice states the purpose of the man's presence in the garden: to tend it and keep it.
  Genesis 3:23-24 — Narrative reversal: God's placing the human in the garden is reversed by expulsion from it and settlement opposite it after transgression.
  Ezekiel 28:13 — Shared imagery: Ezekiel's lament locates its figure in the delight of God's paradise, reusing a divine-garden setting without identifying its ruler of Tyre with Adam.

Genesis 2:9
And God caused every tree that was beautiful to behold and good for food to spring up from the earth, with the tree of life in the midst of the garden, and the tree of the knowledge of good and evil.
  Genesis 3:6 — Narrative development: The woman later considers the prohibited tree good for food and pleasing to the eyes, recalling the garden's food-bearing and attractive trees while leading to transgression.
  Genesis 3:22-24 — Narrative development: The tree of life introduced here later becomes the object of guarded access after the human's expulsion.
  Proverbs 3:18 — Shared imagery: Wisdom is compared to a tree of life for those who hold her, extending the life-giving tree image into wisdom instruction.
  Ezekiel 47:12 — Shared imagery: The vision's riverbank trees supply enduring fruit for food and growth for health, paralleling the garden's nourishing tree imagery without equating the two settings.

Genesis 2:10
And a river flows out of Eden to water the garden, and from there it divides into four branches.
  Sirach 24:25-27 — Allusion: Sirach uses the Pishon, Tigris, Euphrates and Gihon as images of abundant wisdom and instruction, recalling the four rivers in the surrounding Eden description.
  Ezekiel 47:1-12 — Shared imagery: Water flowing from the holy place gives life and sustains food-bearing trees, a visionary counterpart to the river watering the garden; no geographical identity is asserted.
  Psalms 103:10-14 — Thematic connection: The psalm's flowing waters sustain animals, birds and vegetation, corresponding to the garden river's provision of water for life.

Genesis 2:11
The name of the first is Pishon; it flows around the whole land of Havilah, where there is gold.
  Sirach 24:25 — Allusion: Sirach compares wisdom's abundance to the Pishon, reusing the river named first in the Eden description rather than specifying its geographical location.
  Genesis 2:12 — Narrative continuation: The next verse develops the description of Havilah by identifying its good gold and other stones, explaining the mineral notice attached to the Pishon.

Genesis 2:12
And the gold of that land is good; there also are the carbuncle and the green stone.
  Ezekiel 28:13 — Shared imagery: Ezekiel's paradise description combines gold and precious stones, including carbuncle, with the divine garden setting, corresponding to this garden-region mineral imagery.

Genesis 2:13
The name of the second river is Gihon; it flows around the whole land of Ethiopia.
  Sirach 24:27 — Allusion: Sirach likens abundant instruction to the Gihon, reusing the river name from the Eden account without settling the location of its course.

Genesis 2:14
The third river is the Tigris; it flows opposite Assyria. And the fourth river is the Euphrates.
  Sirach 24:25-26 — Allusion: Sirach pairs images of the Tigris and Euphrates with overflowing wisdom and understanding, reusing both rivers named in this verse.

Genesis 2:15
And the Lord God took the man whom He had formed and placed him in the garden, to tend it and keep it.
  Genesis 2:5 — Narrative development: The man now receives the tending task whose absence was noted when the field lacked a human cultivator.
  Genesis 3:23 — Narrative contrast: Cultivation continues after expulsion, but the place changes from the garden God provided to the earth outside it.
  Sirach 7:15 — Thematic connection: Sirach commends laborious work and farming as created by the Most High, corresponding to the pre-transgression cultivation task given by God.
  Genesis 1:28 — Thematic connection: The garden's tending and keeping task is a particular form of the human responsibility within creation introduced by the earlier dominion commission.

Genesis 2:16
And the Lord God commanded Adam, saying, “You may freely eat from every tree in the garden;
  Genesis 1:29 — Thematic connection: The permission to eat the garden's trees continues God's gift of fruit-bearing trees and seed-bearing plants as human food.
  Genesis 3:2-3 — Explicit reuse: The woman later reports both the permission to eat garden fruit and its exception, recalling the command begun here.
  Genesis 3:11 — Explicit reuse: God's later question recalls the one tree excluded by His command, showing that the generous permission had a specific limit.

Genesis 2:17
but from the tree of the knowledge of good and evil you shall not eat. For on the day you eat from it, you shall surely die.”
  Genesis 3:3-6 — Narrative development: The prohibition and death warning are recalled, denied by the serpent and then transgressed when the woman and man eat.
  Genesis 3:17-19 — Narrative consequence: The later sentence explicitly cites eating from the forbidden tree and ends in return to earth, developing the death warning given here.
  Romans 5:12-14 — Theological interpretation: Paul connects Adam's transgression with sin and death entering human life, interpreting the disobedience and mortality anticipated by the command's warning.
  Wisdom of Solomon 2:23-24 — Thematic connection: Wisdom contrasts God's purpose of human incorruption with death entering through the devil's envy, corresponding to the life-and-death stakes of the garden prohibition.

Genesis 2:18
And the Lord God said, “It is not good for the man to be alone. Let Us make him a helper corresponding to him.”
  Genesis 2:20-23 — Narrative fulfilment: The failure to find a corresponding helper among animals is answered by the woman's formation and the man's recognition of shared bone and flesh.
  Ecclesiastes 4:9-10 — Thematic connection: Ecclesiastes describes the practical good of partnership and help when one falls, corresponding to the need for companionship rather than human isolation.
  1 Corinthians 11:8-12 — Explicit reuse: Paul recalls the woman's formation from and for the man, then qualifies the discussion with mutual dependence in the Lord.

Genesis 2:19
And God also formed from the earth every wild beast of the field and every bird of heaven, and brought them to Adam to see what he would call them. And whatever Adam called each living creature, that was its name.
  Genesis 1:24-25 — Narrative parallel: Both creation accounts attribute the earth's animal life to God's forming activity; this scene focuses on bringing creatures to the human for naming.
  Genesis 2:20 — Narrative fulfilment: The naming task introduced by bringing the creatures to Adam is carried out in the following verse.
  Psalms 8:7-9 — Thematic connection: The psalm places animals and birds under human authority, corresponding to the human role expressed in the naming scene.

Genesis 2:20
And Adam gave names to all the cattle, all the birds of heaven, and all the wild beasts of the field. But for Adam no helper like himself was found.
  Genesis 2:18 — Narrative development: The absence of an animal helper corresponding to Adam clarifies the need identified in God's earlier declaration about being alone.
  Genesis 2:23 — Narrative contrast: The man's recognition of the woman as his bone and flesh contrasts with the absence of a corresponding helper among the named animals.

Genesis 2:21
And God cast a deep sleep upon Adam, and he slept. And He took one of his ribs and filled its place with flesh.
  Genesis 2:22 — Narrative continuation: The rib taken while Adam sleeps is the material God fashions into the woman in the following verse.
  1 Corinthians 11:8 — Explicit reuse: Paul's statement that woman is from man recalls the origin represented by God's taking the rib from Adam.

Genesis 2:22
And the Lord God fashioned the rib He had taken from Adam into a woman, and brought her to Adam.
  1 Corinthians 11:8-12 — Explicit reuse: Paul recalls woman's origin from man and then affirms the subsequent mutual dependence of women and men and God's ultimate agency.
  1 Timothy 2:13 — Explicit reuse: The letter recalls Adam being formed before Eve, appealing to the creation sequence in which the woman is formed after the man.
  Genesis 2:18 — Narrative fulfilment: God's formation and presentation of the woman answers the earlier intention to provide a helper corresponding to the man.

Genesis 2:23
And Adam said: This, at last, is bone of my bones and flesh of my flesh. She shall be called Woman, for she was taken from her husband.
  Genesis 29:14 — Shared wording: Laban recognizes Jacob as his bones and flesh, reusing kinship language comparable to Adam's recognition of the woman's shared human nature.
  Ephesians 5:28-31 — Theological interpretation: The exhortation to love a wife as one's own body develops shared-flesh reasoning and culminates in quotation of the following Genesis marriage statement.
  1 Corinthians 11:8 — Explicit reuse: Paul's statement that woman is from man recalls Adam's recognition that the woman was taken from him.

Genesis 2:24
For this reason a man shall leave his father and his mother and cleave to his wife, and the two shall become one flesh.
  Matthew 19:5-6 — Direct quotation: Jesus quotes leaving father and mother, joining the wife and becoming one flesh, then draws the conclusion that God-joined spouses must not be separated.
  Mark 10:7-9 — Direct quotation: Jesus' marriage teaching quotes the departure from parents and one-flesh union and interprets it as a union God has joined.
  Ephesians 5:31-32 — Direct quotation and interpretation: Ephesians quotes the one-flesh statement and applies its great mystery with reference to Christ and the Church.
  1 Corinthians 6:16 — Direct quotation and application: Paul quotes the two becoming one flesh when warning about sexual union with a prostitute, applying the text's account of bodily union.

Genesis 2:25
And the two were naked, both Adam and his wife, and they were not ashamed.
  Genesis 3:7 — Narrative contrast: The unashamed nakedness before transgression is reversed when the pair recognize their nakedness and make coverings.
  Genesis 3:10 — Narrative contrast: Adam's later fear and hiding because of nakedness contrast with the absence of shame stated here.
  Genesis 3:21 — Narrative development: God's later clothing of Adam and his wife answers their changed condition, contrasting with the initial state of unashamed nakedness.

Genesis 3:1
Now the serpent was more cunning than all the wild beasts upon the earth that the Lord God had made. And the serpent said to the woman, “Why has God said, ‘You shall not eat from any tree in the garden’?”
  2 Corinthians 11:3 — Explicit reuse: Paul explicitly recalls the serpent's cunning deception of Eve while warning about minds being led away from devotion to Christ.
  1 Timothy 2:14 — Explicit reuse: The letter recalls the woman's deception and transgression, corresponding to the serpent's approach to the woman in Genesis.
  Genesis 2:16-17 — Contrast: The serpent's question about a prohibition on every tree contrasts with God's permission to eat freely and His prohibition concerning one tree.

Genesis 3:2
And the woman said to the serpent, “We may eat of the fruit of the trees in the garden.
  Genesis 2:16 — Narrative parallel: The woman's statement that the garden's fruit may be eaten recalls God's earlier general permission concerning its trees.
  Genesis 1:29 — Thematic connection: The permission to eat tree fruit continues the provision of seed-bearing plants and fruit-bearing trees as human food.

Genesis 3:3
But of the fruit of the tree in the midst of the garden, God said, ‘You shall not eat of it, nor shall you touch it, lest you die.’”
  Genesis 2:17 — Explicit reuse: The woman recounts the prohibition and death warning associated with the tree of the knowledge of good and evil.
  Genesis 3:11 — Narrative continuation: God's later question identifies eating from the prohibited tree as the command that has been breached.

Genesis 3:4
And the serpent said to the woman, “You shall surely not die.
  John 8:44 — Thematic connection: Jesus associates the devil with lying and murder from the beginning; this illuminates the serpent's denial of the death warning without supplying a direct quotation.
  Wisdom of Solomon 2:24 — Thematic connection: Wisdom connects the devil's envy with death entering the world, contrasting with the tempter's assurance that death will not follow.
  Genesis 2:17 — Contrast: The serpent's assurance that they will not die directly contradicts God's warning about eating from the prohibited tree.

Genesis 3:5
For God knew that on the day you ate of it, your eyes would be opened, and you would be as gods, knowing good and evil.”
  Genesis 3:7 — Narrative fulfilment: The account later says that both people's eyes were opened, corresponding to this portion of the serpent's promise.
  Genesis 3:22 — Explicit reuse: God's later statement about the human knowing good and evil repeats the knowledge language used in the temptation.
  2 Corinthians 11:3 — Explicit reuse: Paul's warning recalls the serpent's deception of Eve, providing an explicit later reference to this persuasive episode.

Genesis 3:6
And the woman saw that the tree was good for food, pleasing to the eyes, and beautiful to contemplate. And she took of its fruit and ate; she also gave some to her husband with her, and they ate.
  James 1:14-15 — Thematic connection: James describes desire drawing a person into sin and sin producing death, corresponding to the sequence of attraction, eating and its consequences.
  1 John 2:16 — Thematic connection: The letter's contrast of bodily desire and the desire of the eyes with the Father's will illuminates the fruit's bodily and visual attraction.
  Romans 5:19 — Explicit reuse: Paul contrasts the disobedience of the one human with another's obedience, recalling Adam's participation in the transgression.
  Genesis 2:17 — Contrast: Eating the fruit violates the particular prohibition and death warning God had given concerning this tree.

Genesis 3:7
And the eyes of both were opened, and they knew that they were naked. And they sewed fig leaves together and made coverings for themselves.
  Genesis 2:25 — Contrast: Their newly perceived nakedness and improvised coverings contrast with the earlier nakedness that caused no shame.
  Genesis 3:21 — Narrative continuation: God later clothes the couple with skin garments, following their own attempt to cover themselves with fig leaves.
  Genesis 3:5 — Narrative fulfilment: The opening of their eyes corresponds to the serpent's earlier promise, while their discovery is expressed as awareness of nakedness.

Genesis 3:8
And they heard the sound of the Lord God walking in the garden toward evening. And Adam and his wife hid themselves from the face of the Lord God among the trees of the garden.
  Psalms 138:7-12 — Contrast: The psalm asks where one could flee from God's face and denies that distance or darkness conceals a person, contrasting with hiding among the trees.
  Genesis 4:16 — Narrative parallel: Cain's later departure from God's face continues the narrative theme of alienation from the divine presence after wrongdoing.
  Genesis 3:24 — Narrative continuation: The attempted hiding within the garden is followed by expulsion and a guarded way back to the tree of life.

Genesis 3:9
And the Lord God called to Adam and said to him, “Adam, where are you?”
  Genesis 4:9 — Narrative parallel: God's question to Cain about Abel parallels His question to Adam, confronting a person through an inquiry after wrongdoing.
  Psalms 138:1-5 — Thematic connection: The psalm portrays God as already knowing a person's actions and words, giving context for reading the divine question as confrontation rather than a proof of ignorance.

Genesis 3:10
And he said to Him, “I heard Thy voice as Thou didst walk in the garden, and I was afraid because I am naked, and I hid myself.”
  Genesis 2:25 — Contrast: Adam's fear about being naked contrasts with the original description of the couple's unashamed nakedness.
  Hebrews 4:13 — Thematic connection: Hebrews describes every creature as exposed before God's eyes, corresponding to the futility of concealing oneself from Him.
  Psalms 138:7-12 — Contrast: The psalm's account of God's presence even in darkness contrasts with Adam's attempt to escape that presence by hiding.

Genesis 3:11
And He said to him, “Who told you that you are naked? Have you eaten from the tree of which alone I commanded you not to eat?”
  Genesis 2:17 — Explicit reuse: God's question recalls the specific tree prohibition that Adam had received before the temptation.
  Genesis 3:7 — Narrative continuation: The question about the discovery of nakedness refers back to the couple's opened eyes and their making of coverings.
  Genesis 4:9-10 — Narrative parallel: The questioning of Cain similarly moves from an attempted concealment to God's exposure of the wrongdoing.

Genesis 3:12
And Adam said, “The woman whom Thou didst give to be with me—she gave me fruit from the tree, and I ate.”
  Genesis 3:6 — Narrative continuation: Adam's statement that the woman gave him the fruit recounts the transfer and eating already narrated.
  Genesis 2:18-23 — Contrast: Adam's blame refers to the woman God gave him, contrasting with the earlier presentation of the woman as his suitable companion.
  Proverbs 28:13 — Thematic connection: The proverb warns that concealing one's impiety does not prosper, illuminating Adam's attempt to redirect responsibility for eating.

Genesis 3:13
And the Lord God said to the woman, “What is this you have done?” And the woman said, “The serpent deceived me, and I ate.”
  2 Corinthians 11:3 — Explicit reuse: Paul explicitly names the serpent's deception of Eve, corresponding to the explanation the woman gives here.
  1 Timothy 2:14 — Explicit reuse: The letter recalls the woman's being deceived and entering transgression, corresponding to her admission of deception and eating.
  Genesis 3:1-6 — Narrative continuation: The woman's explanation points back to the serpent's speech and the resulting eating described earlier in the chapter.

Genesis 3:14
And the Lord God said to the serpent: Because you have done this, cursed are you above all cattle and all the wild beasts of the earth. Upon your breast and belly you shall go, and earth you shall eat all the days of your life.
  Isaiah 65:25 — Shared imagery: Isaiah's vision includes the serpent eating earth, recalling the serpent's assigned food in the Genesis judgment.
  Genesis 1:24-25 — Contrast: The serpent's curse among the animals contrasts with God's earlier making and approving the land creatures.

Genesis 3:15
And I will put enmity between you and the woman, and between your seed and her seed. He shall watch for your head, and you shall watch for his heel.
  Romans 16:20 — Thematic connection: Paul's promise that God will crush Satan under believers' feet forms a Christian canonical association with conflict involving the serpent's head and the human heel; it is not a quotation of this wording.
  Luke 10:19 — Thematic connection: Jesus speaks of authority to tread on serpents and overcome the enemy's power, forming a later thematic association with human conflict against the serpent rather than a direct quotation.
  Genesis 3:14 — Narrative continuation: The sentence about enmity continues God's judgment on the serpent and explains its hostile relation to the woman's descendants.

Genesis 3:16
And to the woman He said: I will greatly multiply your sorrows and your groaning; in sorrow you shall bear children. You shall turn toward your husband, and he shall rule over you.
  John 16:21 — Shared imagery: Jesus describes a woman's sorrow during childbirth and joy after the birth, using the same connection between bearing children and pain.
  Genesis 35:16-19 — Narrative parallel: Rachel's difficult and fatal childbirth provides a particular later narrative instance of the sorrow associated with childbearing.
  1 Timothy 2:13-15 — Explicit reuse: The passage joins the creation of Adam and Eve, the woman's deception and childbearing; the link does not settle the disputed interpretation of its final statement.

Genesis 3:17
And to Adam He said: Because you listened to the voice of your wife and ate from the tree of which alone I commanded you not to eat, cursed is the earth in your labours; in pain you shall eat of it all the days of your life.
  Genesis 2:16-17 — Explicit reuse: The judgment identifies the eating as a breach of the earlier command permitting the garden's trees except the prohibited one.
  Genesis 5:29 — Explicit reuse: Lamech names Noah in hope of relief from work on the earth that the Lord cursed, explicitly recalling the condition imposed here.
  Romans 8:20-22 — Thematic connection: Paul describes creation subjected to futility and groaning, offering a later theological parallel to the earth's painful condition under judgment.
  Genesis 8:21 — Explicit reuse: After Noah's offering, God speaks of not again cursing the earth because of human deeds, recalling the connection between human conduct and the ground's curse.

Genesis 3:18
Thorns and thistles it shall bring forth for you, and you shall eat the plants of the field.
  Hebrews 6:7-8 — Shared imagery: Hebrews contrasts useful crops with ground producing thorns and thistles and being near a curse, closely matching the agricultural judgment imagery.
  Genesis 2:5 — Contrast: The earlier garden setting describes the field's plants and the absence of a human tiller, contrasting with the later condition of troublesome vegetation and human toil.
  Genesis 1:29 — Contrast: The earlier gift of seed-bearing plants as food is now accompanied by thorns, thistles and the difficulty of obtaining sustenance.

Genesis 3:19
By the sweat of your face you shall eat your bread, until you return to the earth from which you were taken; for earth you are, and unto earth you shall return.
  Genesis 2:7 — Explicit reuse: The return to earth recalls the earlier formation of the human from earth, linking human origin with mortality.
  Ecclesiastes 3:20 — Thematic connection: Ecclesiastes says all came from earth and return to earth, matching the mortality and return described in Genesis.
  Psalms 103:29 — Thematic connection: The psalm describes creatures dying and returning to their earth when their spirit is removed, corresponding to the human return to earth.
  Romans 5:12 — Explicit reuse: Paul links sin and death entering the world through one human, reflecting on the mortality associated with Adam's transgression.
  Hebrews 9:27 — Thematic connection: The appointment of humans to die once corresponds to the mortality pronounced over Adam, without identifying the two passages' different immediate purposes.

Genesis 3:20
And Adam called his wife’s name Life, because she was the mother of all the living.
  Genesis 4:1 — Same person or family: The following narrative names Adam's wife Eve and describes the first recorded birth, continuing the maternal identity introduced by her naming.
  Genesis 2:23 — Literary parallel: Adam's earlier naming of Woman and his later naming of Life both attach an explanation to the woman's designation, while the reasons differ.
  Genesis 5:2 — Literary parallel: The later genealogy likewise joins the naming of humanity with the creation of male and female, providing another naming statement in the account of human origins.

Genesis 3:21
And the Lord God made garments of skin for Adam and his wife, and clothed them.
  Genesis 3:7 — Narrative continuation: The skin garments provided by God follow and replace the couple's attempt to fashion coverings from fig leaves.
  Matthew 6:30 — Thematic connection: Jesus' statement about God's clothing the grass and caring for people offers a later teaching parallel to God providing clothing for the couple.

Genesis 3:22
And God said, “Behold, Adam has become as one of Us, knowing good and evil. And now, lest he stretch forth his hand and take also from the tree of life, and eat, and live for ever—”
  Genesis 2:9 — Narrative continuation: The tree whose fruit could sustain life is the tree of life earlier located within the garden.
  Genesis 3:5 — Explicit reuse: The statement about knowing good and evil repeats the language of the serpent's promise, while the threat of exclusion from life remains.
  Proverbs 3:18 — Shared imagery: Wisdom is described as a tree of life for those who hold to her, reusing the life-giving tree image in a wisdom setting rather than identifying a literal garden tree.

Genesis 3:23
And the Lord God sent him out of the garden of delight to till the earth from which he had been taken.
  Genesis 2:15 — Contrast: The human's removal to till the ground contrasts with his earlier placement within the garden to work and guard it.
  Genesis 2:7 — Explicit reuse: The ground to be tilled is identified with the earth from which the human was formed earlier.
  Wisdom of Solomon 10:1 — Explicit reuse: Wisdom recalls the first-formed father of the world and his transgression, providing a later reflection on Adam's fall rather than a new account of the expulsion.

Genesis 3:24
And He drove Adam out and settled him opposite the garden of delight. And He stationed the cherubim and the flaming sword that turns about, to guard the way to the tree of life.
  Genesis 2:9 — Narrative continuation: The guarded tree of life is the tree previously situated in the garden's midst.
  Genesis 3:22 — Narrative fulfilment: The guarded approach to the tree implements the stated purpose of preventing the human from eating and living for ever.
  Genesis 3:23 — Narrative continuation: The expulsion and guarded route elaborate the preceding statement that God sent the human out of the garden.

Genesis 4:1
Now Adam knew Eve his wife, and she conceived and bore Cain, and said, “I have gained a man through God.”
  Genesis 3:20 — Same person or family: The naming of Adam's wife as mother of the living is followed by her conception and the birth of Cain.
  Genesis 1:28 — Narrative fulfilment: The birth of a child begins the narrative outworking of the blessing to grow, multiply and fill the earth.
  Psalms 126:3 — Thematic connection: The psalm describes children and the fruit of the womb as the Lord's gift, corresponding to Eve's attribution of her child to God's agency.

Genesis 4:2
And again she bore his brother Abel. And Abel became a shepherd of sheep, but Cain tilled the earth.
  Genesis 3:23 — Narrative continuation: Cain's work as a tiller of the earth continues the human task assigned after the expulsion from the garden.
  Genesis 46:32-34 — Thematic connection: Joseph's family is later described as shepherds and keepers of livestock, corresponding to Abel's pastoral occupation.
  Genesis 4:3-4 — Narrative continuation: The brothers' different occupations explain the contrasting materials of their offerings: produce from Cain and flock animals from Abel.

Genesis 4:3
And after some time Cain brought an offering to the Lord from the fruits of the earth.
  Proverbs 3:9 — Thematic connection: The proverb instructs honoring the Lord with firstfruits from righteous labors, paralleling Cain's bringing fruit while adding a righteousness qualification that this verse does not itself supply.
  Exodus 23:19 — Thematic connection: The law's instruction to bring the first produce of the earth to God supplies a later agricultural-offering parallel, without claiming Cain followed that later law.

Genesis 4:4
And Abel also brought an offering from the firstborn of his sheep and from their fat. And God looked with favour upon Abel and his gifts,
  Hebrews 11:4 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews explicitly recalls Abel's offering as accepted through faith and contrasts it with Cain's offering.
  Matthew 23:35 — Same person or family: Jesus identifies Abel as righteous when recalling the shedding of innocent blood, corresponding to the Abel favored in Genesis.

Genesis 4:5
but He gave no heed to Cain and his offerings. And Cain was greatly distressed, and his face fell.
  Hebrews 11:4 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews explicitly contrasts Abel's better offering with Cain's and associates God's favorable testimony with faith.
  1 John 3:12 — Explicit reuse: The letter contrasts Cain's evil works with his brother's righteous works, providing a later moral account of the brothers' difference.
  Proverbs 15:8 — Thematic connection: The proverb contrasts the Lord's rejection of the impious person's sacrifices with acceptance of upright prayer, corresponding to differing divine responses to worship.
  Wisdom of Solomon 10:3 — Explicit reuse: Wisdom describes an unrighteous man departing in anger and killing his brother, recalling the anger that leads into Cain's act.

Genesis 4:6
And the Lord God said to Cain, “Why are you so distressed, and why has your face fallen?
  James 1:19-20 — Thematic connection: James warns that human anger does not produce God's righteousness, corresponding to God's confrontation of Cain's angry disposition.
  Proverbs 14:29 — Thematic connection: The proverb praises a patient person's understanding, providing a wisdom contrast to Cain's distress before he acts against his brother.
  Genesis 4:7 — Narrative continuation: God's following words address Cain's offering and urge stillness, continuing the intervention concerning his distress.

Genesis 4:7
If you offer rightly but do not divide rightly, have you not sinned? Be still. His turning shall be toward you, and you shall rule over him.”
  1 Samuel 15:22 — Thematic connection: Samuel distinguishes obedience from merely presenting sacrifice, providing a later parallel to God's scrutiny of how Cain offers.
  Sirach 35:1-3 — Thematic connection: Sirach associates acceptable offering with keeping commandments and withdrawing from injustice, linking worship with conduct rather than gifts alone.
  Psalms 50:18-19 — Thematic connection: The psalm values a broken and humbled spirit over the mere presentation of burnt offerings, offering a worship-and-disposition parallel without resolving Genesis's difficult wording.

Genesis 4:8
And Cain said to Abel his brother, “Let us go out into the field.” And while they were in the field, Cain rose against Abel his brother and slew him.
  1 John 3:12 — Explicit reuse: The letter explicitly recalls Cain killing his brother and contrasts the brothers' works.
  Jude 1:11 — Explicit reuse: Jude's warning names the way of Cain as a negative pattern, recalling Cain's wrongdoing rather than supplying additional details of the murder.
  Matthew 23:35 — Explicit reuse: Jesus names righteous Abel's shed blood as an early instance in the history of innocent bloodshed.
  Hebrews 11:4 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews recalls Abel's faith and says he still speaks after death, reflecting on the brother murdered in Genesis.
  Wisdom of Solomon 10:3 — Explicit reuse: Wisdom's account of an angry unrighteous man killing his brother corresponds to Cain's fratricide.

Genesis 4:9
And God said to Cain, “Where is Abel your brother?” And he said, “I do not know. Am I my brother’s keeper?”
  Genesis 3:9-13 — Narrative parallel: God's questioning after the garden transgression parallels the questioning of Cain after his brother's death.
  Proverbs 24:11-12 — Thematic connection: The command to rescue those facing death rejects a plea of ignorance because God knows hearts, contrasting with Cain's denial of responsibility for Abel.
  1 John 3:11-12 — Explicit reuse: The command to love one another is contrasted with Cain's murder, answering the refusal of brotherly responsibility expressed in his question.

Genesis 4:10
And God said, “What have you done? The voice of your brother’s blood cries unto Me from the earth.
  Hebrews 12:24 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews compares Jesus' sprinkled blood with Abel's, explicitly recalling the speaking blood associated with the murder.
  Matthew 23:35 — Explicit reuse: Jesus' reference to Abel's righteous blood corresponds to God's exposure of that bloodshed.
  Psalms 9:13 — Thematic connection: The psalm joins God's accounting for bloodshed with His remembrance of the needy person's cry, corresponding to Abel's blood crying to God.

Genesis 4:11
And now you are cursed from the earth, which opened its mouth to receive your brother’s blood from your hand.
  Numbers 35:33 — Thematic connection: The later law says bloodshed pollutes the land, corresponding to the earth's receiving Abel's blood and the resulting curse on Cain.
  Job 16:18 — Shared imagery: Job asks the earth not to cover his blood and his cry not to be silenced, closely paralleling the earth, blood and appeal imagery in Abel's case.
  Genesis 3:17 — Literary parallel: Cain's curse in relation to the ground recalls the earlier judgment connecting Adam's transgression with the cursed earth, though the judgments are distinct.

Genesis 4:12
When you till the earth, it shall no longer yield its strength to you. You shall be groaning and trembling upon the earth.”
  Genesis 3:17-19 — Narrative parallel: Cain's impaired cultivation and painful existence intensify the earlier judgment of toil upon the earth and return to it.
  Deuteronomy 28:38 — Thematic connection: The threatened failure of much sowing to produce a harvest supplies a later agricultural-judgment parallel to Cain's unyielding ground.

Genesis 4:13
And Cain said to the Lord, “My guilt is too great for me to be forgiven.
  Psalms 50:3-4 — Contrast: The psalm appeals to God's abundant mercy for cleansing from sin, contrasting with Cain's declaration that his guilt is beyond forgiveness in this edition.
  1 John 1:9 — Contrast: The promise of forgiveness and cleansing upon confession contrasts with Cain's statement that his guilt is too great to be forgiven.
  Psalms 31:5 — Contrast: The psalm describes acknowledging sin and receiving forgiveness, offering a contrast to Cain's expressed hopelessness about forgiveness.

Genesis 4:14
If Thou dost cast me out this day from the face of the earth, I shall be hidden from Thy face; and I shall be groaning and trembling upon the earth, and whoever finds me shall slay me.”
  Genesis 3:8 — Narrative parallel: Cain's fear of being hidden from God's face recalls the earlier attempt by Adam and his wife to hide from that presence.
  Genesis 4:15 — Narrative continuation: God's following promise and protective mark respond directly to Cain's fear that anyone finding him will kill him.
  Psalms 138:7-12 — Contrast: The psalm denies the possibility of escaping God's face by distance or darkness, providing a theological contrast to Cain's fear of being hidden from Him.

Genesis 4:15
And the Lord God said to him, “Not so. Whoever slays Cain shall incur sevenfold vengeance.” And the Lord God set a mark upon Cain, that no one finding him should slay him.
  Genesis 4:24 — Explicit reuse: Lamech's later statement repeats the sevenfold vengeance attached to Cain and escalates the number for himself.
  Romans 12:19 — Thematic connection: Paul reserves vengeance to God rather than private retaliation, corresponding to God's taking charge of the consequences of killing Cain.

Genesis 4:16
And Cain went out from the face of God and dwelt in the land of Nod, opposite Eden.
  Genesis 3:23-24 — Narrative parallel: Cain's departure to a place opposite Eden follows the earlier expulsion of Adam and settlement opposite the garden.
  Genesis 4:14 — Narrative fulfilment: Cain's departure from God's face continues the exclusion he had feared in his appeal to God.

Genesis 4:17
And Cain knew his wife, and she conceived and bore Enoch. And he was building a city, and named the city after his son Enoch.
  Genesis 11:4 — Thematic connection: The Babel account again links building a city with establishing a name, corresponding to Cain's naming his city after his son without asserting that the two cities are identical.
  Genesis 5:18 — Contrast: The later Enoch belongs to Jared's line, whereas this Enoch is Cain's son; the matching name must not merge the two genealogical identities.

Genesis 4:18
And to Enoch was born Gaidad; and Gaidad begot Maiel; and Maiel begot Methuselah; and Methuselah begot Lamech.
  Genesis 5:18-25 — Contrast: Both family lists contain Enoch, Methuselah and Lamech, but in different lines and sequences; the parallel warns against conflating Cain's descendants with Seth's genealogy.

Genesis 4:19
And Lamech took two wives. The name of the first was Adah, and the name of the second was Zillah.
  Genesis 2:24 — Contrast: Lamech's taking two wives differs from the earlier joining of a man to his wife as one flesh; the comparison records the narrative difference without adding an explicit judgment absent here.
  Genesis 4:23 — Same person or family: The women named as Lamech's wives are the Adah and Zillah whom he directly addresses in his later speech.

Genesis 4:20
And Adah bore Jabal; he was the father of those who dwell in tents and keep livestock.
  Genesis 4:2 — Thematic connection: Jabal's association with keeping livestock develops the pastoral occupation already represented by Abel.
  Genesis 46:32-34 — Thematic connection: Joseph's family later identifies its inherited livelihood as keeping livestock, corresponding to the pastoral way of life associated with Jabal.

Genesis 4:21
His brother’s name was Jubal; he introduced the psaltery and the lyre.
  Psalms 150:3 — Shared imagery: The psalm names the psaltery and lyre as instruments of praise, the same instrument pair associated with Jubal's introduction of music.
  Genesis 31:27 — Shared imagery: Laban's proposed send-off includes music with a lyre, showing another use of the instrument associated here with Jubal.

Genesis 4:22
And Zillah also bore Thobel, a smith who worked bronze and iron with the hammer. And Thobel’s sister was Naamah.
  Exodus 31:3-5 — Thematic connection: The tabernacle craftsman's work includes skilled metalworking in bronze, providing a later craft parallel to Thobel's bronze and iron work.
  1 Chronicles 22:3 — Shared imagery: David prepares iron and abundant bronze for construction, matching the two metals worked by Thobel, without implying personal identity between the craftsmen.

Genesis 4:23
And Lamech said to his wives: Adah and Zillah, hear my voice; wives of Lamech, give ear to my words. For I have slain a man to my own hurt, and a young man to my own bruising.
  Genesis 4:8 — Narrative parallel: Lamech's statement about slaying a man follows Cain's earlier homicide, continuing the narrative's account of violence within Cain's line.
  Exodus 21:24-25 — Contrast: The later law pairs bodily injuries with corresponding injuries, contrasting with Lamech's declaration of slaying in connection with his own hurt and bruising.
  Matthew 5:38-39 — Contrast: Jesus' response to the eye-for-eye principle opposes retaliation, contrasting with Lamech's speech of slaying and heightened vengeance.

Genesis 4:24
For Cain has been avenged sevenfold, but Lamech seventy times seven.
  Genesis 4:15 — Explicit reuse: Lamech explicitly refers to Cain's sevenfold protection and increases the vengeance he claims for himself.
  Matthew 18:21-22 — Contrast: Jesus uses the multiplied seven expression for repeated forgiveness, reversing the escalating vengeance associated with Lamech's boast.

Genesis 4:25
And Adam knew Eve his wife, and she conceived and bore a son, and named him Seth, saying, “For God has raised up another seed for me in place of Abel, whom Cain slew.”
  Genesis 5:3 — Same person or family: The later genealogy records Adam fathering Seth and describes him in Adam's likeness, continuing the birth reported here.
  Genesis 4:8 — Narrative continuation: Eve's explanation of Seth's place in the family explicitly recalls Abel's murder by Cain.
  1 Chronicles 1:1 — Same person or family: Chronicles begins its genealogy with Adam, Seth and Enosh, including the son whose birth is reported here.

Genesis 4:26
And to Seth also a son was born, and he named him Enosh. He hoped to call upon the name of the Lord God.
  Genesis 5:6 — Same person or family: The later genealogy records Seth's fathering Enosh, corresponding to the family relationship introduced here.
  1 Chronicles 1:1 — Same person or family: Chronicles' opening sequence Adam, Seth and Enosh confirms Enosh's place in the family line.
  Genesis 12:8 — Shared wording: Abram later calls upon the Lord's name at an altar, corresponding to the hope of calling upon that name associated with Enosh in this edition.

Genesis 5:1
This is the book of the generations of mankind. On the day when God made Adam, He made him in the image of God.
  Genesis 1:26-27 — Explicit reuse: The genealogical introduction restates the creation of Adam in God's image, the defining description of humanity in the creation account.
  James 3:9 — Thematic connection: James grounds the treatment of human beings in their being made in God's likeness, corresponding to the creation identity recalled here.
  Genesis 9:6 — Explicit reuse: The prohibition of bloodshed appeals again to God's making humanity in His image, repeating the basis of human dignity recalled in this genealogy.

Genesis 5:2
Male and female He made them, and He blessed them, and named them Adam on the day when He made them.
  Genesis 1:27-28 — Explicit reuse: The making of male and female and their blessing restate the paired creation and blessing statements in Genesis 1.
  Matthew 19:4 — Direct quotation: Jesus cites the Creator's making humanity male and female, the creation distinction repeated in the genealogy's introduction.

Genesis 5:3
And Adam lived two hundred and thirty years, and begot a son in his own likeness and image, and named him Seth.
  Genesis 4:25 — Same person or family: The earlier account narrates Seth's birth to Adam and Eve and explains his name; this genealogy locates the same birth within Adam's lifespan.
  1 Chronicles 1:1 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles places Adam and Seth at the head of the ancestral succession, corresponding to the father and named son in this entry.
  Luke 3:38 — Genealogical parallel: Luke's reverse genealogy names Seth immediately before Adam, corresponding to Adam's fathering Seth here; Luke supplies no matching age.

Genesis 5:4
And the days of Adam after he begot Seth were seven hundred years, and he begot sons and daughters.
  Genesis 5:5 — Chronological correspondence: The seven hundred years after Seth's birth contribute to Adam's nine-hundred-and-thirty-year total; the preceding verse supplies the initial two hundred and thirty years.
  Genesis 1:28 — Thematic connection: Adam's further sons and daughters exemplify the human growth and multiplication commanded in the creation blessing, without identifying otherwise unnamed children.

Genesis 5:5
And all the days that Adam lived were nine hundred and thirty years, and he died.
  Genesis 5:3-4 — Chronological correspondence: Adam's nine-hundred-and-thirty-year lifespan is the sum of the two hundred and thirty years before Seth and seven hundred years after him in these supplied readings.
  Genesis 3:19 — Narrative fulfilment: Adam's recorded death brings the narrative to the mortality and return to earth announced to him after his transgression.
  Romans 5:12-14 — Thematic connection: Paul explicitly connects Adam and the reign of death over humanity, providing theological reflection on the mortality first recorded for Adam here.

Genesis 5:6
And Seth lived two hundred and five years, and begot Enosh.
  Genesis 4:26 — Same person or family: The earlier passage also names Enosh as Seth's son, the birth now placed within Seth's genealogical chronology.
  1 Chronicles 1:1 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles places Seth and Enosh consecutively in the ancestral list, corresponding to the father and son named in this entry.
  Luke 3:38 — Genealogical parallel: Luke traces the ancestry through Enosh and Seth in reverse order, corresponding to Seth's fathering Enosh here without asserting agreement about ages.

Genesis 5:7
And Seth lived seven hundred and seven years after he begot Enosh, and begot sons and daughters.
  Genesis 5:8 — Chronological correspondence: Seth's seven hundred and seven later years contribute to his nine-hundred-and-twelve-year total; the previous verse supplies two hundred and five years before Enosh.
  Genesis 5:4 — Literary parallel: Both entries record years lived after the named heir's birth and then additional sons and daughters, preserving the genealogy's repeated family structure.

Genesis 5:8
And all the days of Seth were nine hundred and twelve years, and he died.
  Genesis 5:6-7 — Chronological correspondence: Seth's total of nine hundred and twelve years is accounted for by two hundred and five years before Enosh and seven hundred and seven afterward.
  Genesis 5:5 — Literary parallel: Seth's entry ends with a total lifespan and death, repeating the closing formula established by Adam's entry while giving a different lifespan.

Genesis 5:9
And Enosh lived one hundred and ninety years, and begot Cainan.
  1 Chronicles 1:1-2 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles places Kenan after Enosh and before Mahalalel, the ancestral position occupied by Cainan here; the English names differ and no age agreement is claimed.
  Luke 3:37-38 — Genealogical parallel: Luke's reverse sequence connects Cainan with Enosh, corresponding to the father-son succession in Genesis rather than its chronological figures.

Genesis 5:10
And Enosh lived seven hundred and fifteen years after he begot Cainan, and begot sons and daughters.
  Genesis 5:11 — Chronological correspondence: Enosh's seven hundred and fifteen years after Cainan contribute to the nine-hundred-and-five-year total, following the one hundred and ninety years recorded before that birth.
  Genesis 5:7 — Literary parallel: The post-heir years and additional sons and daughters in Enosh's entry follow the same family-record pattern as Seth's entry.

Genesis 5:11
And all the days of Enosh were nine hundred and five years, and he died.
  Genesis 5:9-10 — Chronological correspondence: Enosh's nine-hundred-and-five-year total equals the one hundred and ninety years before Cainan plus seven hundred and fifteen years afterward in this source.
  Genesis 5:8 — Literary parallel: Enosh's total-lifespan-and-death statement repeats the formula closing Seth's entry and advances the ancestral succession.

Genesis 5:12
And Cainan lived one hundred and seventy years, and begot Mahalalel.
  1 Chronicles 1:2 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles lists Kenan followed by Mahalalel, corresponding to Cainan's fathering Mahalalel here despite the different English form of Cainan's name.
  Luke 3:37 — Genealogical parallel: Luke names Mahalalel and Cainan consecutively in reverse ancestry, corresponding to the parentage recorded here without repeating its ages.

Genesis 5:13
And Cainan lived seven hundred and forty years after he begot Mahalalel, and begot sons and daughters.
  Genesis 5:14 — Chronological correspondence: Cainan's seven hundred and forty later years contribute to his nine-hundred-and-ten-year total; his age of one hundred and seventy at Mahalalel's birth completes the calculation.
  Genesis 5:10 — Literary parallel: Cainan's years after the named son's birth and his additional children use the family-record structure already given for Enosh.

Genesis 5:14
And all the days of Cainan were nine hundred and ten years, and he died.
  Genesis 5:12-13 — Chronological correspondence: Cainan's nine-hundred-and-ten-year lifespan equals one hundred and seventy years before Mahalalel plus seven hundred and forty afterward in the supplied genealogy.
  Genesis 5:11 — Literary parallel: The notice of Cainan's total days and death follows the corresponding notice for Enosh, continuing the repeated mortality formula.

Genesis 5:15
And Mahalalel lived one hundred and sixty-five years, and begot Jared.
  1 Chronicles 1:2 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles places Mahalalel immediately before Jared in its ancestral list, corresponding to the father and named son in this entry.
  Luke 3:37 — Genealogical parallel: Luke's reverse sequence links Jared with Mahalalel, corresponding to the descent recorded here while leaving the genealogical ages unstated.

Genesis 5:16
And Mahalalel lived seven hundred and thirty years after he begot Jared, and begot sons and daughters.
  Genesis 5:17 — Chronological correspondence: Mahalalel's seven hundred and thirty years after Jared contribute to the eight-hundred-and-ninety-five-year total, together with the earlier one hundred and sixty-five years.
  Genesis 5:13 — Literary parallel: Mahalalel's later years and further sons and daughters follow the same post-heir family formula used for Cainan.

Genesis 5:17
And all the days of Mahalalel were eight hundred and ninety-five years, and he died.
  Genesis 5:15-16 — Chronological correspondence: Mahalalel's eight-hundred-and-ninety-five-year lifespan equals the one hundred and sixty-five years before Jared and seven hundred and thirty years afterward recorded here.
  Genesis 5:14 — Literary parallel: Mahalalel's concluding lifespan and death repeat the form of Cainan's conclusion, continuing the lineage's repeated pattern of mortality.

Genesis 5:18
And Jared lived one hundred and sixty-two years, and begot Enoch.
  1 Chronicles 1:2-3 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles places Jared before Enoch, corresponding to the father-son succession stated in this genealogical entry.
  Luke 3:37 — Genealogical parallel: Luke traces ancestry backward through Enoch and Jared, corresponding to Jared's fathering Enoch here without supplying matching chronological ages.

Genesis 5:19
And Jared lived eight hundred years after he begot Enoch, and begot sons and daughters.
  Genesis 5:20 — Chronological correspondence: Jared's eight hundred later years contribute to his nine-hundred-and-sixty-two-year total, together with the one hundred and sixty-two years before Enoch's birth.
  Genesis 5:16 — Literary parallel: The statement of Jared's years after Enoch and his additional children follows the post-heir family pattern used in Mahalalel's entry.

Genesis 5:20
And all the days of Jared were nine hundred and sixty-two years, and he died.
  Genesis 5:18-19 — Chronological correspondence: Jared's nine-hundred-and-sixty-two-year lifespan is accounted for by one hundred and sixty-two years before Enoch and eight hundred years afterward in this source.
  Genesis 5:17 — Literary parallel: Jared's total lifespan and death repeat the concluding form used for Mahalalel, before the narrative introduces Enoch's different outcome.

Genesis 5:21
And Enoch lived one hundred and sixty-five years, and begot Methuselah.
  1 Chronicles 1:3 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles places Enoch followed by Methuselah in the ancestral list, corresponding to the father and son named in this verse.
  Luke 3:37 — Genealogical parallel: Luke's reverse genealogy places Methuselah and Enoch consecutively, corresponding to Enoch's fathering Methuselah here without confirming an age.

Genesis 5:22
And Enoch pleased God for two hundred years after he begot Methuselah, and begot sons and daughters.
  Hebrews 11:5 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews specifically recalls the testimony that Enoch pleased God before being taken away, the distinctive description of his life used here.
  Sirach 44:16 — Explicit reuse: Sirach names Enoch as pleasing the Lord and being taken away, joining the approval described here to the later conclusion of his Genesis entry.
  Genesis 5:24 — Narrative continuation: The entry's conclusion repeats that Enoch pleased God and then records his transfer, completing the distinctive life description introduced in this verse.

Genesis 5:23
And all the days of Enoch were three hundred and sixty-five years.
  Genesis 5:21-22 — Chronological correspondence: The supplied Enoch figures of one hundred and sixty-five years before Methuselah and two hundred years afterward total the three hundred and sixty-five years stated here.
  Genesis 5:24 — Narrative continuation: After giving Enoch's total years, the account records divine transfer rather than the death formula used for neighboring ancestors.

Genesis 5:24
And Enoch pleased God, and was not found, for God transferred him.
  Hebrews 11:5 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews names Enoch and repeats his pleasing God, not being found and being taken away, explicitly interpreting his unusual Genesis conclusion in terms of faith.
  Sirach 44:16 — Explicit reuse: Sirach explicitly remembers Enoch's pleasing the Lord and being taken away, corresponding to both elements of this verse.
  Wisdom of Solomon 4:10-14 — Thematic connection: Wisdom portrays a person pleasing to God who is transferred from life among sinners; the shared approval-and-removal pattern illuminates Enoch's account without naming him in Wisdom.

Genesis 5:25
And Methuselah lived one hundred and sixty-seven years, and begot Lamech.
  1 Chronicles 1:3 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles lists Methuselah immediately before Lamech, corresponding to the named father-son link in this entry without including its ages.
  Luke 3:36-37 — Genealogical parallel: Luke traces the ancestry backward from Lamech to Methuselah, corresponding to the parentage here rather than the supplied chronological figures.

Genesis 5:26
And Methuselah lived eight hundred and two years after he begot Lamech, and begot sons and daughters.
  Genesis 5:27 — Chronological correspondence: Methuselah's eight hundred and two later years contribute to his nine-hundred-and-sixty-nine-year total, with the one hundred and sixty-seven years recorded before Lamech.
  Genesis 5:19 — Literary parallel: The years after the named son's birth and the additional sons and daughters follow the family-record structure of Jared's earlier entry.

Genesis 5:27
And all the days that Methuselah lived were nine hundred and sixty-nine years, and he died.
  Genesis 5:25-26 — Chronological correspondence: Methuselah's nine-hundred-and-sixty-nine-year lifespan equals the one hundred and sixty-seven years before Lamech plus eight hundred and two afterward in the supplied reading.
