  Genesis 5:20 — Literary parallel: Methuselah's total-lifespan-and-death statement returns to the regular conclusion used for Jared, contrasting with Enoch's intervening transfer notice.

Genesis 5:28
And Lamech lived one hundred and eighty-eight years, and begot a son.
  Genesis 5:29 — Narrative continuation: The following verse names the son born to Lamech as Noah and explains his name, resolving the unnamed birth recorded here.
  1 Chronicles 1:3-4 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles places Noah after Lamech, corresponding to the birth whose son is named in the continuation of this Genesis entry; it supplies no age comparison.

Genesis 5:29
And he named him Noah, saying, “This one shall give us rest from our works and from the toil of our hands, and from the earth which the Lord God has cursed.”
  Genesis 3:17-19 — Explicit reuse: Lamech's expectation of rest from labor on the cursed earth recalls the painful work and cursed ground announced to Adam.
  Genesis 8:21-22 — Narrative correspondence: After Noah's offering, God declares that He will not again curse the earth and will sustain its agricultural seasons, corresponding to the hope concerning toil and cursed ground expressed at Noah's naming.

Genesis 5:30
And Lamech lived five hundred and sixty-five years after he begot Noah, and begot sons and daughters.
  Genesis 5:31 — Chronological correspondence: Lamech's five hundred and sixty-five years after Noah contribute to his seven-hundred-and-fifty-three-year total, together with the one hundred and eighty-eight years before that birth.
  Genesis 5:26 — Literary parallel: Lamech's later years and additional children follow the same family-record pattern used in Methuselah's entry, with different chronological figures.

Genesis 5:31
And all the days of Lamech were seven hundred and fifty-three years, and he died.
  Genesis 5:28-30 — Chronological correspondence: Lamech's seven-hundred-and-fifty-three-year lifespan is accounted for by one hundred and eighty-eight years before the son's birth and five hundred and sixty-five afterward in this source.
  Genesis 5:27 — Literary parallel: Lamech's concluding statement of total years and death repeats Methuselah's conclusion, carrying the regular mortality formula to the generation before the flood.

Genesis 5:32
And Noah was five hundred years old, and Noah begot three sons: Shem, Ham, and Japheth.
  Genesis 6:10 — Explicit reuse: The next genealogical notice repeats Noah's three sons by name: Shem, Ham and Japheth, the same family introduced here.
  1 Chronicles 1:4 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles explicitly lists Noah and the same three sons, providing a later summary of this family rather than a repetition of Noah's age.
  Luke 3:36 — Genealogical parallel: Luke carries its ancestry through Shem and Noah, selecting one of the three branches introduced here without mentioning Ham or Japheth.

Genesis 6:1
And it came to pass, when mankind began to increase upon the earth and daughters were born to them,
  Genesis 1:28 — Narrative correspondence: Humanity's increase over the earth corresponds to the creation blessing's command to grow, multiply and fill the earth, before the flood narrative describes the accompanying corruption.
  Genesis 5:4 — Narrative correspondence: Adam's sons and daughters exemplify the family growth whose widening across the earth forms the setting of this passage.
  Matthew 24:38 — Explicit reuse: Jesus describes eating, drinking and marriage in the days before Noah entered the ark, recalling the expanding human society before the flood.

Genesis 6:2
that the sons of God saw that the daughters of men were beautiful, and took wives for themselves from all whom they chose.
  Genesis 6:4 — Narrative continuation: The later verse returns to the unions of the sons of God and daughters of men and describes the resulting offspring, without resolving the disputed identity of the sons of God.
  Matthew 24:38 — Thematic connection: Jesus names marrying and being given in marriage as part of life before the flood, corresponding to the taking of wives described here without explaining the sons of God's identity.

Genesis 6:3
And the Lord God said, “My Spirit shall not abide in these men for ever, for they are flesh; and their days shall be a hundred and twenty years.”
  Psalms 77:38-39 — Thematic connection: The psalm associates divine restraint of destruction with remembering that humans are flesh and a departing spirit, corresponding to the mortality and limited divine forbearance described here.
  1 Peter 3:20 — Thematic connection: Peter recalls God's patience during the preparation of Noah's ark, illuminating divine forbearance before the flood without determining the meaning of the hundred and twenty years.

Genesis 6:4
Now giants were upon the earth in those days, and afterward also, whenever the sons of God went in to the daughters of men and they bore children to them. These were the giants of old, the men of renown.
  Wisdom of Solomon 14:6 — Explicit reuse: Wisdom describes arrogant giants perishing while the world's hope survives on a vessel, directly connecting the ancient giants with the flood-and-preservation setting.
  Sirach 16:7 — Explicit reuse: Sirach remembers ancient giants who rebelled in their strength and for whom God did not make atonement, recalling the primeval giants named in Genesis without resolving their parentage.
  Genesis 6:2 — Narrative correspondence: This verse resumes the sons of God taking the daughters of men and specifies the offspring, elaborating the earlier notice of those unions.

Genesis 6:5
And the Lord God saw that the wickedness of mankind had multiplied upon the earth, and that each one continually set his heart upon evil all his days.
  Genesis 8:21 — Explicit reuse: After the flood, God's declaration again describes the human mind's bent toward evil, corresponding to the heart's persistent evil in the pre-flood diagnosis.
  Matthew 15:19 — Thematic connection: Jesus identifies evil reasonings and destructive deeds as proceeding from the heart, corresponding to Genesis's account of wrongdoing rooted in persistent inward intention.
  Psalms 13:1-3 — Thematic connection: The psalm joins corrupt human conduct, God's inspection of humanity and pervasive turning aside, matching the divine assessment of widespread wickedness here.

Genesis 6:6
And God took to heart that He had made man upon the earth, and pondered it.
  Genesis 8:21 — Narrative parallel: Both passages describe God considering humanity's condition before announcing His response; the later decision restrains the destruction introduced in this earlier scene.
  Isaiah 63:10 — Thematic connection: Isaiah portrays human disobedience provoking God's holy spirit and resulting in divine opposition, corresponding to God's personal response to human wrongdoing without substituting another edition's wording for Genesis.

Genesis 6:7
And God said, “I will blot out from the face of the earth the mankind I have made, together with the cattle, the creeping things, and the birds of heaven; for I am angered that I made them.”
  Genesis 7:21-23 — Narrative fulfilment: The flood account records the deaths of the same human and animal groups and their removal from the earth, carrying out the announced destruction.
  Genesis 1:24-27 — Contrast: The threatened removal of humans and land creatures reverses the earlier making of those living groups; the connection concerns the narrative's creation-and-destruction movement.
  2 Peter 3:6 — Explicit reuse: Peter recalls the former world's destruction by floodwater, corresponding to the judgment upon earthly life announced here.

Genesis 6:8
But Noah found grace before the Lord God.
  Sirach 44:17 — Explicit reuse: Sirach singles out righteous Noah as the means through whom a remnant survived the flood, corresponding to Noah's favorable standing amid the announced judgment.
  Hebrews 11:7 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews remembers Noah's faithful response and his household's salvation, expanding on the exceptional divine favor noted here.
  Genesis 19:19 — Narrative parallel: Lot also speaks of finding mercy that preserves his life from coming calamity; the correspondence is favorable treatment amid judgment, not an equation of the two characters.

Genesis 6:9
These are the generations of Noah. Noah was a righteous man, blameless in his generation; Noah pleased God.
  Sirach 44:17 — Explicit reuse: Sirach explicitly calls Noah perfect and righteous and links him to survival through wrath, repeating the character description of this genealogical notice.
  Ezekiel 14:14 — Explicit reuse: Ezekiel names Noah alongside Daniel and Job as a paradigmatically righteous person, drawing on the righteous character assigned to him here.
  Hebrews 11:7 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews interprets Noah's preparation of the ark through faith and calls him an heir of righteousness, corresponding to the righteous man approved by God in Genesis.

Genesis 6:10
And Noah begot three sons: Shem, Ham, and Japheth.
  Genesis 5:32 — Explicit reuse: This notice repeats the same three named sons of Noah already introduced at the close of the preceding genealogy.
  1 Chronicles 1:4 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles lists Noah's sons as Shem, Ham and Japheth, matching the threefold family named here.

Genesis 6:11
And the earth was corrupt before God, and the earth was filled with unrighteousness.
  Psalms 13:1-3 — Thematic connection: The psalm describes corrupt practices and a humanity collectively turned aside, corresponding to the earth's pervasive unrighteousness before God.
  Genesis 6:5 — Narrative correspondence: The earlier diagnosis of multiplied human wickedness explains the corruption and unrighteousness filling the earth in this verse.

Genesis 6:12
And the Lord God looked upon the earth, and it was corrupt, for all flesh had corrupted its way upon the earth.
  Psalms 13:2-3 — Shared imagery: The psalm likewise pictures the Lord looking upon humanity and finding collective deviation, closely matching God's inspection of corrupted human ways.
  Genesis 6:9 — Contrast: Noah's righteousness and blamelessness stand against the surrounding corruption, explaining the narrative's distinction between the preserved man and the judged earth.

Genesis 6:13
And God said to Noah, “The time of all mankind has come before Me, for the earth is filled with unrighteousness because of them; and behold, I am destroying them and the earth.
  Genesis 7:23 — Narrative fulfilment: The later account describes living creatures being blotted from the earth while Noah survives in the ark, fulfilling the announced judgment and its implied distinction.
  2 Peter 2:5 — Explicit reuse: Peter recalls judgment on the ancient ungodly world by flood alongside Noah's preservation, corresponding to the destruction announced to Noah here.
  Sirach 44:17 — Explicit reuse: Sirach places Noah's preservation within a time of wrath when the flood came, recalling the judgment announced in this divine speech.

Genesis 6:14
“Therefore make yourself an ark of squared timber. Make compartments within the ark, and coat it inside and out with pitch.
  Hebrews 11:7 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews explicitly remembers Noah's preparing the ark after divine instruction, corresponding to the command to construct the vessel here.
  Wisdom of Solomon 14:6 — Thematic connection: Wisdom depicts the world's surviving hope taking refuge on a wooden vessel steered by God, corresponding to the wooden ark commanded for preservation.
  Genesis 7:23 — Narrative fulfilment: The account of Noah and those with him surviving in the ark shows the preserving purpose of the vessel whose construction is commanded here.

Genesis 6:15
And this is how you shall make it: the ark shall be three hundred cubits long, fifty cubits wide, and thirty cubits high.
  Genesis 6:22 — Narrative fulfilment: The statement that Noah did everything commanded encompasses the specified dimensions of the ark, without providing an independent measurement record.
  Hebrews 11:7 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews recalls Noah's obedient preparation of an ark in response to divine instruction; this links the construction specification to its later faith interpretation without repeating the dimensions.

Genesis 6:16
Make the ark taper toward the top, and finish it to a cubit above. Set the door of the ark in its side, and make it with lower, second, and third storeys.
  Genesis 7:16 — Narrative fulfilment: The flood narrative records God's closing the ark after entry, corresponding to the functional entrance specified in its construction instructions.
  Hebrews 11:7 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews recalls the preparation of Noah's ark for household preservation, providing later reflection on the building instructions without equating the roof detail with a different feature.

Genesis 6:17
“And behold, I am bringing a flood of water upon the earth, to destroy all flesh beneath heaven in which is the breath of life. Everything upon the earth shall die.
  Genesis 7:21-22 — Narrative fulfilment: The account of all moving flesh and dry-land beings with the breath of life dying fulfils the announced destruction by floodwater.
  2 Peter 3:6 — Explicit reuse: Peter recalls the world's being flooded with water and perishing, corresponding to the water judgment announced here.
  Matthew 24:38-39 — Explicit reuse: Jesus recalls Noah's entrance into the ark and the flood's sweeping people away, corresponding to the destruction announced in Genesis.

Genesis 6:18
But I will establish My covenant with you. And you shall enter the ark, you and your sons, your wife and your sons’ wives with you.
  Genesis 7:7 — Narrative fulfilment: Noah's entry with his sons, wife and sons' wives carries out the household entrance commanded in the covenant-and-preservation promise.
  Genesis 9:9-11 — Narrative continuation: The later declaration establishes God's covenant with Noah and his descendants and specifies the promise concerning future floods, developing the covenant first announced here.
  1 Peter 3:20 — Explicit reuse: Peter identifies the people saved through the water in Noah's ark as eight, corresponding to the household group named for entry here.
  Hebrews 11:7 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews describes Noah's ark as prepared for his household's salvation, corresponding to God's preservation promise and named family entry.

Genesis 6:19
“And from all the cattle, all the creeping things, all the wild beasts, and all flesh, you shall bring two of each into the ark, to sustain them with you. They shall be male and female.
  Genesis 7:9 — Narrative fulfilment: The animals' entering Noah's ark two by two, male and female, fulfils the paired preservation command stated here.
  Genesis 7:16 — Narrative fulfilment: The flood narrative reiterates that the entering creatures were male and female of all flesh, matching the commanded composition of the preserved groups.
  Genesis 8:17 — Narrative continuation: God's later command to release the ark's creatures onto the earth completes the preservation and renewed life for which the pairs were brought aboard.

Genesis 6:20
Of all the winged birds according to their kind, all the cattle according to their kind, and all the creeping things upon the earth according to their kinds, two of each shall come to you, male and female, to be sustained with you.
  Genesis 7:14-15 — Narrative fulfilment: The later entrance list includes birds, cattle and creeping things by kind and in pairs, corresponding to the categories and arrangement commanded here.
  Genesis 1:21-25 — Explicit reuse: The preservation command repeats the creation account's grouping of living creatures according to kinds, carrying those distinctions through the flood.
  Genesis 8:19 — Narrative continuation: The animals' exit according to their kinds completes the preservation of the kinds specified for entrance into the ark.

Genesis 6:21
“And take for yourself every kind of food that is eaten, and gather it to yourself. It shall be food for you and for them.”
  Genesis 1:29-30 — Thematic connection: The creation account assigns food to both humanity and animals, corresponding to the ark instruction to gather food for Noah and the creatures together.
  Genesis 41:35-36 — Narrative parallel: Joseph's plan likewise gathers and stores food before an announced catastrophe so that life will be preserved; it is a provision-before-crisis parallel, not a claim that the same foods were stored.

Genesis 6:22
And Noah did all that the Lord God commanded him; so he did.
  Genesis 7:5 — Literary parallel: The later obedience summary repeats Noah's doing everything the Lord God commanded, confirming the narrative's portrayal of his faithful response.
  Hebrews 11:7 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews interprets Noah's obedient preparation of the ark as an act of faith and reverent fear, corresponding to the completion of God's commands here.
  Exodus 40:16 — Literary parallel: Moses' doing everything the Lord commanded uses the same comprehensive obedience pattern, comparing faithful response to detailed divine construction instructions without identifying the two structures.

Genesis 7:1
And the Lord God said to Noah, “Enter the ark, you and all your household, for I have seen you righteous before Me in this generation.
  Genesis 6:9 — Narrative continuation: The invitation to enter because Noah is righteous resumes the earlier description of Noah as righteous and blameless in his generation.
  Hebrews 11:7 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews connects Noah's obedient preparation of the ark with his household's salvation and his righteousness according to faith.
  Sirach 44:17 — Explicit reuse: Sirach recalls Noah as perfect and righteous and connects that character with the remnant preserved through the flood.

Genesis 7:2
“Of the clean animals, bring in with you by sevens, male and female; and of the unclean animals, by twos, male and female.
  Genesis 8:20 — Narrative continuation: The later sacrifice uses clean animals after the flood, showing a subsequent use of the clean creatures distinguished in the boarding command.
  Leviticus 11:46-47 — Thematic connection: The law explicitly distinguishes clean and unclean animals, the categories already differentiated in Noah's instructions; the passages are not treated as identical legislation.
  Genesis 6:19 — Narrative elaboration: The earlier command requires male and female animals for preservation; this verse adds the separate numbers for clean and unclean animals.

Genesis 7:3
Of the clean birds of heaven also, by sevens, male and female; and of the unclean birds, by twos, male and female, to preserve their seed upon all the earth.
  Genesis 6:20 — Narrative elaboration: The earlier instruction includes birds according to kind for preservation, and this verse specifies their clean and unclean groupings and surviving seed.
  Genesis 8:20 — Narrative continuation: Noah's later offering of clean birds corresponds to the clean birds specifically brought aboard in this instruction.
  Genesis 1:22 — Thematic connection: The initial blessing commands birds to multiply on earth, while Noah's instruction preserves their seed through the coming destruction.

Genesis 7:4
“For in seven days I will bring rain upon the earth for forty days and forty nights, and I will blot out from the face of the earth every living thing that I have made.”
  Genesis 6:7 — Narrative fulfilment: The announced blotting out of living creatures gives a definite time and means for the destruction previously declared because of human evil.
  Genesis 7:12 — Narrative fulfilment: The forty days and nights of rain later reported fulfil the duration of rain announced to Noah.
  Genesis 7:23 — Narrative fulfilment: The account of living things being blotted out records the destruction announced here while distinguishing those preserved in the ark.

Genesis 7:5
And Noah did all that the Lord God commanded him.
  Genesis 6:22 — Literary parallel: Both verses summarize Noah's response with the statement that he did everything God commanded, framing his preparation and entry as obedience.
  Hebrews 11:7 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews interprets Noah's preparation in response to divine instruction as action taken in reverent faith for his household's safety.

Genesis 7:6
Noah was six hundred years old when the flood of water came upon the earth.
  Genesis 5:32 — Chronological continuation: The earlier genealogy identifies Noah as five hundred when his sons are introduced, while this verse dates the flood to his six-hundredth year.
  Genesis 8:13 — Chronological continuation: The flood begins when Noah is six hundred, and the later drying of the earth is dated in his six-hundred-and-first year.

Genesis 7:7
And Noah entered the ark with his sons, his wife, and his sons’ wives, because of the waters of the flood.
  Genesis 6:18 — Narrative fulfilment: Noah's entry with his wife, sons and their wives carries out the earlier instruction naming precisely those household groups.
  1 Peter 3:20 — Explicit reuse: Peter recalls the few persons, eight in all, brought safely through the water in the ark, corresponding to the household entering here.
  Luke 17:26-27 — Explicit reuse: Jesus recalls Noah entering the ark before the flood destroyed the others, using that entry as the decisive boundary in His warning.

Genesis 7:8
Of the birds, the clean animals, the unclean animals, and all the creeping things upon the earth,
  Genesis 7:2-3 — Narrative fulfilment: The arriving birds and clean and unclean animals match the separate categories God had commanded Noah to bring into the ark.
  Genesis 6:20 — Narrative fulfilment: Birds, cattle and creeping creatures were promised to come for preservation, and the boarding account now enumerates those creature groups.

Genesis 7:9
two by two they entered the ark with Noah, male and female, as God had commanded him.
  Genesis 6:19 — Narrative fulfilment: The two-by-two male-and-female entry follows the earlier preservation command concerning paired living creatures.
  Genesis 7:16 — Literary parallel: The later boarding summary repeats both the male-and-female description and the statement that entry followed God's command.
  Genesis 8:19 — Narrative counterpart: The paired entry of creatures is followed after the flood by their exit according to kinds, showing the preservation purpose reaching its outcome.

Genesis 7:10
And after seven days the waters of the flood came upon the earth.
  Genesis 7:4 — Narrative fulfilment: The arrival of floodwater after seven days fulfils the warning that rain would begin in seven days.
  Genesis 7:11 — Narrative elaboration: The following dated account specifies the opening of the deep's fountains and heaven's floodgates behind the arrival of floodwater.

Genesis 7:11
In the six hundredth year of Noah’s life, in the second month, on the twenty-seventh day of the month, on that day all the fountains of the deep burst open, and the floodgates of heaven were opened.
  Genesis 8:2 — Narrative reversal: The closing of the deep's fountains and heaven's floodgates reverses the opening of the same sources of water described here.
  Genesis 8:14 — Chronological counterpart: The earth's final drying is dated to the same month and twenty-seventh day in the following year of Noah's life; the supplied dates are preserved.
  Genesis 1:6-7 — Narrative reversal: The opening of water sources above and below reverses the creation ordering that distinguished upper and lower waters; this is a structural comparison.

Genesis 7:12
And rain fell upon the earth for forty days and forty nights.
  Genesis 7:4 — Narrative fulfilment: The reported forty days and nights of rain fulfil the exact rain duration announced in God's warning.
  Genesis 8:2 — Narrative reversal: The later restraint of rain from heaven ends the rainfall whose duration is described here.

Genesis 7:13
On that very day Noah entered the ark, with Shem, Ham, and Japheth, the sons of Noah, and Noah’s wife and the three wives of his sons with him.
  Genesis 6:18 — Narrative fulfilment: The named family entering the ark corresponds to God's earlier instruction to bring Noah, his sons and the wives of the household.
  Genesis 8:18 — Narrative counterpart: The same family groups later come out of the ark, completing the entry-and-exit sequence of household preservation.
  1 Peter 3:20 — Explicit reuse: Peter's recollection of eight persons saved through water corresponds to Noah, his wife, his three sons and their three wives listed here.

Genesis 7:14
And every wild beast according to its kind, all the cattle according to their kind, every creeping thing that moves upon the earth according to its kind, and every bird according to its kind
  Genesis 1:24-25 — Shared categories: The boarding list repeats creation's categories of wild animals, cattle and creeping things according to kind, emphasizing preservation of the created groups.
  Genesis 6:20 — Narrative fulfilment: The kinds of birds and land creatures entering fulfil the earlier command to preserve creatures according to their kinds.
  Genesis 8:19 — Narrative counterpart: The creatures later leave the ark according to their kinds, matching the kinds enumerated as entering in this verse.

Genesis 7:15
entered the ark with Noah, two by two of all flesh in which is the breath of life.
  Genesis 6:19 — Narrative fulfilment: The arrival of pairs of living flesh fulfils God's instruction to bring pairs into the ark to keep them alive.
  Genesis 6:17 — Contrast: The flood warning threatens flesh possessing the breath of life, whereas this verse identifies breath-bearing creatures entering the place of preservation.
  Genesis 7:22 — Contrast: The creatures entering with life's breath contrast with those on dry land whose breath of life does not preserve them outside the ark.

Genesis 7:16
Those that entered were male and female of all flesh, as God had commanded Noah. And the Lord God closed the ark from the outside.
  Genesis 6:16 — Narrative continuation: The construction instructions place a door in the ark, and the boarding account concludes with the Lord closing the ark from outside.
  Genesis 7:9 — Literary parallel: Both boarding summaries identify male and female creatures and explicitly state that Noah followed God's command.
  Luke 17:26-27 — Explicit reuse: Jesus recalls the distinction between entering the ark and being overtaken by the flood; the closure marks the completed entry in Genesis.

Genesis 7:17
And the flood was upon the earth for forty days and forty nights. And the waters increased and lifted the ark, and it rose above the earth.
  Genesis 7:4 — Narrative fulfilment: The forty-day flood period corresponds to the announced forty days and nights of rain, now described as lifting the ark above the earth.
  Wisdom of Solomon 10:4 — Explicit reuse: Wisdom recalls the righteous man being saved by humble wood while the earth was flooded, corresponding to the ark rising on the floodwaters.
  Genesis 7:18 — Narrative continuation: The next statement continues the account of increasing water and the ark's flotation, confirming the lifting of the ark described here.

Genesis 7:18
And the waters prevailed and increased greatly upon the earth, and the ark floated upon the waters.
  Wisdom of Solomon 10:4 — Explicit reuse: The righteous man being steered by humble wood during the flood corresponds to Noah's ark floating upon the prevailing waters.
  Genesis 8:4 — Narrative reversal: The ark's eventual rest on the mountains contrasts with its flotation while the floodwater is prevailing.

Genesis 7:19
And the waters prevailed exceedingly upon the earth, and covered all the high mountains beneath heaven.
  Genesis 8:5 — Narrative reversal: The mountains' later appearance as waters diminish reverses their complete covering under the prevailing floodwater.
  2 Peter 3:5-6 — Explicit reuse: Peter recalls the former world being overwhelmed with water and perishing, corresponding to the account's universal description of the flood.

Genesis 7:20
The waters rose fifteen cubits above them and covered all the high mountains.
  Genesis 7:19 — Narrative elaboration: The fifteen-cubit statement specifies how far the waters overtopped the high mountains whose covering the previous verse reports.
  Genesis 8:5 — Narrative reversal: The later exposure of mountain tops contrasts with the high waters covering the mountains; the target does not repeat the fifteen-cubit measurement.

Genesis 7:21
And all flesh that moved upon the earth died: birds, cattle, wild beasts, every creeping thing that moves upon the earth, and all mankind.
  Genesis 6:17 — Narrative fulfilment: The death of flesh upon the earth fulfils God's earlier warning that the flood would destroy flesh possessing the breath of life.
  2 Peter 2:5 — Explicit reuse: Peter describes the flood brought upon the ungodly ancient world while Noah was preserved, corresponding to the destruction narrated here.
  Genesis 1:24-26 — Narrative reversal: The destroyed groups of animals and humanity recall the created land creatures and humans, presenting the flood as destruction of previously made life.

Genesis 7:22
Everything that had the breath of life, everything upon the dry land, died.
  Genesis 2:7 — Contrast: God's imparting life's breath to the human contrasts with the death of dry-land beings possessing life's breath in the flood.
  Genesis 6:17 — Narrative fulfilment: The warning specifically concerns flesh possessing the breath of life, the same life-bearing category declared dead here.
  Genesis 7:15 — Contrast: Breath-bearing creatures preserved inside the ark contrast with the breath-bearing life that dies on dry land outside it.

Genesis 7:23
And He blotted out every living thing upon the face of the whole earth: mankind, cattle, creeping things, and the birds of heaven. They were blotted out from the earth, and only Noah remained, and those with him in the ark.
  Genesis 6:7 — Narrative fulfilment: The blotting out of humans, cattle, creeping things and birds matches the groups named in God's earlier declaration of destruction.
  2 Peter 2:5 — Explicit reuse: Peter recalls the destruction of the ungodly world with Noah preserved as one of eight, matching the contrast between destruction and the ark's survivors.
  Sirach 44:17 — Explicit reuse: Sirach's description of a remnant left through righteous Noah corresponds to Noah and his companions surviving when other life is blotted out.

Genesis 7:24
And the waters remained high upon the earth for one hundred and fifty days.
  Genesis 8:3 — Chronological continuation: The later report again names one hundred and fifty days, now as the point after which the waters had receded and diminished.
  Genesis 8:4 — Narrative continuation: The ark's dated resting on the mountains follows the period of high water and marks a new stage in the flood's recession.

Genesis 8:1
And God remembered Noah, and all the wild beasts, all the cattle, all the birds, and all the creeping things that were with him in the ark. And God brought a wind upon the earth, and the waters subsided.
  Genesis 1:2 — Narrative parallel: God's bringing a wind over the flooded earth recalls divine action over the waters before habitable land appears; wind and Spirit are not identified as identical here.
  Exodus 14:21 — Narrative parallel: The Lord's use of wind to move sea water and expose dry ground parallels the wind and water recession in Noah's deliverance.
  Genesis 19:29 — Narrative parallel: God's remembering Abraham during the cities' destruction and rescuing Lot parallels remembering Noah while bringing the flood toward its end.

Genesis 8:2
And the fountains of the deep and the floodgates of heaven were closed, and the rain from heaven was restrained.
  Genesis 7:11 — Narrative reversal: The fountains of the deep and heaven's floodgates now close, reversing their opening at the flood's beginning.
  Genesis 7:12 — Narrative reversal: The restraint of rain from heaven brings to an end the rainfall earlier described as lasting forty days and nights.

Genesis 8:3
And the waters steadily withdrew from the earth; after one hundred and fifty days the waters had receded and diminished.
  Genesis 7:24 — Chronological continuation: The one-hundred-and-fifty-day marker connects the report of high floodwater with the report of its recession and decrease.
  Genesis 1:9 — Narrative parallel: The withdrawal of water so that land can reappear recalls creation's gathering of water and appearance of dry land.

Genesis 8:4
And in the seventh month, on the twenty-seventh day of the month, the ark rested upon the mountains of Ararat.
  Genesis 7:18 — Narrative reversal: The ark now rests on mountains after previously floating on waters that prevailed over the earth.
  Genesis 8:5 — Narrative continuation: The further appearance of mountain tops follows the ark's rest on the mountains as the water continues diminishing.

Genesis 8:5
And the waters continued to diminish until the tenth month. In the eleventh month, on the first day of the month, the tops of the mountains appeared.
  Genesis 7:19-20 — Narrative reversal: The mountain tops' exposure reverses the flood's earlier covering of all the high mountains, including the stated overtopping measurement.
  Genesis 8:13 — Narrative continuation: The appearance of mountain tops is an earlier stage of drying than Noah's later observation that water had disappeared from the ground's face.

Genesis 8:6
And after forty days Noah opened the window of the ark which he had made.
  Genesis 8:13 — Narrative parallel: Noah's opening the ark's window and later removing its covering are successive acts used to observe the retreat of the water.
  Genesis 7:12 — Literary parallel: Forty days mark the earlier rainfall and a later waiting interval before the window opens; the two intervals are not treated as the same dates.

Genesis 8:7
And he sent forth the raven to see whether the waters had subsided. It went out and did not return until the waters had dried from the earth.
  Genesis 8:8-9 — Narrative comparison: The raven's mission to test whether water had subsided is followed by the dove's mission, whose return gives an explicit report about resting conditions.
  Genesis 8:13 — Narrative continuation: The water recession tested by sending the raven is later confirmed by Noah's direct observation after removing the ark's covering.

Genesis 8:8
And he sent forth the dove after it, to see whether the waters had subsided from the face of the earth.
  Genesis 8:7 — Narrative comparison: The dove continues the testing mission begun with the raven, with both birds sent to discover whether the water had subsided.
  Genesis 8:11 — Narrative outcome: The dove's later return with an olive sprig supplies the sign by which Noah knows the waters tested in this mission have subsided.

Genesis 8:9
But the dove found no resting place for its feet, and returned to him in the ark, for water covered the whole face of the earth. And he stretched forth his hand, took it, and brought it to himself within the ark.
  Genesis 8:11-12 — Narrative contrast: The dove's inability to find a resting place and its return contrast with its later olive sprig and eventual nonreturn as the earth dries.
  Genesis 8:5 — Narrative distinction: Mountain tops can be visible while the dove still finds no resting place; the passages preserve distinct stages rather than equating visible summits with a habitable earth.

Genesis 8:10
And he waited another seven days, and again sent forth the dove from the ark.
  Genesis 8:12 — Literary parallel: Both reports describe Noah waiting another seven days before sending the dove again, structuring the successive tests of the earth's condition.
  Genesis 8:11 — Narrative outcome: The dove's evening return with an olive sprig is the reported outcome of the second sending after Noah's seven-day wait.

Genesis 8:11
And the dove returned to him toward evening, bearing an olive sprig with a leaf in its beak. And Noah knew that the waters had subsided from the earth.
  Genesis 8:8-9 — Narrative contrast: The olive sprig and Noah's resulting knowledge answer the earlier test in which the dove returned without finding a resting place.
  Genesis 8:13 — Narrative continuation: The sprig gives Noah indirect evidence that waters have subsided, before the later direct observation of the ground after the covering is removed.

Genesis 8:12
And he waited another seven days, and again sent forth the dove; and it returned to him no more.
  Genesis 8:9 — Narrative contrast: The dove that previously returned because it lacked a resting place now returns no more, marking changed conditions as the flood recedes.
  Genesis 8:10 — Literary parallel: The repeated seven-day wait and sending of the dove continues the testing pattern used in the preceding mission.

Genesis 8:13
And in the six hundred and first year of Noah’s life, in the first month, on the first day of the month, the waters had disappeared from the earth. And Noah removed the covering of the ark which he had made, and saw that the waters had disappeared from the face of the earth.
  Genesis 7:6 — Chronological continuation: The flood was dated when Noah was six hundred, while this inspection of the drying earth is dated in his six-hundred-and-first year.
  Genesis 8:14 — Chronological continuation: The disappearance of water observed in the first month precedes the final statement that the earth was dry in the second month.
  Genesis 8:6 — Narrative parallel: Removing the ark's covering to inspect the ground follows the earlier opening of its window during the water's recession.

Genesis 8:14
And in the second month, on the twenty-seventh day of the month, the earth was dry.
  Genesis 7:11 — Chronological counterpart: The earth is declared dry on the second month's twenty-seventh day, matching the flood's starting month and day in the previous year of Noah's life.
  Genesis 8:16 — Narrative consequence: The completed drying of the earth supplies the setting for God's subsequent command that Noah and his household leave the ark.

Genesis 8:15
And the Lord God spoke to Noah, saying,
  Genesis 7:1 — Narrative counterpart: God's speaking to Noah at the end of the confinement corresponds to His earlier speech commanding entry into the ark.
  Genesis 8:16-17 — Narrative continuation: The following command to leave with the household and creatures supplies the actual content of the divine speech introduced here.

Genesis 8:16
“Come out of the ark, you and your wife, your sons and your sons’ wives with you.
  Genesis 7:7 — Narrative counterpart: The household groups commanded to come out are the same wife, sons and sons' wives previously described entering with Noah.
  Genesis 6:18 — Narrative counterpart: The exit command repeats the family groups named in the earlier covenant-and-entry promise, showing that the promised household has been preserved.
  1 Peter 3:20 — Explicit reuse: Peter's eight persons brought safely through water correspond to the preserved household now being called out of the ark.

Genesis 8:17
Bring out with you all the wild beasts that are with you, and all flesh, both birds and cattle, and every creeping thing that moves upon the earth. And grow and multiply upon the earth.”
  Genesis 1:22 — Thematic connection: The renewed growth language at the creatures' release recalls creation's multiplication blessing on living creatures, while this source command itself addresses Noah.
  Genesis 9:1 — Literary parallel: The command to grow and multiply on leaving the ark anticipates the explicit blessing of Noah and his sons to multiply and fill the earth.
  Genesis 6:20 — Narrative outcome: The earlier command preserves creature kinds in the ark, while this command returns the preserved birds, cattle and creeping creatures to the earth.

Genesis 8:18
And Noah came out, with his wife, his sons, and his sons’ wives.
  Genesis 7:13 — Narrative counterpart: The same household groups named at entry are named coming out, completing the account of their preservation together.
  Genesis 8:16 — Narrative fulfilment: Noah's departure with his wife, sons and their wives carries out the household exit command just given by God.
  Hebrews 11:7 — Explicit reuse: The household's emerging alive illustrates the salvation of Noah's household for which Hebrews says he prepared the ark in faith.

Genesis 8:19
And all the wild beasts, all the cattle, every bird, and every creeping thing that moves upon the earth came out of the ark according to their kinds.
  Genesis 7:14 — Narrative counterpart: The creature kinds leaving the ark correspond to the kinds enumerated at boarding, showing continuity of the preserved animal groups.
  Genesis 1:24-25 — Shared categories: The wild animals, cattle and creeping creatures leaving according to kind repeat the land-creature categories in creation's original account.
  Genesis 6:20 — Narrative fulfilment: The exit of birds and land creatures according to kind realizes the earlier instruction to keep those creature kinds alive through the flood.

Genesis 8:20
And Noah built an altar to God. And he took of every kind of clean animal and every kind of clean bird, and offered whole burnt offerings upon the altar.
  Genesis 7:2-3 — Narrative continuation: The clean animals and clean birds used in Noah's offerings correspond to the clean groups explicitly provided for in the ark instructions.
  Leviticus 1:3-9 — Thematic connection: The later burnt-offering law describes an animal offered on an altar and its fragrant result; it illuminates the offering form without making Noah subject to later Sinai legislation.
  Genesis 4:4 — Narrative parallel: Abel's animal offering and Noah's offering are distinct early acts of presenting creatures to God; no identity of sacrifice regulations is asserted.

Genesis 8:21
And the Lord God smelled the sweet fragrance, and the Lord God considered and said, “I will never again curse the earth because of the deeds of mankind, for the mind of man is bent intently upon evil from his youth. Therefore I will never again strike down all living flesh as I have done.
  Genesis 6:5 — Literary parallel: God's assessment of the human mind as bent toward evil recalls the pre-flood assessment of the heart's continual evil, despite the new promise of preservation.
  Genesis 3:17 — Thematic connection: The promise not again to curse the earth because of humanity's deeds looks back to the earlier cursing of the ground in response to Adam.
  Isaiah 54:9-10 — Explicit reuse: Isaiah recalls the water of Noah's time and God's sworn assurance, using that precedent to explain His covenant mercy.
  Ephesians 5:2 — Shared sacrificial imagery: Paul describes Christ's self-offering as a fragrant offering to God, sharing the acceptable-offering imagery of Noah's sacrifice without identifying the sacrifices.

Genesis 8:22
All the days of the earth, seedtime and harvest, cold and heat, summer and spring shall not cease by day or night.”
  Genesis 1:14 — Thematic connection: The continuing days, nights and seasons depend on the ordered times for which God appointed the heavenly lights in creation.
  Psalms 73:16-17 — Thematic connection: The psalm assigns day, night and seasons to God's ordering, corresponding to the natural cycles He promises will continue after the flood.
  Jeremiah 5:24 — Thematic connection: Jeremiah speaks of God giving appointed rain and preserving harvest times, corresponding to the promise that seedtime and harvest will continue.

Genesis 9:1
And God blessed Noah and his sons, and said to them, “Grow and multiply, and fill the earth and subdue it.
  Genesis 1:28 — Explicit reuse: The blessing to grow, multiply, fill and subdue the earth renews the original blessing and vocation given to humanity at creation.
  Genesis 8:17 — Literary parallel: The blessing on Noah and his sons repeats the growth and multiplication command given when the ark's creatures were released.
  Genesis 9:7 — Literary parallel: The later command repeats growth, multiplication and filling the earth, framing the intervening instructions about creatures, food and human life.

Genesis 9:2
The dread and fear of you shall fall upon every wild beast of the earth, every bird of heaven, everything that moves upon the earth, and every fish of the sea. Into your hands I have given them.
  Genesis 1:26 — Thematic connection: Humanity's new relation of dread and authority over fish, birds and land creatures corresponds to the creature groups first assigned to human dominion.
  Psalms 8:7-9 — Thematic connection: The psalm lists beasts, birds and fish placed beneath human authority, corresponding to the creature groups given into Noah's family's hands.
  Sirach 17:4 — Explicit reuse: Sirach explicitly joins fear of humanity upon living flesh with dominion over beasts and winged creatures, closely corresponding to this description.

Genesis 9:3
“Every living thing that moves shall be food for you. As I gave the green plants, so I have given you all things.
  Genesis 1:29-30 — Explicit comparison: The new permission to eat living creatures explicitly compares that gift with the earlier provision of green plants to humans and animals.
  Genesis 9:4 — Qualification: The permission to eat moving creatures is immediately qualified by the prohibition on flesh with its lifeblood, so the food grant is not read without its limit.
  1 Timothy 4:3-5 — Thematic connection: Paul describes foods as created for thankful reception and speaks of God's creation as good, offering a later treatment of food as divine provision rather than a new flood narrative.

Genesis 9:4
But flesh with its lifeblood you shall not eat.
  Leviticus 17:10-14 — Thematic connection: The later law forbids eating blood because the life of flesh is in its blood, providing the stated rationale for the lifeblood restriction in Genesis.
  Deuteronomy 12:23-25 — Thematic connection: Deuteronomy likewise identifies blood with life and prohibits eating the life with the flesh, closely matching Genesis's food restriction.
  Acts 15:28-29 — Thematic connection: The apostolic letter instructs Gentiles to abstain from blood and strangled food, continuing the concern for blood in food without treating the audiences as identical.

Genesis 9:5
“And surely I will require your blood, the blood of your lives. From every beast I will require it, and from the hand of a fellow man I will require the life of man.
  Genesis 4:10-11 — Thematic connection: God's requiring human blood recalls His response to Abel's blood crying from the earth and His holding Cain accountable for that bloodshed.
  Exodus 21:28-29 — Thematic connection: The law concerning an ox that kills a person holds the beast, and in specified circumstances its negligent owner, accountable, illustrating accountability involving beasts and human blood.
  Genesis 9:6 — Narrative elaboration: The following statement specifies the response to shedding human blood and grounds the protection of human life in God's image.

Genesis 9:6
Whoever sheds the blood of man, his own blood shall be shed in return; for in the image of God I made man.
  Genesis 1:27 — Explicit reuse: The protection of human life is expressly grounded in God's making humanity in His image, recalling creation's image declaration.
  Exodus 20:15 — Thematic connection: The command not to murder expresses the protection of human life whose divine-image rationale is supplied in Genesis; the supplied Greek-based Exodus labels this command 20:15.
  James 3:9 — Explicit reuse: James also grounds conduct toward other people in their being made in God's likeness, applying the image principle to speech rather than bloodshed.

Genesis 9:7
“But you, grow and multiply, and fill the earth and multiply upon it.”
  Genesis 1:28 — Explicit reuse: The repeated command to grow, multiply and fill the earth renews the creation blessing after the flood.
  Genesis 9:1 — Literary parallel: The repeated multiplication command closes the unit begun by the blessing on Noah and his sons, surrounding instructions about food and human life.

Genesis 9:8
And God spoke to Noah and to his sons with him, saying,
  Genesis 6:18 — Narrative continuation: God's address to Noah and his sons introduces the establishment of the covenant earlier promised before the family entered the ark.
  Genesis 9:9-11 — Narrative continuation: The following promise to Noah, his descendants and living creatures supplies the covenant content of the divine speech introduced here.

Genesis 9:9
“Behold, I establish My covenant with you and with your seed after you,
  Genesis 6:18 — Narrative fulfilment: The covenant now established with Noah and his seed fulfils the earlier promise that God would establish His covenant with Noah.
  Genesis 17:7 — Covenant parallel: God later promises an enduring covenant with Abraham and his seed; both promises name descendants, while their distinct covenant provisions remain intact.
  Sirach 44:18 — Explicit reuse: Sirach recalls the everlasting covenant established with Noah to prevent all flesh's destruction by flood, summarizing the covenant introduced here.

Genesis 9:10
and with every living creature that is with you: the birds, the cattle, and all the wild beasts of the earth with you, all that came out of the ark.
  Genesis 6:19-20 — Narrative outcome: The living creatures included in the covenant are the creature groups earlier commanded to enter the ark for preservation.
  Genesis 8:19 — Narrative continuation: The covenant's birds, cattle and wild beasts are the same preserved creature groups described leaving the ark.
  Genesis 9:16 — Literary parallel: The later rainbow promise again explicitly includes every living creature of all flesh, confirming the broad creature scope of this covenant.

Genesis 9:11
“And I will establish My covenant with you: never again shall all flesh perish by the waters of a flood, and never again shall there be a flood of water to destroy the whole earth.”
  Genesis 8:21 — Narrative elaboration: The covenant's assurance against another flood destroying all flesh makes explicit the preservation promise given after Noah's sacrifice.
  Isaiah 54:9-10 — Explicit reuse: Isaiah recalls God's assurance in Noah's time and uses that sworn precedent to explain the stability of His covenant mercy.
  Sirach 44:18 — Explicit reuse: Sirach directly recalls the covenant established with Noah so that all flesh would not be wiped out by a flood.

Genesis 9:12
And the Lord God said to Noah, “This is the sign of the covenant which I give between Me and you and every living creature with you, for everlasting generations:
  Genesis 17:11 — Covenant parallel: The later covenant with Abraham also has an expressly named sign, circumcision, while Noah's sign concerns the bow and all living creatures.
  Exodus 31:13 — Covenant parallel: The Sabbath is later called a sign between God and Israel through their generations; the shared covenant-sign form does not make it Noah's sign.
  Genesis 9:17 — Literary parallel: The concluding statement repeats that the sign establishes a covenant between God and all flesh, matching the sign's scope announced here.

Genesis 9:13
I set My bow in the cloud, and it shall be a sign of the covenant between Me and the earth.
  Sirach 43:11-12 — Shared imagery: Sirach calls readers to contemplate the rainbow and bless its Maker, sharing the bow-in-heaven image attributed to God's action in Genesis.
  Ezekiel 1:28 — Shared imagery: Ezekiel compares the radiance around the divine appearance with a bow in cloud on a rainy day, reusing the rainbow image without restating Noah's covenant.
  Genesis 9:16 — Narrative elaboration: The bow designated as a covenant sign here is later described as the object God sees when remembering His covenant with living flesh.

Genesis 9:14
“And when I gather clouds over the earth, My bow shall appear in the cloud.
  Genesis 9:13 — Narrative elaboration: The appearance of the bow when clouds gather describes the observable setting of the covenant sign just established.
  Ezekiel 1:28 — Shared imagery: The prophetic image also locates the bow in cloud on a rainy day, corresponding to the bow's cloud setting in Genesis.
  Sirach 43:11-12 — Shared imagery: The rainbow's appearance in the sky is the same created phenomenon Sirach invites readers to observe and praise its Maker for.

Genesis 9:15
And I will remember My covenant between Me and you and every living creature of all flesh; and the waters shall never again become a flood to blot out all flesh.
  Genesis 8:1 — Literary parallel: God's remembering His covenant to preserve flesh recalls His remembering Noah and the creatures when He began ending the flood.
  Exodus 2:24 — Covenant parallel: God later hears Israel and remembers His covenant with the patriarchs; both scenes describe covenant remembrance as the ground for preserving people.
  Isaiah 54:9-10 — Explicit reuse: Isaiah recalls Noah's water and God's sworn assurance as the precedent for His enduring merciful covenant.

Genesis 9:16
“My bow shall be in the cloud, and I will look upon it and remember the everlasting covenant between Me and every living creature of all flesh upon the earth.”
  Sirach 44:18 — Explicit reuse: Sirach summarizes the everlasting covenant with Noah as preventing the destruction of all flesh by flood, corresponding to the covenant remembered at the bow.
  Genesis 9:10 — Literary parallel: The promise again includes every living creature of all flesh, reaffirming the creature groups named earlier as covenant participants.
  Genesis 17:7 — Covenant parallel: The later Abrahamic covenant is likewise described as everlasting, while its descendants and promises remain distinct from Noah's creature-wide covenant.

Genesis 9:17
And God said to Noah, “This is the sign of the covenant which I have established between Me and all flesh upon the earth.”
  Genesis 9:12 — Literary parallel: The sign's concluding explanation repeats the opening declaration of a covenant between God and all living flesh.
  Sirach 44:18 — Explicit reuse: Sirach's summary of Noah's everlasting covenant against a flood's wiping out all flesh corresponds to this concluding covenant statement.

Genesis 9:18
Now the sons of Noah who came out of the ark were Shem, Ham, and Japheth. And Ham was the father of Canaan.
  Genesis 5:32 — Genealogical correspondence: The sons emerging from the ark are the same Shem, Ham and Japheth introduced as Noah's three sons before the flood.
  Genesis 10:1 — Genealogical continuation: The following table of nations begins with the same three sons and their post-flood offspring, extending the family introduced here.
  1 Chronicles 1:4 — Genealogical correspondence: Chronicles names Noah, Shem, Ham and Japheth in its ancestral sequence, corresponding to the patriarch and three sons identified here.
  Genesis 10:6 — Genealogical correspondence: The later list explicitly includes Canaan among Ham's sons, corresponding to the parentage singled out at this family's introduction.

Genesis 9:19

  Genesis 10:32 — Narrative elaboration: The table of nations concludes that nations were dispersed from Noah's sons after the flood, elaborating the spread of people asserted here.
  Genesis 10:1 — Genealogical continuation: The following genealogies identify the descendants born to Noah's three sons after the flood, providing the family basis for their later dispersion.

Genesis 9:20
And Noah began to till the ground, and planted a vineyard.
  Genesis 3:23 — Thematic connection: Noah's cultivation of the ground continues the human task of working the earth described after Adam's expulsion from the garden.
  Genesis 5:29 — Narrative continuation: Noah's earlier naming was connected with toil on the cursed earth, while this episode depicts him beginning agricultural work and planting a vineyard.
  Genesis 9:21 — Narrative consequence: The wine Noah drinks in the next verse is the narrative outcome of his planting the vineyard, linking agricultural work with the ensuing episode.

Genesis 9:21
And he drank of the wine and became drunk, and lay uncovered in his house.
  Proverbs 20:1 — Thematic connection: Proverbs warns that wine leads to undisciplined conduct, corresponding to the intoxication and exposed condition described in Noah's episode.
  Proverbs 23:29-35 — Thematic connection: The extended warning about wine describes its deceptive attraction and harmful effects, providing a later wisdom treatment of the intoxication seen here.
  Genesis 9:24 — Narrative consequence: Noah's later awakening from wine identifies the intoxication from which he recovers after the exposure episode.

Genesis 9:22
And Ham, the father of Canaan, saw his father’s nakedness, and went out and told his two brothers outside.
  Genesis 9:23 — Narrative contrast: Ham's seeing his father's nakedness and telling his brothers contrasts with their careful covering while turning their faces away.
  Exodus 20:12 — Ethical contrast: The command to honor father and mother supplies a later ethical comparison for the contrasting treatment of Noah by his sons; it does not add unreported acts to Ham's behavior.
  Genesis 10:6 — Genealogical correspondence: The parenthetical identification of Ham as Canaan's father corresponds to Canaan's later explicit inclusion among Ham's sons.

Genesis 9:23

  Genesis 9:22 — Narrative contrast: The brothers' deliberate avoidance of seeing their father's nakedness contrasts with Ham's seeing and reporting it in the preceding verse.
  Exodus 20:12 — Thematic connection: The brothers' protective treatment of their exposed father provides a narrative comparison with the later command to honor one's parents.
  Proverbs 23:22 — Thematic connection: The instruction to heed one's father and not despise one's mother shares the concern for respectful treatment of parents displayed in the covering of Noah.

Genesis 9:24

  Genesis 9:21 — Narrative continuation: Noah's awakening from wine follows the earlier drinking and intoxication, connecting his recovery with the subsequent response.
  Genesis 9:25-27 — Narrative consequence: The curse and blessings that follow are Noah's narrated response after awakening and learning what had happened; no additional unreported act is inferred.

Genesis 9:25

Genesis 9:26

Genesis 9:27

Genesis 11:1

Genesis 11:2

Genesis 11:3

  Exodus 1:14 — Shared construction activity: Both passages describe clay and brickmaking as human construction work, while Exodus specifically places that work under forced labor rather than the mutual plan described here.

Genesis 11:4

Genesis 11:5

Genesis 11:6

  Genesis 11:1 — Narrative development: God's assessment that the people share one language recalls the unity of speech stated at the story's beginning and explains their coordinated project.
  Genesis 11:7 — Narrative response: The decision to confound speech directly addresses the linguistic unity identified in the divine assessment here.

Genesis 11:7
“Come, let Us go down and confound their language there, so that they may no longer understand one another’s speech.”
  Genesis 1:26 — Shared divine-speech form: The plural deliberative expression Let Us recalls the creation speech's plural wording; the comparison does not independently identify its addressees or establish a doctrine from the grammar.
  Acts 2:4-8 — Thematic contrast: The loss of mutual understanding in Genesis contrasts with hearers understanding the Spirit-enabled speakers in their own languages at Pentecost.
  Genesis 11:9 — Narrative fulfilment: The naming explanation reports that the Lord confounded the languages, carrying out the decision announced here.

Genesis 11:8

  Genesis 11:4 — Narrative reversal: God's scattering reverses the builders' explicitly stated wish to make a name before they were scattered over the earth.

  Deuteronomy 32:8-9 — Thematic connection: The later poem likewise speaks of the Most High scattering Adam's sons and dividing nations, corresponding to divine ordering of the dispersed human groups.

Genesis 11:9

  Acts 2:6-8 — Thematic contrast: The crowd's hearing and understanding in their own languages contrasts with the confusion of mutual speech in Genesis; Acts does not explicitly call the event a reversal of this verse.

Genesis 11:20
And Reu lived one hundred and thirty-two years, and begot Serug.
  Luke 3:35 — Genealogical parallel: Luke places Serug before Reu in its reverse ancestry, corresponding to Reu's fathering Serug here.
  1 Chronicles 1:25-26 — Genealogical comparison: Chronicles places Ragau before Serug across these units, corresponding to the Reu-to-Serug sequence while retaining its supplied name form.

Genesis 11:21
And Reu lived two hundred and seven years after he begot Serug, and begot sons and daughters, and died.
  Genesis 11:20 — Chronological continuation: The remaining years continue Reu's lifespan after the preceding birth of Serug; neither the years nor other children are imported from a different edition.
  Luke 3:35 — Genealogical context: Luke names Serug and Reu in the same reverse ancestry, supplying the family correspondence without a matching lifespan notice.

Genesis 11:22
And Serug lived one hundred and thirty years, and begot Nahor.
  Luke 3:34-35 — Genealogical parallel: Luke links Nahor with Serug across the two units of its reverse list, corresponding to Serug's fathering Nahor here.
  1 Chronicles 1:26 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles places Serug before Nahor in the ancestral sequence, matching the family relationship without supplying Serug's age.

Genesis 11:23
And Serug lived two hundred years after he begot Nahor, and begot sons and daughters, and died.
  Genesis 11:22 — Chronological continuation: Serug's two hundred remaining years continue the lifespan after the preceding record of Nahor's birth.
  Luke 3:34-35 — Genealogical context: Luke preserves Nahor and Serug as consecutive ancestors, identifying the same line without corroborating Serug's remaining years or unnamed children.

Genesis 11:24
And Nahor lived seventy-nine years, and begot Terah.
  Luke 3:34 — Genealogical parallel: Luke names Terah before Nahor in its reverse list, corresponding to Nahor's fathering Terah here without repeating Nahor's age.
  1 Chronicles 1:26 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles places Nahor before Terah, preserving the parent-line succession given a birth age in Genesis.

Genesis 11:25
And Nahor lived one hundred and twenty-nine years after he begot Terah, and begot sons and daughters, and died.
  Genesis 11:24 — Chronological continuation: Nahor's post-birth years extend the lifespan after the preceding record of Terah's birth, using the actual one-hundred-and-twenty-nine-year reading.
  Luke 3:34 — Genealogical context: Luke retains Terah and Nahor in the corresponding ancestral line, without supplying the remaining lifespan, other children or death notice.

Genesis 11:26
And Terah lived seventy years, and begot Abram, Nahor, and Haran.
  Joshua 24:2 — Genealogical correspondence: Joshua's retrospective explicitly names Terah as father of Abraham and Nahor, corresponding to two of the sons in this family heading.
  Luke 3:34 — Genealogical parallel: Luke places Abraham immediately before Terah in its reverse list, corresponding to Abram's descent from Terah; Luke's earlier Nahor is not identified with the brother named here.
  Genesis 11:27 — Genealogical repetition: The following family notice repeats Terah's three sons and adds Haran's son Lot, developing the heading rather than dating all three births to one simultaneous event.

Genesis 11:27
These are the generations of Terah. Terah begot Abram, Nahor, and Haran; and Haran begot Lot.
  Joshua 24:2-3 — Genealogical and narrative continuation: Joshua recalls Terah's family beyond the River and God's taking Abraham onward, supplying a retrospective of the family setting introduced here.
  Genesis 12:4-5 — Genealogical continuation: The subsequent migration names Abram with Lot his brother's son, continuing the nephew relationship established by Haran's fathering Lot.

Genesis 11:28
And Haran died in the presence of Terah his father, in the land of his birth, in the country of the Chaldeans.
  Genesis 11:27 — Genealogical context: The preceding family notice identifies Haran as Terah's son and Lot's father, giving the relationships relevant to Haran's death here.
  Genesis 11:31 — Narrative continuation: After Haran's death, the migration includes his son Lot as Terah's grandson; it continues the surviving family's story without confusing Haran the man with Harran the destination.

Genesis 11:29
And Abram and Nahor took wives for themselves. The name of Abram’s wife was Sara, and the name of Nahor’s wife was Milcah, the daughter of Haran, who was the father of Milcah and Iscah.
  Genesis 22:20-23 — Genealogical continuation: The later report again names Milcah as Nahor's wife and lists their descendants through Bethuel to Rebekah, continuing this marriage notice.
  Genesis 17:15 — Name-change continuation: The later divine naming statement changes Sara to Sarah, continuing the wife's identity while preserving the supplied spelling difference rather than substituting Sarai here.

Genesis 11:30
And Sara was barren, and bore no children.
  Genesis 16:1 — Narrative repetition: The later Hagar account opens by repeating that Sara bore Abram no children, continuing the barrenness identified here.
  Genesis 18:11-14 — Narrative development: Sarah's advanced age and the promise of a son develop the childlessness introduced here, without treating the later promise as already fulfilled in this verse.
  Hebrews 11:11 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews recalls Sarah's barrenness and later power to conceive through faith, corresponding to the condition from which the birth-promise narrative develops.

Genesis 11:31
And Terah took Abram his son, Lot the son of Haran, his grandson, and Sara his daughter-in-law, the wife of Abram his son. And he led them out of the country of the Chaldeans to go into the land of Canaan. But he came as far as Harran and settled there.
  Acts 7:2-4 — Explicit reuse: Stephen recalls departure from the country of the Chaldeans, residence in Haran and later movement onward; the comparison preserves Genesis's attribution of the household departure to Terah.
  Nehemiah 9:7 — Explicit reuse: The prayer recalls God's choosing Abram and bringing him out of the country of the Chaldeans, giving the divine-action perspective on the migration whose household leader is Terah here.
  Joshua 24:2-3 — Explicit reuse: Joshua recalls the family beyond the River and God's taking Abraham through the land, providing a retrospective of the ancestral migration without identifying every intermediate stage.

Genesis 11:32
And the days of Terah in Harran were two hundred and five years, and Terah died in Harran.
  Acts 7:4 — Explicit reuse: Stephen places Abraham's onward movement from Haran after his father's death, corresponding to the transition from Terah's death in Harran to Abram's subsequent departure.
  Genesis 12:4-5 — Narrative continuation: The next chapter describes Abram's departure from Harran for Canaan after this closing notice of Terah's death; no harmonized birth-year reconstruction is imposed.

Genesis 12:1
And the Lord said to Abram, “Go out from your land, and from your kindred, and from your father’s house, to the land that I will show you.
  Acts 7:2-4 — Explicit reuse: Stephen recalls God's appearance to Abraham, quotes the command to leave land and relatives, and relates the departure through Haran; his chronological presentation is retained on its own terms.
  Hebrews 11:8 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews describes Abraham obeying the call to go to an unknown place of inheritance, interpreting the departure commanded here as faith.
  Genesis 24:7 — Explicit reuse: Abraham later recalls the God who took him from his father's house and native land and promised this land to his seed.
  Nehemiah 9:7 — Explicit reuse: The prayer recalls God's choosing Abram and bringing him from the Chaldeans, placing the departure within the later recollection of divine election.

Genesis 12:2
And I will make you a great nation, and I will bless you and make your name great; and you shall be blessed.
  Genesis 17:4-6 — Promise development: The later covenant expands the promise of a great nation into fatherhood of many nations and the rise of kings from Abraham.
  Genesis 18:18 — Explicit reuse: The later declaration again joins Abraham becoming a great nation with the blessing of the nations through him.
  Sirach 44:19 — Explicit reuse: Sirach remembers Abraham as a great father of a multitude of nations, reflecting the great-nation promise in its praise of the ancestors.

Genesis 12:3
And I will bless those who bless you, and those who curse you I will curse; and in you all the tribes of the earth shall be blessed.”
  Galatians 3:8-9 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Paul recalls the blessing of all nations in Abraham and interprets it as Scripture's advance proclamation of justification by faith.
  Acts 3:25-26 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Peter recalls the promise that all earth's families will be blessed in Abraham's seed and describes the Servant's blessing as turning people from wickedness.
  Genesis 22:18 — Promise development: The later oath repeats blessing for all nations, now expressly in Abraham's seed and in connection with his obedience.
  Genesis 27:29 — Shared blessing formula: Isaac's blessing of Jacob likewise pronounces a curse on those who curse him and blessing on those who bless him, continuing the protective blessing formula.

Genesis 12:4
And Abram went as the Lord had spoken to him, and Lot went with him. Now Abram was seventy-five years old when he went out from Harran.
  Hebrews 11:8 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews recalls Abraham's obedient departure when called, corresponding to Abram going as the Lord had spoken.
  Acts 7:4 — Explicit reuse: Stephen recounts Abraham's movement from the Chaldean land to Haran and then to the promised land, corresponding to the departure from Harran described here.
  Genesis 11:31 — Narrative continuation: The earlier family journey ends with settlement in Harran; Abram's departure now resumes the movement toward Canaan with Lot still accompanying him.

Genesis 12:5
And Abram took Sara his wife, and Lot his brother’s son, and all the possessions they had acquired, and every soul they had acquired in Harran; and they set out for the land of Canaan, and came into the land of Canaan.
  Genesis 11:31 — Narrative continuation: Sara and Lot belong to the family party previously taken toward Canaan by Terah, and the resumed journey now reaches that destination.
  Genesis 13:1 — Narrative counterpart: Abram's wife, possessions and Lot later accompany his return from Egypt, preserving the household groups introduced on the journey into Canaan.
  Hebrews 11:9 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews describes Abraham's life as a foreigner in the promised land, developing the sojourn that begins with the household's arrival in Canaan.

Genesis 12:6
And Abram passed through the length of the land, to the place of Shechem, to the high oak. Now the Canaanites lived in the land at that time.
  Genesis 33:18-20 — Same place and narrative parallel: Jacob later arrives at Shechem in Canaan, camps there and establishes an altar, revisiting the place reached by Abram in his land journey.
  Genesis 35:4 — Same place: The later Shechem scene also locates an act at a tree, but its terebinth is not identified as the very oak named in Abram's journey.
  Joshua 24:32 — Same place: Joshua's conclusion places Joseph's burial at Shechem in Jacob's acquired field, continuing the biblical significance of the place reached here without identifying the parcels.

Genesis 12:7
And the Lord appeared to Abram and said to him, “To your seed I will give this land.” And there Abram built an altar to the Lord, who had appeared to him.
  Genesis 13:15 — Promise renewal: After Lot's separation God again promises the land to Abram and his seed, renewing the promise first attached here to God's appearance.
  Genesis 15:18 — Promise development: The later covenant repeats the land gift to Abram's seed and adds named river boundaries to the promise.
  Genesis 17:8 — Promise development: The covenant later specifies all Canaan as the land of sojourning given to Abraham and his seed as an enduring possession.
  Acts 7:5 — Explicit reuse: Stephen recalls God's promise to give the land to Abraham and his seed even while Abraham had no child and no present inheritance there.

Genesis 12:8
And he departed from there to the mountain east of Bethel, and pitched his tent there, with Bethel toward the sea and Ai toward the east. And there he built an altar to the Lord and called upon the name of the Lord.
  Genesis 13:3-4 — Narrative return: The later journey returns explicitly to the former tent and altar between Bethel and Ai, where Abram again calls on the Lord's name.
  Genesis 26:25 — Narrative parallel: Isaac later builds an altar, calls upon the Lord's name and pitches his tent, repeating the combination of worship and sojourning seen here.
  Genesis 21:33 — Literary parallel: Abraham again calls upon the Lord's name at the Well of the Oath, continuing the worship practice described at his altar near Bethel.

Genesis 12:9
And Abram departed, and journeyed, and camped in the wilderness.
  Genesis 13:3 — Narrative return: The later return retraces Abram's route through the wilderness toward his earlier Bethel camp, recalling the wilderness stages begun here.
  Genesis 12:10 — Narrative continuation: The following famine explains the next major movement in Abram's journey, from travel through the land and wilderness to a temporary stay in Egypt.

Genesis 12:10
And there was a famine in the land; and Abram went down into Egypt to stay there, for the famine had become severe in the land.
  Genesis 26:1-2 — Explicit retrospective contrast: Isaac's later famine is expressly distinguished from the earlier famine in Abraham's time, and Isaac is instructed not to go down to Egypt.
  Genesis 42:1-2 — Narrative parallel: Jacob later sends his sons to Egypt for grain so that they may live during scarcity, paralleling Egypt's role as refuge from famine without equating the episodes.

Genesis 12:11
And when Abram drew near to enter Egypt, Abram said to Sara his wife, “I know that you are a woman with a beautiful face.
  Genesis 12:14 — Narrative fulfilment: The Egyptians' later observation that Sara is very beautiful fulfils the expectation underlying Abram's concern before entering Egypt.
  Genesis 26:7 — Narrative parallel: Isaac similarly fears being killed because of Rebekah's beauty and describes his wife as his sister, repeating the wife-and-sister danger motif.
  Genesis 20:2 — Narrative parallel: The later Gerar episode again identifies Sarah as Abraham's wife while he presents her as his sister out of fear for his life.

Genesis 12:12
When the Egyptians see you, they will say, ‘This is his wife’; and they will kill me, but they will keep you alive.
  Genesis 20:11 — Explicit reuse: Abraham later explains that he feared men would kill him for his wife's sake, repeating the danger anticipated in Egypt.
  Genesis 26:7 — Narrative parallel: Isaac's explanation likewise joins his wife's beauty with fear that local men might kill him, corresponding to Abram's stated anxiety.

Genesis 12:13
So say, ‘I am his sister,’ that it may be well with me for your sake, and my soul shall live because of you.”
  Genesis 20:2 — Literary parallel: Abraham repeats the declaration that Sarah is his sister in the later Gerar episode, where it again precedes a ruler's taking her.
  Genesis 20:12-13 — Explicit retrospective explanation: Abraham later explains the kinship claim and says he asked Sarah to use it wherever they travelled; this recalls the agreement proposed here without endorsing its deception.
  Genesis 26:7 — Narrative parallel: Isaac later uses the same sister claim concerning Rebekah in order to avoid feared death, repeating the protective strategy used here.

Genesis 12:14
And when Abram entered Egypt, the Egyptians saw that the woman was very beautiful.
  Genesis 12:11 — Narrative fulfilment: The Egyptians' perception of Sara's beauty confirms the feature Abram had named before entering their land.
  Genesis 26:7 — Narrative parallel: Rebekah's beauty likewise becomes a reason for feared danger in the later wife-and-sister account, while the two women and episodes remain distinct.

Genesis 12:15
And Pharaoh’s rulers saw her and praised her to Pharaoh; and they brought her into Pharaoh’s house.
  Genesis 20:2-3 — Narrative parallel: Abimelech later takes Sarah after the sister claim, and God identifies her as a man's wife; both episodes place the patriarch's wife in a ruler's household.
  Genesis 12:19 — Narrative consequence: Pharaoh's later complaint explicitly acknowledges that he took Sara as wife because of Abram's sister claim, explaining the household transfer narrated here.

Genesis 12:16
And they treated Abram well for her sake; and he had sheep, and calves, and donkeys, male slaves and female slaves, mules and camels.
  Genesis 13:2 — Narrative continuation: The subsequent description of Abram's great livestock and material wealth follows the account of gifts received during the Egyptian episode.
  Genesis 20:14 — Narrative parallel: The later Gerar ruler also gives Abraham livestock and servants in the Sarah episode, though that gift accompanies her restoration rather than her initial taking.

Genesis 12:17
And God afflicted Pharaoh and his house with great and grievous afflictions because of Sara, Abram’s wife.
  Genesis 20:17-18 — Narrative parallel: The Lord's affliction of Abimelech's household because of Sarah and its later healing parallel divine intervention in Pharaoh's household because of Sara.
  Psalms 104:14-15 — Thematic recollection: The psalm recalls God's protection of the patriarchs by rebuking kings on their behalf, providing a poetic counterpart to the affliction of Pharaoh's household.
  Genesis 12:3 — Narrative connection: The earlier protective blessing promises divine opposition to those who curse Abram; the affliction here illustrates divine protection without being labelled a direct quotation of that promise.

Genesis 12:18
And Pharaoh called Abram and said, “What is this you have done to me? Why did you not tell me that she is your wife?
  Genesis 20:9-10 — Narrative parallel: Abimelech later confronts Abraham about conduct that brought danger on his kingdom, paralleling Pharaoh's rebuke over Sara's concealed marital status.
  Genesis 26:9-10 — Narrative parallel: The ruler in Isaac's episode likewise challenges the claim that the woman is a sister and points to the offence it could have caused.

Genesis 12:19
Why did you say, ‘She is my sister,’ so that I took her as my wife? And now, look, your wife is before you; take her and hurry away.”
  Genesis 20:7 — Narrative parallel: God later commands Abimelech to return the married woman, corresponding to Pharaoh's returning Sara to Abram after the truth is exposed.
  Genesis 26:10-11 — Narrative parallel: The later Gerar episode also moves from rebuke over the sister claim to protection of the actual marriage, while its particular circumstances remain distinct.

Genesis 12:20
And Pharaoh commanded men to escort Abram away, with his wife and all that he had, and Lot with him.
  Genesis 13:1 — Narrative fulfilment: Abram's departure from Egypt with his wife, possessions and Lot carries out the escorted removal ordered by Pharaoh.
