  Genesis 20:15 — Narrative contrast: Abimelech later offers Abraham freedom to remain in his land, contrasting with Pharaoh's instruction that Abram depart from Egypt.
  Exodus 12:31-32 — Narrative parallel: A later Pharaoh also orders departure while allowing the departing people to take livestock; this is a departure motif, not an identification of the episodes.

Genesis 13:1
And Abram went up out of Egypt into the wilderness, he and his wife, and all that he had, and Lot with him.
  Genesis 12:20 — Narrative fulfilment: The household's return out of Egypt follows Pharaoh's command to escort Abram away with his wife, possessions and Lot.
  Genesis 12:5 — Narrative counterpart: The wife, possessions and Lot who accompanied Abram into Canaan remain the household groups accompanying his return from Egypt.

Genesis 13:2
Now Abram was very rich in livestock, and in silver, and in gold.
  Genesis 12:16 — Narrative continuation: The livestock and servants gained in the Egyptian episode precede the report of Abram's considerable wealth, though this verse also names silver and gold.
  Genesis 24:35 — Explicit retrospective description: Abraham's servant later attributes his master's livestock, silver, gold and household wealth to the Lord's blessing, describing the same patriarch's prosperity.

Genesis 13:3
And he went back the way he had come, into the wilderness as far as Bethel, to the place where his tent had been before, between Bethel and Ai,
  Genesis 12:8 — Explicit geographical return: The location between Bethel and Ai is expressly the site of Abram's former tent, making the journey a return to the earlier camp.
  Hebrews 11:9 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews describes Abraham dwelling in tents as a foreigner in the promised land, the way of life illustrated by his return to this tent site.

Genesis 13:4
to the place of the altar, where he had made it at first. And there Abram called upon the name of the Lord.
  Genesis 12:8 — Explicit reuse: Abram returns to the altar where he had first called on the Lord's name, directly recalling the earlier worship scene near Bethel.
  Genesis 21:33 — Literary parallel: Abraham later calls on the Lord's name at the Well of the Oath, continuing the same named practice of worship in another setting.
  Genesis 26:25 — Narrative parallel: Isaac later builds an altar and calls on the Lord's name beside his tent, repeating the patriarchal worship-and-sojourning pattern.

Genesis 13:5
And Lot also, who journeyed with Abram, had sheep, and cattle, and tents.
  Genesis 12:4-5 — Narrative continuation: Lot's journey with Abram and the family's acquired possessions were introduced before entry into Canaan; his own livestock now helps explain the coming separation.
  Genesis 14:12 — Narrative continuation: The later capture of Lot and his baggage shows the possessions of the companion who is here described as independently keeping livestock and tents.

Genesis 13:6
And the land could not hold them while they lived together, for their possessions were many; and they could not live together.
  Genesis 36:6-7 — Literary parallel: Esau and Jacob later separate because their possessions are too great for shared residence and the land cannot support them, closely repeating this explanation.
  Genesis 13:11 — Narrative consequence: Lot's choice and the relatives' separation carry out the relocation made necessary by the land's inability to sustain their combined possessions.

Genesis 13:7
And there was strife between the herdsmen of Abram’s livestock and the herdsmen of Lot’s livestock. Now the Canaanites and the Perizzites lived in the land at that time.
  Genesis 26:19-22 — Narrative parallel: Isaac's shepherds later contend over scarce water and move to uncontested space, paralleling pastoral conflict and relocation over resources.
  Genesis 13:8 — Narrative response: Abram's appeal to end strife between the herdsmen directly addresses the conflict reported here.
  Genesis 12:6 — Narrative setting: The earlier journey already noted Canaanites in the land; their continuing presence is part of the setting within which the herdsmen's dispute occurs.

Genesis 13:8
And Abram said to Lot, “Let there be no strife between me and you, nor between my herdsmen and your herdsmen; for we are brothers.
  Genesis 12:5 — Kinship context: The earlier identification of Lot as Abram's brother's son explains the broader family sense of brothers in Abram's peace appeal.
  Matthew 5:9 — Thematic connection: Jesus' blessing of peacemakers supplies a later ethical comparison for Abram's initiative to prevent family and herdsmen's strife.
  Genesis 13:9 — Narrative development: Abram follows the appeal for peace with a concrete proposal allowing Lot to choose a separate part of the land.

Genesis 13:9
Look, is not all the land before you? Separate yourself from me. If you go to the left, I will go to the right; and if you go to the right, I will go to the left.”
  Genesis 13:11 — Narrative fulfilment: Lot's selection of the Jordan country and departure implement the choice and separation Abram offers here.
  Genesis 36:6-7 — Narrative parallel: Esau later leaves Jacob because their extensive possessions prevent living together, paralleling a practical separation between wealthy relatives.
  Romans 12:18 — Thematic connection: Paul's instruction to pursue peace as far as one's own action permits corresponds to Abram's initiative in yielding the first choice to prevent strife.

Genesis 13:10
And Lot lifted up his eyes and saw all the country around the Jordan, that it was all well watered, before God overthrew Sodom and Gomorrah, like the garden of God and like the land of Egypt, as far as Zoar.
  Genesis 2:8-10 — Explicit imagery: The narrator's comparison with God's garden recalls the garden watered by its river, explaining the attraction of the well-watered Jordan country.
  Genesis 19:24-25 — Narrative anticipation: The parenthetical reference to Sodom and Gomorrah's future overthrow anticipates the destruction later narrated by fire and sulphur.
  Genesis 19:22-23 — Same place and narrative continuation: Zoar, named in the limit of Lot's view here, later becomes the city to which he escapes before the surrounding destruction.

Genesis 13:11
And Lot chose for himself all the country around the Jordan; and Lot departed from the east, and they separated, each from his brother.
  Genesis 13:9 — Narrative fulfilment: Lot exercises the choice of land Abram offered, and the separation resolves their inability to remain together.
  Genesis 13:12 — Narrative elaboration: The following verse states the resulting residences of the two relatives after Lot chooses the Jordan country.
  Genesis 14:12 — Narrative consequence: Lot's later capture specifically occurs because he lives in Sodom, continuing the consequences of the relocation chosen here.

Genesis 13:12
And Abram lived in the land of Canaan; but Lot lived in a city of the surrounding country, and pitched his tent in Sodom.
  Genesis 14:12 — Explicit narrative continuation: The capture account explicitly states that Lot lived in Sodom, confirming the residence introduced by his move here.
  Genesis 19:1 — Narrative continuation: Lot later sits at Sodom's gate when the angels arrive, locating him in the city toward which he has moved.

Genesis 13:13
Now the men of Sodom were exceedingly wicked and sinful before God.
  Genesis 18:20 — Explicit reuse: The Lord later describes Sodom and Gomorrah's outcry and exceedingly great sins, developing the moral assessment introduced here.
  Genesis 19:4-9 — Narrative illustration: The hostile crowd's intended violence toward Lot's visitors supplies a later concrete narrative instance of the city's wickedness.
  2 Peter 2:6-8 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Peter recalls Sodom's ungodliness and Lot's distress at lawless conduct, interpreting the moral environment summarized here.

Genesis 13:14
And God said to Abram, after Lot had separated himself from him, “Lift up your eyes and look from the place where you now are, toward the north, and the south, and the east, and the sea.
  Genesis 12:7 — Promise renewal: God's renewed address after Lot's separation continues the land promise first given at His earlier appearance to Abram.
  Genesis 28:14 — Promise parallel: The later promise to Jacob also describes expansion toward four directions, echoing the breadth of the land displayed to Abram.

Genesis 13:15
For all the land that you see I will give to you and to your seed for ever.
  Genesis 12:7 — Promise renewal: The promise to give land to Abram's seed is repeated with the expanded declaration that the land he sees belongs to him and his seed for ever.
  Genesis 17:8 — Promise development: The later covenant identifies all Canaan as the enduring possession of Abraham and his seed, elaborating the land promise given here.
  Nehemiah 9:8 — Explicit retrospective interpretation: The prayer recalls God's covenant to give the land to Abram and his offspring and praises His fulfilment of that promise.
  2 Chronicles 20:7 — Explicit reuse: Jehoshaphat's prayer recalls God's giving the land for ever to Abraham's descendants, appealing to the enduring gift promised here.

Genesis 13:16
And I will make your seed like the sand of the earth. If anyone can count the sand of the earth, then your seed too shall be counted.
  Genesis 22:17 — Promise development: The later oath compares the multiplication of Abraham's seed to the stars and seashore sand, developing the innumerable-sand image used here.
  Genesis 28:14 — Promise renewal: Jacob later receives the same sand-of-the-earth image for numerous descendants, continuing the ancestral promise in the supplied edition's wording.
  Hebrews 11:12 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews recalls Abraham's descendants becoming innumerable like heavenly stars and seashore sand, interpreting the multitude promised to him.
  Sirach 44:21 — Explicit reuse: Sirach recalls God's assurance to multiply Abraham like dust and raise his offspring like stars, preserving a related multitude image with its own wording.

Genesis 13:17
Rise, pass through the length and breadth of the land; for I will give it to you.”
  Genesis 12:6 — Narrative correspondence: Abram's earlier passage through the length of the land corresponds to the renewed instruction to walk its length and breadth under God's promise.
  Genesis 13:15 — Promise context: The command to traverse the land is grounded in the immediately preceding promise that the land will be given to Abram and his seed.

Genesis 13:18
And Abram moved his tent, and came and lived beside the oak of Mamre, which was in Hebron; and there he built an altar to the Lord.
  Genesis 18:1 — Narrative setting: The later appearance of God occurs beside the oak of Mamre, continuing the dwelling place established by Abram here.
  Genesis 23:19 — Same place: The burial notice identifies Mamre with Hebron, the same location paired with the oak and Abram's camp in this verse.

Genesis 14:1
And in the reign of Amraphel king of Shinar, Arioch king of Ellasar, and Chedorlaomer king of Elam, and Thargal king of nations
  Genesis 14:9 — Explicit narrative repetition: The later battle account repeats the same four invading kings and their kingdoms, identifying the coalition introduced here.
  Genesis 10:10 — Same region: The earlier account locates Babylon and other cities in Shinar, providing a prior biblical reference for the region whose king belongs to this coalition.

Genesis 14:2
made war with Balla king of Sodom, and Barsa king of Gomorrah, and Sennaar king of Admah, and Symobor king of Zeboiim, and the king of Balak, which is Zoar.
  Genesis 14:8 — Explicit narrative repetition: The same five city-kings are named again when they draw up their battle lines, continuing the coalition introduced here.
  Deuteronomy 29:22 — Same cities and later judgment: Deuteronomy's warning recalls the overthrow of Sodom, Gomorrah, Admah and Zeboiim, four cities whose kings are listed in this earlier conflict.

Genesis 14:3
All these joined together at the salt ravine, which is the Salt Sea.
  Genesis 14:8-10 — Narrative elaboration: The following battle takes place in the salt valley and describes its bitumen pits, developing the location of the coalition's gathering.
  Deuteronomy 3:17 — Same geographical reference: Deuteronomy's border description also names the Salt Sea, the water body used here to identify the ravine; neither passage fixes a modern battle site.

Genesis 14:4
For twelve years they served Chedorlaomer, but in the thirteenth year they rebelled.
  Genesis 14:5 — Narrative consequence: The next dated campaign follows the thirteenth-year rebellion against Chedorlaomer, supplying the military response in the fourteenth year.
  Genesis 14:9 — Narrative context: The ensuing battle names Chedorlaomer among the invading kings, connecting the revolt against his rule with the coalition conflict.

Genesis 14:5
And in the fourteenth year Chedorlaomer came with the kings who were with him, and struck the giants in Ashteroth-karnaim, and strong nations with them, and the Ommaeans in the city of Shaveh,
  Genesis 14:4 — Chronological continuation: The fourteenth-year campaign follows the twelve years of service and thirteenth-year rebellion, maintaining the sequence stated in the account.
  Genesis 14:9 — Narrative continuation: Chedorlaomer and his accompanying kings appear again in the battle against the five city-kings, continuing the same campaign.

Genesis 14:6
and the Horites in the mountains of Seir, as far as the terebinth of Paran, which is in the wilderness.
  Deuteronomy 2:12 — Same people and region: Deuteronomy likewise locates the earlier Horites in Seir and recounts their later displacement by Esau's descendants, corresponding to the people and mountain region named here.
  Genesis 36:20-21 — Genealogical and regional correspondence: The later genealogy names Seir the Horite and his chiefs in Edom, supplying a related record of the Horite population and region encountered by the campaign.

Genesis 14:7
And they turned back and came to the Spring of Judgment, which is Kadesh; and they struck all the rulers of Amalek, and the Amorites who lived in Hazezon-tamar.
  Genesis 14:6 — Narrative itinerary: The return toward Kadesh and the Amorite settlements follows the preceding southward movement through Seir toward Paran; no modern route reconstruction is added.
  Genesis 14:8-9 — Narrative continuation: The campaign's attacks on the surrounding peoples precede the five city-kings' battle against the invading coalition.

Genesis 14:8
And the king of Sodom, and the king of Gomorrah, and the king of Admah, and the king of Zeboiim, and the king of Balak, which is Zoar, went out and drew up their battle lines in the salt valley
  Genesis 14:2 — Explicit narrative repetition: The five kings drawing up for battle are the same city-kings listed at the opening of the conflict.
  Genesis 14:9 — Narrative counterpart: The next verse names the four invading kings against whom the five city-kings take their battle positions.

Genesis 14:9
against Chedorlaomer king of Elam, and Thargal king of nations, and Amraphel king of Shinar, and Arioch king of Ellasar: four kings against five.
  Genesis 14:1 — Explicit narrative repetition: The four invading kings and their kingdoms repeat the coalition introduced at the account's beginning.
  Genesis 14:8 — Narrative counterpart: The immediately preceding line names the five opposing kings, supplying the other side of the four-against-five comparison.

Genesis 14:10
Now the salt valley was full of bitumen pits. And the king of Sodom and the king of Gomorrah fled and fell into them; and those who remained fled to the hill country.
  Genesis 14:3 — Narrative setting: The bitumen pits and flight are located in the same salt ravine previously identified as the coalition's gathering place.
  Genesis 14:11 — Narrative consequence: The seizure of Sodom and Gomorrah's horses and food follows their kings' flight and the defeat in the salt valley.

Genesis 14:11
And they took all the horses of Sodom and Gomorrah, and all their food, and departed.
  Genesis 14:16 — Narrative reversal: Abram later brings back the horses of Sodom, reversing the capture of horses and other spoils narrated here.
  Genesis 14:21 — Narrative continuation: The king of Sodom's later proposal concerns the recovered men and horses, continuing the disposition of the spoils taken in this raid.

Genesis 14:12
And they also took Lot, Abram’s brother’s son, and his baggage, and departed; for he lived in Sodom.
  Genesis 13:12 — Explicit narrative continuation: Lot's capture is explained by his residence in Sodom, the city in which he had settled after separating from Abram.
  Genesis 14:16 — Narrative reversal: Abram's later recovery of Lot and his possessions reverses their capture and removal by the invading kings.

Genesis 14:13
And one of those who escaped came and told Abram, the man from beyond. Now he lived beside the oak of Mamre the Amorite, the brother of Eshcol and the brother of Aunan; and these were sworn allies of Abram.
  Genesis 13:18 — Narrative setting: The escapee finds Abram at the oak of Mamre, the dwelling place established after Lot's separation.
  Genesis 14:24 — Explicit narrative repetition: Mamre, Eshcol and Aunan are named again as the men entitled to their portion after the rescue, confirming the allies introduced here.

Genesis 14:14
And when Abram heard that Lot his brother had been taken captive, he counted his own slaves born in his house, three hundred and eighteen, and pursued them as far as Dan.
  Genesis 12:5 — Kinship context: The earlier identification of Lot as Abram's brother's son explains the broader family use of brother when Abram hears of his capture.
  Genesis 14:15-16 — Narrative fulfilment: The pursuit with Abram's household force leads to the night attack and the recovery of Lot, people and possessions.
  Genesis 17:23 — Shared household category: The later circumcision account also distinguishes men born within Abraham's house, corresponding to the household category mobilized here without identifying the later group as the same three hundred and eighteen.

Genesis 14:15
And he fell upon them by night, he and his servants, and struck them; and he pursued them as far as Hobah, which is to the left of Damascus.
  Genesis 14:14 — Narrative fulfilment: The night attack and further pursuit carry out the rescue expedition begun when Abram hears of Lot's capture.
  Genesis 14:16 — Narrative consequence: The successful attack is followed by the restoration of Lot, the captured people and possessions, showing the expedition's result.
  Hebrews 7:1 — Explicit retrospective context: Hebrews recalls Abraham's return from the defeat of the kings as the occasion of Melchizedek's meeting, referring back to the victory narrated in this campaign.

Genesis 14:16
And he brought back all the horses of Sodom; and he brought back Lot his brother, and his possessions, and the women, and the people.
  Genesis 14:11-12 — Narrative reversal: The recovered horses, Lot and his possessions correspond to the spoils and captive taken from Sodom in the earlier raid.
  Genesis 14:21 — Narrative continuation: The king of Sodom's later proposal concerns the people and horses Abram has recovered, continuing the question of their disposition.

Genesis 14:17
And the king of Sodom went out to meet him, after his return from the defeat of Chedorlaomer and the kings with him, at the valley of Shaveh, which was the King’s Plain.
  Genesis 14:15-16 — Narrative continuation: The king of Sodom's meeting takes place after Abram's victory and recovery of captives, the outcome narrated immediately before his return.
  Genesis 14:21 — Narrative development: The king who comes out to meet Abram later asks for the men while offering the horses, supplying the purpose of his ensuing conversation.

Genesis 14:18
And Melchizedek king of Salem brought out loaves and wine; and he was priest of God Most High.
  Hebrews 7:1-3 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Hebrews recalls Melchizedek as king of Salem and priest of God Most High, then develops the figure's priestly significance in relation to the Son of God.
  Psalms 109:4 — Priestly correspondence: The psalm invokes enduring priesthood according to Melchizedek's order, reusing the priestly figure introduced here without restating the bread-and-wine action.
  Hebrews 6:20 — Theological interpretation: Hebrews describes Jesus as high priest according to Melchizedek's order, explicitly interpreting the significance of the priestly figure named in Genesis.

Genesis 14:19
And he blessed Abram and said, “Blessed be Abram by God Most High, who created the heaven and the earth;
  Hebrews 7:6-7 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Hebrews recalls Melchizedek blessing the bearer of the promises and interprets the blessing in terms of greater and lesser.
  Genesis 1:1 — Shared divine identity: The blessing identifies God Most High as maker of heaven and earth, recalling the opening attribution of heaven and earth's making to God.
  Genesis 14:22 — Shared wording: Abram repeats God Most High's creation of heaven and earth in his own oath, taking up the divine title used in Melchizedek's blessing.

Genesis 14:20
and blessed be God Most High, who has delivered your enemies into your power.” And he gave him a tenth of all.
  Hebrews 7:2-6 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Hebrews expressly identifies Abraham as giving Melchizedek a tenth and uses that gift to develop the priest's greatness.
  Hebrews 7:9-10 — Theological interpretation: Hebrews develops the tenth-giving scene by treating Levi as paying through his ancestor Abraham; that is the later text's interpretation rather than a statement added to Genesis.
  Genesis 28:22 — Thematic connection: Jacob later vows a tenth of what God gives him, providing another ancestral act of tenth-giving while differing from Abram's gift following victory.

Genesis 14:21
And the king of Sodom said to Abram, “Give me the men, and take the horses for yourself.”
  Genesis 14:16 — Narrative continuation: The request for the men and offer of the horses concerns the people and possessions recovered in Abram's rescue.
  Genesis 14:23 — Narrative response: Abram's refusal to let the king claim to have enriched him answers the offer of captured horses and other property.

Genesis 14:22
And Abram said to the king of Sodom, “I will stretch out my hand to God Most High, who created the heaven and the earth,
  Genesis 14:19 — Shared wording: Abram's oath repeats the title God Most High and the making of heaven and earth used in Melchizedek's blessing.
  Genesis 24:3 — Shared oath language: Abraham later binds his servant by an oath invoking the Lord as God of heaven and earth, corresponding to the divine title attached to his sworn refusal here.

Genesis 14:23
that I will take neither a thread nor a sandal fastening of anything that is yours, so that you may not say, ‘I have made Abram rich,’
  Genesis 14:21 — Narrative response: The refusal to accept even small items of property answers the king of Sodom's proposal that Abram take the recovered horses for himself.
  Genesis 14:24 — Narrative qualification: The following statement limits Abram's refusal by allowing food already consumed and the portion belonging to his allies.
  Genesis 24:35 — Thematic connection: The servant later credits Abraham's wealth to the Lord's blessing, corresponding to Abram's refusal to let the king of Sodom claim credit for enriching him.

Genesis 14:24
except what the young men have eaten, and the portion of the men who went with me: Eshcol, Aunan, and Mamre. They shall take a portion.”
  Genesis 14:13 — Explicit narrative repetition: Eshcol, Aunan and Mamre are the sworn allies introduced when Abram receives news of Lot's capture, and they now retain entitlement to their portion.
  Genesis 14:23 — Narrative qualification: Abram distinguishes his allies' share and the young men's consumed food from his own refusal to accept the king's property.

Genesis 15:1
After these things the word of the Lord came to Abram in a vision, saying, “Do not fear, Abram; I shield you. Your reward shall be exceedingly great.”
  Genesis 26:24 — Promise parallel: God's later appearance to Isaac likewise begins with reassurance against fear and promises His presence and the multiplication of offspring.
  Hebrews 11:6 — Thematic connection: Hebrews describes God as rewarding those who approach Him in faith, corresponding to the reward promised as Abram receives God's word.

Genesis 15:2
And Abram said, “Master, what wilt Thou give me? I depart childless; and the son of Masek, the slave woman born in my house, is this Damascus Eliezer.”
  Genesis 16:1 — Narrative context: The later statement that Sara bore no children confirms the childlessness underlying Abram's question about a household heir.
  Acts 7:5 — Explicit reuse: Stephen recalls God's promise of possession and offspring when Abraham had no child, the predicament expressed in Abram's question.

Genesis 15:3
And Abram said, “Since Thou hast given me no seed, a slave born in my house shall inherit from me.”
  Genesis 15:4 — Narrative answer: God's immediate answer rejects the household heir Abram has proposed and promises an heir who will come from Abram himself.
  Genesis 25:5 — Narrative outcome: Abraham's eventual giving of his possessions to Isaac supplies the later inheritance outcome, contrasting with his proposed household-slave heir.

Genesis 15:4
And immediately the voice of the Lord came to him, saying, “This man shall not inherit from you; but the one who will come from you, he shall inherit from you.”
  Genesis 17:19 — Promise development: The later promise identifies Sarah's future son Isaac and the covenant established with him, making the promised heir more specific.
  Genesis 21:12 — Promise development: God later identifies Isaac as the line in which Abraham's offspring will be called, further specifying the promised personal descendant.

Genesis 15:5
And He brought him outside and said to him, “Look now toward heaven, and count the stars, if you can count them.” And He said, “So shall your seed be.”
  Romans 4:18 — Direct quotation and interpretation: Paul quotes So shall your seed be while describing Abraham's hope of becoming father of many nations.
  Hebrews 11:12 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews recalls descendants as numerous as the stars of heaven, corresponding to God's stellar image for Abram's offspring.
  Sirach 44:21 — Explicit reuse: Sirach recalls God's assurance that Abraham's offspring would be raised like the stars, reusing this multiplication image.
  Genesis 22:17 — Promise repetition: The later oath again compares Abraham's multiplied offspring with heaven's stars, now also adding the sand image.

Genesis 15:6
And Abram believed God, and it was reckoned to him as righteousness.
  Romans 4:3 — Direct quotation: Paul quotes Abram's believing God and its reckoning as righteousness as the scriptural basis for his argument about justification.
  Galatians 3:6-9 — Direct quotation and interpretation: Paul quotes the reckoning of Abraham's belief as righteousness and applies it to the identity of people of faith.
  James 2:21-23 — Direct quotation and interpretation: James quotes this statement and relates its fulfilment to Abraham's faith working with his action in offering Isaac.
  Romans 4:22-24 — Direct quotation and application: Paul repeats the reckoning language and applies the written account beyond Abraham to those believing in the God who raised Jesus.

Genesis 15:7
And He said to him, “I am God, who brought you out from the country of the Chaldeans, to give you this land to inherit.”
  Nehemiah 9:7-8 — Explicit reuse: The prayer recalls God's choosing Abram, bringing him from the Chaldeans and covenanting to give land to his offspring.
  Acts 7:4-5 — Explicit reuse: Stephen recalls Abraham's departure from the Chaldeans and God's promise of land possession to him and his offspring.
  Exodus 6:4 — Explicit reuse: God's later speech recalls establishing the patriarchal covenant to give the land of their sojourning, corresponding to the land grant announced here.

Genesis 15:8
And he said, “Master Lord, how shall I know that I shall inherit it?”
  Genesis 15:17-18 — Narrative answer: The passage through the divided pieces and the explicit land covenant provide the narrative response to Abram's request for assurance.
  Hebrews 6:17-18 — Thematic connection: Hebrews describes God's confirmation of His unchanging promise as encouragement for its heirs; this is a promise-assurance comparison, not a claim that Genesis 15 records the later oath quotation.

Genesis 15:9
And He said to him, “Take for Me a heifer three years old, and a female goat three years old, and a ram three years old, and a turtledove, and a pigeon.”
  Genesis 15:10 — Narrative fulfilment: Abram takes the requested creatures and arranges their pieces, carrying out the command that prepares the covenant scene.
  Leviticus 1:14 — Shared offering categories: Turtledoves and pigeons also appear as the bird categories in the later offering law; the comparison does not equate the covenant scene with Sinai's prescribed rite.

Genesis 15:10
And he took all these for Him, and divided them down the middle, and laid the pieces opposite one another; but he did not divide the birds.
  Genesis 15:17 — Narrative outcome: The divided pieces arranged by Abram become the space through which the fiery manifestation passes in the covenant scene.
  Leviticus 1:17 — Ritual comparison: The later bird-offering instruction likewise says not to divide the bird; this particular handling parallel does not identify the two rites as the same legislation.

Genesis 15:11
And birds came down upon the bodies, upon their divided pieces; and Abram sat down with them.
  Genesis 15:10 — Narrative continuation: The bodies and divided pieces upon which birds descend are the creatures Abram has just arranged in preparation for the covenant.

Genesis 15:12
And about sunset a trance fell upon Abram; and behold, a great dark fear fell upon him.
  Genesis 2:21 — Narrative parallel: Abram's trance and Adam's divinely imposed deep sleep are comparable altered states within scenes of divine action; their purposes and events remain distinct.
  Genesis 15:17 — Narrative development: The sunset and darkness accompanying Abram's trance lead into the later sunset appearance of fire among the divided pieces.

Genesis 15:13
And it was said to Abram, “Know for certain that your seed shall live as strangers in a land not their own; and they shall enslave them, and afflict them, and humble them for four hundred years.
  Acts 7:6 — Explicit reuse: Stephen repeats the prediction of Abraham's offspring living as strangers and suffering enslavement and mistreatment for four hundred years.
  Exodus 1:11-14 — Narrative fulfilment: The account of Egyptian overseers, affliction and forced labour describes the oppression of Abraham's descendants anticipated in the promise.
  Exodus 12:40-41 — Chronological comparison: Exodus gives four hundred and thirty years for residence in Egypt and Canaan; its different span is preserved beside Genesis's four-hundred-year affliction prediction without forced harmonization.

Genesis 15:14
But I will judge the nation they serve; and afterward they shall come out here with much baggage.
  Acts 7:7 — Explicit reuse: Stephen repeats God's judgment of the enslaving nation and the descendants' subsequent departure, applying the promise in his historical summary.
  Exodus 12:35-38 — Narrative fulfilment: Israel's departure with Egyptian silver, gold, clothing and abundant livestock corresponds to the promised exit with much baggage.
  Exodus 6:6 — Promise repetition: God's later promise to ransom Israel with great judgment restates deliverance from the enslaving Egyptians anticipated here.

Genesis 15:15
But you shall depart to your fathers in peace, buried at a good old age.
  Genesis 25:7-10 — Narrative fulfilment: Abraham's death in a good old age and burial by Isaac and Ishmael provide the later outcome of this personal assurance.
  Hebrews 11:13 — Thematic connection: Hebrews places the patriarchs' deaths within continuing faith in promises not fully received, illuminating Abraham's peaceful death before his descendants' later inheritance.

Genesis 15:16
And in the fourth generation they shall return here; for the sins of the Amorites are not yet filled up.”
  Exodus 3:8 — Promise development: God's later deliverance announcement names the Amorites among the peoples of the land to which Israel will be brought.
  Deuteronomy 9:4-6 — Thematic connection: Deuteronomy links dispossession of the nations with their ungodliness and God's patriarchal covenant, corresponding to the moral and promissory grounds given here.

Genesis 15:17
And as the sun was setting, a flame appeared; and behold, a smoking furnace, and torches of fire which passed between these divided pieces.
  Genesis 15:10 — Narrative continuation: The fiery manifestation passes between the very pieces Abram prepared, connecting the manifestation with the covenant's arranged setting.
  Exodus 19:18 — Shared divine-presence imagery: Sinai's divine descent is accompanied by fire and furnace-like smoke, sharing the smoking-furnace and fire imagery of Abram's vision without making the events identical.
  Genesis 15:18 — Narrative interpretation: The following verse explicitly identifies the day's event as God's covenant with Abram and states its land promise.

Genesis 15:18
On that day the Lord made a covenant with Abram, saying, “To your seed I will give this land, from the river of Egypt to the great river, the river Euphrates:
  Nehemiah 9:8 — Explicit reuse: Nehemiah's prayer explicitly recalls the covenant to give the peoples' land to Abram and his offspring.
  Deuteronomy 1:7-8 — Explicit reuse: The instruction to inherit the patriarchs' promised land names the great river Euphrates, corresponding to the boundary and inheritance promise here.
  Psalms 104:8-11 — Explicit reuse: The Greek-numbered psalm remembers God's covenant with Abraham and His promise of Canaan as an inherited portion.
  Galatians 3:16-18 — Theological interpretation: Paul discusses the promises to Abraham and his seed and inheritance through promise, giving a later Christ-focused interpretation rather than restating only the geographical boundary.

Genesis 15:19
the Kenites, and the Kenizzites, and the Kadmonites,
  Genesis 15:18 — Narrative context: The preceding covenant statement introduces the land and its inhabitants, supplying the grammatical and promissory setting for these named peoples.
  1 Samuel 15:6 — Same named people: Saul later addresses the Kenite in relation to Israel's exodus, providing another biblical occurrence of one people named here without identifying the other listed groups.

Genesis 15:20
and the Hittites, and the Perizzites, and the Rephaim,
  Exodus 3:8 — Same named peoples: God's later land-deliverance promise again names Hittites and Perizzites among its inhabitants, corresponding to two groups in this verse.
  Nehemiah 9:8 — Explicit reuse: The prayer recalls the covenant's land grant and lists corresponding Hittite and Perizzite groups among its inhabitants, with its supplied transliterations retained.

Genesis 15:21
and the Amorites, and the Canaanites, and the Hivites, and the Girgashites, and the Jebusites.”
  Exodus 3:8 — Promise repetition: The later deliverance promise lists Amorites, Canaanites, Hivites, Girgashites and Jebusites among the peoples of the promised land.
  Deuteronomy 7:1 — Same named peoples: Deuteronomy's list of seven nations in the inheritance land includes all five groups named in this verse.
  Nehemiah 9:8 — Explicit reuse: Nehemiah's prayer recalls the land covenant and corresponding peoples, including Amorites, Canaanites, Jebusites and Girgashites under its supplied spellings.

Genesis 16:1
Now Sara, Abram’s wife, bore him no children; and she had an Egyptian slave woman whose name was Hagar.
  Genesis 11:30 — Narrative continuity: The earlier statement of Sara's barrenness supplies the continuing circumstance behind the introduction of her slave woman Hagar.
  Galatians 4:22-25 — Explicit reuse and allegory: Paul recalls Abraham's son by a slave woman and explicitly identifies Hagar in his covenant allegory; the allegory is later interpretation, not Genesis's own stated explanation.

Genesis 16:2
And Sara said to Abram, “Look, the Lord has kept me from bearing children. So go in to my slave woman, that you may have children through her.” And Abram listened to the voice of Sara.
  Genesis 30:3-4 — Narrative parallel: Rachel later gives Bilhah to Jacob so that she may have children through her, paralleling Sara's proposed use of her slave woman to obtain offspring.
  Genesis 16:15 — Narrative outcome: Hagar's later bearing a son to Abram is the outcome of the arrangement Sara proposes here.
  Galatians 4:22-23 — Theological interpretation: Paul contrasts the slave woman's son born according to flesh with the free woman's son through promise, interpreting the two births in relation to God's promise.

Genesis 16:3
And Sara, Abram’s wife, took Hagar the Egyptian, her slave woman, after Abram had lived ten years in the land of Canaan, and gave her to Abram her husband to be his wife.
  Genesis 12:4-5 — Chronological context: Abram's earlier departure at seventy-five and arrival in Canaan provide the background for the stated ten years of residence; no precise conception date is inferred.
  Genesis 25:12 — Genealogical correspondence: Ishmael's genealogy identifies Hagar as the Egyptian slave woman of Sarah who bore Abraham's son, preserving the household identity introduced here.
  Galatians 4:22-25 — Explicit reuse and allegory: Paul recalls the slave woman's son and identifies Hagar within a later allegorical comparison of covenants, using the household distinction narrated here.

Genesis 16:4
And he went in to Hagar, and she conceived; and she saw that she was with child, and her mistress was dishonoured in her sight.
  Genesis 16:5 — Narrative consequence: Sara's complaint repeats that Hagar's pregnancy led to her mistress being dishonoured in Hagar's sight, developing the conflict reported here.
  Genesis 21:9-10 — Narrative parallel: The later conflict over Hagar's son again produces tension between Sarah and the slave woman's household, while its immediate circumstances differ from this pregnancy dispute.

Genesis 16:5
And Sara said to Abram, “I am wronged by you. I gave my slave woman into your bosom; but when I saw that she was with child, I was dishonoured in her sight. May God judge between me and you.”
  Genesis 16:4 — Narrative context: The preceding report of conception and dishonour explains the grievance Sara now voices to Abram.
  Genesis 16:6 — Narrative answer: Abram responds to Sara's grievance by placing Hagar in her hands, after which Sara's affliction drives Hagar to flee.

Genesis 16:6
And Abram said to Sara, “Look, your slave woman is in your hands; do with her as you please.” And Sara afflicted her, and she fled from her face.
  Genesis 16:9 — Narrative reversal: The angel's command to return to her mistress directly answers Hagar's flight from Sara described here.
  Genesis 21:14-19 — Narrative parallel: Hagar later again leaves Abraham's household and encounters divine help in a water-related wilderness crisis; the later departure is distinguished from this flight.

Genesis 16:7
And an angel of the Lord found her by the spring of water in the wilderness, by the spring on the way to Shur.
  Genesis 21:17-19 — Narrative parallel: An angel again addresses Hagar in the wilderness and God's intervention provides water, paralleling the divine encounter by a spring here.
  Genesis 16:14 — Narrative continuation: The later well notice names and locates the water site associated with Hagar's encounter.

Genesis 16:8
And the angel of the Lord said to her, “Hagar, Sara’s slave woman, where have you come from, and where are you going?” And she said, “I am fleeing from the face of Sara my mistress.”
  Genesis 16:6 — Narrative context: Hagar's statement that she is fleeing from her mistress directly recalls the affliction and departure narrated earlier.
  Genesis 21:17 — Narrative parallel: In the later wilderness episode an angel again addresses Hagar by name and speaks to her distress, preserving continuity of the person and divine care.

Genesis 16:9
And the angel of the Lord said to her, “Return to your mistress, and humble yourself under her hands.”
  Genesis 16:6 — Narrative reversal: The command to return under her mistress's hands reverses the direction of Hagar's flight from Sara's affliction.
  Galatians 4:24-25 — Theological interpretation: Paul's later allegory explicitly associates Hagar with slavery, building on the household condition retained in the return command rather than prescribing it anew here.

Genesis 16:10
And the angel of the Lord said to her, “I will greatly multiply your seed, and it shall be too numerous to count.”
  Genesis 17:20 — Promise repetition: God's later answer concerning Ishmael repeats blessing and abundant multiplication, giving further form to the offspring promise made to Hagar.
  Genesis 25:13-16 — Narrative outcome: Ishmael's later genealogy names twelve rulers according to their nations, supplying a concrete descendant outcome of Hagar's multiplication promise.
  Genesis 21:18 — Promise repetition: The later assurance that Hagar's child will become a great nation renews the promise of abundant offspring.

Genesis 16:11
And the angel of the Lord said to her, “Behold, you are with child, and shall bear a son, and shall call his name Ishmael, for the Lord has heard your affliction.
  Genesis 16:15 — Narrative fulfilment: Abram's naming the son Ishmael fulfils the name specified before Hagar gives birth.
  Genesis 21:17 — Literary parallel: God's later hearing of the child's voice repeats the hearing-and-distress motif attached to Ishmael's announced name here.
  Genesis 25:12 — Genealogical correspondence: The later genealogy explicitly identifies Ishmael as Abraham's son born through Hagar, confirming the child announced in this encounter.

Genesis 16:12
He shall be a wild man; his hands shall be against all, and the hands of all against him; and he shall live opposite all his brothers.”
  Genesis 25:18 — Narrative fulfilment: Ishmael's later settlement is expressly described as opposite all his brothers, repeating the location-relative-to-family statement here.
  Genesis 21:20-21 — Narrative development: The later account places Ishmael in the wilderness as an archer; it develops his life outside the household without equating wilderness residence with every conflict phrase here.

Genesis 16:13
And Hagar called the name of the Lord who spoke to her, “Thou art God who has looked upon me”; for she said, “For I also saw before me Him who appeared to me.”
  Genesis 32:31 — Naming parallel: Jacob later names a place in response to seeing God and surviving, paralleling Hagar's naming response to the divine appearance without equating their encounters.
  Genesis 21:19 — Thematic connection: God's opening Hagar's eyes to see a well in the later wilderness crisis continues the motif of divine attention and seeing associated with her encounter.

Genesis 16:14
Therefore she called the well, “The Well Where I Saw Before Me.” Behold, it is between Kadesh and Bered.
  Genesis 16:7 — Narrative context: The earlier spring encounter supplies the event behind Hagar's subsequent naming and locating of the well.
  Genesis 24:62 — Related place-name imagery: Isaac later travels near the Well of the Vision, a related sight-associated well designation in the Abraham narrative; the supplied labels are retained without a new geographical identification.

Genesis 16:15
And Hagar bore Abram a son; and Abram called the name of his son, whom Hagar bore to him, Ishmael.
  Genesis 16:11 — Narrative fulfilment: The son's birth and naming as Ishmael carry out the earlier angelic announcement to Hagar.
  Genesis 25:12 — Genealogical correspondence: The later Ishmael genealogy repeats the same father, mother and birth relationship stated here.
  Galatians 4:22-23 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Paul recalls Abraham's son by the slave woman and contrasts that birth with the promised birth through the free woman.

Genesis 16:16
Now Abram was eighty-six years old when Hagar bore Ishmael to Abram.
  Genesis 17:24-25 — Chronological correspondence: Abraham's later age of ninety-nine and Ishmael's thirteen years correspond to Abraham's eighty-six years at Ishmael's birth, checking only the supplied chronology.

Genesis 17:1
And when Abram was ninety-nine years old, the Lord appeared to Abram and said to him, “I am your God; be pleasing before Me, and be blameless.
  Genesis 16:16 — Chronological continuation: The account advances from Abram's eighty-six years at Ishmael's birth to ninety-nine when this covenant appearance occurs.
  Genesis 6:9 — Thematic connection: Noah is described as blameless and pleasing God, corresponding to the conduct now required of Abram; the two men and narrative settings remain distinct.
  Genesis 35:11 — Promise-formula parallel: God later tells Jacob I am your God and promises multiplying nations and kings, reusing the divine self-identification and patriarchal promise form.

Genesis 17:2
And I will set My covenant between Me and you, and will multiply you exceedingly.”
  Genesis 15:18 — Covenant continuation: The earlier covenant with Abram concerning the land supplies the preceding covenant scene now developed with a multiplication promise.
  Genesis 22:17 — Promise repetition: The later oath again promises greatly multiplied offspring, continuing the multiplication assurance of this covenant speech.

Genesis 17:3
And Abram fell upon his face; and God spoke to him, saying,
  Genesis 17:17 — Literary parallel: Abraham again falls on his face later in this same conversation, when he responds to the announced birth in his and Sarah's old age.
  Ezekiel 1:28 — Shared response imagery: Ezekiel falls upon his face before hearing the divine voice, a comparable bodily response to revelation without identifying his vision with Abram's appearance.

Genesis 17:4
“As for Me, behold, My covenant is with you, and you shall be a father of a multitude of nations.
  Romans 4:17 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Paul quotes the following name explanation about Abraham's fatherhood of many nations, continuing the multinational promise stated here within his account of Abraham's faith.
  Galatians 3:7-9 — Theological interpretation: Paul describes people of faith as Abraham's children and recalls blessing for the nations, giving a later faith-focused interpretation of the patriarch's multinational fatherhood.
  Genesis 12:2-3 — Promise development: The earlier great-nation promise and blessing for all earthly tribes are expanded here into Abraham's fatherhood of many nations.

Genesis 17:5
And your name shall no longer be called Abram, but your name shall be Abraham; for I have made you a father of many nations.
  Romans 4:17 — Direct quotation: Paul quotes I have made you father of many nations, the explanation attached to Abram's change of name here.
  Nehemiah 9:7 — Explicit reuse: The prayer recalls God's choosing Abram and giving him the name Abraam, preserving its supplied transliteration of the renamed patriarch.
  Genesis 35:10 — Name-change parallel: God later changes Jacob's name to Israel through a similar no-longer-called formula, a parallel act involving a different patriarch.

Genesis 17:6
And I will increase you exceedingly, exceedingly, and will make nations of you; and kings shall come from you.
  Genesis 35:11 — Promise repetition: God's later promise to Jacob likewise joins multiplication, nations and kings coming from him, continuing the patriarchal promise language.
  Sirach 44:19 — Explicit reuse: Sirach calls Abraham a great father of a multitude of nations, recalling the broad descendant promise given here.
  Genesis 17:16 — Literary parallel: The later blessing attached to Sarah's promised child also speaks of nations and kings, repeating this covenant speech's royal and multinational theme.

Genesis 17:7
And I will establish My covenant between Me and you and your seed after you throughout their generations, as an everlasting covenant, to be your God and the God of your seed after you.
  Psalms 104:8-10 — Explicit reuse: The Greek-numbered psalm remembers God's everlasting covenant with Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, corresponding to the covenant across generations promised here.
  Exodus 6:4-7 — Explicit reuse: God recalls the patriarchal covenant, remembers Israel's slavery and promises to be their God, continuing the covenant relationship with Abraham's descendants.
  Galatians 3:16-18 — Theological interpretation: Paul interprets the promises to Abraham and his seed in relation to Christ and inheritance by promise; that is later interpretation of the covenant language.

Genesis 17:8
And I will give to you, and to your seed after you, the land where you live as a stranger, all the land of Canaan, as an everlasting possession; and I will be their God.”
  Nehemiah 9:8 — Explicit reuse: The prayer recalls God's covenant to give the peoples' land to Abraham and his offspring and His fulfilment of that word.
  Deuteronomy 1:8 — Explicit reuse: Israel is commanded to inherit the land God swore to the patriarchs and their offspring, corresponding to Canaan's grant here.
  Hebrews 11:9 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews describes Abraham's residence as a foreigner in the promised land with fellow heirs, preserving the tension between sojourning and promised possession.
  Psalms 104:11 — Explicit reuse: The psalm quotes God's gift of Canaan as an inherited portion, corresponding to the named land and inheritance promise here.

Genesis 17:9
And God said to Abraham, “And you shall keep My covenant, you and your seed after you throughout their generations.
  Genesis 17:23 — Narrative fulfilment: Abraham's same-day circumcision of the household is his narrated response to the covenant-keeping command and its subsequent specification.
  Genesis 18:19 — Narrative development: God later describes Abraham commanding his children and household to keep the Lord's ways, corresponding to the intergenerational responsibility stated here.
  Sirach 44:20 — Explicit reuse: Sirach recalls Abraham's keeping God's law and entering the covenant, interpreting the covenant obedience commanded here.

Genesis 17:10
And this is the covenant that you shall keep, between Me and you, and your seed after you throughout their generations: every male among you shall be circumcised.
  Acts 7:8 — Explicit reuse: Stephen explicitly recalls God giving Abraham the covenant of circumcision, corresponding to the covenant requirement instituted here.
  Romans 4:11 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Paul identifies Abraham's circumcision as a sign and a seal of prior faith-righteousness, interpreting the circumcision requirement in the larger promise sequence.
  Sirach 44:20 — Explicit reuse: Sirach describes Abraham establishing a covenant in his flesh, corresponding to the embodied covenant requirement in this passage.

Genesis 17:11
And you shall be circumcised in the flesh of your foreskin; and it shall be a sign of the covenant between Me and you.
  Romans 4:11 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Paul explicitly calls circumcision a sign and interprets it as a seal of Abraham's already-reckoned faith-righteousness.
  Colossians 2:11-12 — Theological contrast: Colossians describes a circumcision not made by hands in connection with Christ and baptism, a later spiritual comparison distinguished from the physical sign prescribed here.

Genesis 17:12
And every male child among you, eight days old, shall be circumcised throughout your generations, the slave born in your house, and the slave bought with silver from any foreigner who is not of your seed.
  Leviticus 12:3 — Explicit reuse: The later law likewise specifies circumcision of the foreskin on the eighth day, repeating the timing in the covenant command.
  Genesis 21:4 — Narrative fulfilment: Abraham circumcises Isaac on the eighth day as God commanded, applying the timing instituted in this passage.
  Luke 1:59 — Practice continuation: John's circumcision on the eighth day is a later narrative instance of the day specified in the covenant command.
  Luke 2:21 — Practice continuation: Jesus' circumcision after eight days provides another later narrative instance of the covenant's prescribed timing.

Genesis 17:13
The slave born in your house, and the slave bought with silver, shall surely be circumcised; and My covenant shall be upon your flesh as an everlasting covenant.
  Exodus 12:44 — Practice continuation: The Passover law likewise requires circumcision of a household slave or one bought with silver, preserving the household scope while adding its own festival setting.
  Genesis 17:27 — Narrative fulfilment: The circumcision of all household men, born there or bought from foreign nations, carries out the two household categories explicitly required here.
  Sirach 44:20 — Explicit reuse: Sirach recalls Abraham's covenant in his flesh, corresponding to the embodied and enduring covenant described in the command.

Genesis 17:14
And the uncircumcised male who is not circumcised in the flesh of his foreskin on the eighth day, that soul shall be destroyed from his kindred; for he has broken My covenant.”
  Exodus 12:48 — Thematic connection: The Passover law excludes an uncircumcised person from its meal and requires circumcision for participation, another covenant-boundary concern without equating its sanction with Genesis's wording.
  Exodus 4:24-26 — Narrative comparison: The dangerous lodging-place encounter is followed by Zipporah's circumcision of her son and the threat's withdrawal, linking circumcision with danger while retaining the supplied episode's ambiguous pronouns.

Genesis 17:15
And God said to Abraham, “Sara your wife shall no longer be called Sara, but Sarah shall be her name.
  Genesis 21:1-3 — Narrative continuation: The birth account subsequently uses Sarah's new name when recording God's visitation and Isaac's birth, preserving the renamed wife identified here.
  Genesis 35:10 — Name-change parallel: God's later renaming of Jacob uses a comparable replacement-name formula; the particular Sara-to-Sarah distinction remains unchanged.

Genesis 17:16
And I will bless her, and will give you a child by her; and I will bless him, and he shall become nations, and kings of nations shall come from him.”
  Genesis 21:1-3 — Narrative fulfilment: Sarah's conception and bearing of Isaac are expressly described as occurring according to God's word, fulfilling the promised child through her.
  Hebrews 11:11 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews recalls barren and aged Sarah receiving power to conceive because she regarded the Promiser as faithful.
  Romans 4:19-21 — Theological interpretation: Paul discusses Abraham's aged body, Sarah's womb and trust in God's ability to fulfil the promise, interpreting the birth assurance given here.

Genesis 17:17
And Abraham fell upon his face and laughed, and said in his mind, “Shall a child be born to a man a hundred years old? And shall Sarah, who is ninety years old, bear a child?”
  Genesis 18:11-14 — Narrative parallel: Sarah later also laughs at a promised birth in the couple's old age, and the divine response emphasizes that no word is impossible with God.
  Genesis 21:5-7 — Narrative fulfilment: The later birth account records Abraham at a hundred and Sarah's joyful laughter, providing the outcome of the age-and-birth question raised here.
  Romans 4:19-21 — Theological interpretation: Paul presents Abraham's response to the aged couple's birth promise as faith in God's ability, a later interpretation that does not replace Genesis's report of laughter.

Genesis 17:18
And Abraham said to God, “Let this Ishmael live before Thee.”
  Genesis 17:20 — Narrative answer: God's response explicitly says He has heard Abraham concerning Ishmael and promises him blessing, multiplication and national descendants.
  Genesis 21:11-13 — Narrative parallel: Abraham later again shows concern for his son, and God assures him that the slave woman's son will become a nation because he is Abraham's offspring.

Genesis 17:19
And God said to Abraham, “Yes; behold, Sarah your wife shall bear you a son, and you shall call his name Isaac. And I will establish My covenant with him as an everlasting covenant, and with his seed after him.
  Genesis 21:2-3 — Narrative fulfilment: Sarah bears the promised son, and Abraham gives him the specified name Isaac, fulfilling both the birth and naming announcement.
  Romans 9:7-9 — Theological interpretation: Paul interprets the promise through Isaac and Sarah's future son in distinguishing children of promise from descent alone.
  Galatians 4:28 — Theological interpretation: Paul calls his readers children of promise like Isaac, using the child's promised origin in his later covenant argument.

Genesis 17:20
And concerning Ishmael, behold, I have heard you. Behold, I have blessed him, and I will increase him and multiply him exceedingly; he shall beget twelve nations, and I will make him a great nation.
  Genesis 16:10 — Promise continuity: The blessing and multiplication for Ishmael renew the earlier promise of uncountable offspring made to Hagar.
  Genesis 25:12-16 — Narrative fulfilment: Ishmael's genealogy names twelve rulers according to their nations, corresponding to the twelve nations promised here.
  1 Chronicles 1:29-31 — Genealogical correspondence: Chronicles records Ishmael's sons under its supplied name forms, corresponding to the descendant group whose twelvefold scope is promised here.
  Genesis 21:13 — Promise repetition: God later again assures Abraham that Ishmael will become a great nation because he is Abraham's offspring.

Genesis 17:21
But I will establish My covenant with Isaac, whom Sarah shall bear to you at this season next year.”
  Genesis 18:10 — Promise repetition: The later announcement again associates Sarah's son with the return of the appointed season, repeating the time-specific Isaac promise.
  Genesis 21:2 — Narrative fulfilment: Sarah's birth is expressly dated to the season God had spoken of, fulfilling this appointed-time promise.
  Romans 9:9 — Explicit reuse: Paul quotes the appointed-time assurance that Sarah will have a son, recalling the time and mother specified in the promise.

Genesis 17:22
And He finished speaking with him; and God went up from Abraham.
  Genesis 35:13 — Literary parallel: God's going up after speaking with Jacob repeats the departure formula that closes His conversation with Abraham here.
  Genesis 18:33 — Literary parallel: The later conversation with Abraham likewise closes with the Lord departing after He finishes speaking, marking the end of a divine encounter.

Genesis 17:23
And Abraham took Ishmael his son, and all the slaves born in his house, and all the slaves bought with silver, every male among the men of Abraham’s house; and he circumcised their foreskins on that very day, as God had spoken to him.
  Genesis 17:10-13 — Narrative fulfilment: Abraham's circumcision of Ishmael and every household male, born there or bought, carries out the categories specified in the covenant command.
  Acts 7:8 — Explicit reuse: Stephen recalls the giving of the circumcision covenant, whose first household enactment is narrated here before the later circumcision of Isaac.

Genesis 17:24
Now Abraham was ninety-nine years old when he circumcised the flesh of his foreskin.
  Genesis 17:1 — Chronological correspondence: Abraham's stated ninety-nine years at circumcision matches the age given at the beginning of the covenant appearance.
  Romans 4:9-11 — Theological interpretation: Paul places Abraham's circumcision after his faith was reckoned righteous; this dated circumcision record belongs to the later stage of that sequence.

Genesis 17:25
And Ishmael his son was thirteen years old when he was circumcised in the flesh of his foreskin.
  Genesis 16:16 — Chronological correspondence: Ishmael's thirteen years at circumcision correspond to Abraham's eighty-six years at his birth when read with Abraham's current ninety-nine years in the preceding verse.

Genesis 17:26
On that very day Abraham and Ishmael his son were circumcised.
  Genesis 17:23 — Narrative summary: The father-and-son same-day notice summarizes their inclusion in the household circumcision Abraham performs in obedience to God's command.

Genesis 17:27
And all the men of his house, both the slaves born in his house and the slaves bought with silver from foreign nations, he circumcised.
  Genesis 17:12-13 — Narrative fulfilment: The final household notice repeats both the home-born and purchased categories whose circumcision was expressly required in the covenant instructions.
  Exodus 12:44 — Practice continuation: The later Passover law again includes household slaves and those bought with silver in circumcision, corresponding to the inclusive household categories enacted here.

Genesis 18:1
And God appeared to him beside the oak of Mamre, as he sat at the door of his tent at noon.
  Genesis 13:18 — Narrative continuation: The appearance beside Mamre's oak takes place where Abram earlier pitched his tent and built an altar, continuing the established setting.
  Genesis 17:1 — Narrative parallel: Both scenes begin with God's appearing to Abraham before speaking further about the promise and his household.

Genesis 18:2
And he lifted up his eyes and looked, and behold, three men stood over him. And when he saw them, he ran from the door of his tent to meet them and bowed to the earth,
  Genesis 19:1 — Narrative parallel: Abraham's rising to meet the visitors and bowing is mirrored by Lot's reception of the two angels at Sodom.
  Hebrews 13:2 — Thematic allusion: The exhortation recalls people entertaining angels through hospitality, corresponding to this reception of extraordinary visitors without naming Abraham explicitly.

Genesis 18:3
and said, “Lord, if indeed I have found grace before you, do not pass by your servant.
  Genesis 19:2 — Hospitality parallel: Both hosts address their visitors respectfully as servants and ask them to turn aside rather than continue on their journey.
  Genesis 18:5 — Narrative development: Abraham's request that the visitors not pass by is followed by his concrete offer of bread and their acceptance.

Genesis 18:4
Let water now be brought, and let them wash your feet; and refresh yourselves under the tree.
  Genesis 19:2 — Hospitality parallel: Lot's invitation likewise offers lodging and footwashing, repeating the practical care for travelers offered by Abraham.
  Genesis 24:32 — Shared practice: The later household reception provides water for the visitor's feet and his companions, corresponding to Abraham's offered footwashing.
  John 13:4-5 — Thematic connection: Jesus' washing His disciples' feet gives a later example of footwashing as service; the occasions and participants remain distinct.

Genesis 18:5
And I will bring bread, and you shall eat; and afterward you shall go on your way, since you have turned aside to your servant.” And they said, “Do as you have said.”
  Genesis 19:3 — Hospitality parallel: Lot later brings the visitors into his house and feeds them, corresponding to Abraham's accepted offer of food before their departure.
  Hebrews 13:2 — Thematic allusion: The command not to forget hospitality illuminates Abraham's providing food for the visitors; Hebrews does not explicitly name this meal.

Genesis 18:6
And Abraham hurried to the tent, to Sarah, and said to her, “Hurry, knead three measures of fine flour, and make cakes baked in the embers.”
  Genesis 19:3 — Hospitality parallel: Sarah's preparation of cakes and Lot's baking of unleavened bread are concrete acts of feeding the visitors in the paired hospitality scenes.
  Genesis 18:8 — Narrative outcome: The hurried preparations are followed by Abraham's setting the prepared meal before the visitors and standing beside them.

Genesis 18:7
And Abraham ran to the cattle, and took a tender and good calf, and gave it to the servant; and he hurried to prepare it.
  Genesis 18:8 — Narrative outcome: The calf entrusted to the servant for preparation is subsequently placed before the visitors as part of their meal.
  Luke 15:23-24 — Shared banquet imagery: The parable's prepared calf marks a welcoming feast, comparable to Abraham's generous meal while remaining a distinct family-reunion story.

Genesis 18:8
And he took curds, and milk, and the calf he had prepared, and set them before them; and they ate. And he stood beside them under the tree.
  Genesis 19:3 — Hospitality parallel: The visitors eat Abraham's prepared meal, just as the angels later eat the feast prepared in Lot's house.
  Hebrews 13:2 — Thematic allusion: The meal illustrates hospitality to extraordinary guests of the kind recalled in Hebrews, without asserting that Hebrews identifies its unnamed hosts here.

Genesis 18:9
And he said to him, “Where is Sarah your wife?” And he answered and said, “There, in the tent.”
  Genesis 17:15-19 — Narrative continuation: The question identifies Sarah as Abraham's wife, the woman whom the earlier promise expressly names as the mother of Isaac.
  Genesis 18:10 — Narrative development: The question about Sarah's location leads directly to the promised son that she hears announced from the tent door.

Genesis 18:10
And he said, “I will surely return to you at this season, when the time comes round; and Sarah your wife shall have a son.” And Sarah heard at the door of the tent, being behind him.
  Genesis 17:21 — Promise reiterated: The promise that Sarah will bear a son at the returning season repeats the earlier appointed-season promise concerning Isaac.
  Romans 9:9 — Direct quotation: Romans expressly quotes the promised return at this time and Sarah's bearing a son as the word of promise.
  Genesis 21:1-3 — Narrative fulfilment: Sarah's later conception and birth of Isaac at the spoken season are narrated as the fulfilment of God's promise.

Genesis 18:11
Now Abraham and Sarah were old, advanced in days; and the way of women had ceased with Sarah.
  Romans 4:19 — Explicit reuse: Paul recalls Abraham's aged body and Sarah's deadened womb, corresponding to the age and ceased childbearing described here.
  Hebrews 11:11 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews recalls Sarah receiving power concerning seed when past the proper age, corresponding to the human obstacle named in Genesis.
  Genesis 17:17 — Narrative parallel: Abraham's earlier inward question likewise names his and Sarah's advanced ages when considering the promised birth.

Genesis 18:12
And Sarah laughed within herself, saying, “It has not happened to me until now, and my lord is old.”
  Genesis 17:17 — Literary parallel: Sarah's inward laughter and appeal to old age parallel Abraham's earlier laughter and question about their ages.
  Genesis 21:6 — Narrative reversal: Sarah's later declaration that the Lord made laughter for her follows the promised child's birth, transforming the laughter motif.
  1 Peter 3:6 — Explicit reuse: Peter recalls Sarah calling Abraham lord, corresponding to her designation of her husband as my lord in this inward speech.

Genesis 18:13
And the Lord said to Abraham, “Why did Sarah laugh within herself, saying, ‘Shall I indeed bear a child, though I have grown old?’
  Genesis 18:12 — Narrative response: The Lord's question addresses the laughter and old-age reasoning that Sarah has just expressed within herself.
  Psalms 138:1-3 — Thematic connection: The psalm describes God's knowing a person's reasonings from afar, corresponding to His response to Sarah's inward laughter.

Genesis 18:14
Is any word impossible with God? At this season I will return to you, when the time comes round; and Sarah shall have a son.”
  Luke 1:36-37 — Shared promise language: Gabriel's assurance that no word from God will be powerless accompanies an unexpected pregnancy, corresponding to the assurance about Sarah.
  Romans 4:20-21 — Theological interpretation: Paul describes confidence that God can perform what He promises, corresponding to the question about any word being impossible with God.
  Genesis 21:1-2 — Narrative fulfilment: The later birth at the appointed season demonstrates the fulfilment of the renewed promise to Sarah.

Genesis 18:15
And Sarah denied it, saying, “I did not laugh”; for she was afraid. And He said, “No, but you did laugh.”
  Genesis 18:12-13 — Narrative contrast: Sarah's fearful denial is set against the preceding account of her inward laughter and the Lord's question about it.
  Genesis 21:6 — Narrative contrast: The laughter she now denies is later openly acknowledged as the Lord's gift after Isaac's birth.

Genesis 18:16
And the men rose from there and looked down toward Sodom and Gomorrah; and Abraham went with them to escort them on their way.
  Genesis 18:22 — Narrative continuation: The visitors' orientation toward Sodom is followed by their actual departure toward the city while Abraham remains before the Lord.
  Genesis 19:1 — Narrative continuation: The visitors' journey toward Sodom leads to the two angels' arrival there at evening.

Genesis 18:17
And the Lord said, “Shall I hide from Abraham My servant what I am doing?
  Amos 3:7 — Thematic connection: Amos connects the Lord's deeds with His revealing discipline to prophetic servants, corresponding to the decision to disclose the impending action to Abraham.
  Genesis 20:7 — Character correspondence: The later passage explicitly calls Abraham a prophet and describes his intercession, illuminating his role as recipient of divine disclosure here.

Genesis 18:18
Abraham shall surely become a great and numerous nation, and all the nations of the earth shall be blessed in him.
  Genesis 12:2-3 — Promise reiterated: The declaration repeats the earlier promises of a great nation and blessing reaching the earth's tribes through Abraham.
  Galatians 3:8 — Direct quotation and interpretation: Paul cites the blessing of all nations in Abraham and interprets it in relation to God's justification of the nations by faith.
  Genesis 17:4-6 — Promise parallel: The earlier covenant announcement describes Abraham as father of many nations, corresponding to the numerous-nation promise repeated here.

Genesis 18:19
For I knew that he would command his sons and his household after him; and they shall keep the ways of the Lord, to do righteousness and judgment, that the Lord may bring upon Abraham all that He has spoken to him.”
  Deuteronomy 6:6-7 — Thematic connection: The later command to teach God's words to one's sons corresponds to Abraham's responsibility to direct his household in the Lord's ways.
  Psalms 77:5-7 — Thematic connection: The psalm describes fathers teaching succeeding generations to hope in God and seek His commands, corresponding to Abraham's household instruction.
  Genesis 17:7-9 — Covenant continuity: The covenant with Abraham and his seed includes its keeping through generations, corresponding to the household's continuing righteousness and judgment.

Genesis 18:20
And the Lord said, “The cry of Sodom and Gomorrah has increased, and their sins are exceedingly great.
  Genesis 13:13 — Narrative development: The earlier notice that Sodom's inhabitants are exceedingly wicked develops here into a great cry and impending divine action.
  Genesis 19:13 — Narrative fulfilment: The angels later explain that the city's cry has risen before the Lord and that they were sent to destroy it.
  Ezekiel 16:49-50 — Explicit reuse: Ezekiel recalls Sodom's arrogance, luxury, neglect of the needy and lawlessness, providing a later account of the city's guilt and removal.

Genesis 18:21
So I will go down and see whether they are being brought to an end according to their cry which comes to Me; and if not, that I may know.”
  Genesis 11:5 — Narrative parallel: Both narratives describe the Lord coming down to see human circumstances before the account proceeds to divine action.
  Exodus 3:7-8 — Thematic comparison: The Lord's hearing a cry and coming down also appears in the Exodus deliverance announcement, though the threatened and rescued parties differ.
  Genesis 19:13 — Narrative continuation: The angels' statement that the cry has risen and the Lord sent them gives the subsequent explanation of the announced descent and action.

Genesis 18:22
And the men turned from there and came to Sodom; but Abraham stood before the Lord.
  Genesis 19:1 — Narrative continuation: The departure of the men toward Sodom is followed by the arrival of two angels in the city.
  Genesis 18:23 — Narrative continuation: Abraham's remaining before the Lord introduces his drawing near to question the treatment of righteous and ungodly people.

Genesis 18:23
And Abraham drew near and said, “Wilt Thou destroy the righteous with the ungodly, and shall the righteous be as the ungodly?
  Genesis 18:25 — Argument developed: Abraham's question about destroying righteous and ungodly together is developed into an appeal to the justice of the Judge of all the earth.
  2 Peter 2:7-9 — Theological interpretation: Peter distinguishes righteous Lot's rescue from the punishment of the unrighteous, addressing the distinction raised in Abraham's question.
  Exodus 23:7 — Thematic comparison: The later command not to kill the innocent and righteous shares Abraham's concern to distinguish their treatment from the ungodly.

Genesis 18:24
If there are fifty righteous in the city, wilt Thou destroy them? Wilt Thou not spare the whole place for the sake of the fifty righteous, if they are there?
  Genesis 18:26 — Question and answer: The Lord's response directly grants the proposed preservation of the place if fifty righteous people are found there.
  Jeremiah 5:1 — Thematic comparison: Jeremiah links finding one person doing justice in Jerusalem with mercy for the city, a related city-preservation appeal with a different number and setting.

Genesis 18:25
By no means shalt Thou do such a thing, to kill the righteous with the ungodly, so that the righteous shall be as the ungodly. By no means! Thou who judgest all the earth, wilt Thou not do justice?”
  Psalms 9:8-9 — Thematic connection: The psalm describes God's judgment of the inhabited world in righteousness and uprightness, corresponding to Abraham's appeal to universal divine justice.
  Deuteronomy 32:4 — Thematic connection: The song declares God's ways just and free of unrighteousness, corresponding to the appeal that the earth's Judge must do justice.
  Romans 3:5-6 — Theological parallel: Paul rejects the suggestion that God is unjust in bringing wrath because God judges the world, sharing Abraham's appeal to righteous divine judgment.

Genesis 18:26
And the Lord said, “If I find in Sodom fifty righteous within the city, I will spare the whole place for their sake.”
  Genesis 18:24 — Question and answer: The assurance of sparing the place for fifty answers Abraham's exact proposal concerning fifty righteous inhabitants.
  Jeremiah 5:1 — Thematic comparison: Both passages connect the presence of righteous conduct in a city with mercy for it, while retaining their different cities and numerical conditions.

Genesis 18:27
And Abraham answered and said, “Now I have begun to speak to the Lord, though I am earth and ashes.
  Genesis 2:7 — Shared human imagery: Abraham's description of himself as earth and ashes recalls humanity's formation from the earth and emphasizes his creaturely humility.
  Job 42:6 — Shared wording: Job also describes himself as earth and ashes, providing another expression of human abasement in speech before God.

Genesis 18:28
If the fifty righteous lack five, wilt Thou destroy the whole city for the five?” And He said, “I will surely not destroy it if I find forty-five there.”
  Genesis 18:24-26 — Intercession developed: The request lowers the earlier condition from fifty to forty-five while retaining the appeal that righteous inhabitants should preserve the place.

Genesis 18:29
And he spoke to Him yet again and said, “What if forty are found there?” And He said, “I will surely not destroy it for the sake of the forty.”
  Genesis 18:28 — Intercession developed: Abraham's request concerning forty continues the preceding exchange about forty-five and receives the same assurance against destruction for their sake.

Genesis 18:30
And he said, “I pray Thee, Lord, allow me to speak: what if thirty are found there?” And He said, “I will surely not destroy it if I find thirty there.”
  Genesis 18:29 — Intercession developed: The request concerning thirty follows the preceding forty-person request, continuing the same appeal and promise of preservation.

Genesis 18:31
And he said, “Since I may speak to the Lord, what if twenty are found there?” And He said, “I will surely not destroy it for the sake of the twenty.”
  Genesis 18:30 — Intercession developed: Abraham asks leave to continue from thirty to twenty, and the Lord again answers in terms of sparing for those righteous people.

Genesis 18:32
And he said, “I pray Thee, Lord, allow me to speak once more: what if ten are found there?” And He said, “I will surely not destroy it for the sake of the ten.”
  Genesis 18:26 — Intercession developed: The final request for ten stands at the end of the declining numerical requests that began with the promise concerning fifty.
  Jeremiah 5:1 — Thematic comparison: The final ten-person appeal can be compared with Jeremiah's later city-mercy condition concerning one just person, without equating the two settings.

Genesis 18:33
And the Lord departed when He had finished speaking to Abraham; and Abraham returned to his place.
  Genesis 19:27 — Narrative continuation: Abraham later returns to the place where he had stood before the Lord, recalling the setting of the completed intercession.
  Genesis 18:22 — Narrative frame: The Lord's departure closes the encounter that continued after Abraham remained standing before Him as the other visitors left.

Genesis 19:1
And the two angels came to Sodom at evening; and Lot sat beside the gate of Sodom. And when Lot saw them, he rose up to meet them, and bowed his face to the earth,
  Genesis 18:22 — Narrative continuation: The two angels' arrival at Sodom follows the departure toward Sodom described at the end of Abraham's meal scene.
  Genesis 13:12 — Narrative development: Lot's residence at Sodom's gate develops the earlier notice that he settled in the surrounding country and pitched his tent in Sodom.
  Hebrews 13:2 — Thematic allusion: The passage about hospitality to angels corresponds to Lot's reception of visitors explicitly called angels here, though Hebrews leaves its hosts unnamed.

Genesis 19:2
and said, “Behold, my lords, turn aside into your servant’s house, and lodge there, and wash your feet; and you shall rise early and go on your way.” And they said, “No; but we will lodge in the square.”
  Genesis 18:4 — Hospitality parallel: Lot's offer of lodging and footwashing parallels Abraham's earlier provision of water and refreshment for the visitors.
  Judges 19:15-21 — Narrative parallel: The Gibeah account likewise moves a traveler from the city square into a host's house, where footwashing and a meal are provided.
