  Genesis 24:32 — Shared practice: The later household reception supplies water to wash the traveler's and companions' feet, corresponding to Lot's offer to his guests.

Genesis 19:3
And he urged them greatly; and they turned aside to him, and entered into his house. And he made them a feast with drink, and baked unleavened bread for them; and they ate.
  Genesis 18:6-8 — Hospitality parallel: Lot's feast and baked bread correspond to the prepared bread and animal meal offered by Abraham to the visitors.
  Judges 19:21 — Narrative parallel: Both hosts bring guests into a house and provide a meal before the hostile city's men surround the dwelling.
  Hebrews 13:2 — Thematic allusion: The meal for visitors who are angels illustrates the hospitality to angels recalled in Hebrews without identifying its unnamed examples explicitly.

Genesis 19:4
Before they lay down, the men of the city, the Sodomites, surrounded the house, from young to old, all the people together.
  Judges 19:22 — Narrative parallel: Both accounts describe men of the city surrounding a host's house and demanding access to the male visitor or visitors.
  Genesis 19:9 — Narrative escalation: The gathering around the house develops into pressure upon Lot and an attempt to break the door.

Genesis 19:5
And they called Lot out, and said to him, “Where are the men who came to you tonight? Bring them out to us, that we may lie with them.”
  Judges 19:22-24 — Narrative parallel: The hostile demand for the male guests and the host's response have a close narrative counterpart in the Gibeah account, with distinct participants and outcome.
  Jude 1:7 — Later interpretation: Jude explicitly recalls Sodom and nearby cities in connection with sexual immorality and other flesh; the comparison does not resolve every phrase's interpretation.
  2 Peter 2:7-8 — Explicit reuse: Peter describes Lot's distress at the inhabitants' lawless conduct, corresponding to the threatening behavior narrated around his house.

Genesis 19:6
And Lot went out to them at the entrance, and shut the door behind him.
  Judges 19:23 — Narrative parallel: The host in Gibeah also goes outside to address the men around his house, paralleling Lot's intervention at the entrance.
  Genesis 19:10 — Narrative reversal: Lot's going out and shutting the door is reversed when the visitors draw him back inside and close it again.

Genesis 19:7
And he said to them, “By no means, brothers; do not act wickedly.
  Judges 19:23 — Shared narrative wording: Both hosts address the crowd as brothers and implore them not to act wickedly toward the guest in the house.
  Genesis 19:9 — Narrative response: The crowd's accusation that Lot is judging them follows his appeal not to act wickedly and leads to a greater threat against him.

Genesis 19:8
I have two daughters who have not known a man; I will bring them out to you, and do with them as you please. Only do nothing unjust to these men, for they have come under the shelter of my roof beams.”
  Judges 19:24 — Narrative parallel: The Gibeah host likewise offers women to protect a male guest; the comparison records the reported offers without treating either as a divine command or endorsement.
  Genesis 19:15 — Narrative continuation: Lot's two daughters later belong to the household urged to escape, preserving their identity without claiming the earlier offer was carried out.

Genesis 19:9
And they said, “Stand back there. You came alone to live as a stranger; will you also pass judgment? So now we will afflict you more than them.” And they pressed hard upon the man, Lot, and drew near to break the door.
  Genesis 19:10-11 — Narrative reversal: The crowd's attempt to force the door is checked by the visitors' rescue of Lot and the crowd's blinding.
  Judges 19:22-25 — Narrative comparison: Both hostile crowds persist around the house despite the host's appeal, while the Genesis account's angelic intervention differs from the Gibeah outcome.

Genesis 19:10
But the men stretched out their hands, and drew Lot to them into the house, and shut the door of the house.
  Genesis 19:6 — Narrative reversal: The visitors bring Lot back through the door after he had gone outside to address the crowd and shut it behind him.
  Genesis 19:16 — Narrative parallel: The visitors' taking Lot into safety anticipates the angels' later taking the household by the hand to lead them out of danger.

Genesis 19:11
And they struck the men who were at the door of the house with blindness, from small to great; and they became helpless in seeking the door.
  2 Kings 6:18-20 — Narrative parallel: Elisha's opponents are also struck with blindness, interrupting their hostile purpose; their later guidance and restored sight remain a distinct outcome.
  Genesis 19:9 — Narrative reversal: The men who had pressed toward breaking the door become helpless in trying to find that same door after being struck blind.

Genesis 19:12
And the men said to Lot, “Do you have anyone here, sons-in-law, or sons, or daughters? Or if you have anyone else in the city, bring them out of this place.
  Genesis 19:14 — Narrative development: The invitation to bring relatives out is followed by Lot's warning to the sons-in-law to leave the threatened city.
  Genesis 6:18 — Deliverance parallel: The call to bring Lot's household out of danger can be compared with the household explicitly included in Noah's ark promise; the two deliverances remain distinct.

Genesis 19:13
For we will destroy this place, because their cry has risen before the Lord; and the Lord has sent us to blot it out.”
  Genesis 18:20-21 — Narrative fulfilment: The angels connect their destructive mission with the cry that had prompted the Lord's announced action against Sodom and Gomorrah.
  Genesis 19:24-25 — Narrative fulfilment: The later rain of fire and overthrow of the cities carries out the destruction the angels announce here.
  2 Peter 2:6-9 — Explicit reuse: Peter recalls Sodom and Gomorrah's overthrow and Lot's rescue as an example of God's differing treatment of ungodly and godly people.

Genesis 19:14
And Lot went out and spoke to his sons-in-law, who had taken his daughters, and said, “Arise, and depart from this place; for the Lord will blot out the city.” But he seemed to his sons-in-law like someone joking.
  Genesis 19:12 — Narrative fulfilment: Lot's attempt to warn the sons-in-law carries out the instruction to bring any relatives out of the city.
  Luke 17:28-29 — Later interpretation: Jesus recalls ordinary life continuing in Lot's days until his departure and the fire, comparable to the warning that the sons-in-law do not take seriously.

Genesis 19:15
And at dawn, the angels urged Lot, saying, “Arise, take your wife and your two daughters whom you have, and depart, so that you do not perish with the lawless deeds of the city.”
  Genesis 19:17 — Narrative development: The urgent instruction to depart is expanded into specific escape directions after the household is brought outside.
  Luke 17:29 — Explicit reuse: Jesus places the destructive fire on the day Lot left Sodom, corresponding to the separation of Lot's escape from the city's fate.

Genesis 19:16
And they were troubled; and the angels took hold of his hand, and the hand of his wife, and the hands of his two daughters, because the Lord spared him.
  2 Peter 2:7-9 — Explicit reuse: Peter expressly describes the Lord's rescue of righteous Lot, corresponding to the angels' taking the household out because the Lord spared him.
  Genesis 19:19 — Narrative response: Lot's subsequent acknowledgment of mercy and preservation of his life responds to the saving action narrated here.

Genesis 19:17
And when they had brought them outside, they said, “Save your own life. Do not look behind you or stand anywhere in the surrounding country. Escape to the mountain, so that you are not swept away with them.”
  Luke 17:31-32 — Explicit reuse: Jesus' warning not to turn back is followed by the command to remember Lot's wife, recalling the escape instruction and its later breach.
  Genesis 19:26 — Narrative consequence: The wife's looking behind her and becoming salt is narrated after the explicit instruction not to look back during the escape.
  Genesis 19:30 — Narrative continuation: Lot eventually settles in the mountain after the initial command to escape there, without requiring an added judgment on his intervening request.

Genesis 19:18
And Lot said to them, “I beg you, lord,
  Genesis 19:19-20 — Speech continued: The following explanation of mercy, danger and the nearby city supplies the content of Lot's appeal introduced here.

Genesis 19:19
for your servant has found mercy before you, and you have shown me your great righteousness by preserving my life. Yet I cannot escape to the mountain, for calamities may overtake me, and I may die.
  Genesis 19:16 — Narrative response: Lot's statement that his life was preserved by mercy recalls the Lord's sparing him and the angels' taking hold of the household.
  Genesis 19:21-22 — Petition and response: The assurance about the nearby city and its entry answers Lot's stated fear that he cannot reach the mountain.

Genesis 19:20
Behold, this city is near enough for me to flee to, and it is small. There I shall be saved; is it not small? And my soul shall live.”
  Genesis 19:21-22 — Petition and response: The request to escape into the small nearby city receives permission, and the account names that city Zoar.
  Genesis 14:2 — Same place: The earlier list of kings identifies Balak as Zoar, supplying a prior narrative reference to the city into which Lot now asks to flee.

Genesis 19:21
And he said to him, “Behold, I have regarded your face in this matter also, that I will not overthrow the city of which you have spoken.
  Genesis 19:20 — Petition and response: The promise not to overthrow the city directly answers Lot's request that his life be preserved by fleeing there.
  Genesis 18:26 — Thematic parallel: Both exchanges include a promise to spare a place in response to an appeal involving preservation of people, with different places and stated conditions.

Genesis 19:22
So hurry and be saved there; for I can do nothing until you enter it.” Therefore he called the name of that city Zoar.
  Genesis 19:23 — Narrative fulfilment: Lot's entry into Zoar carries out the instruction to hurry there before the destructive action can proceed.
  Luke 17:29 — Explicit reuse: Jesus recalls Lot's departure before the fire falls, corresponding to the ordered relation between his safe escape and the city's destruction.

Genesis 19:23
The sun rose upon the earth, and Lot entered into Zoar.
  Genesis 19:22 — Narrative fulfilment: The reported arrival in Zoar fulfils the immediate command to reach the city in safety.
  Luke 17:29 — Explicit reuse: Jesus' recollection places the heavenly fire on the day of Lot's departure, matching the sequence of escape followed by destruction.

Genesis 19:24
And the Lord rained upon Sodom and Gomorrah sulphur and fire from the Lord out of the heaven;
  Luke 17:28-29 — Explicit reuse: Jesus explicitly recalls fire and sulfur raining from heaven upon Sodom after Lot's departure.
  2 Peter 2:6 — Explicit reuse: Peter names Sodom and Gomorrah as cities reduced to ashes and condemned to overthrow, recalling their fiery destruction.
  Jude 1:7 — Explicit reuse: Jude recalls Sodom, Gomorrah and neighboring cities undergoing the penalty of fire, using their fate as a warning example.
  Deuteronomy 29:22 — Explicit reuse: Deuteronomy compares a devastated land of sulfur and salt with the overthrow of Sodom, Gomorrah and neighboring cities.

Genesis 19:25
and He overthrew those cities, and all the surrounding country, and all that lived in the cities, and all that sprang from the earth.
  Genesis 13:10 — Narrative reversal: The earlier description of the fertile region explicitly anticipates God's overthrow of Sodom and Gomorrah, now narrated as completed.
  Isaiah 13:19 — Explicit reuse: Isaiah compares Babylon's future fate with God's overthrow of Sodom and Gomorrah, using this destruction as the precedent.
  Genesis 19:28 — Narrative outcome: Abraham's later view of flame rising from the region presents the visible aftermath of the overthrow described here.

Genesis 19:26
And his wife looked behind her, and became a pillar of salt.
  Luke 17:32 — Explicit reuse: Jesus directly commands His hearers to remember Lot's wife in the context of a warning against turning back.
  Wisdom of Solomon 10:7 — Explicit reuse: Wisdom's standing pillar of salt as a memorial to a disbelieving soul recalls the salt-pillar outcome narrated for Lot's wife.
  Genesis 19:17 — Narrative consequence: Her looking behind her follows the earlier prohibition against looking back while escaping the threatened region.

Genesis 19:27
And Abraham rose early in the morning, and went to the place where he had stood before the Lord.
  Genesis 18:22 — Narrative continuation: The location where Abraham now stands is identified by his earlier standing before the Lord during the intercession scene.
  Genesis 18:33 — Narrative continuation: Abraham's morning return to the encounter's place follows the conclusion of the Lord's conversation and Abraham's return to his own place.

Genesis 19:28
And he looked toward Sodom and Gomorrah, and toward the land of the surrounding country; and he saw, and behold, a flame rose from the earth as the vapour of a furnace.
  Genesis 19:24-25 — Narrative aftermath: The rising flame Abraham sees is the narrated aftermath of the heavenly fire and overthrow of the cities.
  Deuteronomy 29:22 — Explicit reuse: Deuteronomy's image of sulfur, salt and a burned land explicitly recalls Sodom's overthrow, corresponding to the devastated region Abraham observes.

Genesis 19:29
And when the Lord blotted out all the cities of the surrounding country, God remembered Abraham, and sent Lot out from the midst of the overthrow, when the Lord overthrew the cities where Lot lived.
  Genesis 18:23-32 — Narrative outcome: The account's statement that God remembered Abraham follows Abraham's sustained appeal concerning the righteous within the threatened city.
  Genesis 8:1 — Narrative parallel: God's remembering Noah while ending the flood provides another preservation scene using divine remembrance amid widespread destruction.
  2 Peter 2:7-9 — Explicit reuse: Peter's account of the Lord rescuing righteous Lot corresponds to Lot's removal from the overthrow, without replacing Genesis's mention of Abraham.
  Wisdom of Solomon 10:6 — Explicit reuse: Wisdom recalls a righteous man being rescued as he fled fire descending on the Five Cities, corresponding to Lot's deliverance from the region's destruction.

Genesis 19:30
And Lot went up out of Zoar, and lived in the mountain, and his two daughters with him; for he was afraid to live in Zoar. And he lived in the cave, he and his two daughters with him.
  Genesis 19:17 — Narrative continuation: Lot eventually lives in the mountain named in the original escape direction, after first receiving permission to enter Zoar.
  Genesis 19:19-22 — Narrative development: The move away from Zoar follows Lot's earlier appeal to reach it instead of the mountain, preserving both stages of the account.

Genesis 19:31
And the older said to the younger, “Our father is old, and there is no man upon the earth to come in to us as is the way of all the earth.
  Genesis 19:32 — Speech developed: The older daughter's statement about their father's age and absence of a man leads into her proposal for preserving offspring.
  Genesis 19:36-38 — Narrative outcome: The conceptions and births that follow answer the daughters' stated concern about producing offspring, without validating their claim about all the earth.

Genesis 19:32
Come, let us give our father wine to drink, and lie with him, and raise up seed from our father.”
  Genesis 19:33 — Narrative fulfilment: The older daughter's proposal involving wine and intercourse is carried out in the next verse's account of the first night.
  Leviticus 18:6-7 — Ethical contrast: The later prohibition on uncovering close relatives' nakedness supplies a legal contrast to the reported proposal; Genesis itself does not state that law in this speech.

Genesis 19:33
And they gave their father wine to drink that night; and the older went in and lay with her father that night. And he did not know when she lay down, nor when she arose.
  Genesis 19:34 — Narrative continuation: The older daughter later recounts the first night's intercourse and proposes the corresponding action for her younger sister.
  Genesis 9:21 — Thematic comparison: Noah's intoxication and exposure provide another account of wine-related vulnerability; the participants' acts and their awareness are not equated.

Genesis 19:34
And the next day the older daughter said to the younger, “Behold, I lay with our father yesterday. Let us give him wine to drink tonight also; and go in and lie with him, and let us raise up seed from our father.”
  Genesis 19:32 — Literary parallel: The renewed proposal repeats the wine-and-offspring plan, now directing the younger daughter to carry it out.
  Genesis 19:35 — Narrative fulfilment: The younger daughter's intercourse on the second night carries out the proposal made in this verse.

Genesis 19:35
And they gave their father wine to drink that night also; and the younger went in and lay with her father. And he did not know when she lay down, nor when she arose.
  Genesis 19:33 — Literary parallel: The second-night account repeats the first-night pattern of wine, intercourse and the father's lack of awareness when the daughter lies down or rises.
  Genesis 19:36 — Narrative outcome: The following statement that both daughters conceived gives the reproductive outcome of the two nights' reported actions.

Genesis 19:36
And both the daughters of Lot conceived by their father.
  Genesis 19:33-35 — Narrative outcome: The pregnancies follow the two reported acts of intercourse, with the older and younger daughters distinguished in the preceding account.
  Genesis 19:37-38 — Narrative continuation: The two subsequent birth notices identify the sons resulting from the two conceptions described here.

Genesis 19:37
And the older daughter bore a son, and called his name Moab, saying, “From my father.” He is the father of the Moabites to this day.
  Deuteronomy 2:9 — Genealogical correspondence: Deuteronomy addresses the Moabites while identifying the protected family as sons of Lot, corresponding to Moab's descent from Lot narrated here.
  Genesis 19:38 — Genealogical parallel: The following birth notice supplies the other daughter’s son and the corresponding neighboring people, keeping the two brothers' lines distinct.

Genesis 19:38
And the younger daughter also bore a son, and called his name Amman, “Son of my kindred.” He is the father of the Ammanites to this day.
  Deuteronomy 2:19 — Genealogical correspondence: Deuteronomy explicitly identifies the sons of Ammon with the sons of Lot, corresponding to the ancestor named Amman in this supplied Genesis reading.
  Genesis 19:37 — Genealogical parallel: The paired birth notices relate Moabites and Ammanites to Lot through distinct daughters and sons rather than merging their identities.

Genesis 20:1
And Abraham journeyed from there to the land toward the south, and lived between Kadesh and Shur, and lived as a stranger in Gerar.
  Genesis 10:19 — Same place: Gerar appears among the places marking the Canaanite border, providing an earlier geographical reference for Abraham's residence without fixing a modern route.
  Genesis 26:1-3 — Narrative parallel: Isaac later resides in Gerar during a famine and receives a command to sojourn; the settings correspond without identifying the rulers named Abimelech as one individual.
  Genesis 21:34 — Narrative continuation: The later notice that Abraham sojourned in the Philistine land continues the period of foreign residence introduced by his movement to Gerar.

Genesis 20:2
And Abraham said concerning Sarah his wife, “She is my sister”; for he feared to say, “She is my wife,” lest the men of the city should slay him for her sake. And Abimelech king of Gerar sent and took Sarah.
  Genesis 12:11-15 — Narrative parallel: The earlier Egypt episode likewise joins fear for the patriarch's life, a sister claim and a ruler's taking his wife, while preserving the different ruler and setting.
  Genesis 26:7-9 — Narrative parallel: Isaac later calls Rebekah his sister because he fears death for her sake, repeating the wife-and-sister motif with a different couple.
  Genesis 20:11-13 — Explicit explanation: Abraham's later answer explains the fear and the standing agreement with Sarah that underlie the sister declaration reported here.

Genesis 20:3
And God came to Abimelech in his sleep by night, and said, “Behold, you are to die because of the woman whom you have taken; for she is married to a husband.”
  Genesis 12:17 — Narrative parallel: God's earlier affliction of Pharaoh's household because of the patriarch's wife corresponds to intervention protecting Sarah in another ruler's household.
  Genesis 31:24 — Narrative parallel: God later warns Laban in a night dream not to speak evil to Jacob, providing another warning that restrains harm involving a patriarch.
  Exodus 20:13 — Thematic connection: The later command forbids adultery, the marital wrong threatened in this episode; the supplied Greek-based Exodus numbers that command 20:13.
  Genesis 20:7 — Narrative development: The later instruction specifies returning the married woman and links compliance with life, developing the initial death warning given in the dream.

Genesis 20:4
But Abimelech had not touched her; and he said, “Lord, wilt Thou destroy a nation that is ignorant and righteous?
  Genesis 18:23-25 — Literary parallel: Abimelech's appeal not to destroy an ignorant and righteous nation recalls Abraham's earlier plea to distinguish the righteous from the ungodly; both are appeals within their scenes.
  Genesis 20:6 — Narrative confirmation: God acknowledges Abimelech's pure heart and states that He prevented contact with Sarah, responding to the king's plea that he had not touched her.

Genesis 20:5
Did he not say to me, ‘She is my sister’? And she herself said to me, ‘He is my brother.’ I have done this with a pure heart and with righteousness of hands.”
  Genesis 20:13 — Narrative explanation: Abraham later describes the standing arrangement that Sarah should call him her brother, explaining the woman's declaration reported by Abimelech.
  Genesis 20:6 — Narrative response: God's acknowledgement of the king's pure heart directly answers his claim to have acted with a pure heart, while also crediting divine restraint.

Genesis 20:6
And God said to him in his sleep, “I also knew that you did this with a pure heart; and I spared you, that you would not sin against Me. Therefore I did not allow you to touch her.
  Genesis 31:7 — Thematic connection: Jacob later attributes Laban's inability to harm him to God's restraint, paralleling God's prevention of wrongdoing in the Sarah episode without equating the circumstances.
  Psalms 104:14-15 — Thematic recollection: The psalm recalls God preventing harm to the patriarchs and rebuking kings, a poetic counterpart to the divine restraint protecting Sarah and Abraham.
  Genesis 20:4-5 — Narrative response: The divine answer acknowledges the pure-heart plea and absence of contact reported by Abimelech rather than leaving his claim without a response.

Genesis 20:7
Now therefore return the wife to the man, for he is a prophet; and he shall pray for you, and you shall live. But if you do not return her, know that you shall die, you and all that is yours.”
  Genesis 20:17 — Narrative fulfilment: Abraham later prays and God heals the household, carrying out the intercession God directs Abimelech to seek here.
  Psalms 104:14-15 — Thematic recollection: The psalm's protection of God's anointed ones and prophets corresponds to God's identifying Abraham as a prophet while protecting his marriage.
  James 5:16 — Thematic connection: James joins prayer for others with healing and the strength of a righteous petition, corresponding to the intercession and recovery promised in this scene.
  Job 42:8-10 — Narrative parallel: God also directs Job to pray for others under judgment and accepts that intercession; the target concerns forgiveness rather than repeating this episode's household illness.

Genesis 20:8
And Abimelech rose early in the morning, and called all his servants, and spoke all these words in their ears; and all the men were very afraid.
  Genesis 20:3-7 — Narrative response: The king's morning report and his servants' fear follow the night's warning, explanation and command to return Sarah.
  Genesis 31:29 — Narrative parallel: Laban later recounts a divine warning received the previous night, providing another scene in which a foreign household leader reports God's restraint involving a patriarch.

Genesis 20:9
And Abimelech called Abraham, and said to him, “What is this that you have done to us? Have we sinned against you, that you have brought upon me and upon my kingdom a great sin? You have done to me a deed which no man would do.”
  Genesis 12:18-19 — Narrative parallel: Pharaoh likewise confronts Abram over concealing his wife's marital status and the danger caused by the sister claim.
  Genesis 26:9-10 — Narrative parallel: Isaac's later Gerar ruler similarly rebukes the sister claim because it could bring an unwitting offence on the community.

Genesis 20:10
And Abimelech said to Abraham, “What did you see, that you did this thing?”
  Genesis 20:11-13 — Narrative response: The following answer explains Abraham's feared danger, reported kinship and earlier arrangement with Sarah, directly answering the king's question about his motive.
  Genesis 12:18-19 — Literary parallel: The earlier ruler's repeated questions about Abram's sister claim anticipate this renewed demand for an explanation after Sarah is taken.

Genesis 20:11
And Abraham said, “For I said, ‘Surely there is no reverence for God in this place; and they will slay me for my wife’s sake.’
  Genesis 12:12 — Explicit narrative repetition: Abraham's fear of being killed for his wife's sake repeats the danger he anticipated when approaching Egypt.
  Genesis 26:7 — Narrative parallel: Isaac later fears that the local men will kill him because of Rebekah's beauty, repeating the stated motive with a different couple.
  Genesis 20:2 — Explicit explanation: The narrator had already attributed the sister claim to fear of being killed; Abraham now states that motive in his own response.

Genesis 20:12
And indeed she is my sister by my father, but not by my mother; and she became my wife.
  Genesis 11:29 — Kinship context: The earlier marriage notice names Sara as Abram's wife, the marital relationship that remains explicit alongside his reported paternal-sister relationship here.
  Genesis 20:2 — Narrative clarification: Abraham's kinship explanation clarifies the sister claim without changing the narrator's identification of Sarah as his wife.
  Leviticus 18:9 — Kinship comparison: The later law specifically names a sister from the father or mother, corresponding to the paternal kinship Abraham reports; it is not presented as a command spoken to him in this scene.

Genesis 20:13
And when God brought me out from my father’s house, I said to her, ‘You shall show me this righteousness: in every place where we come, say of me, “He is my brother.”’”
  Genesis 12:1 — Explicit recollection: Abraham's account of God bringing him from his father's house recalls the departure command that began his land journey.
  Genesis 12:13 — Narrative explanation: The Egypt episode records Abram asking Sara to use the sister claim, illustrating the travel arrangement he now describes as longstanding.

Genesis 20:14
And Abimelech took a thousand didrachms, and sheep, and calves, and male slaves, and female slaves, and gave them to Abraham; and he restored to him Sarah his wife.
  Genesis 12:16 — Narrative parallel: Both ruler episodes include gifts of livestock and servants to the patriarch, but Abimelech's gift accompanies Sarah's restoration rather than her initial taking.
  Genesis 20:7 — Narrative fulfilment: The king's restoration of Sarah carries out God's command to return the wife to her husband.
  Genesis 20:16 — Explicit narrative repetition: The king's speech to Sarah repeats the thousand-didrachm gift and states its purpose, developing the financial gift recorded by the narrator.

Genesis 20:15
And Abimelech said to Abraham, “Behold, my land is before you; live wherever you please.”
  Genesis 12:19-20 — Narrative contrast: Abimelech permits Abraham to live where he wishes, contrasting with Pharaoh's instruction that Abram leave Egypt.
  Genesis 21:34 — Narrative continuation: Abraham's later extended sojourn in the Philistine land follows the permission to remain offered by the ruler.

Genesis 20:16
And to Sarah he said, “Behold, I have given your brother a thousand didrachms. These shall be for the honour of your face, and for all the women with you; and in all things speak the truth.”
  Genesis 20:14 — Explicit narrative repetition: The thousand didrachms mentioned to Sarah are the monetary gift already recorded by the narrator; their stated purpose is retained in this source's own wording.
  Genesis 20:5 — Narrative response: The king's direction to speak truth follows his earlier report that Sarah called Abraham her brother, connecting the speech with the declarations that caused the dispute.

Genesis 20:17
And Abraham prayed to God; and God healed Abimelech, and his wife, and his slave women, and they bore children.
  Genesis 20:7 — Narrative fulfilment: Abraham's prayer and the household's healing fulfil the intercession and life promised when God instructed the king to restore Sarah.
  James 5:16 — Thematic connection: The instruction to pray for others so that they may be healed corresponds to Abraham's intercession followed by the household's recovery.
  Job 42:8-10 — Narrative parallel: Job likewise intercedes for people whom God has rebuked, and God responds favorably; that passage concerns forgiveness and restoration rather than the same illness.

Genesis 20:18
For the Lord had wholly shut every womb from outside in the house of Abimelech, because of Sarah, Abraham’s wife.
  Genesis 12:17 — Narrative parallel: The earlier Pharaoh episode also attributes household affliction to the patriarch's wife, while leaving its illness less specifically described than the closed wombs here.
  Genesis 20:17 — Narrative explanation: The closed-womb notice explains the condition reversed when God heals the women and they bear children in the preceding statement.

Genesis 21:1
And the Lord visited Sarah as He had said, and the Lord did to Sarah as He had spoken.
  Genesis 17:19-21 — Promise fulfilment: God's action toward Sarah fulfils the stated promise that she would bear Isaac at the appointed season.
  Genesis 18:10-14 — Promise fulfilment: The earlier announcement of a son at the returning season is fulfilled when the Lord does for Sarah what He had spoken.
  Hebrews 11:11 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Hebrews recalls Sarah receiving power to conceive and trusting the faithful Promiser, interpreting the fulfilment of the birth promise.

Genesis 21:2
And Sarah conceived, and bore Abraham a son in his old age, at the season of which the Lord had spoken to him.
  Genesis 17:17 — Narrative fulfilment: Abraham's earlier question about a hundred-year-old father and ninety-year-old mother is answered by the birth in his old age.
  Genesis 17:21 — Promise fulfilment: The birth at the stated season corresponds to God's earlier specification that Sarah would bear Isaac at that season in the following year.
  Romans 9:9 — Explicit reuse: Paul cites the appointed-time promise that Sarah will have a son, the promise whose fulfilment this birth records.
  Hebrews 11:11 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Hebrews explicitly recalls Sarah conceiving beyond the proper age, corresponding to the old-age birth narrated here.

Genesis 21:3
And Abraham called the name of his son who was born to him, whom Sarah bore to him, Isaac.
  Genesis 17:19 — Narrative fulfilment: Abraham's naming the son Isaac carries out the divine command that Sarah's promised son receive that name.
  Genesis 25:19 — Genealogical correspondence: The later genealogy identifies Isaac as Abraham's son, continuing the parentage and named identity established at the birth.
  Genesis 22:2 — Narrative continuation: The later command names Isaac as Abraham's beloved son, carrying forward the identity of the son named here without adding an interpretation of the name.

Genesis 21:4
And Abraham circumcised Isaac on the eighth day, as God had commanded him.
  Genesis 17:12 — Command fulfilment: Circumcision on Isaac's eighth day follows the specific age prescribed for male children in the covenant command.
  Acts 7:8 — Explicit reuse: Stephen directly recalls Abraham fathering Isaac and circumcising him on the eighth day, summarizing the act narrated here.
  Leviticus 12:3 — Ritual parallel: The later birth law likewise specifies circumcision on the eighth day, preserving the same ritual timing in a different legislative setting.
  Luke 2:21 — Ritual parallel: Luke records Jesus' circumcision when eight days are completed, providing a later instance of the same timing without identifying the two births.
  Philippians 3:5 — Ritual parallel: Paul identifies himself as circumcised on the eighth day, showing the same ancestral ritual timing as a feature of his Israelite background.

Genesis 21:5
Now Abraham was a hundred years old when Isaac his son was born to him.
  Genesis 17:17 — Chronological fulfilment: The stated hundred-year age matches the age Abraham had mentioned while wondering whether he could father the promised child.
  Romans 4:19 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Paul recalls Abraham being about a hundred years old in discussing his faith and bodily condition, corresponding to the age recorded at Isaac's birth.

Genesis 21:6
And Sarah said, “The Lord has made laughter for me; for whoever hears shall rejoice with me.”
  Genesis 18:12-15 — Narrative reversal: Sarah's joyful laughter at the birth follows the earlier scene of laughter and questioning about a child in old age; the different contexts remain distinct.
  Genesis 17:17 — Shared response motif: Abraham's earlier laughter at the promise provides the preceding family laughter motif, now joined by Sarah's announced joy at its fulfilment.
  Luke 1:57-58 — Narrative parallel: Elizabeth's neighbors and relatives later rejoice with her when the Lord's mercy is displayed in a son's birth, paralleling Sarah's expectation of shared rejoicing.

Genesis 21:7
And she said, “Who shall tell Abraham that Sarah is nursing a child? For I have borne a son in my old age.”
  Hebrews 11:11 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Hebrews recalls Sarah's conception beyond the proper age, corresponding to her wonder that she nurses a son in old age.
  Genesis 18:11-14 — Promise fulfilment: The earlier account stresses Sarah's age and the promised son, providing the context for her astonishment at nursing him.
  Genesis 17:17 — Narrative fulfilment: Sarah's nursing answers the earlier question whether a ninety-year-old Sarah could bear a child, without supplying a separate new age for the weaning scene.

Genesis 21:8
And the child grew, and was weaned; and Abraham made a great feast on the day that Isaac his son was weaned.
  1 Samuel 1:22-24 — Life-stage parallel: Hannah later nurses Samuel until weaning before bringing him to the Lord, providing another narrative in which weaning marks a new stage without equating the celebrations.
  Genesis 21:9-10 — Narrative development: The account moves from Isaac's weaning feast to Sarah's observation and inheritance demand, locating the ensuing household dispute after this celebration.

Genesis 21:9
And Sarah saw the son of Hagar the Egyptian, who was born to Abraham, playing with Isaac her son.
  Galatians 4:28-29 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Paul later describes the flesh-born son's conduct as persecution of the promise-born son. Genesis's supplied wording remains playing with Isaac rather than being replaced by Paul's interpretive term.
  Genesis 16:15-16 — Genealogical context: The earlier birth account names Hagar's son Ishmael and identifies Abraham as his father, supplying the identity of the older son described here.
  Genesis 25:9 — Narrative continuation: Isaac and Ishmael later appear together as Abraham's sons at his burial, continuing the two-son family relationship without erasing their different inheritance stories.

Genesis 21:10
And she said to Abraham, “Cast out this slave woman and her son; for the son of this slave woman shall not inherit with my son Isaac.”
  Galatians 4:30 — Direct quotation and interpretation: Paul quotes the expulsion and non-inheritance statement in his discussion of slave and free children, making its later allegorical use explicit.
  Genesis 25:5-6 — Inheritance parallel: The later disposition gives Isaac Abraham's possessions while other sons receive gifts and are sent away, corresponding to the distinction of Isaac's inheritance without identifying every recipient with Ishmael.

Genesis 21:11
And the saying seemed exceedingly harsh in Abraham’s sight, concerning his son.
  Genesis 17:18 — Narrative parallel: Abraham had earlier asked that Ishmael live before God, providing a preceding expression of concern for the son whose removal now troubles him.
  Genesis 21:12-13 — Narrative response: God directly addresses Abraham's distress and joins Isaac's named line with a separate promise for the slave woman's son.

Genesis 21:12
And God said to Abraham, “Do not let the saying be harsh in your sight concerning the child and the slave woman. In all that Sarah says to you, listen to her voice; for in Isaac seed shall be called to you.
  Romans 9:7-8 — Direct quotation and interpretation: Paul quotes the naming of seed through Isaac and interprets it in terms of children of promise, explicitly distinguishing descent from the promise's particular line.
  Hebrews 11:18 — Direct quotation: Hebrews directly quotes the statement that offspring will be named through Isaac when discussing Abraham's later testing.
  Genesis 17:19-21 — Promise continuity: The earlier covenant promise likewise specifies Isaac while separately blessing Ishmael, corresponding to the distinction God states here.
  Galatians 4:28 — Theological interpretation: Paul identifies believers as children of promise like Isaac, interpreting the particular promise-line whose significance is stated in Genesis.

Genesis 21:13
And I will also make the son of this slave woman a great nation, for he is your seed.”
  Genesis 17:20 — Promise renewal: The great-nation assurance renews the earlier explicit blessing of Ishmael, including numerous descendants and twelve nations.
  Genesis 21:18 — Promise repetition: The angel's later command again promises to make the child a great nation, reaffirming the assurance given to Abraham here.
  Genesis 25:12-16 — Genealogical outcome: The later genealogy identifies Ishmael and twelve rulers according to their nations, providing a concrete descendant record following the great-nation promise.

Genesis 21:14
And Abraham rose in the morning, and took loaves and a skin of water, and gave them to Hagar, and laid them upon her shoulder, and the child also, and sent her away. And she departed, and wandered in the wilderness near the Well of the Oath.
  Genesis 16:6-7 — Narrative parallel: Hagar had earlier left Sarah's presence and encountered an angel by wilderness water; the later departure with her child is a distinct episode.
  Galatians 4:30-31 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: The later letter quotes the expulsion and develops the distinction between slave and free children; its allegory is not substituted for Genesis's narrative wording.
  Genesis 21:10-13 — Narrative fulfilment: Abraham's sending Hagar and the child away follows Sarah's demand and God's response, including the separate promise for this son.

Genesis 21:15
And the water in the skin ran out; and she cast the child beneath a fir tree.
  Genesis 21:19 — Narrative reversal: The depleted water skin is later filled at the well God enables Hagar to see, reversing the immediate crisis of having no water.
  Exodus 15:22-25 — Thematic parallel: The later wilderness journey also presents a lack of drinkable water answered by divine provision, while its bitter-water problem differs from Hagar's empty skin.

Genesis 21:16
And she went and sat opposite him, far off, about a bowshot; for she said, “I will not see the death of my child.” And she sat opposite him; and the child cried aloud and wept.
  Genesis 21:17 — Narrative response: The next statement expressly says God hears the child's voice, responding to the child crying and weeping in this supplied reading.
  Genesis 21:19 — Narrative reversal: The child whom Hagar fears will die is later given water to drink after the well is disclosed, answering the scene's threatened death.

Genesis 21:17
And God heard the voice of the child from the place where he was; and an angel of God called to Hagar out of the heaven, and said to her, “What is wrong, Hagar? Do not fear; for God has heard the voice of your child from the place where he is.
  Genesis 16:7-11 — Narrative parallel: The earlier angelic encounter addresses Hagar and connects Ishmael's naming with the Lord's hearing her affliction; the renewed encounter now concerns the child's voice.
  Exodus 3:7 — Thematic connection: God's later declaration that He has heard His afflicted people's cry parallels divine attention to the endangered child's voice without equating the situations.
  Genesis 21:16 — Narrative response: The stated hearing of the child answers the voice and weeping attributed to him in the preceding verse, rather than silently transferring that action to Hagar.

Genesis 21:18
Arise, take the child, and hold him with your hand; for I will make him a great nation.”
  Genesis 17:20 — Promise renewal: The assurance of a great nation recalls the earlier blessing naming Ishmael and his numerous descendants.
  Genesis 21:13 — Promise repetition: The promise now spoken to Hagar repeats God's assurance to Abraham that the slave woman's son will become a great nation.
  Genesis 25:12-16 — Genealogical outcome: Ishmael's later descendant list names twelve rulers by their nations, continuing the future announced for the child here.

Genesis 21:19
And God opened her eyes, and she saw a well of living water; and she went, and filled the skin with water, and gave the child to drink.
  Genesis 16:7 — Narrative parallel: An earlier encounter with Hagar likewise occurs by a wilderness spring, linking the two water-and-divine-help scenes while keeping their locations and circumstances distinct.
  Exodus 15:22-25 — Thematic parallel: Both wilderness crises receive divine help that makes water available for drinking; the target describes bitter water made sweet rather than opening Hagar's eyes to a well.
  Genesis 21:15 — Narrative reversal: Filling the water skin and giving the child a drink reverses the empty-skin crisis described before the angel's intervention.

Genesis 21:20
And God was with the child, and he grew, and lived in the wilderness, and became an archer.
  Genesis 16:11-12 — Narrative continuation: The earlier naming and future description concern Hagar's son Ishmael, whose growth and wilderness life now continue; becoming an archer is not quoted as a specific earlier prediction.
  Luke 1:80 — Life-stage parallel: John later grows and remains in the wilderness before his public appearance, providing a comparable growth-and-wilderness notice without identifying the children's vocations.

Genesis 21:21
And he lived in the wilderness of Paran; and his mother took him a wife from the land of Egypt.
  Genesis 16:1 — Family context: The earlier account identifies Hagar as Egyptian, supplying the mother's background alongside her arranging an Egyptian wife for her son here.
  Genesis 25:12 — Genealogical continuation: The later genealogy explicitly identifies Ishmael as Hagar's son by Abraham, supplying the named family identity for the child whose marriage is arranged here.

Genesis 21:22
And at that time Abimelech, and Ahuzzath his groomsman, and Phicol the chief commander of his army, spoke to Abraham, saying, “God is with you in all that you do.
  Genesis 26:26-29 — Narrative parallel: The later Isaac episode repeats the Abimelech, Ahuzzath and Phicol delegation and recognition of God's presence; the parallel does not establish that similarly named rulers across generations are one person.
  Genesis 39:2-3 — Literary parallel: Joseph's Egyptian master later recognizes that the Lord is with him and prospers his work, paralleling a foreign observer's acknowledgement of God's favor toward a patriarchal figure.

Genesis 21:23
Now therefore swear to me by God that you will not wrong me, nor my seed, nor my name; but according to the righteousness which I have shown to you, you shall deal with me, and with the land where you have lived as a stranger.”
  Genesis 26:28-29 — Covenant parallel: The later Gerar delegation likewise asks for a non-harm agreement and appeals to previous treatment, repeating the diplomatic form without equating every participant.
  Genesis 20:14-15 — Narrative background: The earlier gifts, restoration and permission to remain supply the ruler's prior treatment of Abraham to which the request for reciprocal righteousness appeals.
  Joshua 2:12 — Oath parallel: Rahab likewise requests an oath of reciprocal mercy based on mercy she has shown, providing a later example of a sworn request grounded in prior kindness.

Genesis 21:24
And Abraham said, “I will swear.”
  Genesis 21:31 — Narrative fulfilment: The later naming explanation states that both parties swore, completing Abraham's agreement to take the oath.
  Genesis 26:31 — Covenant parallel: Isaac and the later Gerar delegation likewise swear to one another and part in safety, matching the oath-making form rather than identifying the episodes.

Genesis 21:25
And Abraham reproved Abimelech concerning the wells of water which Abimelech’s servants had taken away.
  Genesis 26:19-22 — Narrative parallel: Isaac's later shepherds also face disputes over wells and move through contested water sites, repeating a practical issue involving the patriarchal household in the Gerar region.
  Genesis 21:30 — Narrative resolution: Abraham's later explanation makes the seven lambs a witness that he dug the well, addressing the ownership dispute raised here.

Genesis 21:26
And Abimelech said to him, “I did not know who had done this thing; you did not tell me, nor did I hear of it until today.”
  Genesis 21:25 — Narrative response: The king's claim not to have known answers Abraham's allegation that the king's servants took the wells; the claim is preserved as his response rather than an independently established verdict.
  Genesis 20:4-5 — Literary parallel: Abimelech earlier also answered a threatening allegation by appealing to his lack of culpable knowledge and pure intention, while the two disputes remain distinct.

Genesis 21:27
And Abraham took sheep and calves, and gave them to Abimelech; and the two made a covenant.
  Genesis 26:30-31 — Covenant parallel: The later Isaac agreement also joins hospitality and an oath of peace; Genesis's sheep-and-cattle gift here is not rewritten as a sacrifice.
  Genesis 21:32 — Explicit narrative repetition: The concluding notice again states that the parties made a covenant at the Well of the Oath, completing the agreement first recorded here.

Genesis 21:28
And Abraham set seven ewe lambs of the flock apart.
  Genesis 21:30 — Narrative explanation: Abraham's later answer states that the seven ewe lambs are a witness to his digging the well, explaining why he sets them apart.

Genesis 21:29
And Abimelech said to Abraham, “What are these seven ewe lambs which you have set apart?”
  Genesis 21:30 — Narrative response: Abraham's following answer directly explains the purpose of the seven ewe lambs about which the king asks.

Genesis 21:30
And Abraham said, “You shall take these seven ewe lambs from my hand, that they may be a witness to me that I have dug this well.”
  Genesis 21:25 — Narrative resolution: The lambs' role as witness to the well's digging answers the earlier complaint that Abimelech's servants had taken the water sites.
  Genesis 31:48-49 — Witness parallel: Jacob and Laban later designate a heap and pillar as witnesses between them, providing another agreement with a visible witness while differing from the lambs used here.

Genesis 21:31
Therefore he called the name of that place the Well of Swearing, because there they both swore.
  Genesis 26:31-33 — Naming and oath parallel: The later Isaac account likewise connects an oath with the Well of the Oath name; its supplied report that no water was found is retained without harmonizing the accounts.
  Genesis 21:32 — Same named place: The following covenant conclusion calls the site the Well of the Oath, continuing the naming explanation that uses Well of Swearing here.
  Genesis 22:19 — Same place: Abraham later returns to and resides at the Well of the Oath after the testing episode, continuing the place established in this naming account.

Genesis 21:32
And they made a covenant at the Well of the Oath. And Abimelech rose up, and Ahuzzath his groomsman, and Phicol the chief commander of his army; and they returned to the land of the Philistines.
  Genesis 26:26-31 — Narrative parallel: The later Isaac scene again has the named Gerar delegation make an oath and depart safely, matching the diplomatic sequence without proving personal identities across generations.
  Genesis 21:27 — Explicit narrative repetition: The concluding covenant notice repeats the agreement first recorded when Abraham gave sheep and cattle to Abimelech.

Genesis 21:33
And Abraham planted a field at the Well of the Oath, and there called upon the name of the Lord, the Everlasting God.
  Genesis 12:8 — Literary parallel: Abraham again calls on the Lord's name at a sojourning site, continuing the named worship practice previously associated with his altar near Bethel.
  Genesis 26:25 — Narrative parallel: Isaac later builds an altar, calls on the Lord's name and pitches his tent, continuing patriarchal worship in the Well of the Oath setting without inserting an altar into this verse.
  Psalms 89:2 — Thematic connection: The psalm addresses God as existing from age to age before creation, corresponding to Abraham's invocation of the Everlasting God.

Genesis 21:34
And Abraham lived as a stranger in the land of the Philistines many days.
  Hebrews 11:9 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews characterizes Abraham's life in the promised land as a foreign sojourn, corresponding to the extended residence as a stranger stated here.
  Genesis 20:15 — Narrative continuation: The extended stay follows the Gerar ruler's permission for Abraham to live wherever he pleased within the land.
  Genesis 26:3 — Promise and sojourning parallel: Isaac later receives a command to live as a stranger with God's blessing in the ancestral land, continuing the family sojourning pattern without conflating father and son.

Genesis 22:1
And after these things God tested Abraham, and said to him, “Abraham, Abraham.” And he said, “Behold, here am I.”
  Hebrews 11:17 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews explicitly recalls Abraham being tested and offering Isaac, interpreting the trial introduced here as an exercise of faith.
  James 2:21-22 — Theological interpretation: James recalls Abraham's offering of Isaac and describes faith working with his actions, interpreting the testing narrative without adding a completed slaughter.
  Sirach 44:20 — Explicit reuse: Sirach's ancestral praise says Abraham was found faithful under testing, corresponding to the trial announced at this narrative's opening.

Genesis 22:2
And He said, “Take your son, your beloved, whom you have loved, Isaac, and go to the high land; and offer him there for a whole burnt offering upon one of the mountains which I shall tell you.”
  Hebrews 11:17-18 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Hebrews recalls the command involving Isaac as the offering of the promise-bearer's only son and cites Isaac's promised lineage; its only-son description is retained beside Genesis's beloved wording.
  Genesis 21:12 — Promise context: The earlier assurance names Isaac as the line through which Abraham's seed will be called, explaining the promise placed under tension by this command concerning Isaac.
  Romans 8:32 — Thematic comparison: Paul's description of God not sparing His own Son supplies a later costly-son-giving comparison; it is not a claim that Isaac and Christ are the same person or that this verse is quoted.

Genesis 22:3
And Abraham rose in the morning, and saddled his female donkey, and took with him two servants and Isaac his son. And he split wood for the whole burnt offering, and arose and went, and came to the place of which God had spoken to him.
  Genesis 22:9-10 — Narrative fulfilment: The prepared wood and journey lead to the altar, wood arrangement and knife action later narrated at the commanded place.
  Hebrews 11:17 — Theological interpretation: Hebrews interprets Abraham's responsive action in the Isaac trial as faith, providing a later perspective on the preparation and departure described here.

Genesis 22:4
On the third day Abraham lifted up his eyes, and saw the place far off.
  Genesis 22:2 — Narrative development: The place sighted from a distance corresponds to the mountain destination God had promised to identify, without introducing a place-name absent from this edition.
  Genesis 22:9 — Narrative continuation: The later arrival at the place God had named completes the approach marked here by Abraham's distant sighting.

Genesis 22:5
And Abraham said to his servants, “Stay here with the female donkey; and I and the lad will go over there, and worship, and return to you.”
  Hebrews 11:19 — Theological interpretation: Hebrews attributes to Abraham confidence that God can raise the dead and describes receiving Isaac back figuratively, supplying a later perspective on the announced return.
  Genesis 22:19 — Narrative outcome: Abraham's later return to the servants answers the return announced here; the target itself does not separately name Isaac in that returning party.

Genesis 22:6
And Abraham took the wood of the whole burnt offering, and laid it upon Isaac his son; and he took the fire in his hand, and the knife. And the two went together.
  Genesis 22:7 — Narrative development: Isaac's subsequent question names the fire and wood being carried but asks about the absent sacrificial animal, developing the preparations listed here.
  Genesis 22:9 — Narrative continuation: The wood laid on Isaac is later arranged on the altar beneath him, linking his carrying role with the offering's preparation.
  John 19:17 — Thematic comparison: John describes Jesus bearing His cross toward the place of crucifixion, a thematic comparison with Isaac carrying the offering's wood. John does not name Isaac or explicitly quote this passage.

Genesis 22:7
And Isaac spoke to Abraham his father, and said, “Father.” And he said, “What is it, child?” And he said, “Behold the fire and the wood; but where is the sheep for the whole burnt offering?”
  Genesis 22:8 — Narrative response: Abraham's answer that God will provide a sheep directly responds to Isaac's question about the missing animal for the offering.
  Genesis 22:13 — Narrative outcome: The ram discovered and offered instead of Isaac resolves the narrative's question about the sacrificial animal without changing the wording of Isaac's question.

Genesis 22:8
And Abraham said, “God will provide for Himself a sheep for the whole burnt offering, child.” And the two went together,
  Genesis 22:13 — Narrative outcome: The discovery and offering of a ram instead of Isaac supplies the animal whose provision Abraham anticipates in his answer.
  Genesis 22:14 — Narrative connection: The subsequent place-name The Lord Has Seen and appearance saying follow the provision scene, connecting Abraham's assurance with the remembrance of the place without claiming a new etymology.

Genesis 22:9
and came to the place of which God had spoken to him. And Abraham built an altar there, and laid the wood upon it, and bound together the feet of Isaac his son, and laid him upon the altar, upon the wood.
  James 2:21 — Explicit reuse: James explicitly recalls Abraham offering Isaac upon the altar, corresponding to the binding and placement narrated here rather than asserting Isaac was killed.
  Hebrews 11:17 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews recalls Abraham offering Isaac when tested, interpreting the altar action as faith in the context of the received promises.
  Genesis 22:3 — Narrative fulfilment: The altar's arranged wood and the arrival at the appointed place carry forward the wood preparation and journey begun earlier.

Genesis 22:10
And Abraham stretched out his hand to take the knife, to slaughter his son.
  Genesis 22:12 — Narrative interruption: The command not to lay a hand on the lad interrupts the knife action that was about to lead to slaughter.
  James 2:21 — Explicit reuse: James refers to the offering of Isaac on the altar as Abraham's action; this comparison does not turn the attempted knife action into an accomplished killing.

Genesis 22:11
And an angel of the Lord called to him out of the heaven, and said to him, “Abraham, Abraham.” And he said, “Behold, here am I.”
  Genesis 22:1 — Literary parallel: The doubled address Abraham, Abraham and his response Behold, here am I repeat the testing scene's opening summons and response.
  Genesis 22:15 — Literary framing: The later notice explicitly calls the angelic address a second call from heaven, identifying this intervention as the earlier call in the pair.

Genesis 22:12
And he said, “Do not lay your hand upon the lad or do anything to him; for now I know that you fear God, and have not spared your beloved son for my sake.”
  Hebrews 11:17-19 — Theological interpretation: Hebrews recalls the son offered under testing and received back figuratively, interpreting the preservation of Isaac that follows the command to stop.
  Romans 8:32 — Shared wording and thematic comparison: Paul's statement that God did not spare His own Son recalls the son-not-spared language, while applying it to God's gift of Christ rather than to an actual death of Isaac.
  Genesis 22:16 — Explicit repetition: The later oath repeats that Abraham has not spared his beloved son for God's sake, restating the evaluation announced in this intervention.

Genesis 22:13
And Abraham lifted up his eyes and looked, and behold, one ram was caught by its horns in a sabek plant. And Abraham went and took the ram, and offered it for a whole burnt offering instead of Isaac his son.
  Genesis 22:8 — Narrative outcome: The ram supplied for the offering answers Abraham's earlier assurance that God would provide an animal.
  Hebrews 11:19 — Theological interpretation: Hebrews describes Abraham receiving Isaac back figuratively, providing a later interpretation of the spared son when a ram is offered in his place.
  Genesis 22:2 — Narrative transformation: The ram's being offered instead of Isaac changes the immediate execution of the original whole-burnt-offering command while retaining its offering form.

Genesis 22:14
And Abraham called the name of that place The Lord Has Seen, that they might say today, “In the mountain the Lord appeared.”
  Genesis 22:8 — Narrative connection: The naming of the place follows Abraham's earlier assurance of divine provision, connecting the remembrance with God's action without replacing the supplied name with another edition's wording.
  Genesis 22:13 — Narrative context: The ram's appearance and substitution immediately precede the place-name and appearance saying, supplying the narrated occasion for the memorial.

Genesis 22:15
And an angel of the Lord called to Abraham a second time out of the heaven,
  Genesis 22:11 — Explicit narrative repetition: The second heavenly call refers back to the angel's earlier call that stopped Abraham's action against Isaac.
  Genesis 22:16-18 — Narrative continuation: The following oath and blessing supply the content of the second angelic address introduced here.

Genesis 22:16
saying, “By Myself I have sworn, says the Lord: because you have done this thing, and have not spared your beloved son for My sake,
  Hebrews 6:13 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews explicitly recalls God swearing by Himself when making the promise to Abraham, corresponding to the self-sworn oath announced here.
  Genesis 22:12 — Explicit repetition: The oath repeats the earlier finding that Abraham has not spared his beloved son, linking the blessing with the completed test.

Genesis 22:17
surely I will bless you, and greatly multiply your seed as the stars of the heaven, and as the sand upon the shore of the sea; and your seed shall inherit the cities of their adversaries.
  Hebrews 6:14 — Direct quotation: Hebrews quotes the doubled blessing and multiplication promise, directly recalling the emphatic assurance given in this oath.
  Hebrews 11:12 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews describes Abraham's multitude of descendants through the stars and innumerable seashore sand images used in this blessing.
  Genesis 24:60 — Shared blessing formula: Rebekah's later family blessing likewise says that her seed shall inherit the cities of its adversaries, repeating the victory formula in this edition.
  Genesis 15:5 — Promise development: The earlier assurance compares Abraham's seed with the uncountable stars, the image repeated and expanded with seashore sand in the oath.
  Sirach 44:21 — Explicit reuse: Sirach recalls God's oath to multiply Abraham and raise his offspring like stars, while retaining its own dust and inheritance wording.

Genesis 22:18
And in your seed all the nations of the earth shall be blessed, because you have listened to My voice.”
  Acts 3:25-26 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Peter recalls blessing for all earth's families in Abraham's seed and interprets the Servant's blessing as turning people from wicked deeds.
  Galatians 3:8-9 — Explicit reuse of promise: Paul recalls the blessing of all nations in Abraham and interprets that promise in relation to faith, drawing on the shared Abrahamic blessing formula.
  Genesis 12:3 — Promise renewal: The oath renews the earlier assurance that all earth's tribes will be blessed in Abraham, now naming his seed and obedience.
  Genesis 26:4-5 — Promise renewal: The later promise to Isaac repeats blessing for all nations in the seed and expressly connects it with Abraham's obedience to God's voice.
  Galatians 3:16 — Theological interpretation: Paul interprets the Abrahamic promises concerning seed with reference to Christ; that is a later interpretation of the promise tradition rather than an added name in Genesis.

Genesis 22:19
And Abraham returned to his servants; and they arose and went together to the Well of the Oath. And Abraham lived at the Well of the Oath.
  Genesis 21:33-34 — Narrative return: The return to the Well of the Oath resumes Abraham's residence at the place where he had earlier called on the Lord's name and sojourned.
  Genesis 22:5 — Narrative outcome: Abraham returns to the servants whom he had told to remain while he and the lad went on to worship.

Genesis 22:20
And after these things Abraham was told, “Behold, Milcah also has borne sons to Nahor your brother:
  Genesis 11:29 — Genealogical continuation: The earlier marriage notice identifies Milcah as Nahor's wife, the family relationship now developed through the report of their sons.
  Genesis 24:15 — Genealogical continuation: The later introduction of Rebekah repeats Milcah, Nahor and their son Bethuel, continuing the family report introduced here.

Genesis 22:21
Uz the firstborn, and Buz his brother, and Kemuel the father of the Syrians,
  Genesis 22:20 — Genealogical context: The preceding heading identifies Milcah and Nahor as the parents for the list beginning with Uz, Buz and Kemuel.

Genesis 22:22
and Chesed, and Azau, and Pildash, and Jidlaph, and Bethuel.”
  Genesis 22:23 — Genealogical continuation: The following statement identifies Bethuel as Rebekah's father and closes the eight-son list to which the names here belong.
  Genesis 24:15 — Genealogical correspondence: The later Rebekah narrative identifies Bethuel as Milcah's son by Nahor, continuing the lineage of the final son named here without giving parallel records for every brother.

Genesis 22:23
And Bethuel begot Rebekah. Milcah bore these eight sons to Nahor, Abraham’s brother.
  Genesis 24:15 — Genealogical repetition: The later introduction again identifies Rebekah as Bethuel's daughter and Bethuel as son of Milcah and Nahor, repeating the principal family line.
  Genesis 24:24 — Explicit genealogical repetition: Rebekah herself later names Bethuel, Milcah and Nahor when identifying her family to Abraham's servant.

Genesis 22:24
And his concubine, whose name was Reumah, also bore Tabek, and Gaham, and Tochos, and Mocha.
  Genesis 22:20-23 — Genealogical contrast: The sons of Nahor's concubine follow the earlier list of Milcah's sons, distinguishing the two maternal branches without equating their members or adding inheritance rules.

Genesis 23:1
And the life of Sarah was a hundred and twenty-seven years.
  Genesis 23:2 — Narrative continuation: The following death notice closes Sarah's lifespan statement and gives the place and Abraham's mourning response.

Genesis 23:2
And Sarah died in the city of Arbok, which is in the hollow, that is, Hebron, in the land of Canaan; and Abraham came to lament Sarah and to mourn.
  Genesis 25:9-10 — Explicit burial recollection: Abraham's later burial record identifies the purchased cave and recalls that Sarah his wife is buried there, continuing the burial story begun by her death.
  Genesis 49:31 — Explicit burial recollection: Jacob later remembers Abraham and Sarah's burial at the family cave, looking back to the death and burial narrated in this chapter.
  Genesis 23:19 — Narrative outcome: The later burial of Sarah opposite Mamre completes the funeral purpose introduced by her death and Abraham's mourning.

Genesis 23:3
And Abraham rose up from his dead, and spoke to the sons of Heth, saying,
  Genesis 23:4 — Narrative continuation: Abraham's request for a burial possession supplies the content of his address to the sons of Heth after rising from his dead.
  Genesis 23:2 — Narrative context: Sarah's death and Abraham's lament explain the funeral circumstances in which he now approaches the local community.

Genesis 23:4
“I am a resident foreigner and a sojourner among you. So give me a possession for burial among you, that I may bury my dead away from me.”
  Hebrews 11:9-13 — Explicit thematic recollection: Hebrews describes Abraham's foreign residence and the patriarchs' confession that they were strangers and sojourners, corresponding to his self-description before the landholders.
  Psalms 38:13 — Shared wording: The psalmist calls himself a sojourner and temporary resident like his fathers, repeating the ancestral resident-foreigner language used in Abraham's request.
  Genesis 47:9 — Thematic connection: Jacob later describes his own and his fathers' lives as sojourning, continuing the ancestral understanding of life as temporary residence.

Genesis 23:5
And the sons of Heth answered Abraham, saying,
  Genesis 23:6 — Narrative continuation: The community's offer of a choicest tomb supplies the actual answer introduced by this brief speech notice.

Genesis 23:6
“No, lord; hear us. You are a king from God among us. Bury your dead in the choicest of our tombs; for none of us shall withhold his tomb from you, that you may bury your dead there.”
