  Genesis 24:51 — Narrative elaboration: The family's next statement gives practical effect to its refusal to oppose the mission by permitting Rebekah to become the master's son's wife.

Genesis 24:51
Behold, Rebekah is before you; take her and depart, and let her be the wife of your master’s son, as the Lord has spoken.”
  Genesis 24:4 — Narrative fulfilment: The permission for Rebekah to become Isaac's wife realizes Abraham's instruction to seek that marriage within the ancestral family.
  Genesis 24:58 — Narrative confirmation: Rebekah's subsequent declaration that she will go adds her own answer to the family's permission given here.
  Genesis 24:67 — Narrative outcome: Isaac's taking Rebekah as his wife completes the marriage arrangement the family authorizes in this statement.

Genesis 24:52
And when Abraham’s servant heard these words, he bowed to the earth before the Lord.
  Genesis 24:50-51 — Narrative response: The servant bows in worship after hearing the family's agreement that the marriage mission may proceed, directly responding to their words.
  Genesis 24:26-27 — Literary parallel: The servant had earlier worshipped and blessed the Lord after the well encounter, and now again worships after the marriage agreement.

Genesis 24:53
And the servant brought out articles of silver and gold, and garments, and gave them to Rebekah; and he gave gifts to her brother and her mother.
  Genesis 24:22 — Narrative development: The earlier jewelry gifts to Rebekah are followed by further silver, gold and garments for her and gifts for her family, developing the mission's giving of gifts.

Genesis 24:54
And they ate and drank, he and the men with him, and slept. And he rose in the morning, and said, “Send me away, that I may go to my master.”
  Genesis 24:33 — Narrative sequence: The meal now takes place after the mission speech and agreement, completing the eating the servant earlier deferred until he had spoken.
  Genesis 24:56 — Narrative continuation: The servant's request to be sent home is immediately developed by his appeal not to delay a journey he credits the Lord with prospering.

Genesis 24:55
And her brothers and her mother said, “Let the maiden abide with us about ten days, and afterward she shall go.”
  Genesis 24:56 — Narrative contrast: The family's request for about ten days is answered by the servant's request not to be hindered, placing the two proposed departure schedules in direct contrast.
  Genesis 24:58 — Narrative resolution: Rebekah's willingness to go supplies the personal answer sought after the family proposes delaying her departure.

Genesis 24:56
And he said to them, “Do not hinder me, since the Lord has prospered my journey; send me away, that I may go to my master.”
  Genesis 24:40 — Narrative outcome: The servant now regards the journey as prospered by the Lord, corresponding to the assurance of divine help that he reported from Abraham.
  Genesis 24:58-61 — Narrative fulfilment: Rebekah agrees to go and the household sends her away with the servant, carrying out his request for an unhindered return.

Genesis 24:57
And they said, “Let us call the maiden, and ask of her own mouth.”
  Genesis 24:58 — Narrative response: The family's consultation is answered by Rebekah's own declaration that she will travel with the man.
  Genesis 24:8 — Narrative connection: Asking Rebekah herself addresses the question of the woman's willingness, which Abraham had expressly included in the oath's conditions.

Genesis 24:58
And they called Rebekah, and said to her, “Will you go with this man?” And she said, “I will go.”
  Genesis 24:5 — Narrative answer: Rebekah's affirmative answer resolves the servant's original uncertainty about whether a woman would follow him to Isaac's land.
  Genesis 24:8 — Narrative answer: Her willingness means the mission can proceed without invoking the release condition for a woman unwilling to come.
  Genesis 24:61 — Narrative fulfilment: Rebekah's actual departure on the camels with her maidservants carries out her stated decision to go.

Genesis 24:59
And they sent away Rebekah their sister, and her possessions, and Abraham’s servant, and those who were with him.
  Genesis 24:61 — Narrative fulfilment: Rebekah and her companions mount the camels and depart, carrying out the household's sending her away with Abraham's servant.

Genesis 24:60
And they blessed Rebekah their sister, and said to her, “You are our sister; become thousands of ten thousands, and let your seed inherit the cities of their adversaries.”
  Genesis 22:17 — Shared blessing formula: The family blessing that Rebekah's seed inherit adversaries' cities repeats the city-inheritance promise already given to Abraham's seed.
  Genesis 28:3 — Family blessing parallel: Isaac later blesses Jacob with increase into assemblies of nations, continuing the multiplication blessing across the family descended through Rebekah.

Genesis 24:61
And Rebekah arose, and her maidservants, and they mounted the camels, and went with the man; and the servant took Rebekah and departed.
  Genesis 24:58 — Narrative fulfilment: Mounting the camels and following the servant realizes Rebekah's expressed willingness to make the journey.
  Genesis 24:10 — Narrative counterpart: The return with Rebekah on camels completes the servant's outward mission begun with Abraham's ten camels and gifts; no unchanged camel count is inferred.

Genesis 24:62
Now Isaac was journeying through the wilderness near the Well of the Vision; and he lived in the land toward the south.
  Genesis 25:11 — Same place: The later account again locates Isaac by the Well of the Vision, preserving the identical supplied place label without imposing an unverified equivalence with another well name.
  Genesis 24:63 — Narrative continuation: Isaac's presence in this region provides the setting for his going into the plain and seeing the approaching camels.

Genesis 24:63
And Isaac went out to meditate in the plain toward evening; and he lifted up his eyes, and saw camels coming.
  Genesis 24:61 — Narrative convergence: The camels Isaac sees are the arriving party whose departure with Rebekah was narrated earlier, bringing the two journey lines together.
  Genesis 24:65 — Narrative elaboration: The servant identifies the man walking in the plain as his master, clarifying for Rebekah the identity already given by the narrator here.

Genesis 24:64
And Rebekah lifted up her eyes, and saw Isaac, and leaped down from the camel,
  Genesis 24:63 — Narrative counterpart: Rebekah's seeing Isaac is paired with Isaac's preceding sight of the camels, describing the meeting from both sides without changing the supplied leaped-down action.
  Genesis 24:65 — Narrative continuation: Her question about the man and the servant's answer follow her sight of Isaac and descent from the camel.

Genesis 24:65
and said to the servant, “Who is that man walking in the plain to meet us?” And the servant said, “It is my master.” And she took her veil, and covered herself.
  Genesis 24:64 — Narrative context: Rebekah's earlier sight of the man supplies the immediate occasion for asking his identity and covering herself with her veil.
  Genesis 24:67 — Narrative outcome: The man identified as the servant's master is Isaac, whose taking Rebekah as his wife completes the encounter; the veil itself is not made into an unstated universal rule.

Genesis 24:66
And the servant told Isaac all the things which he had done.
  Genesis 24:2-9 — Narrative completion: The servant's report to Isaac closes the commission entrusted by Abraham and accepted under oath at the chapter's beginning.
  Genesis 24:51-61 — Narrative report: The marriage agreement, Rebekah's consent and the departure are the completed events underlying the servant's report of what he has done.

Genesis 24:67
And Isaac entered into his mother’s house, and took Rebekah, and she became his wife, and he loved her; and Isaac was comforted concerning Sarah his mother.
  Genesis 25:20 — Explicit retrospective identification: The later genealogy explicitly names Rebekah as Isaac's wife, with her Bethuel-and-Laban family identity, corresponding to the marriage concluded here.
  Genesis 23:1-2 — Narrative continuation: Sarah's earlier death and Abraham's mourning supply the loss to which Isaac's comfort concerning his mother now responds; the supplied mother’s-house wording is retained.

Genesis 25:1
And Abraham took another wife, whose name was Keturah.
  1 Chronicles 1:32 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles names Keturah and her sons by Abraham, identifying the same family while calling her Abraham's concubine rather than replacing Genesis's wife designation.
  Genesis 25:4 — Genealogical framing: The closing statement identifies the listed descendants as Keturah's sons, completing the family section opened by Abraham's marriage here.

Genesis 25:2
And she bore him Zimran, Jokshan, Medan, Midian, Ishbak, and Shuah.
  1 Chronicles 1:32 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles lists Keturah's six sons in corresponding order, retaining its supplied forms Zembran, Jexan, Madan, Midian, Sobak and Soe rather than silently standardizing Genesis's names.
  Genesis 25:4 — Genealogical framing: The subsequent list closes by naming Keturah as mother of these branches, confirming the family heading introduced by her six sons.

Genesis 25:3
And Jokshan begot Sheba, Teman, and Dedan; and the sons of Dedan were Raguel, Nabdeel, Asshurim, Letushim, and Leummim.
  1 Chronicles 1:32 — Genealogical comparison: Chronicles names Jexan's sons Saba and Daidan, corresponding to the Sheba-and-Dedan portion of Jokshan's branch; it does not reproduce Teman or Dedan's expanded descendants here.
  Genesis 25:2 — Genealogical context: The preceding list identifies Jokshan as Keturah's son by Abraham, supplying the parent-line context for this next generation.

Genesis 25:4
And the sons of Midian were Ephah, Epher, Hanoch, Abira, and Elraga. All these were the sons of Keturah.
  1 Chronicles 1:33 — Genealogical comparison: Chronicles lists five sons of Midian and closes with Keturah's family, corresponding to the sequence here while preserving Gaipha, Opher, Enoch, Abida and Eldaa as its English forms.
  Genesis 25:2 — Genealogical context: The earlier list places Midian among Abraham and Keturah's sons, identifying the branch whose children conclude this family section.

Genesis 25:5
And Abraham gave all that he had to Isaac his son.
  Genesis 24:36 — Explicit retrospective correspondence: The servant had already stated that Abraham gave Isaac all he had; this disposition repeats that account of the principal heir's property.
  Genesis 25:6 — Narrative qualification: The following verse distinguishes gifts and separate settlement for the concubines' sons from the comprehensive property grant made to Isaac.

Genesis 25:6
But to the sons of his concubines Abraham gave gifts, and while he was still alive he sent them away from Isaac his son, eastward into the land of the east.
  Genesis 21:10-14 — Narrative comparison: The earlier separation of Hagar and her son also distinguishes Isaac's inheritance from another branch of Abraham's household, though its circumstances and wording differ from this gift-and-departure notice.
  1 Chronicles 1:32 — Genealogical context: Chronicles calls Keturah Abraham's concubine and lists her sons, supplying one named family branch relevant to the concubine-sons distinction without identifying every son intended here.

Genesis 25:7
And these were the years of the days of Abraham’s life which he lived: a hundred and seventy-five years.
  Genesis 21:5 — Chronological comparison: The earlier account dates Isaac's birth when Abraham was one hundred, providing a prior life milestone alongside the final age of one hundred and seventy-five stated here.
  Genesis 25:8 — Narrative closure: The following death notice closes the life whose total years have just been stated, joining the age record with Abraham's death and addition to his people.

Genesis 25:8
And Abraham’s strength failed, and he died in a good old age, an old man and full of days; and he was added to his people.
  Genesis 15:15 — Promise fulfilment: God's earlier assurance that Abram would depart to his fathers and be buried at a good old age corresponds to the old-age death described here.
  Genesis 35:29 — Literary parallel: Isaac's later death likewise uses failing strength, old age, fullness of days and addition to family, repeating the ancestral death formula in another generation.

Genesis 25:9
And Isaac and Ishmael his sons buried him in the double cave, in the field of Ephron the son of Zohar the Hittite, which is opposite Mamre,
  Genesis 23:17-19 — Explicit geographical continuation: The cave opposite Mamre had been established as Abraham's burial property and used for Sarah, identifying the family burial place now used by Isaac and Ishmael.
  Genesis 49:29-31 — Explicit retrospective correspondence: Jacob later identifies the same cave and recalls Abraham and Sarah's burial there, confirming the family burial setting of this account.

Genesis 25:10
the field and the cave which Abraham bought from the sons of Heth. There they buried Abraham and Sarah his wife.
  Genesis 23:16-20 — Explicit retrospective correspondence: The earlier purchase account records the silver, field, cave and confirmation by the sons of Heth, supplying the transaction recalled by this burial notice.
  Genesis 49:31 — Explicit reuse: Jacob's later burial instructions expressly recall Abraham and Sarah being buried together in this ancestral cave.

Genesis 25:11
And after Abraham died, God blessed Isaac his son; and Isaac lived by the Well of the Vision.
  Genesis 24:62 — Same place and narrative continuation: Isaac had already been journeying near the Well of the Vision; his residence there after Abraham's death continues that named setting.
  Genesis 26:24 — Promise continuation: God later identifies Himself as Abraham's God and assures Isaac of blessing and numerous seed, developing the divine blessing on Isaac stated after his father's death.

Genesis 25:12
And these are the generations of Ishmael, Abraham’s son, whom Hagar the slave woman of Sarah bore to Abraham.
  Genesis 16:15 — Genealogical correspondence: The earlier birth account identifies Hagar as the mother of Abram's son Ishmael, matching the parentage in this genealogical heading.
  1 Chronicles 1:28 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles names Isaac and Ishmael as Abraham's sons, placing the Ishmael branch within the same paternal family.
  Galatians 4:22-24 — Theological interpretation: Paul recalls Abraham's son by the slave woman and explicitly interprets Hagar's story allegorically; that later interpretation is distinguished from the genealogy's parentage statement.

Genesis 25:13
And these are the names of the sons of Ishmael, by the names of their generations: Ishmael’s firstborn Nebaioth, and Kedar, Nabdeel, and Massam,
  1 Chronicles 1:29 — Genealogical comparison: Chronicles repeats Ishmael's firstborn and the opening four sons, with Nabaioth and Mabsan where Genesis has Nebaioth and Massam; the original forms are preserved.
  Genesis 28:9 — Genealogical continuation: Esau's later marriage notice names Ishmael's daughter Mahalath as Nebaioth's sister, supplying a further family reference to the firstborn listed here.
  Isaiah 60:7 — Same named groups: Isaiah later pairs Kedar's sheep and Nabaioth's rams in a worship vision, providing another biblical setting for two named groups in Ishmael's line without reconstructing their later history.

Genesis 25:14
and Mishma, Iduma, and Massa,
  1 Chronicles 1:30 — Genealogical comparison: Chronicles continues the corresponding Ishmael list with Masma, Idouma and Masse, retaining different English forms from Mishma, Iduma and Massa here.
  Genesis 25:13 — Genealogical context: The preceding heading names Ishmael and begins his sons' list, supplying the parentage for the three names continued in this verse.

Genesis 25:15
and Choddad, Teman, Jetur, Naphish, and Kedemah.
  1 Chronicles 1:30-31 — Genealogical comparison: Chronicles preserves the final groups as Choddad, Thaiman, Jettour, Naphes and Kedma, corresponding to this closing sequence while retaining its supplied spellings.
  Genesis 25:16 — Genealogical summary: The following statement gathers the listed sons as twelve rulers according to their nations, explaining the status of the groups whose names conclude here.

Genesis 25:16
These are the sons of Ishmael, and these are their names in their tents and their dwellings: twelve rulers according to their nations.
  Genesis 17:20 — Promise fulfilment: God's earlier promise that Ishmael would beget twelve nations corresponds to the twelve rulers and their nations summarized after the sons' list.
  1 Chronicles 1:29-31 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles provides the corresponding twelve-name list of Ishmael's sons, supplying a parallel family catalogue without repeating the tents-and-dwellings description.
  Genesis 21:18 — Promise continuation: The earlier assurance that Hagar's child would become a great nation supplies the wider promise context for the national groups summarized here.

Genesis 25:17
And these were the years of Ishmael’s life: a hundred and thirty-seven years. And his strength failed, and he died, and was added to his family.
  Genesis 16:15 — Narrative closure: The earlier account of Hagar bearing Ishmael opens the life whose total years and death are now recorded; it does not supply a separate attestation of the lifespan.
  Genesis 25:8 — Literary parallel: Ishmael's failing strength, death and addition to family repeat the death-formula elements used earlier for his father Abraham.

Genesis 25:18
And he lived from Havilah to Shur, which is opposite Egypt as one goes toward the Assyrians. He lived opposite all his brothers.
  Genesis 16:12 — Explicit reuse: The angel's earlier statement that Ishmael would live opposite all his brothers corresponds to the dwelling relation stated at the end of his family account.
  Genesis 16:7 — Geographical context: Hagar's earlier encounter occurred at a wilderness spring on the way to Shur, a named boundary point in this later description of Ishmael's dwelling range.

Genesis 25:19
And these are the generations of Isaac, Abraham’s son: Abraham begot Isaac.
  Genesis 21:2-3 — Genealogical correspondence: The earlier birth account names Abraham as father of Sarah's son Isaac, the parentage repeated in this new family heading.
  1 Chronicles 1:34 — Genealogical parallel: Chronicles repeats that Abraham begot Isaac and then names Isaac's sons Esau and Jacob, corresponding to the family line opened here.
  Matthew 1:2 — Genealogical parallel: Matthew's genealogy begins with Abraham fathering Isaac and Isaac fathering Jacob, preserving the same ancestral relationship.

Genesis 25:20
And Isaac was forty years old when he took Rebekah, the daughter of Bethuel the Syrian from Mesopotamia, the sister of Laban the Syrian, to be his wife.
  Genesis 24:15 — Genealogical correspondence: The earlier encounter identifies Rebekah as born to Bethuel in Nahor's family, corresponding to the paternal identity repeated in this marriage notice.
  Genesis 24:67 — Narrative retrospective: The servant-journey account concludes with Isaac taking Rebekah as wife, the marriage now summarized with Isaac's age and her family connections.
  Genesis 28:5 — Genealogical correspondence: Jacob's later journey again identifies Laban as Bethuel's son and Rebekah's brother, preserving the maternal family relationships listed here.

Genesis 25:21
And Isaac prayed to the Lord concerning Rebekah his wife, because she was barren; and God heard him, and Rebekah his wife conceived.
  Genesis 30:22-23 — Narrative parallel: God later hears Rachel and opens her womb so that she conceives, paralleling the answer to prayer and conception in Rebekah's barrenness.
  1 Samuel 1:19-20 — Narrative parallel: The Lord's remembering Hannah and her conception after prayer provide another account of divinely answered childlessness, while the women and promises remain distinct.

Genesis 25:22
And the children leaped within her; and she said, “If it is to be this way with me, why is this happening to me?” And she went to inquire of the Lord.
  Genesis 25:23 — Narrative response: The following oracle answers Rebekah's inquiry by describing two nations and the relationship between the peoples represented by her children.
  Romans 9:10-12 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Paul recalls Rebekah's conception and the oracle given before the twins' birth, interpreting the prenatal setting in relation to divine calling.

Genesis 25:23
And the Lord said to her, “Two nations are in your womb, and two peoples shall be separated from within you; and one people shall surpass the other, and the greater shall serve the lesser.”
  Romans 9:10-13 — Direct quotation and interpretation: Paul quotes the older serving the younger and applies the oracle to divine election before the twins had done good or bad, distinguishing his interpretation from additional claims in Genesis.
  Genesis 27:29 — Narrative correspondence: The later blessing makes Jacob lord over his brother, corresponding to the older-and-younger relation announced before birth.
  Genesis 27:40 — Narrative correspondence: Isaac's later words to Esau include serving his brother and eventual release from the yoke, developing the fraternal service theme of the oracle.

Genesis 25:24
And her days to give birth were fulfilled, and behold, there were twins in her womb.
  Genesis 25:22 — Narrative outcome: The birth of twins confirms that two children were involved in the movement and distress that led Rebekah to inquire of the Lord.
  Romans 9:10-12 — Explicit reuse: Paul recalls the children conceived by Rebekah and Isaac before their birth, supplying a later interpretation of the twin pregnancy completed here.

Genesis 25:25
And the firstborn son came out red, all over like a hairy hide; and he was named Esau.
  Genesis 27:11 — Explicit retrospective correspondence: Jacob later describes Esau as hairy in contrast with his own smooth body, recalling the physical feature emphasized at Esau's birth.
  Genesis 25:30 — Naming development: The later red-stew episode supplies the Edom naming explanation for the same Esau introduced with a red appearance here, while the two descriptions remain distinct.

Genesis 25:26
And afterward his brother came out, and his hand held Esau’s heel; and he was named Jacob. And Isaac was sixty years old when Rebekah bore them.
  Hosea 12:4 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Hosea describes Jacob tripping up his brother in the womb, a later retrospective associated with the heel-holding birth motif; its prenatal wording is retained without recasting Genesis's narrative sequence.
  Genesis 27:36 — Explicit retrospective interpretation: Esau later connects Jacob's name with twice supplanting him, recalling the brother rivalry associated with the birth naming while preserving Esau's character speech as such.
  Genesis 25:20 — Chronological correspondence: Isaac's age of sixty at the twins' birth follows his recorded marriage age of forty, linking two stated milestones without importing different chronological figures.

Genesis 25:27
And the young men grew; and Esau was a man skilled in hunting, a man of the countryside, but Jacob was a man without pretence, dwelling in a house.
  Genesis 27:3-4 — Narrative development: Isaac later sends Esau to hunt in the plain and prepare game, developing the hunting skill and country life assigned to him here.
  Genesis 25:28 — Narrative development: The following preference statement connects Isaac's love of Esau with the food from his hunting and contrasts Rebekah's love of Jacob.

Genesis 25:28
And Isaac loved Esau, because his hunting supplied him with food; but Rebekah loved Jacob.
  Genesis 27:3-4 — Narrative continuation: Isaac's later request for hunted game and savoury food develops the food-related affection for Esau described here.
  Genesis 27:6-10 — Narrative development: Rebekah's later plan to obtain the blessing for Jacob develops the maternal preference stated here, without making the earlier verse narrate the later deception.

Genesis 25:29
And Jacob cooked a stew; and Esau came from the field, exhausted.
  Genesis 25:34 — Narrative outcome: The prepared stew is later given with bread and identified as lentil stew, completing the meal whose preparation and Esau's exhaustion begin here.
  Hebrews 12:16 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews recalls Esau selling his birthright for one meal, interpreting the transaction that develops from his arrival while Jacob is cooking.

Genesis 25:30
And Esau said to Jacob, “Let me taste some of this red stew, for I am exhausted.” Therefore his name was called Edom.
  Genesis 36:1 — Explicit identification: The later family heading expressly identifies Esau as Edom, retaining the name whose explanation is attached to the red-stew request here.
  Genesis 25:34 — Narrative fulfilment: The later statement that Esau eats bread and lentil stew completes the request for food made in this verse.

Genesis 25:31
And Jacob said to Esau, “Sell me your birthright today.”
  Hebrews 12:16 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews expressly recalls Esau selling his birthright for a single meal, the bargain proposed by Jacob here.
  Genesis 27:36 — Explicit retrospective reference: Esau later names the loss of his birthright as the first of two grievances against Jacob, recalling this proposed sale within his own complaint.

Genesis 25:32
And Esau said, “Behold, I am going to die; and what use is this birthright to me?”
  Genesis 25:34 — Narrative evaluation: The narrator's conclusion that Esau despised his birthright evaluates the choice anticipated by his question about its usefulness in his exhausted condition.
  Hebrews 12:16 — Theological interpretation: Hebrews characterizes Esau's sale for one meal as profane conduct, supplying a later moral interpretation of the valuation expressed here.

Genesis 25:33
And Jacob said to him, “Swear to me today.” And he swore to him; and Esau sold his birthright to Jacob.
  Hebrews 12:16 — Explicit reuse: The later warning expressly states that Esau sold his birthright for one meal, recalling the sworn sale concluded here.
  Genesis 27:36 — Explicit retrospective reference: Esau's later complaint recalls the birthright's loss to Jacob; the complaint is distinguished from the narrator's account here that Esau swore and sold it.

Genesis 25:34
And Jacob gave Esau bread and lentil stew; and he ate and drank, and rose and went away. Thus Esau despised his birthright.
  Hebrews 12:16 — Explicit reuse and interpretation: Hebrews recalls the single-meal sale and condemns Esau's profane valuation, corresponding to the narrator's judgment that he despised his birthright.
  Genesis 27:36 — Narrative continuation: The birthright's loss is later remembered alongside the loss of the blessing, keeping the two events distinct in Esau's complaint.

Genesis 26:1
And there was a famine in the land, besides the former famine which was in the time of Abraham; and Isaac went to Abimelech king of the Philistines, to Gerar.
  Genesis 12:10 — Explicit retrospective contrast: The earlier famine drove Abram to Egypt, and this verse expressly distinguishes Isaac's famine from the famine in Abraham's time.
  Genesis 20:1 — Same place and narrative parallel: Abraham earlier sojourned in Gerar, the place Isaac now visits; the shared place and ruler-name pattern do not establish one historical Abimelech across generations.

Genesis 26:2
And the Lord appeared to him and said, “Do not go down into Egypt; live in the land that I shall tell you about.
  Genesis 12:10 — Narrative contrast: Abram earlier went to Egypt because of the famine; Isaac is now explicitly told not to go there. The comparison contrasts Abram's narrated action with God's command to Isaac.
  Genesis 12:1 — Shared guidance pattern: The earlier call sends Abram to the land God will show him, while Isaac is told to reside in the land God will indicate, sharing dependence on divine direction.

Genesis 26:3
“And live as a stranger in this land, and I will be with you and bless you; for to you and to your seed I will give all this land, and I will establish My oath which I swore to Abraham your father.
  Genesis 17:7-8 — Promise renewal: The ancestral covenant promises the land of sojourning to Abraham and his seed, the inheritance now expressly renewed to Isaac.
  Genesis 22:16-18 — Explicit oath connection: The earlier divine oath to Abraham promises blessing and multiplied seed, supplying the sworn promise God says He will establish with Isaac.
  Hebrews 11:9 — Explicit retrospective interpretation: Hebrews calls Isaac and Jacob fellow heirs of Abraham's promise while dwelling as foreigners, corresponding to the promised inheritance given to Isaac as a sojourner.

Genesis 26:4
“And I will multiply your seed as the stars of heaven, and give to your seed all this land; and in your seed all the nations of the earth shall be blessed,
  Genesis 15:5 — Promise renewal: God's comparison of Abram's seed with the stars is repeated in the renewed promise of abundant descendants to Isaac.
  Genesis 22:17-18 — Promise renewal: The earlier oath combines star-like multiplication and blessing for all nations in Abraham's seed, the two promises brought together again for Isaac.
  Galatians 3:8-9 — Theological interpretation: Paul interprets the nations' blessing through Abraham in terms of faith, providing a later treatment of the blessing promise renewed here rather than a claim that Genesis states Paul's full argument.

Genesis 26:5
“because Abraham your father obeyed My voice, and kept My charges, My commandments, My ordinances, and My statutes.”
  Genesis 22:18 — Explicit reuse: The earlier oath likewise connects the blessing of all nations with Abraham's listening to God's voice, matching the obedience reason given here.
  Genesis 18:19 — Thematic connection: God's earlier description joins Abraham's household instruction with keeping the Lord's ways and doing righteousness and judgment, corresponding to the obedience praised here without asserting a complete pre-Sinai legal corpus.

Genesis 26:6
And Isaac lived in Gerar.
  Genesis 26:1 — Narrative continuation: Isaac's settled residence in Gerar continues the arrival at Gerar introduced at the start of the famine account.
  Genesis 20:1 — Same place: Abraham had earlier lived as a stranger in Gerar, providing a prior patriarchal setting for Isaac's residence in the same named place.

Genesis 26:7
And the men of the place asked concerning Rebekah his wife, and he said, “She is my sister”; for he feared to say, “She is my wife,” lest the men of the place should kill him because of Rebekah, for she was beautiful to behold.
  Genesis 12:11-13 — Narrative parallel: Abram similarly connects his wife's beauty with fear of being killed and requests a sister claim, paralleling Isaac's strategy without identifying the two wives or episodes.
  Genesis 20:2 — Narrative parallel: Abraham's later sister declaration concerning Sarah is likewise explained by fear of death, closely corresponding to Isaac's stated fear concerning Rebekah.

Genesis 26:8
And he remained there a long time; and Abimelech king of Gerar looked through the window and saw Isaac playing with Rebekah his wife.
  Genesis 26:9 — Narrative consequence: The ruler's observation is followed by his explicit recognition that Rebekah is Isaac's wife and his challenge to the sister claim.
  Genesis 24:67 — Marital identity: The earlier marriage account establishes Rebekah as Isaac's wife, the actual relationship disclosed by the ruler's observation here without specifying an unreported act.

Genesis 26:9
And Abimelech called Isaac and said to him, “Surely she is your wife! Why did you say, ‘She is my sister’?” And Isaac said to him, “For I thought, ‘I may die because of her.’”
  Genesis 12:18-19 — Narrative parallel: Pharaoh likewise challenges Abram over concealing his wife's marital status behind a sister claim, repeating the ruler's confrontation motif.
  Genesis 20:9-11 — Narrative parallel: The earlier Gerar ruler questions Abraham, who explains fear of being killed for his wife; this corresponds to Isaac's explanation without proving the rulers' historical identity.

Genesis 26:10
And Abimelech said to him, “What is this that you have done to us? One of my kin might easily have lain with your wife, and you would have brought an unwitting offence upon us.”
  Genesis 20:9-10 — Narrative parallel: The earlier ruler also challenges the patriarch over conduct that endangered his kingdom, corresponding to Abimelech's concern over an unwitting offence here.
  Genesis 12:18-19 — Narrative comparison: Pharaoh's rebuke concerns actually taking the woman as wife, whereas this ruler warns of what one of his kin might have done; both confront the concealed marriage without equating the outcomes.

Genesis 26:11
And Abimelech charged all his people, saying, “Whoever touches this man or his wife shall be liable to death.”
  Genesis 20:6 — Protection parallel: God's prevention of Abimelech touching Sarah provides a related protection scene, while the present verse expresses protection through the ruler's public command concerning Isaac and Rebekah.
  Genesis 26:10 — Narrative response: The prohibition on touching the couple answers the danger of unwitting offence that the ruler has just identified.

Genesis 26:12
And Isaac sowed in that land, and in that year he reaped barley a hundredfold; and the Lord blessed him.
  Genesis 26:3 — Promise outcome: The hundredfold barley harvest and explicit blessing correspond to God's earlier assurance that He would be with Isaac and bless him in the land.
  Genesis 26:13 — Narrative development: The next statement develops the successful blessed harvest into Isaac's continuing increase and greatness.

Genesis 26:13
And the man was exalted, and continued to grow greater, until he became very great.
  Genesis 24:35 — Ancestral parallel: The servant had described Abraham as greatly blessed and exalted with abundant property, a similar prosperity pattern now attributed to his son Isaac.
  Genesis 26:16 — Narrative consequence: Isaac's increasing greatness leads to the ruler's later statement that he has become far mightier than the local people and must depart.

Genesis 26:14
And he had flocks of sheep, herds of cattle, and much farmland; and the Philistines envied him.
  Genesis 13:2-6 — Narrative comparison: Extensive livestock and possessions earlier prevented Abram and Lot from sharing space, providing another account of wealth creating pressure on residence without equating that conflict with Philistine envy.
  Genesis 26:16 — Narrative consequence: The report of wealth and Philistine envy is followed by the ruler's request that the now-mighty Isaac leave.

Genesis 26:15
And all the wells which his father’s servants had dug in his father’s time, the Philistines stopped up and filled with earth.
  Genesis 21:25 — Ancestral water dispute: Abraham had earlier reproved an Abimelech over servants taking his wells, providing a preceding family dispute over water without identifying every well or ruler as the same.
  Genesis 26:18 — Narrative reversal: Isaac's later reopening of the stopped wells reverses the blocking with earth reported here and expressly recalls his father's wells.

Genesis 26:16
And Abimelech said to Isaac, “Depart from us, for you have become far mightier than we.”
  Genesis 26:13-14 — Narrative explanation: The preceding growth of Isaac's property and the Philistines' envy explain the background to the demand that he depart as the mightier party.
  Genesis 26:17 — Narrative fulfilment: Isaac's departure and encampment in the valley carry out the ruler's demand that he leave.

Genesis 26:17
And Isaac departed from there, and camped in the valley of Gerar, and lived there.
  Genesis 26:16 — Narrative fulfilment: The relocation follows directly from Abimelech's instruction that Isaac depart, preserving the sequence of expulsion and renewed settlement.
  Genesis 26:19-22 — Narrative setting: The subsequent well-digging and disputes unfold in the valley of Gerar where Isaac now camps, giving the setting for the next narrative unit.

Genesis 26:18
And Isaac again dug the wells of water which the servants of Abraham his father had dug, and which the Philistines had stopped up after Abraham his father died; and he called them by the names which Abraham his father had given them.
  Genesis 26:15 — Narrative reversal: The earlier statement that the Philistines filled Abraham's wells with earth is reversed by Isaac's digging them again and restoring their inherited names.
  Genesis 21:25-31 — Ancestral water context: Abraham's earlier dispute and oath concern his ownership of a dug well, supplying ancestral context for the wells Isaac reopens without identifying all of them as that single well.

Genesis 26:19
And Isaac’s servants dug in the valley of Gerar, and found there a well of living water.
  Genesis 26:20 — Narrative consequence: The discovery of the well is followed by a dispute over who owns its water, developing the consequence of this successful digging.
  Genesis 26:32 — Narrative contrast: This digging finds living water, whereas the later servants' report in the supplied edition says they found no water; the distinct outcomes are preserved rather than harmonized.

Genesis 26:20
And the shepherds of Gerar contended with Isaac’s shepherds, saying that the water was theirs; and he called the name of the well Injustice, for they wronged him.
  Genesis 21:25 — Ancestral dispute parallel: The earlier complaint that servants seized Abraham's wells parallels the competing claim to water made against Isaac's shepherds here.
  Genesis 26:21 — Narrative repetition: The next well also provokes dispute and receives a conflict-related name, continuing the pattern introduced by the well named Injustice.

Genesis 26:21
And Isaac departed from there and dug another well, and they disputed over that also; and he called its name Enmity.
  Genesis 26:20 — Narrative repetition: The second dispute repeats the earlier contested-water episode, while the supplied names Injustice and Enmity remain distinct.
  Genesis 26:22 — Narrative contrast: Isaac's further move reaches a well over which there is no contention, contrasting with the dispute attached to Enmity.

Genesis 26:22
And he departed from there and dug another well, and they did not contend over it; and he called its name Broad Space, saying, “For now the Lord has made room for us, and has increased us in the land.”
  Genesis 26:20-21 — Narrative contrast: The absence of contention at Broad Space contrasts with the two preceding disputed wells, explaining Isaac's acknowledgment that the Lord has made room.
  Genesis 26:24 — Thematic continuation: Isaac's acknowledgment of the Lord's provision is followed by God's assurance of His presence, blessing and multiplied seed.

Genesis 26:23
And he went up from there to the Well of the Oath.
  Genesis 21:31-33 — Same place and ancestral setting: Abraham's earlier oath and worship are associated with the Well of the Oath, the named place Isaac now reaches; the separate naming scenes are retained.
  Genesis 26:33 — Narrative continuation: The later naming statement again associates the city with Oath, developing the place name introduced by Isaac's arrival here without replacing either account.

Genesis 26:24
And the Lord appeared to him that night and said, “I am the God of Abraham your father. Do not fear, for I am with you, and I have blessed you, and will multiply your seed for the sake of Abraham your father.”
  Genesis 17:19-21 — Promise renewal: God's earlier commitment to establish His covenant with Isaac supplies the ancestral promise behind the renewed assurance of blessing to him.
  Genesis 22:16-18 — Ancestral promise connection: The oath following Abraham's obedience promises blessing and numerous seed, corresponding to God's assurance that Isaac's blessing is for Abraham's sake.
  Genesis 28:13-15 — Promise parallel: The later appearance to Jacob similarly names the ancestral God, commands him not to fear and promises presence, land and descendants, continuing the familial assurance pattern.

Genesis 26:25
And he built an altar there, and called upon the name of the Lord, and pitched his tent there; and Isaac’s servants dug a well there.
  Genesis 12:8 — Literary parallel: Abram also builds an altar, calls upon the Lord's name and pitches his tent, the combination of worship and residence repeated in Isaac's scene.
  Genesis 21:33 — Ancestral worship parallel: Abraham had called on the Lord's name at the Well of the Oath, corresponding to Isaac's worship in the same named setting.

Genesis 26:26
And Abimelech came to him from Gerar, and Ahuzzath his groomsman, and Phicol the commander of his army.
  Genesis 21:22 — Narrative parallel: The earlier delegation to Abraham also names Abimelech, Ahuzzath and Phicol in the same roles, a repeated narrative pattern that does not establish historical identity across generations.
  Genesis 26:28-29 — Narrative development: The visitors' later request explains the purpose of their arrival: an oath and covenant protecting them from harm because they recognize the Lord's blessing on Isaac.

Genesis 26:27
And Isaac said to them, “Why have you come to me, when you hated me and sent me away from you?”
  Genesis 26:16 — Explicit retrospective reference: Isaac's charge that they sent him away recalls Abimelech's earlier command that he depart because he had become mightier.
  Genesis 26:28-29 — Dialogue contrast: The delegation answers Isaac's accusation with a request for peace and its own claim of good treatment; the comparison preserves the different speakers' perspectives.

Genesis 26:28
And they said, “We have clearly seen that the Lord was with you, and we said, ‘Let there be an oath with a curse between us and you, and let us make a covenant with you,
  Genesis 21:22-24 — Narrative parallel: The earlier delegation likewise recognizes God with Abraham and requests a sworn commitment, paralleling the recognition and covenant request made to Isaac.
  Genesis 26:24 — Narrative correspondence: The visitors' observation that the Lord is with Isaac corresponds to the divine assurance of His presence given earlier in the chapter.

Genesis 26:29
“‘that you shall do us no harm, even as we have not loathed you, and as we have treated you well and sent you away in peace.’ And now you are blessed by the Lord.”
  Genesis 26:27 — Dialogue contrast: The delegation's claim that it treated Isaac well and sent him away peacefully stands beside Isaac's earlier accusation of hatred and expulsion; neither voice is silently made the narrator's verdict.
  Genesis 21:23 — Narrative parallel: The earlier oath request similarly asks the patriarch not to wrong the ruler and appeals to past treatment, repeating the negotiated non-harm commitment.

Genesis 26:30
And he made a feast for them, and they ate and drank.
  Genesis 31:54 — Covenant-meal parallel: Jacob later offers sacrifice and shares a meal with his kinsmen after an oath agreement, providing another covenant-associated meal without identifying the ceremonies as one regulation.
  Genesis 26:31 — Narrative continuation: The meal is followed in the morning by mutual oaths and peaceful departure, placing hospitality within the agreement's completion.

Genesis 26:31
And they rose in the morning and swore one to another; and Isaac sent them away, and they departed from him in safety.
  Genesis 21:31-32 — Ancestral covenant parallel: The earlier Well-of-the-Oath story likewise joins mutual swearing, covenant making and the delegation's departure, corresponding to the oath and release here.
  Genesis 32:1 — Narrative parallel: After the preceding covenant meal, Laban rises in the morning and departs, paralleling the morning separation after agreement in Isaac's story.

Genesis 26:32
And that same day Isaac’s servants came and told him concerning the well which they had dug, and said, “We found no water.”
  Genesis 26:19 — Narrative contrast: The earlier servants found a well of living water, while this supplied report says no water was found; the difference is preserved in the cross-reference.
  Genesis 26:33 — Narrative continuation: The following naming statement is attached to this well report, while the negative no-water wording remains intact rather than being replaced to ease the transition.

Genesis 26:33
And he called it Oath; therefore the name of the city is Well of the Oath to this day.
  Genesis 21:31-33 — Same place and naming parallel: Abraham's earlier oath account also names the Well of Swearing or Oath, corresponding to the oath-based place name stated here while retaining both narratives.
  Genesis 26:31 — Narrative explanation: The mutual swearing just completed supplies the oath context for Isaac's naming the well Oath and the city's continuing name.

Genesis 26:34
And Esau was forty years old, and took as wives Judith the daughter of Beeri the Hittite, and Basemath the daughter of Elon the Hivite.
  Genesis 28:6-9 — Explicit retrospective development: The later narrative says Esau recognizes that Canaanite wives displease Isaac and takes another wife, developing the family-marriage conflict introduced here.
  Genesis 36:2-3 — Genealogical comparison: The later Esau wife list has different names and parental descriptions; it is a related family record, not a basis for silently equating every woman or changing Elon the Hivite here.

Genesis 26:35
And they contended with Isaac and Rebekah.
  Genesis 27:46 — Narrative continuation: Rebekah later voices her weariness over the daughters of Heth and objects to Jacob marrying from the land, continuing the family contention reported here.
  Genesis 28:8-9 — Explicit retrospective development: Esau later sees that the daughters of Canaan displease Isaac and adds a wife from Ishmael's family, responding to the dissatisfaction associated with his earlier marriages.

Genesis 27:1
And when Isaac was old and his eyes were too dim to see, he called Esau his elder son and said to him, “My son.” And he said, “Behold, here am I.”
  Genesis 48:10 — Narrative parallel: Jacob later likewise has eyes dim with age as his grandsons are brought near to him for blessing, providing a generational parallel to Isaac's failing sight.
  Genesis 25:28 — Narrative background: The earlier account says Isaac loved Esau because his hunting supplied food, explaining the preference underlying this summons of the elder son.

Genesis 27:2
And he said, “Behold, I have grown old, and do not know the day of my death.
  Genesis 35:28-29 — Narrative continuation: Isaac's eventual death is recorded with his total age and burial by both sons; the later notice does not make his death immediate in this scene.
  Genesis 27:4 — Narrative explanation: The stated uncertainty about death explains Isaac's ensuing request to eat and bless his son before he dies.

Genesis 27:3
“Now therefore take your gear, your quiver and your bow, and go out into the plain, and hunt game for me.
  Genesis 25:27-28 — Narrative background: Esau is already described as a skilled hunter whose game pleases Isaac, matching the task and preference in the request.
  Genesis 27:31 — Narrative fulfilment: Esau later returns with prepared food from his hunting, carrying out the commission given before the deception intervenes.

Genesis 27:4
“And prepare for me savoury food such as I love, and bring it to me, that I may eat, and that my soul may bless you before I die.”
  Genesis 27:25 — Narrative counterpart: Isaac later eats the food presented by Jacob and prepares to bless him, following the meal-before-blessing sequence originally intended for Esau.
  Genesis 27:7 — Explicit reported repetition: Rebekah repeats the food and blessing request to Jacob, transmitting the plan she overheard while retaining her reported wording.

Genesis 27:5
And Rebekah heard Isaac speaking to Esau his son. And Esau went into the plain to hunt game for his father.
  Genesis 27:6-10 — Narrative consequence: Rebekah's following instructions arise from her hearing Isaac's request while Esau leaves to hunt, explaining her opportunity to act.
  Genesis 27:30-31 — Narrative continuation: Esau's later return with his prepared game completes the hunting departure recorded here, after Jacob has already received a blessing.

Genesis 27:6
And Rebekah said to Jacob her younger son, “Behold, I heard your father speaking to Esau your brother, saying,
  Genesis 27:3-4 — Explicit reported repetition: Rebekah introduces her report of the hunting, food and blessing instructions Isaac had given to Esau.
  Genesis 27:7 — Narrative continuation: The following quoted request supplies the content of the conversation Rebekah says she heard.

Genesis 27:7
“‘Bring me game, and prepare savoury food for me, that I may eat and bless you before the Lord before I die.’
  Genesis 27:4 — Explicit reported repetition: Rebekah's report repeats the requested savoury meal and blessing before death; before the Lord belongs to the report's own wording.
  Genesis 27:10 — Narrative response: Her subsequent plan transfers the delivery of the desired meal to Jacob so that he, rather than Esau, receives the blessing.

Genesis 27:8
“Now therefore, my son, listen to me in what I command you.
  Genesis 27:13 — Literary repetition: Rebekah again instructs Jacob to obey her voice when he raises the danger of a curse, continuing her direction of the plan.
  Genesis 27:14 — Narrative fulfilment: Jacob obtains the goats and brings them to his mother, carrying out the command she begins giving here.

Genesis 27:9
“Go to the flock, and bring me from there two tender and good young goats; and I will prepare them as savoury food for your father, as he loves.
  Genesis 27:14 — Narrative fulfilment: Rebekah's preparation of savoury food follows Jacob's obtaining the goats, implementing the ingredients and cooking plan stated here.
  Genesis 27:3-4 — Narrative contrast: The goats prepared by Rebekah replace the hunted game Isaac requested from Esau, distinguishing the actual preparation from the intended meal.

Genesis 27:10
“And you shall bring it to your father, and he shall eat, that your father may bless you before he dies.”
  Genesis 27:17-19 — Narrative fulfilment: Rebekah gives the food to Jacob and he presents it to Isaac while claiming Esau's identity, carrying out the intended transfer of the meal and blessing opportunity.
  Genesis 27:23 — Narrative outcome: Isaac's failure to recognize Jacob and his blessing of him realize the outcome Rebekah seeks, without presenting the method as morally endorsed.

Genesis 27:11
And Jacob said to Rebekah his mother, “Esau my brother is a hairy man, and I am a smooth man.
  Genesis 25:25 — Explicit physical correspondence: Esau's birth description already calls him hairy, supplying the physical distinction Jacob fears Isaac will detect.
  Genesis 27:16 — Narrative response: Rebekah covers Jacob's bare arms and neck with goat skins, addressing the hairy-versus-smooth difference he raises.

Genesis 27:12
“Perhaps my father will feel me, and I shall be before him as one who treats him with contempt, and shall bring a curse upon myself, and not a blessing.”
  Genesis 27:21-23 — Narrative development: Isaac does feel Jacob as Jacob feared, but the disguise prevents recognition and the narrated outcome is a blessing rather than the feared curse.
  Genesis 27:13 — Narrative response: Rebekah directly answers Jacob's concern about bringing a curse on himself by saying it should be upon her and repeating her command.

Genesis 27:13
And his mother said to him, “Upon me be your curse, my child; only obey my voice, and go and bring them to me.”
  Genesis 27:12 — Narrative response: The offered assumption of the curse answers the risk Jacob has just raised; the narrative does not here report that such a curse actually falls on Rebekah.
  Genesis 27:8 — Literary repetition: Rebekah renews her demand that Jacob obey her voice, repeating her earlier instruction after his objection.

Genesis 27:14
And he went and took them, and brought them to his mother; and his mother prepared savoury food, as his father loved.
  Genesis 27:9 — Narrative fulfilment: Jacob's obtaining the goats and Rebekah's cooking complete the specific food-preparation plan she proposed.
  Genesis 27:17 — Narrative continuation: The prepared food is next placed in Jacob's hands for delivery, moving the plan from preparation to presentation.

Genesis 27:15
And Rebekah took the fine clothing of Esau her elder son, which was with her in the house, and clothed Jacob her younger son.
  Genesis 27:27 — Narrative consequence: Isaac later smells the garments during the blessing scene, showing the role of the clothing with which Rebekah dresses Jacob.
  Genesis 27:35 — Narrative disclosure: Isaac later identifies the blessing's acquisition as deceit, supplying his retrospective description of the impersonation that includes this clothing disguise.

Genesis 27:16
And she put the skins of the young goats upon his arms, and upon the bare parts of his neck.
  Genesis 27:11 — Narrative response: The goat skins address Jacob's concern that Esau is hairy while he is smooth, providing a physical means of impersonation.
  Genesis 27:23 — Narrative outcome: Isaac's later failure to recognize Jacob is expressly linked to the hands feeling hairy like Esau's, showing the disguise's effect.

Genesis 27:17
And she gave the savoury food and the loaves which she had prepared into the hands of Jacob her son.
  Genesis 27:25 — Narrative fulfilment: Isaac later eats the food and drinks the wine Jacob presents, completing the delivery initiated when Rebekah hands him the prepared meal.
  Genesis 27:9-10 — Narrative fulfilment: The prepared food in Jacob's hands carries out Rebekah's plan that he deliver a meal to obtain the blessing.

Genesis 27:18
And he brought them to his father, and said, “Father.” And he said, “Behold, here am I. Who are you, my child?”
  Genesis 27:19 — Narrative response: Jacob's claim to be Esau supplies the answer to Isaac's question about the identity of the approaching son.
  Genesis 27:32 — Literary parallel: Isaac later asks the arriving Esau who he is, repeating the identity question with the actual elder son after the deception.

Genesis 27:19
And Jacob said to his father, “I am Esau your firstborn. I have done as you told me. Arise, sit, and eat of my game, that your soul may bless me.”
  Genesis 27:24 — Explicit repetition: Jacob repeats the same claimed identity when Isaac asks again whether he is Esau, maintaining the impersonation.
  Genesis 27:35 — Narrative disclosure: Isaac's later statement that the brother came with deceit identifies the nature of Jacob's claim rather than treating him as the actual firstborn Esau.

Genesis 27:20
And Isaac said to his son, “How is it that you have found it so quickly, my child?” And he said, “Because the Lord your God put it before me.”
  Genesis 27:14 — Narrative contrast: The account attributes the meal's preparation to Rebekah after Jacob obtains goats, distinguishing the narrated action from his claim that the Lord put the game before him.
  Genesis 27:21 — Narrative response: Isaac follows the question about the meal's speed with a demand to feel the speaker, continuing his effort to establish the son's identity.

Genesis 27:21
And Isaac said to Jacob, “Draw near to me, that I may feel you, my child, whether you are my son Esau or not.”
  Genesis 27:12 — Narrative development: Isaac's demand to feel Jacob brings about the very examination Jacob feared when discussing the risk with Rebekah.
  Genesis 27:22 — Narrative fulfilment: Jacob draws near and Isaac feels him, carrying out the physical examination requested here.

Genesis 27:22
And Jacob drew near to Isaac his father; and he felt him and said, “The voice is Jacob’s voice, but the hands are Esau’s hands.”
  Genesis 27:11 — Narrative background: The earlier hairy-versus-smooth distinction explains why Isaac contrasts the voice with the hands in trying to identify his son.
  Genesis 27:16 — Narrative explanation: The goat skins placed on Jacob's arms explain why the hands feel like Esau's despite the recognizable voice.

Genesis 27:23
And he did not recognize him, for his hands were hairy as the hands of Esau his brother; and he blessed him.
  Genesis 27:16 — Narrative outcome: The narrator's explanation that Isaac was deceived by hairy hands recalls the skins used to cover Jacob's bare parts.
  Genesis 27:33 — Narrative contrast: Isaac's later astonishment on discovering the earlier blessing contrasts with his failure to recognize Jacob during this first encounter.

Genesis 27:24
And he said, “Are you my son Esau?” And he said, “I am.”
  Genesis 27:19 — Explicit repetition: Jacob's affirmation repeats the false identity he had already asserted on entering with the meal.
  Genesis 27:32 — Narrative contrast: Esau's later true identification as the firstborn contrasts with Jacob's affirmation of that identity before the deception is exposed.

Genesis 27:25
And he said, “Bring it to me, that I may eat of your game, my child, and that my soul may bless you.” And he brought it to him, and he ate; and he brought him wine, and he drank.
  Genesis 27:4 — Narrative counterpart: Eating before blessing follows Isaac's original meal request, although Jacob rather than the intended Esau is now presenting the food.
  Genesis 27:17 — Narrative fulfilment: The meal placed in Jacob's hands by Rebekah is now consumed by Isaac, completing its delivery.

Genesis 27:26
And Isaac his father said to him, “Draw near to me, and kiss me, my child.”
  Genesis 27:27 — Narrative fulfilment: The requested kiss is given in the following verse, and the resulting closeness allows Isaac to smell the garments before blessing.

Genesis 27:27
And he drew near and kissed him; and he smelled the fragrance of his garments, and blessed him, and said, “Behold, the fragrance of my son is as the fragrance of a full field which the Lord has blessed.
  Genesis 27:15 — Narrative explanation: The garments Isaac smells are those Rebekah had taken from Esau and put on Jacob, connecting the sensory impression with the disguise.
  Genesis 27:28 — Blessing development: The comparison with a field blessed by the Lord leads into the specific gifts of dew, earth's richness, grain and wine.

Genesis 27:28
“And may God give you of the dew of heaven, and of the richness of the earth, and abundance of grain and wine.
  Deuteronomy 33:13 — Shared blessing imagery: Moses' later blessing of Joseph likewise joins the Lord's blessing of land with heavenly dew and the deep's springs, corresponding to Isaac's agricultural imagery.
  Deuteronomy 33:28 — Shared blessing imagery: The later picture of Israel upon Jacob's land combines grain, wine and heaven's dew, repeating the principal fertility images in Isaac's blessing.
  Genesis 27:37 — Explicit blessing summary: Isaac later summarizes the blessing as sustaining Jacob with grain and wine, directly recalling gifts announced here.

Genesis 27:29
“Let nations serve you, and rulers shall bow down to you. Be lord over your brother, and your father’s sons shall bow down to you. Cursed be the one who curses you, and blessed be the one who blesses you.”
  Genesis 25:23 — Promise correspondence: The earlier oracle concerning the brothers' peoples says the greater will serve the lesser, corresponding to the service and brotherly rule in the blessing without endorsing the deceptive means.
  Genesis 12:3 — Shared blessing formula: The promise to Abraham likewise assigns blessing to his blessers and curse to his cursers, continuing the ancestral protection formula.
  Numbers 24:9 — Shared blessing formula: Balaam's later blessing also calls those who bless Israel blessed and those who curse him cursed, repeating the protective blessing pattern.

Genesis 27:30
And when Isaac had finished blessing Jacob his son, and Jacob had gone out from the presence of Isaac his father, Esau his brother came from his hunting.
  Genesis 27:5 — Narrative continuation: Esau's return completes the hunting departure recorded while Rebekah began arranging Jacob's intervention.
  Genesis 27:33 — Narrative consequence: The arrival after Jacob's departure leads to Isaac's astonished recognition that he already ate and blessed someone before Esau came.

Genesis 27:31
And he also prepared savoury food, and brought it to his father, and said to his father, “Let my father arise and eat of his son’s game, that your soul may bless me.”
  Genesis 27:3-4 — Narrative fulfilment: Esau prepares and presents the meal as Isaac originally requested, asking for the blessing that was intended to follow it.
  Genesis 27:25 — Literary parallel: The presentation of food with a request for the father's blessing repeats the meal-and-blessing sequence already enacted with Jacob.

Genesis 27:32
And Isaac his father said to him, “Who are you?” And he said, “I am your firstborn son Esau.”
  Genesis 27:19 — Narrative contrast: The actual Esau's identification exposes the earlier claim by Jacob to be Esau the firstborn.
  Genesis 25:25 — Genealogical correspondence: The birth account identifies Esau as the firstborn, confirming the parentage and birth-order identity he states here.

Genesis 27:33
And Isaac was exceedingly astonished, and said, “Who then hunted game for me and brought it to me? And I ate of all before you came, and blessed him; and let him be blessed.”
  Genesis 27:23 — Narrative discovery: Isaac's statement that he has already blessed someone looks back to the blessing given while he failed to recognize Jacob.
  Genesis 27:35 — Narrative explanation: Isaac's subsequent answer identifies the earlier visitor as the brother who came with deceit, explaining his astonishment.
  Hebrews 11:20 — Theological interpretation: Hebrews later characterizes Isaac's blessings of both sons concerning the future as faith, without declaring the deception itself faithful.

Genesis 27:34
And when Esau heard the words of Isaac his father, he cried with a great and exceedingly bitter cry, and said, “Bless me also, father!”
  Hebrews 12:17 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews recalls Esau's later desire to inherit the blessing and his tears, directly corresponding to the bitter entreaty narrated here.
  Genesis 27:38 — Explicit repeated entreaty: Esau again asks to be blessed and weeps, continuing the plea and grief introduced by his first bitter cry.

Genesis 27:35
And he said to him, “Your brother came with deceit, and took your blessing.”
  Genesis 27:19 — Narrative disclosure: Isaac's description of deceit refers back to Jacob's false claim to be the firstborn Esau.
  Genesis 27:15-16 — Narrative disclosure: The clothing and skin disguise provide the narrated means behind Isaac's statement that the brother obtained the blessing deceitfully.

Genesis 27:36
And he said, “He was rightly named Jacob, for he has now supplanted me these two times: he took my birthright, and now he has taken my blessing.” And Esau said to his father, “Have you not reserved a blessing for me, father?”
  Genesis 25:29-34 — Explicit retrospective comparison: Esau recalls the birthright episode, while the earlier narrator expressly records that he sold and despised it; his accusation is not substituted for that account.
  Genesis 25:26 — Name-and-brother motif: Jacob's birth naming follows his holding Esau's heel, supplying the earlier brother-related name setting for Esau's later supplanting wordplay without a new etymological claim.
  Hebrews 12:16 — Explicit retrospective contrast: Hebrews likewise recalls Esau's sale of the birthright for one meal, preserving the sale emphasis alongside his later claim that Jacob took it.

Genesis 27:37
And Isaac answered and said to Esau, “If I have made him your lord, and made all his brothers his household slaves, and sustained him with grain and wine, what shall I do for you, my child?”
  Genesis 27:28-29 — Explicit blessing summary: Isaac summarizes the grain, wine and dominion over brothers already spoken in Jacob's blessing.
  Genesis 25:23 — Promise correspondence: The statement that Jacob has been made lord over the brothers corresponds to the earlier oracle about the greater serving the lesser.

Genesis 27:38
And Esau said to his father, “Do you have only one blessing, father? Bless me also, father.” And Isaac was deeply moved; and Esau lifted up his voice and wept.
  Hebrews 12:17 — Explicit reuse: Hebrews recalls Esau seeking the blessing with tears, corresponding to his repeated entreaty and weeping without settling every implication of the repentance phrase.
  Genesis 27:34 — Explicit repeated entreaty: The earlier great and bitter cry already included the request Bless me also, which Esau renews here.

Genesis 27:39
And Isaac his father answered and said to him, “Behold, your dwelling shall be of the richness of the earth, and of the dew of heaven from above.
  Genesis 27:28 — Blessing comparison: Both supplied blessings use richness of the earth and dew of heaven; this edition's positive wording for Esau is not replaced with an away-from formulation.
  Hebrews 11:20 — Explicit retrospective interpretation: Hebrews refers to Isaac blessing both Jacob and Esau concerning future things, including the blessing addressed to Esau here.

Genesis 27:40
“And you shall live by your sword, and you shall serve your brother; but when you cast down, you shall loosen his yoke from your neck.”
  2 Kings 8:20-22 — Later historical comparison: The later account of Edom breaking away from Judah provides a political counterpart to release from the brother's yoke, without claiming that one event exhausts the saying's meaning.
  Genesis 25:23 — Promise correspondence: The service relationship between the brothers' peoples recalls the earlier oracle, while this blessing adds the prospect of loosening the yoke.
  Genesis 27:29 — Blessing counterpart: Esau's service to his brother corresponds to the authority over the brother conferred in Jacob's blessing, with a future release also stated here.

Genesis 27:41
And Esau bore a grudge against Jacob because of the blessing with which his father had blessed him; and Esau said in his mind, “Let the days of mourning for my father draw near, that I may kill Jacob my brother.”
  Genesis 4:8 — Narrative comparison: Cain's killing of his brother provides an earlier brother-hostility comparison; Esau's statement here remains a threat rather than an accomplished murder.
  Genesis 27:42 — Narrative consequence: Rebekah subsequently learns of Esau's threat and calls Jacob, responding to the murderous intent reported here.

Genesis 27:42
And the words of Esau her elder son were told to Rebekah; and she sent and called Jacob her younger son, and said to him, “Behold, Esau your brother threatens to kill you.
  Genesis 27:41 — Explicit threat report: Rebekah's warning reports the intent already attributed to Esau, giving Jacob knowledge of the danger.
  Genesis 27:43 — Narrative response: The warning is followed by a concrete instruction to flee to Laban in Harran, explaining the action Rebekah takes after learning of the threat.

Genesis 27:43

  Genesis 28:2 — Narrative continuation: Isaac's subsequent marriage instruction also directs Jacob to flee into Mesopotamia to the maternal family, corresponding to the destination Rebekah names.
  Genesis 29:12-14 — Narrative outcome: Jacob later identifies himself as Rebekah's son and is welcomed into Laban's house, reaching the maternal family designated as his refuge.

Genesis 27:44

  Genesis 27:45 — Grammatical and narrative continuation: The following verse completes the supplied fragment about fury by describing the hoped-for turning away of Esau's anger and the plan to bring Jacob back.
  Genesis 31:41 — Duration contrast: The expected few days contrast with Jacob's later account of twenty years in Laban's house; the comparison does not reconstruct unreported travel dates.

Genesis 27:45
“and anger of your brother turn away from you, and he forgets what you have done to him. Then I will send and bring you back from there, lest I be bereaved of you both in one day.”
  Genesis 33:4 — Narrative contrast: The brothers' eventual embrace and shared weeping contrast with the hostility from which Jacob fled, without asserting that the narrator says Esau forgot every grievance.
  Genesis 27:41 — Narrative context: Esau's plan to kill Jacob explains Rebekah's fear of losing both sons and her desire for his anger to turn away.

Genesis 27:46
And Rebekah said to Isaac, “I am weary of my life because of the daughters of the sons of Heth. If Jacob takes a wife from the daughters of this land, why should I live?”
  Genesis 26:34-35 — Narrative background: Esau's local wives had already been described as contending with Isaac and Rebekah, providing the family context for her objection to another local marriage.
  Genesis 28:1 — Narrative response: Isaac's next instruction forbids Jacob taking a Canaanite wife, responding to Rebekah's stated concern about daughters of the land.

Genesis 28:1

  Genesis 24:3-4 — Ancestral marriage parallel: Abraham had likewise instructed that Isaac's wife be sought from his family rather than the Canaanite daughters, establishing the earlier family-marriage policy.
  Genesis 27:46 — Narrative response: The marriage prohibition follows Rebekah's complaint about daughters of the land and her concern over Jacob's future wife.
  Genesis 26:34-35 — Narrative background: The contention associated with Esau's local marriages provides the earlier family circumstance behind avoiding such a marriage for Jacob.

Genesis 28:2

  Genesis 24:24-29 — Genealogical context: The earlier Rebekah story identifies Bethuel's family and her brother Laban, supplying the maternal kinship network named as Jacob's marriage destination.

Genesis 28:3

  Genesis 17:4-6 — Ancestral promise correspondence: The promised multitude of nations for Abraham supplies the ancestral background to Isaac's wish that Jacob increase into assemblies of nations.

Genesis 28:4

  Genesis 17:7-8 — Ancestral promise correspondence: The Abrahamic covenant promises Canaan to Abraham and his seed, the inherited blessing and sojourning land Isaac invokes for Jacob.

Genesis 28:5

  Genesis 29:12-14 — Narrative outcome: Jacob's reception by Laban as his sister's son completes the journey to the mother's family introduced at his departure.

Genesis 28:6

  Genesis 28:1-2 — Explicit reported repetition: Esau observes the blessing and marriage instructions already narrated, supplying the reason for his subsequent choice of another wife.

Genesis 28:8

Genesis 28:9

Genesis 28:10

Genesis 28:11

Genesis 28:12

  John 1:51 — Explicit imagery reuse: Jesus' saying repeats the image of heaven and angels ascending and descending, now upon the Son of Man; John does not repeat the word ladder.

Genesis 28:13

  Genesis 26:24 — Shared divine assurance: God had likewise identified Himself through Abraham and promised His presence to Isaac, the ancestral form of assurance now extended to Jacob.
  Genesis 12:7 — Ancestral promise correspondence: The earlier appearance to Abraham also promises land to his seed, establishing the land-gift formula now addressed to Jacob.
  Genesis 35:12 — Promise renewal: The later divine word to Jacob again names the land given to Abraham and Isaac and gives it to Jacob and his seed.

Genesis 28:14
“And your seed shall be as the sand of the earth, and shall spread toward the sea, and toward the south, and toward the north, and toward the east; and in you and in your seed all the tribes of the earth shall be blessed.
  Genesis 13:16 — Promise renewal: The earlier promise makes Abraham's seed like uncountable earth-sand, the same multitude image applied to Jacob's seed in this supplied edition.
  Genesis 12:3 — Promise renewal: The assurance of blessing for all earth's tribes continues the earlier Abrahamic blessing formula, now explicitly including Jacob and his seed.
  Genesis 26:4 — Promise renewal: The promise to Isaac likewise joins numerous offspring with blessing for all nations through his seed, continuing the ancestral promise extended here to Jacob.

Genesis 28:15
“And behold, I am with you, guarding you on every road wherever you go; and I will bring you back into this land, for I will not forsake you until I have done all that I have spoken to you.”
  Genesis 31:3 — Promise continuation: God later commands Jacob to return to his father's land and again promises to be with him, continuing the assurance of presence and return given here.
  Genesis 35:3 — Explicit retrospective acknowledgement: Jacob later recalls the God who heard him in affliction, stayed with him and preserved him on his journey, looking back on the promised protection.
  Genesis 48:15-16 — Retrospective blessing: Jacob's later blessing recalls God's lifelong nourishment and the angel's deliverance from evils, developing the protection theme without reducing it to this one journey.
  Hebrews 13:5 — Shared divine assurance: Hebrews applies God's assurance of not leaving or forsaking to its readers, sharing the non-abandonment promise form without explicitly identifying this verse as its sole source.

Genesis 28:16

  Genesis 28:12-13 — Narrative response: Jacob's recognition on awakening responds to the dream and the Lord's self-identification, rather than to a previously narrated knowledge of the site's holiness.

Genesis 28:17

Genesis 28:18

Genesis 28:19

Genesis 28:22

Genesis 29:1

Genesis 29:3

Genesis 29:4

Genesis 29:5

Genesis 29:6

Genesis 29:7

Genesis 29:8

Genesis 29:10

Genesis 29:11

  Genesis 33:4 — Family-greeting parallel: Esau later runs to meet Jacob, embraces and kisses him, and weeps, providing an emotional family greeting in a different setting without equating the people or circumstances.

Genesis 29:12

Genesis 29:13

Genesis 29:14

Genesis 29:22

Genesis 29:26

Genesis 29:27

Genesis 29:29

Genesis 29:30

Genesis 29:31

Genesis 29:32
