  Genesis 31:31 — Reported explanation: Jacob later explains that he feared Laban might take his daughters and possessions, supplying his stated reason for leaving without notice.

Genesis 31:21
And he fled, he and all that he had, and crossed the River, and set out toward the mountain of Gilead.
  Genesis 31:23 — Narrative continuation: Laban's pursuit ends in the mountain of Gilead, the destination toward which Jacob's flight is directed here.
  Genesis 31:25 — Same narrative setting: The confrontation takes place where Jacob and Laban establish their camps in the mountain region already named as Jacob's route.

Genesis 31:22
And on the third day it was told to Laban the Syrian that Jacob had fled.
  Genesis 31:20 — Narrative consequence: The third-day report follows Jacob's concealment of his flight from Laban, explaining why Laban learns of the departure afterward rather than beforehand.
  Genesis 31:23 — Narrative continuation: The report is followed by Laban gathering his kinsmen and pursuing Jacob; the distinct three-day notice and seven-day journey are not collapsed into one interval.

Genesis 31:23
And he took all his kinsmen with him, and pursued him a journey of seven days, and overtook him in the mountain of Gilead.
  Genesis 31:21 — Narrative correspondence: The pursuit overtakes Jacob in the Gilead region toward which he had fled after crossing the River.
  Genesis 31:25 — Explicit narrative repetition: The following camp description repeats that Laban overtook Jacob and locates both parties in the mountain setting.

Genesis 31:24
And God came to Laban the Syrian in a dream by night, and said to him, “Take care not to speak evil to Jacob.”
  Genesis 31:29 — Explicit retrospective repetition: Laban later reports the divine warning from the previous night, repeating the command not to speak evil to Jacob.
  Genesis 20:3-7 — Protective-dream parallel: God earlier warned Abimelech in a night dream in a patriarchal household dispute, providing another protective intervention while the persons and commands remain distinct.

Genesis 31:25
And Laban overtook Jacob. And Jacob had pitched his tent in the mountain; and Laban stationed his kinsmen in the mountain of Gilead.
  Genesis 31:23 — Narrative repetition: The camping scene follows the pursuit's arrival in Gilead, repeating the overtaking already announced at the journey's end.
  Genesis 31:26 — Narrative continuation: Laban's confrontation and accusations begin after the two parties' camps have been located in the same mountain region.

Genesis 31:26
And Laban said to Jacob, “What have you done? Why did you flee secretly, and defraud me, and lead away my daughters as captives of the sword?
  Genesis 31:20 — Narrative corroboration and limit: The narrator confirms that Jacob did not tell Laban he was fleeing, supporting the secrecy part of Laban's accusation without adopting his captive-of-the-sword description.
  Genesis 31:14-16 — Reported-perspective contrast: The wives' earlier assent to leaving stands beside Laban's allegation that Jacob led them away as captives; the contrasting accounts are preserved rather than silently merged.

Genesis 31:27
“If you had told me, I would have sent you away with gladness and music, with tambourines and lyre.
  Genesis 31:20 — Explicit retrospective accusation: Laban's complaint that the departure was concealed corresponds to the narrator's earlier description of Jacob leaving without informing him.
  Genesis 32:1 — Farewell comparison: Laban later kisses and blesses his descendants before departing, providing an actual farewell alongside his claim here about how he would have sent the family away.

Genesis 31:28
“I was not deemed worthy to kiss my children and my daughters. Now you have acted foolishly.
  Genesis 32:1 — Narrative outcome: The later morning farewell includes the kissing of sons and daughters which Laban says he was earlier denied.
  Genesis 31:17 — Family context: The departure with wives and children identifies the family members whose unannounced removal prompts Laban's complaint about a missed farewell.

Genesis 31:29
“And now my hand has power to harm you; but the God of your father said to me yesterday, ‘Take care not to speak evil to Jacob.’
  Genesis 31:24 — Explicit narrative repetition: Laban's reported warning repeats the night-dream command actually narrated earlier, including its restriction on speaking evil.
  Genesis 31:7 — Reported correspondence: Laban's acknowledgment of divine restraint corresponds to Jacob's prior claim that God did not permit him to cause harm, without making every aspect of either speech independently proven.

Genesis 31:30
“Now therefore you have gone, for you greatly longed to depart to your father’s house; but why have you stolen my gods?”
  Genesis 31:19 — Narrative explanation: The narrator has already identified Rachel as the person who stole Laban's idols, explaining the gods-related question within his broader accusation against Jacob's party.
  Genesis 31:32 — Narrative response: Jacob responds to the accusation with an offer to identify property and a severe declaration about the gods, while the narrator explicitly states his ignorance of Rachel's theft.

Genesis 31:31
And Jacob answered and said to Laban, “For I thought, ‘What if you should take your daughters from me, and all that is mine?’
  Genesis 31:20 — Reported explanation: Jacob's fear about losing the daughters and his property supplies the stated motive for the secretly concealed departure narrated earlier.
  Genesis 31:26 — Dialogue contrast: Jacob's concern that Laban might take the daughters from him answers Laban's opposing complaint that Jacob has led the daughters away as captives.

Genesis 31:32
“Identify what is yours among my possessions, and take it.” And he identified nothing with him. And Jacob said to him, “With whomever you find your gods, he shall not live before our kinsmen.” But Jacob did not know that Rachel his wife had stolen them.
  Genesis 31:19 — Dramatic irony: The narrator's earlier attribution of the theft to Rachel explains the danger of Jacob's declaration and his lack of knowledge, without making it a fulfilled death sentence.
  Genesis 31:34-35 — Narrative outcome: Rachel's concealment and Laban's unsuccessful search explain why the gods are not found under the condition Jacob has announced.

Genesis 31:33
And Laban entered and searched Leah’s dwelling, and found nothing; and he went out from Leah’s dwelling and searched Jacob’s dwelling and the dwelling of the two slave women, and found nothing. And he entered Rachel’s dwelling also.
  Genesis 31:34 — Narrative explanation: The following verse discloses where Rachel has hidden the idols, explaining the failure to discover them during the search.
  Genesis 31:37 — Narrative consequence: Jacob's later demand for evidence follows the search that found none of the disputed goods, developing his challenge to Laban's accusation.

Genesis 31:34
And Rachel had taken the idols, and put them in the camel’s saddle gear, and sat upon them.
  Genesis 31:19 — Explicit narrative continuation: The objects hidden in the saddle gear are the father's idols which Rachel had earlier stolen while Laban was shearing sheep.
  Genesis 31:35 — Narrative development: Rachel's claim that she cannot rise helps sustain the concealment beneath her; the claim about her condition remains reported speech rather than independently established medical information.

Genesis 31:35
And she said to her father, “Do not take it badly, my lord; I cannot rise before you, for the customary condition of women is upon me.” And Laban searched the whole dwelling, and did not find the idols.
  Genesis 31:34 — Narrative explanation: The idols' placement beneath Rachel supplies the immediate context for her request not to stand and the failed search of her dwelling.
  Genesis 31:33 — Narrative continuation: The search of Rachel's dwelling completes the sequence of searches begun with the other household quarters, preserving the supplied order of that search.

Genesis 31:36
And Jacob was angry, and contended with Laban; and Jacob answered and said to Laban, “What is my offence, and what is my sin, that you have pursued me
  Genesis 31:37 — Narrative development: Jacob's challenge about offence and pursuit leads directly into his demand that Laban produce discovered property before the kinsmen.
  Genesis 31:38-42 — Speech development: The angry challenge introduces Jacob's detailed account of service, hardship, wages and divine protection, all presented as his defense to Laban.

Genesis 31:37
“and searched all my belongings? What have you found of all your household goods? Set it here before my kinsmen and your kinsmen, and let them judge between us two.
  Genesis 31:33-35 — Narrative context: The failure to find the idols or disputed goods in the search supplies the context for Jacob's demand that Laban lay out actual evidence.
  Genesis 31:44 — Adjudication-and-covenant development: The dispute's appeal to judging kinsmen is followed by a covenant invoking God as witness, moving the confrontation toward an agreed boundary without deciding every property claim.

Genesis 31:38
“These twenty years I have been with you; your sheep and your goats have not miscarried, and I have not eaten the rams of your flock.
  Genesis 30:29 — Explicit retrospective correspondence: Jacob had previously appealed to Laban's knowledge of his service and the flocks' condition, the claim now expanded into twenty years of care.
  Genesis 31:41 — Explicit chronological repetition: Jacob's later summary repeats twenty years in Laban's house and divides the service into years for the daughters and sheep.

Genesis 31:39
“I brought you no animal taken by wild beasts; I myself paid for what was stolen by day and what was stolen by night.
  Exodus 22:11-12 — Later legal comparison: The later deposit law distinguishes stolen animals requiring restitution from wild-animal losses with evidence, supplying a legal comparison without making Jacob subject to later Sinai legislation or equating all the claims.
  1 Samuel 17:34-35 — Shepherding comparison: David also recounts sheep threatened by wild animals when defending his shepherding competence; Jacob's account of accountability is distinguished from David's narrated rescue actions.

Genesis 31:40
“By day I was consumed by the heat, and by night by the frost; and sleep departed from my eyes.
  2 Corinthians 11:27 — Thematic hardship parallel: Paul's catalog of labor, sleepless nights and cold provides a later hardship comparison with Jacob's heat, frost and loss of sleep, while the occupations and occasions remain distinct.
  Genesis 31:38-39 — Speech context: The preceding account of prolonged flock care and losses explains the pastoral labor whose physical hardships Jacob now describes.

Genesis 31:41
“These twenty years I have been in your house; I served you fourteen years for your two daughters, and six years among your sheep; and you defrauded me of my wages of ten ewe lambs.
  Genesis 29:20-30 — Explicit service recollection: The earlier marriage account records seven years of service and another seven after the marriages, corresponding to Jacob's retrospective fourteen years for Laban's daughters.
  Genesis 31:7 — Explicit complaint repetition: Jacob's defense repeats the earlier complaint about wages of ten lambs, preserving the present ewe-lamb wording rather than converting it to a count of ten changes.

Genesis 31:42
“Unless the God of my father Abraham and the Fear of Isaac had been with me, you would now have sent me away empty. God saw my humiliation and the toil of my hands, and rebuked you yesterday.”
  Genesis 28:15 — Promise correspondence: God's earlier assurance of presence and guarding supplies the promise context for Jacob's claim that the ancestral God prevented his being sent away empty.
  Genesis 31:24 — Explicit retrospective reference: The preceding night-dream warning to Laban is the narrated intervention to which Jacob's statement that God rebuked him yesterday refers.

Genesis 31:43
And Laban answered and said to Jacob, “The daughters are my daughters, and the sons are my sons, and the livestock are my livestock; and all that you see is mine and my daughters’. What shall I do today to these women, or to their children whom they have borne?
  Genesis 31:14-16 — Reported ownership contrast: Laban claims daughters, children and livestock as his, whereas the daughters earlier questioned their inheritance and claimed the transferred wealth for themselves and their children.
  Genesis 31:9 — Reported ownership contrast: Laban's claim to the livestock stands beside Jacob's assertion that God transferred it to him; the cross-reference preserves the competing perspectives rather than supplying a legal ruling.

Genesis 31:44
“Now therefore come, let us make a covenant, I and you, and let it be a witness between me and you.” And he said to him, “Behold, no one is with us; see, God is witness between me and you.”
  Genesis 26:28-31 — Covenant parallel: The earlier agreement with Isaac likewise combines a non-harm commitment, oath and shared meal, providing an ancestral covenant pattern rather than identical parties or provisions.
  Genesis 31:53 — Narrative fulfilment: The later appeal to the ancestral God as judge and Jacob's oath by the Fear of Isaac enact the witness-and-agreement purpose introduced here.

Genesis 31:45
And Jacob took a stone and set it up as a pillar.
  Genesis 28:18 — Shared memorial action: Jacob earlier set up a stone as a pillar after his divine encounter; the physical memorial action recurs here for a covenant witness without identifying the stones or purposes as the same.
  Joshua 24:26-27 — Witness-stone parallel: Joshua later sets up a great stone and calls it a testimony in a covenant setting, corresponding to the stone witness developed from Jacob's pillar.

Genesis 31:46
And Jacob said to his kinsmen, “Gather stones.” And they gathered stones and made a heap, and ate and drank there beside the heap. And Laban said to him, “This heap bears witness between me and you today.”
  Genesis 31:54 — Covenant-meal correspondence: The later notice of Jacob's sacrifice and kinsmen eating and drinking continues the covenant-meal setting, without deciding whether the accounts describe separate meals or recapitulated actions.
  Genesis 26:30 — Covenant-meal parallel: Isaac's earlier covenant scene likewise includes a meal for the visiting party, providing a related act of hospitality around an agreement.

Genesis 31:47
And Laban called it Heap of Witness, but Jacob called it Witness Heap.
  Genesis 31:48-49 — Naming explanation: The following witness and watch statements explain the named memorial's covenant role, while both supplied English name forms remain intact without a new linguistic derivation.

Genesis 31:48

  Joshua 24:27 — Witness-stone parallel: Joshua's later stone is likewise declared a testimony in a covenant context, corresponding to the heap and pillar functioning as witnesses here.

Genesis 31:49
and The Watch, because he said, “May God watch between me and you, for we shall be separated one from another.
  Hebrews 4:13 — Thematic connection: Hebrews describes all things as exposed to God's sight and accountable to Him, corresponding to invoking God's watch over parties once they are separated.
  Genesis 31:50 — Covenant development: The following condition concerning treatment of the daughters states a future concern placed under the divine watch invoked here.

Genesis 31:50

  Genesis 31:49 — Covenant context: The appeal that God watch the separated parties supplies the witness context for Laban's conditions about his daughters and additional wives.
  Genesis 31:52 — Covenant comparison: The later crossing boundary extends the agreement's concern with preventing harm, alongside the household-treatment condition stated here without turning either clause into a universal marriage law.

Genesis 31:52

  Genesis 31:44 — Covenant fulfilment: The prohibition on crossing for harm supplies a concrete boundary to the covenant that Laban proposed between the two parties.
  Genesis 31:48 — Explicit memorial reference: The non-harm clause names the same heap and pillar previously declared witnesses, preserving the memorial's role in the boundary agreement.

Genesis 31:53

  Genesis 31:42 — Shared ancestral invocation: Jacob's earlier defense invokes Abraham's God and the Fear of Isaac, titles recalled in the covenant oath without independently resolving every ancestral-God formulation in the dialogue.
  Genesis 31:44 — Covenant fulfilment: The appeal to divine judgment and Jacob's oath enact the agreement's earlier invocation of God as witness between the parties.

Genesis 31:54
And Jacob offered a sacrifice in the mountain, and called his kinsmen; and they ate and drank, and slept in the mountain.
  Genesis 26:30 — Covenant-meal parallel: Isaac's covenant scene also contains a shared feast, corresponding to Jacob's meal with his kinsmen while preserving the distinct participants and sacrificial detail.
  Genesis 32:1 — Narrative continuation: The kinsmen's eating, drinking and sleeping are followed by Laban's morning farewell and departure; the actual next unit in this edition is Genesis 32:1.

Genesis 32:1
And Laban rose in the morning, and kissed his sons and his daughters, and blessed them; and Laban turned back and departed to his own place.
  Genesis 31:28 — Narrative outcome: Laban's kissing of his descendants answers the earlier complaint that the secret departure denied him that farewell.

Genesis 32:2

  Genesis 28:12 — Divine-encounter parallel: Jacob's earlier dream involved angels of God on the ladder, providing a preceding angelic encounter on the journey now continuing homeward without identifying the angels individually.

  Psalms 90:11 — Thematic connection: The psalm speaks of angels commanded to guard a person on his ways, corresponding to angelic presence along Jacob's return journey without claiming the psalm explicitly names him.

Genesis 32:3

  Genesis 32:2 — Naming context: Jacob's naming of Encampments follows his sight of God's camp and the angels' meeting, supplying the narrated reason for the place name.
  Genesis 28:17-19 — Divine-encounter naming parallel: Jacob earlier recognized a place as God's house and named it after his encounter, paralleling the naming of a place associated with God's camp here.

Genesis 32:4

  Genesis 36:8 — Same person and region: The later statement explicitly identifies Esau as Edom and locates him in Seir, matching the brother and region to which Jacob sends messengers.

Genesis 32:5

  Genesis 31:41 — Explicit retrospective context: Jacob's statement that he has stayed with Laban corresponds to the completed twenty-year service period he had just described to Laban.

Genesis 32:6
“‘And I have oxen and donkeys and sheep, and male and female slaves; and I have sent to tell my lord Esau, that your servant may find grace in your sight.’”
  Genesis 30:43 — Narrative correspondence: The earlier wealth summary names livestock, slaves, camels and donkeys, providing the prosperity background for the possessions Jacob now reports to Esau.

Genesis 32:7

Genesis 32:8

  Genesis 32:9 — Narrative explanation: Jacob's stated hope that one camp might escape explains the division into two camps made under fear here.
  Genesis 33:1-2 — Narrative comparison: Jacob later arranges the children with the women as Esau approaches, another protective household arrangement whose specific grouping differs from the two camps here.

Genesis 32:9

  Genesis 32:8 — Narrative explanation: The previous division of people and livestock supplies the two camps on which Jacob's escape strategy depends.
  Genesis 33:4 — Narrative contrast: Esau's eventual embrace and shared weeping contrast with the attack scenario Jacob fears; the fear is not turned into an event that actually occurred.

Genesis 32:10

  Genesis 31:3 — Explicit retrospective reference: Jacob's prayer recalls the command to return to his family land, while preserving his prayer's own hurry-and-good wording beside the earlier return-and-presence statement.
  Genesis 28:15 — Promise context: The earlier assurance that God would guard Jacob and return him to the land supplies the promise background for his appeal to the ancestral God.

Genesis 32:11

  Genesis 28:20-22 — Vow-and-gratitude correspondence: The earlier vow requested guarding, food, clothing and safe return; Jacob's present prayer acknowledges the righteousness and truth shown to him in his enlarged household.

Genesis 32:12

Genesis 32:13

  Genesis 28:14 — Promise recollection: The earlier promise compares Jacob's seed with the sand of the earth, corresponding to the multitude image recalled here while retaining this prayer's sand-of-the-sea wording.

Genesis 32:22

Genesis 32:23

Genesis 32:28

Genesis 32:29

  Genesis 35:10 — Explicit naming repetition: God later repeats that Jacob will no longer be called Jacob but Israel, corresponding to the name-change statement made in the wrestling scene.
  Hosea 12:4-5 — Explicit retrospective interpretation: The explanation of strength with God corresponds to Hosea's account of prevailing with God and an angel; the texts' differing descriptions remain visible without a forced identity resolution.

Genesis 32:30

  Judges 13:17-18 — Divine-encounter dialogue parallel: Manoah likewise asks the Lord's messenger's name and receives a question about why he asks, repeating the name-question pattern while preserving the distinct speakers and encounter.
  Genesis 32:27 — Narrative fulfilment: The blessing given here supplies the outcome Jacob had demanded before releasing the wrestling figure.

Genesis 32:31
And Jacob called the name of that place Form of God, “For I have seen God face to face, and my life has been preserved.”
  Genesis 16:13 — Divine-encounter naming parallel: Hagar's naming of the Lord as the God who looked upon her likewise associates a divine encounter with seeing, while Jacob names a place and makes his own face-to-face claim.
  Exodus 33:20 — Theological comparison: God's later statement to Moses that no man can see His face and live is placed beside Jacob's preserved-life claim; the comparison does not supply an independent reconciliation of the passages.
  Genesis 33:10 — Shared seeing-and-face imagery: Jacob later compares seeing Esau's face with seeing God's face when seeking acceptance, reusing face-seeing language without identifying Esau as God.

Genesis 32:32
And the sun rose upon him as he passed Form of God; and he limped because of his thigh.
  Genesis 32:26 — Narrative consequence: The limp at sunrise follows the numbing of Jacob's thigh during the struggle, preserving the injury's supplied wording and narrated effect.
  Genesis 32:27 — Narrative time connection: The sunrise follows the earlier demand for release because dawn had risen, linking the encounter's close with the time marker rather than adding an exact duration.

Genesis 32:33
Therefore the sons of Israel do not eat the sinew that became numb, which is upon the broad part of the thigh, to this day; for he touched the broad part of Jacob’s thigh, the sinew, and it became numb.
  Genesis 32:26 — Explicit etiological recollection: The food-custom explanation repeats the touching and numbing of the thigh during the struggle as its stated narrative reason.
  Genesis 32:32 — Narrative consequence: The preceding report that Jacob limped supplies the bodily aftermath beside the custom's explanation, without adding a claim that the custom originated in later Sinai legislation.

Genesis 33:1
And Jacob lifted up his eyes and saw, and behold, Esau his brother was coming, and four hundred men with him. And Jacob divided the children among Leah and Rachel and the two slave women.
  Genesis 32:7 — Narrative report and encounter: The messengers report Esau approaching with four hundred men; Jacob now sees that same company arriving, bringing the earlier report into the narrated encounter.
  Genesis 32:8-9 — Narrative protective arrangement: Jacob earlier divides his company because he fears an attack; his present division of the children among their mothers continues that protective preparation for meeting Esau.

Genesis 33:2
And he put the two slave women and their sons first, and Leah and her children behind, and Rachel and Joseph last.
  Genesis 32:23 — Household continuity: The earlier crossing names Jacob's two wives, two slave women and eleven children; the present procession arranges those same household groups before the meeting.
  Genesis 33:6-7 — Same-chapter arrangement and enactment: The slave women, Leah, and finally Rachel with Joseph approach in the order Jacob has just assigned, turning the stated arrangement into the family's actual procession.

Genesis 33:3
And he himself went before them, and bowed down to the earth seven times, until he drew near to his brother.
  Genesis 27:29 — Narrative reversal: Isaac's blessing speaks of Jacob's brothers bowing to him; in this meeting Jacob himself bows repeatedly before Esau, creating a visible reversal of the blessing's posture.
  Genesis 32:5 — Narrative enacted deference: Jacob's message calls Esau his lord and himself Esau's servant; going ahead and bowing to the ground expresses that announced deference through action.

Genesis 33:4
And Esau ran to meet him, and embraced and kissed him, and fell upon his neck; and they both wept.
  Genesis 27:41 — Narrative reconciliation contrast: Esau's embrace and shared tears contrast with his earlier intention to kill Jacob over the blessing; the meeting presents reconciliation where Jacob had faced a threat.
  Genesis 45:14-15 — Narrative reunion parallel: Joseph and Benjamin later weep upon one another's necks and Joseph kisses his brothers, repeating the bodily gestures and tears of reunited brothers seen here.
  Luke 15:20 — Reunion imagery parallel: The father in Jesus' parable runs, falls upon his returning son's neck and kisses him, closely paralleling Esau's welcoming gestures without identifying the two stories as the same event.

Genesis 33:5
And he lifted up his eyes and saw the women and the children, and said, “Who are these with you?” And he said, “The children with whom God has shown mercy to your servant.”
  Genesis 48:8-9 — Repeated family question and answer: Israel later asks who Joseph's children are, and Joseph answers that God gave them to him; Jacob here likewise identifies the children by acknowledging God's generosity.
  Genesis 30:22-23 — Narrative divine gift of children: Rachel's conception of Joseph is explicitly attributed to God remembering and hearing her, providing a particular earlier instance of the divine mercy Jacob acknowledges concerning his children.

Genesis 33:6
And the slave women and their children drew near, and bowed down.
  Genesis 33:2 — Same-chapter procession order: The slave women and their children approach first, implementing the first position Jacob assigned to them in the household procession.

Genesis 33:7
And Leah and her children drew near, and bowed down; and afterward Rachel and Joseph drew near, and bowed down.
  Genesis 33:2 — Same-chapter procession order: Leah's group and then Rachel with Joseph approach in the sequence specified earlier, completing the arrangement of the family before Esau.
  Genesis 33:6 — Same-chapter repeated gesture: Leah's group and Rachel with Joseph repeat the bowing already performed by the slave women and their children, extending the act of respect across the household groups.

Genesis 33:8
And he said, “What do you mean by all these companies which I met?” And he said, “That your servant may find grace in your sight, my lord.”
  Genesis 32:17-21 — Narrative preparation explained: Jacob's explanation of the companies identifies the purpose of the spaced herds and repeated gift messages: to find acceptance when he meets Esau.
  Genesis 32:6 — Repeated request for favour: The earlier message asks that Jacob find grace in Esau's sight; Jacob repeats that stated aim when Esau asks about the companies he encountered.

Genesis 33:9
And Esau said, “I have much, brother; let what is yours be yours.”
  Genesis 33:11 — Same-chapter refusal and acceptance: Esau initially declines because he has much, but after Jacob urges him he takes the gift; the paired verses show the negotiation's change from refusal to acceptance.
  Genesis 36:6-7 — Narrative possessions comparison: Esau's declaration that he has much is followed later by a report that the brothers' possessions are too extensive for their households to live together.

Genesis 33:10
And Jacob said, “If I have found grace in your sight, receive the gifts from my hands; for I have seen your face as one might see the face of God, and you will accept me.
  Genesis 32:21 — Narrative intention and fulfilment: Jacob earlier hopes that gifts will appease Esau before he sees his face; he now speaks of seeing that face and receiving acceptance while pressing the gifts upon him.
  Genesis 32:31 — Divine-face imagery comparison: Jacob has just named a place after seeing God face to face; he now uses an explicit comparison with seeing God's face to describe Esau's acceptance, without identifying Esau as God.

Genesis 33:11
“Take my blessings which I have brought to you, for God has shown mercy to me, and I have all things.” And he urged him, and he took them.
  Genesis 32:14-16 — Narrative gift identification: The goats, sheep, camels, cattle and donkeys selected earlier identify the material gift Jacob now urges Esau to receive as a blessing.
  Genesis 33:9 — Same-chapter gift negotiation: Jacob's insistence overcomes Esau's earlier refusal of the property, and the statement that Esau took it closes that exchange.

Genesis 33:12
And he said, “Let us set out and go straight ahead.”
  Genesis 33:13-14 — Same-chapter proposal and answer: Esau proposes that they travel onward together; Jacob's answer explains the vulnerable children and livestock and requests a slower, separate pace.

Genesis 33:13
And he said to him, “My lord knows that the children are tender, and the sheep and cows under my care are nursing; if therefore I drive them hard for one day, all the livestock will die.
  Proverbs 12:10 — Ethical animal-care parallel: Jacob refuses to overdrive nursing animals because they could die; Proverbs likewise associates righteousness with compassion for the lives of one's animals.
  Isaiah 40:11 — Pastoral care imagery: Jacob adjusts travel for vulnerable young and nursing livestock; Isaiah's shepherd image similarly singles out lambs and animals with young for protective care.

Genesis 33:14
“Let my lord go before his servant; and I will make my way slowly, according to the journey before me and the children’s pace, until I come to my lord in Seir.”
  Genesis 32:4 — Narrative destination continuity: Seir is already identified as Esau's country when Jacob sends messengers; Jacob's proposed destination therefore corresponds to the region previously associated with his brother.
  Genesis 33:16-17 — Same-chapter subsequent routes: Esau proceeds toward Seir while Jacob next journeys to Booths; these recorded routes follow the travel discussion without supplying an unreported later visit by Jacob to Seir.

Genesis 33:15
And Esau said, “Let me leave with you some of the people who are with me.” And he said, “Why this? It is enough that I have found grace in your sight, my lord.”
  Genesis 32:12 — Narrative change in encounter: Jacob had feared Esau would strike him down with the mothers and children; Esau now offers to leave some of his company with him, contrasting an offer of assistance with Jacob's earlier fear.
  Genesis 33:10-11 — Same-chapter acceptance motif: Jacob's answer that Esau's favour is enough follows his appeal for acceptance and the accepted gift, continuing the meeting's repeated concern with finding grace in his brother's sight.

Genesis 33:16
And Esau returned that day on his way to Seir.
  Genesis 36:8 — Geographical identification: The later family account explicitly identifies Seir as Esau's dwelling and Esau as Edom, explaining the destination of his return journey here.
  Genesis 32:4 — Narrative return route: Jacob's earlier messengers went to Esau in Seir; Esau's present return toward Seir closes the visit from that previously identified region.

Genesis 33:17
And Jacob journeyed to Booths, and made houses for himself there, and made booths for his livestock; therefore he called the name of that place Booths.
  Genesis 33:13-14 — Narrative livestock-care association: Jacob's concern about overdriving nursing livestock and his later provision of booths both describe care for the herds; the text does not state why he chose to settle at Booths.

Genesis 33:18
And Jacob came to Salem, a city of Shechem, which is in the land of Canaan, when he came from Mesopotamia of Syria; and he camped before the city.
  Genesis 28:15 — Promise and return: God promised to bring Jacob back into this land; the arrival account explicitly places him in Canaan after his journey from Mesopotamia of Syria.
  Genesis 35:9 — Narrative return recalled: The later appearance at Luz again refers to Jacob's return from Mesopotamia of Syria, recalling the same return journey whose Canaan arrival is narrated here.

Genesis 33:19
And he bought the portion of the field where he had pitched his tent from Hamor the father of Shechem for a hundred lambs.
  Joshua 24:32 — Later land-purchase recollection: Joshua recalls Joseph's burial in the Shechem field Jacob acquired for one hundred ewe lambs, directly connecting the later burial possession with this purchase while retaining its supplied seller description.
  Genesis 23:16-18 — Patriarchal land-purchase parallel: Abraham's payment to Ephron establishes his possession of a field, while Jacob pays Hamor for his camping field; both accounts describe acquired property with an explicit purchase price.

Genesis 33:20
And he set up an altar there, and called upon the God of Israel.
  Genesis 12:6-7 — Patriarchal worship at Shechem: Abram earlier reaches Shechem and builds an altar after God's appearance; Jacob likewise establishes an altar in the Shechem setting following his arrival in Canaan.
  Genesis 28:20-22 — Vow and worship on return: Jacob's earlier vow links God's protection and safe return with acknowledging the Lord as his God; after returning he now calls upon the God of Israel at an altar.
  Genesis 35:1 — Narrative altar sequence: The altar near Shechem is followed by God's command to build an altar at Bethel, marking successive places of Jacob's worship after his return.

Genesis 34:1
And Dinah the daughter of Leah, whom she bore to Jacob, went out to see the daughters of the people of the land.
  Genesis 30:21 — Family identity: The birth account names Dinah as Leah's daughter; this episode opens by identifying that same daughter of Leah and Jacob.
  Genesis 46:15 — Genealogical identity: The migration genealogy again names Dinah alongside Leah's sons by Jacob, preserving the family relationship by which this narrative introduces her.

Genesis 34:2
And Shechem the Horite, son of Hamor and ruler of the land, saw her, and seized her, and lay with her, and violated her.
  2 Samuel 13:14 — Narrative violence parallel: Amnon overpowers, violates and lies with Tamar; Shechem likewise seizes, lies with and violates Dinah, forming a parallel of coercive harm without importing Tamar's dialogue into Dinah's story.
  Genesis 34:25-26 — Same-chapter violent aftermath: The later attack kills Shechem and removes Dinah from his house, connecting the brothers' violent retaliation with the assault introduced here without endorsing that retaliation.

Genesis 34:3
And he was attached to the soul of Dinah, Jacob’s daughter, and loved the maiden, and spoke to the maiden according to her heart.
  Genesis 34:8 — Same-chapter desire repeated: Hamor's later claim that Shechem has set his soul upon Dinah repeats the narrator's description of his attachment and carries it into the marriage negotiation.
  2 Samuel 13:15 — Narrative aftermath contrast: Shechem remains attached to Dinah after violating her, whereas Amnon turns to hatred after violating Tamar; the contrast concerns the men's responses and does not excuse either assault.

Genesis 34:4
And Shechem spoke to Hamor his father, saying, “Take this young woman for me to be my wife.”
  Genesis 34:8 — Same-chapter request carried forward: Shechem asks his father to obtain Dinah as his wife; Hamor subsequently conveys that request to Jacob's family on his son's behalf.
  Genesis 34:12 — Same-chapter marriage request repeated: Shechem later repeats his demand for the young woman as wife while offering a large marriage payment, expanding the request first addressed to his father.

Genesis 34:5
And Jacob heard that the son of Hamor had defiled Dinah his daughter; but his sons were with his livestock in the plain, and Jacob kept silence until they came.
  Genesis 34:7 — Same-chapter delayed response: Jacob remains silent while his sons are with the livestock; their return from the plain and distress supply the family response for which he had waited.
  Genesis 34:30 — Same-chapter contrasting responses: Jacob's initial silence while awaiting his sons contrasts with his later explicit rebuke of Simeon and Levi after their attack and its danger to his household.

Genesis 34:6
And Hamor the father of Shechem went out to Jacob to speak with him.
  Genesis 34:8-10 — Same-chapter negotiation opened: Hamor's visit to speak with Jacob leads into his proposal of marriage, intermarriage and settlement, specifying the business for which he comes.

Genesis 34:7
And Jacob’s sons came from the plain; and when they heard, the men were deeply distressed, and were exceedingly grieved, because he had done a disgraceful thing in Israel by lying with Jacob’s daughter; and such a thing must not be.
  2 Samuel 13:12-13 — Verbal and ethical parallel: Tamar protests that such violation must not be done in Israel and speaks of disgrace; Jacob's sons likewise call the assault a disgraceful thing in Israel that must not be.
  Genesis 34:25 — Same-chapter grief and retaliation: The sons' intense distress precedes Simeon and Levi's armed attack; the connection follows the narrative's movement from outrage to violent action without making that action a command.

Genesis 34:8
And Hamor spoke to them, saying, “Shechem my son has set his soul upon your daughter; therefore give her to him to be his wife.
  Genesis 34:3-4 — Same-chapter report of desire: Hamor's description of Shechem's attachment and desired marriage communicates the desire and request already narrated from Shechem's side.
  Genesis 34:11-12 — Same-chapter marriage negotiation: Shechem follows his father's appeal by offering whatever payment the family names, continuing the effort to obtain Dinah as his wife.

Genesis 34:9
“Intermarry with us; give your daughters to us, and take our daughters for your sons.
  Deuteronomy 7:3-4 — Later legal contrast: Hamor proposes giving and taking daughters in intermarriage; Israel's later law forbids that exchange with the listed nations and explains the danger of turning to other gods.
  Genesis 34:16 — Same-chapter proposal echoed: The sons' deceptive answer repeats Hamor's proposed exchange of daughters and wives, incorporating his terms into their condition for becoming one family.

Genesis 34:10
“And live among us; behold, the land is broad before you. Live and trade in it, and acquire possessions in it.”
  Genesis 34:21 — Same-chapter public repetition: The offer to settle, trade and use the broad land is repeated to the city men when Hamor and Shechem seek public agreement.
  Genesis 34:23 — Same-chapter economic motive exposed: Hamor's invitation to acquire possessions is later accompanied by the claim that Jacob's livestock and goods will become the townsmen's, exposing an economic interest behind the settlement proposal.

Genesis 34:11
And Shechem said to her father and to her brothers, “Let me find grace in your sight, and whatever you say, we will give.
  Genesis 34:12 — Same-chapter payment promise specified: Shechem's promise to give whatever the family says is immediately specified as willingness to meet an exceedingly great marriage payment for Dinah.

Genesis 34:12
“Make the marriage payment exceedingly great, and I will give according to whatever you say to me; and give me this young woman to be my wife.”
  Exodus 22:15-16 — Marriage-payment legal comparison: Exodus addresses payment and the father's right to refuse marriage after a man deceives and lies with an unbetrothed virgin; Shechem offers payment after violating Dinah, a different narrated circumstance.
  Genesis 34:4 — Same-chapter repeated marriage demand: The offer of a large payment repeats Shechem's earlier request that his father obtain this young woman as his wife, now made directly to her family.

Genesis 34:13
And Jacob’s sons answered Shechem and Hamor his father with deceit, and spoke to them, because they had defiled Dinah their sister.
  Genesis 27:35 — Family deception motif: Isaac had described Jacob's taking of the blessing as deceit; Jacob's own sons now answer with deceit, repeating the family narrative's explicit description across different actions.
  Genesis 34:25-26 — Same-chapter deceit disclosed by action: The later killing of the circumcised men and removal of Dinah reveal that the apparent agreement to live together was deceptive rather than a sincere settlement promise.

Genesis 34:14
And Simeon and Levi, Dinah’s brothers, the sons of Leah, said to them, “We cannot do this thing, to give our sister to a man who is uncircumcised; for it is a reproach to us.
  Genesis 17:14 — Covenant boundary invoked: The earlier covenant marks uncircumcision as a breach carrying exclusion; Dinah's brothers invoke uncircumcision as a barrier to marriage, without the covenant text authorizing their deceit or killing.
  Genesis 29:33-34 — Family identity: The birth accounts identify Simeon and Levi as Leah's sons, explaining the shared maternal relationship emphasized when they speak as Dinah's brothers.

Genesis 34:15
“On this condition we will become like you and live among you: if you also become as we are, with every male among you circumcised.
  Genesis 17:10-11 — Covenant sign appropriated: God's covenant requires circumcision of every male as its sign; the sons use that same bodily requirement as a condition for living together in their deceptive negotiation.
  Genesis 34:22 — Same-chapter condition repeated: Hamor and Shechem relay the requirement that every male be circumcised to the city, repeating the condition first stated by Jacob's sons.

Genesis 34:16
“And we will give our daughters to you, and take wives for ourselves from your daughters; and we will live among you, and be as one family.
  Genesis 34:9 — Same-chapter proposal echoed: The offered exchange of daughters and wives echoes Hamor's earlier intermarriage proposal, now made conditional on the city's circumcision.
  Genesis 34:21 — Same-chapter public restatement: Hamor and Shechem present the mutual marriage arrangement to their citizens, carrying the negotiated promise into their public appeal.

Genesis 34:17
“But if you will not listen to us to be circumcised, we will take our daughters and depart.”
  Genesis 34:26 — Same-chapter departure contrast: The sons threaten to take the daughters and depart if the condition is refused; the outcome instead removes Dinah by force after the city has accepted it.

Genesis 34:18
And their words pleased Hamor and Shechem the son of Hamor.
  Genesis 34:24 — Same-chapter assent and implementation: The words first please Hamor and Shechem, and the later city response shows their acceptance being extended into circumcision of the men.

Genesis 34:19
And the young man did not delay to do this thing, for he was intent upon Jacob’s daughter; and he was the most honoured of all in his father’s house.
  Genesis 34:3 — Same-chapter persistent attachment: The reason given for Shechem's haste, his intent upon Jacob's daughter, continues the attachment already described after his assault.

Genesis 34:20
And Hamor and Shechem his son came to the gate of their city, and spoke to the men of their city, saying,
  Genesis 23:10 — Public negotiation setting: Ephron answers Abraham before the city community, while Hamor and Shechem address their men at the gate; both negotiations seek a public hearing among the city's people.
  Genesis 34:24 — Same-chapter audience and response: Those addressed at the city gate later listen to Hamor and Shechem and undergo circumcision, providing the public response to this appeal.

Genesis 34:21
“These men are peaceable with us; let them live in the land and trade in it, for behold, the land is broad before them. We will take their daughters to be our wives, and give our daughters to them.
  Genesis 34:9-10 — Same-chapter settlement offer repeated: The speech to the city repeats the earlier offer of intermarriage, residence and trading in a broad land, transferring the proposal to the other negotiating audience.
  Genesis 34:27-29 — Same-chapter peace and plunder contrast: The public description of Jacob's people as peaceable contrasts with the sons' subsequent plundering of the city, livestock, goods and households.

Genesis 34:22
“Only on this condition will the men become like us, to live with us and be one people: that every male among us be circumcised, even as they are circumcised.
  Genesis 34:15-16 — Same-chapter conditional agreement repeated: The city's speakers repeat the sons' condition of circumcision and the promised result of living together as one people.
  Genesis 34:24 — Same-chapter condition accepted: The requirement that every male be circumcised is followed by the recorded circumcision of every male who listens to the city's leaders.

Genesis 34:23
“And shall not their livestock, and their possessions, and their four-footed beasts be ours? Only let us become like them, and they will live with us.”
  Genesis 34:28-29 — Same-chapter reversal of acquisition: The leaders anticipate obtaining Jacob's livestock and possessions, but the sons of Jacob instead seize the city's animals, household goods and people.
  Genesis 34:10 — Same-chapter economic proposal compared: The earlier invitation promises Jacob's family room to acquire possessions; the later appeal to their own townsmen anticipates acquiring Jacob's property, showing the proposal from the other side.

Genesis 34:24
And all who went out through the gate of their city listened to Hamor and Shechem his son; and they were circumcised in the flesh of their foreskin, every male.
  Genesis 17:23 — Circumcision narrative comparison: Abraham circumcises every male of his household in response to God's covenant; the city likewise circumcises its males, but here in response to the deceptive marriage negotiation.
  Genesis 34:25 — Same-chapter compliance and vulnerability: The recorded circumcision precedes the attack on the third day while the men are in pain, making their compliance part of the narrative explanation for their vulnerability.

Genesis 34:25
And on the third day, when they were in pain, two of Jacob’s sons, Simeon and Levi, Dinah’s brothers, each took his sword, and entered the city unopposed, and slew every male.
  Genesis 49:5-7 — Later condemnation of violence: Jacob's later words name Simeon and Levi together, condemn their wrath and killing, and announce their division in Israel, providing a later judgment on the brothers' violence.
  Genesis 34:13 — Same-chapter deception and attack: The earlier description of the sons' answer as deceit explains why the apparent agreement ends with Simeon and Levi attacking men made vulnerable by its condition.

Genesis 34:26
And they slew Hamor and Shechem his son with the edge of the sword, and took Dinah from Shechem’s house, and went out.
  Genesis 34:2 — Same-chapter assault and aftermath: The men kill Shechem and take Dinah from his house, returning the account to the woman and assailant named at the episode's opening.
  Genesis 49:6 — Later condemnation of killing: Jacob later refuses association with the brothers' council because in their wrath they slew men; their killing of Hamor and Shechem belongs to the violence narrated here.

Genesis 34:27
And Jacob’s sons came upon the slain, and plundered the city in which they had defiled Dinah their sister.
  Genesis 34:13 — Same-chapter stated retaliation motive: Both verses explain the brothers' actions by referring to Dinah's defilement; the recurring explanation connects the deceitful answer and the later plunder without approving them.
  Genesis 34:23 — Same-chapter acquisition reversed: The city's expectation that Jacob's possessions would become theirs is overturned when Jacob's sons plunder that very city.

Genesis 34:28
And they took their sheep and their oxen and their donkeys, whatever was in the city and whatever was in the plain.
  Genesis 34:23 — Same-chapter livestock reversal: The townsmen hoped to acquire Jacob's livestock, but the sons take the city's sheep, oxen and donkeys, reversing the anticipated direction of acquisition.
  Genesis 34:10 — Same-chapter possession contrast: Hamor offers Jacob room to trade and acquire possessions in the land; the sons instead obtain the city's livestock through violent plunder, a different means from the proposed settlement.

Genesis 34:29
And they took captive all their people, and all their household goods, and their wives; and they plundered whatever was in the city and whatever was in the houses.
  Genesis 34:16 — Same-chapter household union contrasted: The promise to exchange wives and become one family ends instead in seizure of the city's people and wives, contrasting negotiated union with captivity.
  Genesis 34:23 — Same-chapter possession reversal: The expectation of obtaining Jacob's possessions is reversed when Jacob's sons seize the city's household goods and people as part of their plunder.

Genesis 34:30
And Jacob said to Simeon and Levi, “You have made me hated, so that I am offensive to all who live in the land, among the Canaanites and the Perizzites. And I am few in number; and they shall gather against me and cut me down, and I and my household shall be destroyed.”
  Genesis 35:5 — Feared pursuit and protection: Jacob fears that the surrounding inhabitants will gather to destroy his household; the departure account reports divine fear upon the nearby cities and no pursuit.
  Genesis 49:5-7 — Later judgment on the brothers: Jacob's immediate rebuke names Simeon and Levi and the danger their action brings; his final words again name them and condemn their violent wrath.

Genesis 34:31
And they said, “But should they treat our sister as a prostitute?”
  Genesis 34:7 — Same-chapter outrage restated: The closing question about treating their sister as a prostitute restates the brothers' earlier insistence that the violation was a disgrace that must not occur in Israel.
  Genesis 49:6-7 — Justification and later condemnation: The brothers defend their action by appealing to their sister's treatment; Jacob's later condemnation of their wrath preserves a critical judgment alongside that defense.

Genesis 35:1
And God said to Jacob, “Arise, go up to the place Bethel, and live there; and make an altar there to the God who appeared to you when you were fleeing from the presence of Esau your brother.”
  Genesis 28:12-15 — Earlier appearance recalled: God's instruction identifies Bethel by the appearance during Jacob's flight, referring back to the ladder vision and promises given on that earlier journey.
  Genesis 31:13 — Repeated divine instruction: God previously recalls the anointed pillar and vow when commanding Jacob to return; the present instruction directs him to the place of that remembered encounter and worship.

Genesis 35:2
And Jacob said to his household and to all who were with him, “Put away from among you the foreign gods which are with you, and purify yourselves, and change your garments.
  Genesis 31:19 — Household idols context: Rachel earlier steals her father's idols; Jacob now orders the removal of foreign gods from the household, without the narrative identifying every object or its owner.
  Joshua 24:23 — Repeated rejection of foreign gods: Joshua likewise commands the removal of foreign gods and direction of the heart to the God of Israel, paralleling Jacob's command before renewed household worship.
  Exodus 19:10-11 — Ritual preparation parallel: Jacob combines purification and changed garments before going to Bethel; Israel later purifies and washes garments before the Lord's appearance at Sinai.

Genesis 35:3
“And let us arise and go up to Bethel, and make an altar there to the God who heard me in the day of affliction, who was with me and preserved me on the way which I went.”
  Genesis 28:20-22 — Vow and worship on return: Jacob's vow asks divine protection on his journey and connects return with worship; he now recalls that protection while leading the household to make an altar.
  Genesis 32:12 — Prayer and deliverance recalled: Jacob had prayed for deliverance from Esau's hand; his present acknowledgment that God heard him in affliction follows the safe meeting with the brother he feared.
  Genesis 35:1 — Same-chapter command and response: Jacob's proposal to go to Bethel and make an altar carries God's preceding command into instructions for the household.

Genesis 35:4
And they gave to Jacob the foreign gods which were in their hands, and the earrings which were in their ears; and Jacob hid them under the terebinth in Shechem, and destroyed them to this day.
  Genesis 35:2 — Same-chapter command enacted: The household hands over its foreign gods after Jacob commands their removal, making the disposal near Shechem the narrated response to his instruction.
  Genesis 31:19 — Idols removed after earlier theft: Rachel's earlier taking of Laban's idols supplies household context for the later surrender of foreign gods; the comparison does not identify all the surrendered objects as hers.

Genesis 35:5
And Israel departed from Shechem; and the fear of God fell upon the cities around them, and they did not pursue the sons of Israel.
  Genesis 34:30 — Feared attack and absence of pursuit: Jacob fears that the nearby inhabitants will gather to destroy his household; the departure account instead reports fear upon those cities and no pursuit.
  Genesis 28:15 — Promise of protection on the road: God promised to guard Jacob wherever he went; the cities' failure to pursue after divine fear falls upon them presents particular protection during the return journey.

Genesis 35:6
And Jacob came to Luz, which is in the land of Canaan, which is Bethel, he and all the people who were with him.
  Genesis 28:19 — Place-name comparison: The earlier naming account calls the place House of God and gives Oulamlous as the city's former name; this return account calls it Luz and Bethel, preserving the edition's different supplied forms.
  Genesis 48:3 — Later recollection of the place: Jacob later tells Joseph that God appeared to him at Luz in Canaan, recalling the place identified as Luz and Bethel in this arrival account.

Genesis 35:7
And he built an altar there, and called the name of the place Bethel; for there God appeared to him when he was fleeing from the presence of Esau his brother.
  Genesis 28:12-19 — Earlier vision commemorated: Jacob builds the altar where God appeared during his flight from Esau, recalling the earlier vision and naming of the place after waking there.
  Genesis 12:7-8 — Patriarchal altar parallel: Abram builds an altar after God's appearance and later worships near Bethel; Jacob likewise builds an altar connected with divine appearance in the Bethel setting.

Genesis 35:8
And Deborah, Rebekah’s nurse, died below Bethel under the oak; and Jacob called its name Oak of Mourning.
  Genesis 35:19-20 — Same-chapter death and memorial sequence: Deborah's death is marked by the named Oak of Mourning, while Rachel's later death is marked by a tomb pillar, linking distinct losses with remembered places along the journey.

Genesis 35:9
And God appeared to Jacob again in Luz, when he came from Mesopotamia of Syria; and God blessed him.
  Genesis 48:3-4 — Later recollection of blessing: Jacob later recounts God's appearance and blessing at Luz and repeats the promises of multiplication and land, explicitly recalling the encounter introduced here.
  Genesis 31:13 — Earlier appearance remembered: The command to leave Mesopotamia recalls the God who appeared at the place of the pillar and vow; the return narrative now reports another appearance and blessing at Luz.

Genesis 35:10
And God said to him, “Your name is Jacob; you shall no longer be called Jacob, but Israel shall be your name.”
  Genesis 32:29 — Name declaration repeated: The name Israel was first pronounced during the wrestling encounter; God now repeats the replacement of Jacob's name with Israel in the renewed appearance.
  Genesis 17:5 — Patriarchal renaming parallel: God changes Abram's name to Abraham in a covenant setting and now declares Jacob's name to be Israel, pairing patriarchal renaming with promises concerning descendants.

Genesis 35:11
And God said to him, “I am your God. Increase and multiply; nations and assemblies of nations shall be from you, and kings shall come from your loins.
  Genesis 17:6 — Promise to the patriarchal line: God's promise of increase, nations and kings to Abraham is reiterated to Jacob in the closely corresponding promise of nations, assemblies and kings from his descendants.
  Genesis 48:4 — Later promise recollection: Jacob later repeats the promise to increase him and make assemblies of nations, identifying the content of the blessing given in this appearance.

Genesis 35:12
“And the land which I gave to Abraham and Isaac, I have given to you; it shall be yours, and I will give this land to your seed after you.”
  Genesis 28:13 — Land promise renewed: The earlier Bethel appearance promises the land on which Jacob sleeps to him and his seed; the renewed promise again gives the land to Jacob and his descendants.
  Genesis 17:8 — Patriarchal land inheritance: God promises Canaan to Abraham and his seed as a possession; the present declaration expressly carries the land given to Abraham and Isaac forward to Jacob and his seed.
  Genesis 48:4 — Later land-promise recollection: Jacob later tells Joseph that God promised this land to him and his seed as an everlasting possession, recalling the inheritance promise spoken here.

Genesis 35:13
And God went up from him, from the place where He spoke with him.
  Genesis 17:22 — Divine departure formula: After speaking to Abraham, God goes up from him; the same narrated movement closes God's speech to Jacob at the place of the renewed promises.

Genesis 35:14
And Jacob set up a pillar in the place where He spoke with him, a pillar of stone; and he poured a drink offering upon it, and poured oil upon it.
  Genesis 28:18 — Memorial action repeated: Jacob again erects a stone pillar and pours oil upon it, repeating the actions with which he marked the place after the earlier dream.
  Genesis 35:20 — Same-chapter contrasting memorial pillars: Jacob's pillar at the divine encounter and the later pillar on Rachel's tomb both mark remembered places, while serving the distinct purposes of worship and burial remembrance.

Genesis 35:15
And Jacob called the name of the place where God spoke with him Bethel.
  Genesis 28:19 — Place naming recalled: Jacob's present naming of Bethel recalls his earlier naming of the place House of God after the dream, retaining both supplied English forms.
  Genesis 35:7 — Same-chapter place-name refrain: The chapter twice associates the name Bethel with God's appearing or speaking there, repeating the explanation around the altar and the renewed encounter.

Genesis 35:16
And Jacob departed from Bethel, and pitched his tent beyond the tower of Gader. And when he drew near to Chabratha, to enter the land of Ephrath, Rachel gave birth, and had hard labour in the birth.
  Genesis 35:19 — Same-chapter birthplace and burial setting: The birth journey approaches Ephrath, and Rachel's ensuing burial is placed on the road to Ephrath, which the account identifies as Bethlehem.
  Ruth 4:11 — Maternal and place-name association: The blessing in Ruth joins Rachel and Leah with building Israel's house and names Ephrathah and Bethlehem, connecting the mothers and locality also prominent in Rachel's final birth journey.

Genesis 35:17
And during her hard labour the midwife said to her, “Take courage, for this also is a son for you.”
  Genesis 30:24 — Desire for another son: Rachel names Joseph with the request that God add another son; the midwife's assurance during the later labour announces that this child too is a son.
  Genesis 35:24 — Same-chapter family identification: The assurance of another son is followed in the household list by Rachel's two sons, Joseph and Benjamin, identifying the family to which the newborn belongs.

Genesis 35:18
And as her soul was departing, for she was dying, she called his name Son of My Sorrow; but his father called him Benjamin.
  Genesis 30:24 — Maternal naming contrast: Rachel names Joseph while asking for another son; in the later birth she names the child Son of My Sorrow as she dies, contrasting the circumstances of her two naming utterances.
  Genesis 46:21 — Later family identification: The migration genealogy lists Benjamin's descendants under the name assigned here by his father, connecting the birth naming with the later family branch.

Genesis 35:19
And Rachel died, and was buried on the way to Ephrath, which is Bethlehem.
  Genesis 35:20 — Same-chapter burial and memorial: Jacob places a pillar on Rachel's tomb after her burial, marking the grave that the preceding verse locates on the road to Ephrath or Bethlehem.
  Matthew 2:16-18 — Later maternal lament association: Matthew places the killing of Bethlehem's children beside the quotation of Rachel weeping for her children; Genesis associates Rachel's own death and burial with the road to Bethlehem, without making this verse the quoted prophecy.

Genesis 35:20
And Jacob set up a pillar upon her tomb; this is the pillar of Rachel’s tomb to this day.
  Genesis 35:8 — Same-chapter memorial pattern: The pillar preserving Rachel's tomb and the named Oak of Mourning for Deborah each attach remembrance of a woman's death to a distinct place on Jacob's journey.
  Genesis 28:18 — Memorial marker comparison: Jacob earlier sets up a stone pillar after the dream and now places a pillar upon Rachel's grave; both mark remembered places, with their different occasions left explicit.

Genesis 35:22
And when Israel lived in that land, Reuben went and lay with Bilhah his father’s concubine; and Israel heard it, and it appeared evil in his sight. And the sons of Jacob were twelve.
  Genesis 49:3-4 — Later rebuke of Reuben: Jacob's final words accuse Reuben of going up to his father's bed and defiling the couch, directly recalling the misconduct with Bilhah narrated here.
  1 Chronicles 5:1-2 — Later genealogical consequence: Chronicles recalls Reuben's act upon his father's bed when explaining the loss of his firstborn blessing and its assignment to Joseph, supplying a later consequence of this episode.

Genesis 35:23
The sons of Leah: Reuben, Jacob’s firstborn, Simeon, Levi, Judah, Issachar, and Zebulun.
  Genesis 29:32-35 — Birth accounts of Leah's first sons: The earlier births identify Reuben, Simeon, Levi and Judah as Leah's sons in the same order that opens the household list here.
  Genesis 30:17-20 — Birth accounts completing Leah's list: Leah's fifth and sixth sons are named Issachar and Zebulun in their birth accounts, completing the six sons listed under her name here.
  Genesis 46:8-15 — Genealogical continuity: The migration genealogy enumerates the descendants of the same six sons and closes by identifying them as Leah's family, carrying this household list into the next generation.

Genesis 35:24
And the sons of Rachel: Joseph and Benjamin.
  Genesis 30:23-24 — Earlier birth and naming: The earlier account records Rachel bearing and naming Joseph; the household summary now lists him with Benjamin as her two sons.
  Genesis 46:19 — Genealogical identity: The migration genealogy again names Joseph and Benjamin as the sons of Rachel, repeating the maternal grouping given here.

Genesis 35:25
And the sons of Bilhah, Rachel’s slave woman: Dan and Naphtali.
  Genesis 30:4-8 — Birth accounts of Bilhah's sons: The birth narratives identify Bilhah as Rachel's slave woman and record the naming of Dan and Naphtali, the same two sons listed under her name here.
  Genesis 46:23-25 — Genealogical continuity: The later genealogy lists Dan and Naphtali and identifies their branch as Bilhah's sons, preserving the maternal grouping while adding their descendants.

Genesis 35:26
And the sons of Zilpah, Leah’s slave woman: Gad and Asher. These are Jacob’s sons, who were born to him in Mesopotamia of Syria.
  Genesis 30:9-13 — Birth accounts of Zilpah's sons: The earlier narrative identifies Zilpah as Leah's slave woman and records Gad and Asher, the two sons assigned to her in the household summary.
  Genesis 46:16-18 — Genealogical continuity: The migration genealogy gives Gad's and Asher's descendants and closes by naming Zilpah as their maternal ancestor, continuing the family branch summarized here.

Genesis 35:27
And Jacob came to Isaac his father at Mamre, to the city of the plain, which is Hebron, in the land of Canaan, where Abraham and Isaac lived as strangers.
  Genesis 13:18 — Patriarchal residence recalled: Abram had settled beside the oak of Mamre in Hebron; Jacob's arrival at Isaac is placed at Mamre and Hebron where the patriarchs lived as strangers.
  Genesis 28:21 — Vow and return to the father: Jacob's earlier vow anticipates being brought safely to his father's house; the return journey now reaches Isaac at Mamre in Canaan.

Genesis 35:28
And the days of Isaac which he lived were a hundred and eighty years.
  Genesis 25:26 — Isaac's life chronology: Isaac is sixty years old when the twins are born, while this later notice gives his completed lifespan as one hundred and eighty years, joining the two explicit age markers.
  Genesis 27:1-2 — Narrative old age concluded: Isaac earlier acknowledges his old age and uncertainty about his day of death; the later account supplies his total years immediately before reporting his death.

Genesis 35:29
And his strength failed, and he died, and was added to his family, old and full of days; and Esau and Jacob his sons buried him.
  Genesis 25:8-9 — Patriarchal death and sons' burial: Abraham likewise dies old and full of days, is added to his people and is buried by two sons, paralleling Isaac's death formula and burial by Esau and Jacob.
  Genesis 49:31 — Later burial location identified: Jacob's final instructions identify the family cave as the place where Isaac and Rebekah were buried, supplying the burial setting not named in this immediate death notice.
  Genesis 27:41 — Narrative change in the brothers: Esau once planned to kill Jacob when mourning for their father arrived; the death account instead shows Esau and Jacob jointly burying Isaac.

Genesis 36:1
And these are the generations of Esau, who is Edom.
  Genesis 25:30 — Earlier naming recalled: The stew episode explicitly gives Edom as Esau's name; the genealogy opens by repeating that identification before listing his family.
  1 Chronicles 1:34-35 — Parallel ancestral genealogy: Chronicles identifies Esau and Jacob as Isaac's sons and then introduces Esau's sons, supplying the ancestral setting of the family whose generations begin here.

Genesis 36:2
And Esau took wives from the daughters of the Canaanites: Adah the daughter of Elon the Hittite, and Oholibamah the daughter of Anah the son of Zibeon the Hivite,
  Genesis 26:34-35 — Marriage-record comparison with differences: Both accounts report Esau's marriages among the peoples of Canaan, but the supplied names and ethnic descriptions differ: the earlier record names Judith and Basemath daughter of Elon the Hivite, whereas this record names Adah daughter of Elon the Hittite and Oholibamah. The comparison preserves these differences without inventing renamings.
  Genesis 28:8 — Earlier assessment of Canaanite marriages: Esau previously sees that Canaan's daughters displease Isaac; the genealogy now explicitly describes his wives as daughters of the Canaanites, recalling the family setting of that earlier assessment.

Genesis 36:3
and Basemath the daughter of Ishmael, the sister of Nebaioth.
  Genesis 28:9 — Marriage-record comparison with different names: Both records describe a wife of Esau as Ishmael's daughter and Nebaioth's sister, but the earlier passage calls her Mahalath and this one Basemath. The shared relationship is explicit; the supplied personal names are retained without an explanation for their difference.

Genesis 36:4
And Adah bore Eliphaz to Esau, and Basemath bore Reuel.
  Genesis 36:10 — Same-chapter maternal grouping repeated: The later summary again places Eliphaz under Adah and Reuel under Basemath, repeating the two mother-and-son relationships introduced here.
  1 Chronicles 1:35 — Parallel list of Esau's sons: Chronicles likewise begins Esau's sons with Eliphaz, followed by Ragouel; Genesis supplies the mothers and uses Reuel for its second name. The lists are compared without replacing their supplied forms.

Genesis 36:5
And Oholibamah bore Jeush and Jalam and Korah. These are Esau’s sons who were born to him in the land of Canaan.
  Genesis 36:14 — Same-chapter family grouping repeated: The later entry again names Jeush, Jalam and Korah as Oholibamah's sons by Esau and adds her Anah-and-Zibeon ancestry to the birth summary given here.

Genesis 36:6
And Esau took his wives and his sons and his daughters, and all the persons of his house, and all his possessions and all his livestock, and all that he had acquired and all that he had gained in the land of Canaan; and he went out from the land of Canaan, away from the presence of Jacob his brother.
  Genesis 13:11-12 — Family separation and different settlements: Lot separates from Abram and their residences diverge; Esau likewise leaves the country where his brother remains. Both narratives distinguish related households through their separate settlements, without making their destinations identical.
  Genesis 37:1 — Contrasting settlement notices: Esau's household leaves Canaan away from Jacob; the following Jacob narrative explicitly locates Jacob's continuing residence in Canaan, making the brothers' different locations clear.

Genesis 36:7
For their possessions were too great for them to live together, and the land of their sojourning could not support them because of the multitude of their possessions.
  Genesis 13:5-9 — Repeated cause of household separation: Abram and Lot cannot remain together because their possessions exceed what the land can support; the same stated difficulty of abundant livestock and insufficient land explains the separation of Esau and Jacob here.
  Genesis 33:9-11 — Earlier acknowledgment of possessions: Esau earlier says that he has much and Jacob says that he has all things; the narrator now describes their combined possessions as too great for them to remain together, expanding the earlier exchange about their abundance.

Genesis 36:8
And Esau lived in the mountain of Seir; Esau is Edom.
  Joshua 24:4 — Later recollection of Esau's inheritance: Joshua's retrospective states that God gave Mount Seir to Esau as his inheritance, recalling the mountain residence assigned to Esau in this family history.
  Deuteronomy 2:4-5 — Later territorial identification: Deuteronomy identifies the sons of Esau as dwelling in Seir and treats the mountain as their God-given possession, continuing the association between Esau and Seir stated here.

Genesis 36:9
And these are the generations of Esau, the father of Edom, in the mountain of Seir.
  Deuteronomy 2:4 — Ancestral heading and later people: This heading places Esau's generations in Mount Seir; Deuteronomy later calls the people living in Seir the sons of Esau, connecting the ancestral household with the named later population.
  Genesis 36:1 — Same-chapter repeated genealogical heading: The chapter opens with the generations of Esau who is Edom and now repeats the heading with the added description father of Edom in Mount Seir, marking a renewed section of the same family account.

Genesis 36:10
And these are the names of Esau’s sons: Eliphaz the son of Adah, Esau’s wife, and Reuel the son of Basemath, Esau’s wife.
  Genesis 36:4 — Same-chapter maternal identification: The births already identify Eliphaz with Adah and Reuel with Basemath; this summary repeats those relationships before listing the next generation.
  1 Chronicles 1:35 — Parallel list of Esau's sons: Chronicles lists Eliphaz and Ragouel among Esau's sons, corresponding to the opening branches of this summary while retaining its Ragouel alongside Genesis's Reuel and without supplying the mothers named here.

Genesis 36:11
And the sons of Eliphaz were Teman, Omar, Sophar, Gatam, and Kenaz.
  1 Chronicles 1:36 — Parallel Eliphaz-family record: Both records place Teman, Omar, Sophar and Kenaz in Eliphaz's family; Genesis also has Gatam where Chronicles supplies Gootham, and Chronicles includes the Timna-and-Amalek clause. The supplied forms and scope of the entries remain distinct.

Genesis 36:12
And Timna was the concubine of Eliphaz, Esau’s son, and bore Amalek to Eliphaz. These are the sons of Adah, Esau’s wife.
  1 Chronicles 1:36 — Parallel parentage notice: Chronicles includes Amalek from Timna within Eliphaz's family, paralleling the parentage stated here; Genesis additionally identifies Timna as Eliphaz's concubine, a status not supplied in the shorter Chronicles entry.

Genesis 36:13
And these are the sons of Reuel: Nahath, Zerah, Shammah, and Mizzah. These were the sons of Basemath, Esau’s wife.
  Genesis 36:17 — Same-chapter descendants and chiefs: Nahath, Zerah, Shammah and Mizzah recur as Reuel's chiefs under Basemath's branch, carrying the four names in this descendant list into the chapter's chief list.
  1 Chronicles 1:37 — Parallel family list with different forms: Chronicles gives four sons under Ragouel as Nacheth, Zare, Some and Moze, while Genesis gives Reuel's Nahath, Zerah, Shammah and Mizzah. These parallel family records are compared with their differing supplied forms preserved rather than merged into a corrected list.

Genesis 36:14
And these were the sons of Oholibamah, the daughter of Anah the son of Zibeon, Esau’s wife: she bore to Esau Jeush and Jalam and Korah.
  Genesis 36:25 — Same-chapter maternal ancestry: Oholibamah is again named as Anah's daughter in the account of Anah's children, repeating the paternal relationship by which Esau's wife is introduced here.
  Genesis 36:18 — Same-chapter sons and chiefs: Jeush, Jalam and Korah reappear as the chiefs of Oholibamah, carrying the same maternal grouping from this family entry into the chief list.

Genesis 36:15
These are the chiefs, the sons of Esau. The sons of Eliphaz, Esau’s firstborn: chief Teman, chief Omar, chief Sophar, chief Kenaz,
  1 Chronicles 1:36 — Eliphaz family and chief list compared: Teman, Omar, Sophar and Kenaz appear both among Eliphaz's sons in Chronicles and among his chiefs here; Genesis identifies their branch through Esau's firstborn, while Chronicles does not call these entries chiefs.

Genesis 36:16
chief Korah, chief Gatam, chief Amalek. These are the chiefs of Eliphaz in the land of Idumea; these are Adah’s sons.
  Genesis 36:11-12 — Same-chapter family and chief-list difference: The family list gives five sons of Eliphaz and then Amalek from Timna; this chief list includes Korah along with Gatam and Amalek. The two lists are related through Eliphaz but their different membership is retained without adding Korah to the earlier birth list.

Genesis 36:17
And these are the sons of Reuel, Esau’s son: chief Nahath, chief Zerah, chief Shammah, chief Mizzah. These are the chiefs of Reuel in the land of Edom; these are the sons of Basemath, Esau’s wife.
  Genesis 36:13 — Same-chapter family names carried into chief list: The four chiefs Nahath, Zerah, Shammah and Mizzah repeat the four sons already named under Reuel and Basemath, preserving that maternal family branch in the leadership list.

Genesis 36:18
And these are the sons of Oholibamah, Esau’s wife: chief Jeush, chief Jalam, chief Korah. These are the chiefs of Oholibamah.
  Genesis 36:14 — Same-chapter maternal branch retained: The earlier account names Jeush, Jalam and Korah as the sons Oholibamah bore to Esau; the same three names now form her chief list.

Genesis 36:19
These are Esau’s sons, and these are their chiefs. These are the sons of Edom.
  Genesis 36:1 — Same-chapter genealogical frame: The closing summary identifies the sons and chiefs with Edom, returning to the opening identification of Esau as Edom and closing this section of his family account.
  1 Chronicles 1:35-37 — Parallel family outline: Chronicles lists Esau's sons and the Eliphaz and Ragouel branches, supplying a parallel family outline to this summary of Esau's sons and chiefs; its supplied names and its lack of chief titles are retained.

Genesis 36:20
And these are the sons of Seir the Horite, who lived in the land: Lotan, Shobal, Zibeon, Anah,
  1 Chronicles 1:38 — Parallel Seir-family heading: Both records introduce Seir's sons and begin with Lotan; Chronicles continues with Sobal, Sebegon and Ana where Genesis has Shobal, Zibeon and Anah. The parallel family lists are compared without normalizing their spellings.
  Deuteronomy 2:12 — Earlier residents and later displacement: Genesis introduces Seir the Horite and his family as residents of the land; Deuteronomy later remembers the Horites as earlier residents of Seir displaced by Esau's sons, giving a later account concerning that population.

Genesis 36:21
and Dishon, Asar, and Rison. These are the chiefs of the Horite, the son of Seir, in the land of Edom.
  Genesis 36:29-30 — Same-chapter family heads and chiefs: The later chief list repeats Lotan, Shobal, Zibeon, Anah, Dishon, Asar and Rison, carrying the names introduced in the two-part Seir-family list into its leadership summary.
  1 Chronicles 1:38 — Parallel Seir lists with retained variation: Chronicles also gives a seven-name list under Seir, ending Deson, Osar and Daison; Genesis ends its list Dishon, Asar and Rison. The shared family-list setting is compared while the supplied concluding names remain unharmonized.

Genesis 36:22
And Lotan’s sons were Hori and Aiman; and Lotan’s sister was Timna.
  1 Chronicles 1:39 — Lotan-family comparison with different membership: Both records supply Lotan's family and include Aiman, but Genesis names Hori and Aiman as sons and Timna as Lotan's sister, whereas Chronicles lists Chorri, Aiman, Ailath and Namna as sons. The difference in names, membership and relationships is retained.
  Genesis 36:20 — Same-chapter ancestral branch: Lotan is introduced among Seir's sons; this entry supplies Lotan's children and sister, extending the earlier family heading by one branch.

Genesis 36:23
And these are Shobal’s sons: Golon, Manahath, Gaibel, Soph, and Oman.
