  1 Chronicles 1:40 — Parallel family entry with supplied name forms: Chronicles supplies a five-name Sobal branch, including Gaibel, within its Seir genealogy; Genesis supplies Shobal's Golon, Manahath, Gaibel, Soph and Oman. The parallel entries retain their different supplied forms rather than treating every name as an independently established identity.
  Genesis 36:20 — Same-chapter ancestral branch: Shobal is named in the initial Seir-family list; this entry gives Shobal's sons, specifying the next generation of that named branch.

Genesis 36:24
And these are Zibeon’s sons: Aiah and Onan. This is the Onas who found Iamin in the wilderness when he was tending the beasts of burden of Zibeon his father.
  Genesis 36:20 — Same-chapter named family branch: Zibeon appears in the Seir-family heading and is the father named in this entry about Aiah, Onan and the beasts of burden. This connects the branch to its preceding heading without interpreting the supplied Onas or Iamin wording.

Genesis 36:25
And these are Anah’s children: Dishon, and Oholibamah the daughter of Anah.
  Genesis 36:14 — Same-chapter relationship repeated: Esau's wife Oholibamah is previously introduced as Anah's daughter; the relationship recurs in this entry among Anah's children, alongside Dishon.

Genesis 36:26
And these are Dishon’s sons: Amada, Eshban, Ithran, and Cheran.
  1 Chronicles 1:41 — Parallel genealogy with different supplied names: The Chronicles Seir genealogy contains a Deson branch with Emeron, Eseban, Jethran and Charran, while Genesis supplies Dishon's Amada, Eshban, Ithran and Cheran. This is a comparison of the parallel records; their differing names are not replaced by a harmonized personal-identity table.

Genesis 36:27
And these are Asar’s sons: Balaan, Zoukam, Ioukam, and Oukan.
  Genesis 36:21 — Same-chapter family branch: Asar is named within the Seir-family list and now receives a separate entry naming his sons, connecting the detailed branch with its earlier heading.
  1 Chronicles 1:42 — Parallel branch with different son counts: Chronicles gives Osar three sons, Balaan, Zoukan and Jokan, while Genesis gives Asar four, Balaan, Zoukam, Ioukam and Oukan. The common Balaan and the parallel family setting support comparison, with the three-versus-four membership difference left explicit.

Genesis 36:28
And these are Rison’s sons: Uz and Aram.
  Genesis 36:21 — Same-chapter family branch: Rison is named in the preceding Seir-family list; this entry supplies Uz and Aram as his sons, extending that named branch without identifying them with differently named entries in another genealogy.

Genesis 36:29
These are the chiefs of the Horites: chief Lotan, chief Shobal, chief Zibeon, chief Anah,
  Genesis 36:20 — Same-chapter sons and chiefs: Lotan, Shobal, Zibeon and Anah recur from Seir's sons as Horite chiefs, transferring the same four names from the family introduction to the leadership list.
  Genesis 14:6 — Horites in the Seir region: The earlier campaign account explicitly places Horites in the mountains of Seir; the present genealogy names Horite chiefs within the Seir-family account, providing another account of the named population in that region.

Genesis 36:30
chief Dishon, chief Asar, chief Rison. These are the chiefs of the Horites according to their chiefdoms in the land of Edom.
  Genesis 36:21 — Same-chapter concluding names repeated: Dishon, Asar and Rison conclude both the earlier Seir-family list and the present Horite chief list, preserving the same three names across the two summaries.
  Deuteronomy 2:22 — Horite leadership and later territorial account: The genealogy closes the Horite chief list in the land of Edom; Deuteronomy later describes the Horites' displacement before Esau's sons in Seir, a later account of the people whose chiefs are listed here.

Genesis 36:31
And these are the kings who reigned in Edom before a king reigned in Israel.
  1 Chronicles 1:43-50 — Parallel royal succession list: Chronicles follows its Seir-family entries with the succession beginning Balak and continuing through Jobab, Asom and later kings; Genesis introduces the corresponding royal-list section with its explicit before-Israel-kings notice, which the supplied Chronicles introduction does not repeat.
  Genesis 35:11 — Contrasting royal notices for the brothers' peoples: God's preceding blessing promises kings from Jacob; the Edom account then introduces kings already reigning before a king in Israel. Reading the notices together distinguishes the promise to Jacob from the stated chronology of Edom's kings.

Genesis 36:32
And Balak the son of Beor reigned in Edom, and the name of his city was Dennaba.
  1 Chronicles 1:43 — Parallel king and city entry: Both royal lists begin with Balak son of Beor and name his city Dennaba, matching the king, father and city given in this entry.

Genesis 36:33
And Balak died, and Jobab the son of Zerah from Bozrah reigned in his place.
  1 Chronicles 1:44 — Parallel royal succession with name forms retained: Both records place Jobab after Balak's death; Genesis calls him son of Zerah from Bozrah, while Chronicles supplies Zara and Bosorra. The matching succession is retained alongside the different supplied forms.

Genesis 36:34
And Jobab died, and Asom from the land of the Temanites reigned in his place.
  1 Chronicles 1:45 — Parallel royal succession: Both entries place Asom after Jobab's death; Genesis describes his origin as the land of the Temanites, whereas Chronicles supplies the land of the Thaimans, retaining each account's wording.

Genesis 36:35
And Asom died, and Hadad the son of Barad, who struck down Midian in the plain of Moab, reigned in his place; and the name of his city was Geththaim.
  1 Chronicles 1:46 — Parallel king, campaign and city entry: Both entries place the son of Barad after Asom, recall the defeat of Midian in Moab's plain, and name Geththaim as the city; Genesis supplies Hadad and Chronicles Adad without a change of the supplied names.

Genesis 36:36
And Hadad died, and Samlah from Masrekah reigned in his place.
  1 Chronicles 1:47 — Parallel succession with different name forms: Genesis follows Hadad's death with Samlah from Masrekah; the corresponding Chronicles sequence follows Adad with Samaa from Masekka. The parallel succession is compared without rewriting either king or place name.

Genesis 36:37
And Samlah died, and Shaul from Rehoboth by the River reigned in his place.
  1 Chronicles 1:48 — Parallel river-side origin and succession: Both lists next introduce a king from the place beside the River; Genesis names Shaul from Rehoboth after Samlah, while Chronicles gives Saul from Rooboth after Samaa. The corresponding succession retains the supplied names and places.

Genesis 36:38
And Shaul died, and Baal-hanan the son of Achbor reigned in his place.
  1 Chronicles 1:49 — Parallel royal succession and father: Genesis places Baal-hanan son of Achbor after Shaul's death, while Chronicles places Balaennon son of Achobor after Saul. The parallel father-and-successor entry is compared with its different supplied forms intact.

Genesis 36:39
And Baal-hanan the son of Achbor died, and Arad the son of Barad reigned in his place; and the name of his city was Phogor, and his wife’s name was Mehetabel, the daughter of Matred the son of Me-zahab.
  1 Chronicles 1:50 — Parallel succession with substantive differences: Both entries follow the preceding son of Achbor or Achobor with a son of Barad whose city is Phogor, but Genesis names Arad and adds Mehetabel's family, while Chronicles names Adad and omits that wife notice. The differing king names and the additional relationships are preserved.

Genesis 36:40
These are the names of Esau’s chiefs according to their tribes, according to their place, in their regions and in their nations: chief Timna, chief Gola, chief Jether,
  1 Chronicles 1:51 — Parallel concluding chief lists: Both genealogies introduce a concluding Edom chief list and include Gola; Genesis supplies Timna and Jether where Chronicles has Thamana and Jetheth. This compares the lists without equating chief Timna with the concubine named earlier in Genesis.

Genesis 36:41
chief Oholibamah, chief Elah, chief Pinon,
  1 Chronicles 1:52 — Parallel chief-list entry with supplied forms: At this point in their Edom chief lists Genesis gives Oholibamah, Elah and Pinon, while Chronicles gives Elibamas, Elas and Phinon. The entries are compared as parallel lists with their supplied names retained, without resolving each individual's identity.

Genesis 36:42
chief Kenaz, chief Teman, chief Mazar,
  1 Chronicles 1:53 — Parallel chief-list entry with a different final name: Both lists name Kenaz and Teman together; Genesis then gives Mazar where Chronicles gives Mabsar, preserving the difference instead of silently substituting a spelling.

Genesis 36:43
chief Megediel, chief Zaphoim. These are the chiefs of Edom according to their settlements in the land of their possession. This is Esau, the father of Edom.
  1 Chronicles 1:54 — Parallel closing chief-list summary: Both records conclude their Edom chief lists with Megediel and a final chief, but Genesis names Zaphoim and Chronicles Heram. The shared conclusion is compared without identifying the differently named final chiefs as one person.
  Deuteronomy 2:5 — Settlements and recognized possession: Genesis closes by locating Edom's chiefs in their settlements and possessed land; Deuteronomy recognizes Mount Seir as the possession given to Esau's sons, connecting the territorial summary with a later instruction concerning that inheritance.

Genesis 37:1
And Jacob lived in the land where his father had lived as a stranger, in the land of Canaan.
  Genesis 35:27 — Earlier family residence recalled: The return to Isaac locates the patriarchs' sojourning at Mamre and Hebron in Canaan; Jacob's residence is now introduced as the land where his father sojourned, continuing that stated family setting.
  Genesis 36:6-8 — Contrasting settlements of the brothers: Esau leaves Canaan and resides in Mount Seir, whereas the renewed Jacob account opens with Jacob living in Canaan, distinguishing their locations across the two family narratives.

Genesis 37:2
And these are the generations of Jacob. Joseph, being seventeen years old, was tending the sheep with his brothers, a youth among the sons of Bilhah and the sons of Zilpah, his father’s wives; and Joseph brought an evil report to Israel their father.
  Genesis 35:25-26 — Earlier maternal grouping: The earlier family list identifies Dan and Naphtali with Bilhah and Gad and Asher with Zilpah; this flock scene names those two maternal groups among Joseph's companions. The earlier slave-woman descriptions and the present wives description are both retained.
  Genesis 41:46 — Explicit age markers in Joseph's narrative: Joseph is seventeen in this early flock scene and thirty when he later stands before Pharaoh, supplying two stated age markers in his life without assigning an exact duration to every intervening episode.

Genesis 37:3
And Jacob loved Joseph more than all his sons, for he was to him a son of his old age; and he made him a tunic of many colours.
  Genesis 25:28 — Parental preference within the family: Isaac and Rebekah earlier favour different sons, and Jacob now loves Joseph above his other sons; the two accounts explicitly describe unequal parental affection within the same family history.
  Genesis 37:23 — Same-chapter garment and later stripping: The distinctive tunic Jacob gives Joseph is the garment the brothers strip from him, carrying the visible sign of his father's preference into their attack.

Genesis 37:4
And his brothers saw that their father loved him above all his sons, and hated him, and could speak nothing peaceable to him.
  Genesis 45:15 — Estrangement and later restored speech: The brothers cannot speak peaceably to Joseph here; after Joseph later kisses them and weeps over them, the narrator says they speak with him, giving a particular reversal of the earlier estrangement.
  Genesis 37:18 — Same-chapter hostility and murder plot: The brothers' stated hatred is followed by their plot to kill Joseph when they see him approaching, tracing the narrative's movement from hostility into planned violence.

Genesis 37:5
And Joseph dreamed a dream, and told it to his brothers.
  Genesis 42:9 — Dream report and later recollection: Joseph first tells a dream to his brothers; in Egypt the narrator explicitly says he remembers his dreams when those brothers stand before him, connecting the early reports with their later encounter.
  Genesis 37:7 — Same-chapter dream content supplied: The ensuing sheaf account gives the content of the dream introduced here, turning the brief dream notice into Joseph's narrated account of it.

Genesis 37:6
And he said to them, “Hear this dream which I have dreamed.
  Genesis 37:7 — Same-chapter invitation and dream account: Joseph's request that his brothers hear the dream is immediately followed by his account of their sheaves bowing to his sheaf, supplying the content of the announced telling.

Genesis 37:7
“I dreamed that we were binding sheaves in the midst of the plain; and my sheaf arose and stood upright, and your sheaves turned about and bowed down to my sheaf.”
  Genesis 42:6-9 — Dream imagery and later narrated recollection: The dream depicts the brothers' sheaves bowing to Joseph's; later the brothers bow to Joseph while obtaining food and the narrator explicitly says he remembers his dreams. The connection concerns the brothers and the bowing imagery without claiming that every dream detail is separately explained.
  Genesis 43:26 — Later bowing of the brothers: The brothers later bring gifts and bow before Joseph with their faces to the earth, a narrated action corresponding to the bowing represented by their sheaves in the dream.

Genesis 37:8
And his brothers said to him, “Shall you indeed reign over us, or shall you indeed have dominion over us?” And they hated him still more because of his dreams and because of his words.
  Genesis 42:6 — Questioned dominion and later authority: The brothers mock the prospect of Joseph ruling them; the later narrator identifies him as ruler of the land and records their bowing before him, reversing their earlier dismissive question.
  Genesis 45:8 — Later explanation of Joseph's position: The brothers ask whether Joseph will have dominion over them; Joseph later describes himself as ruler of Egypt and interprets that position as God's sending. This keeps the early challenge distinct from Joseph's later theological explanation.

Genesis 37:9
And he saw another dream, and told it to his father and to his brothers, and said, “Behold, I have dreamed another dream, as though the sun and the moon and eleven stars were bowing down to me.”
  Genesis 42:6-9 — Dream imagery and later remembrance: When the brothers bow before Joseph in Egypt, the narrator says he remembers his dreams; that explicit recollection connects their meeting with the second dream too, without resolving every heavenly figure's identity or declaring the entire family tableau literally enacted.
  Genesis 43:26-29 — Later family bowing with Benjamin present: The brothers bow before Joseph and the narrative then identifies Benjamin among them, providing a later family scene to compare with the dream's bowing imagery. It does not report Joseph's mother or father bowing in that scene.

Genesis 37:10
And his father rebuked him, and said to him, “What is this dream which you have dreamed? Shall I and your mother and your brothers indeed come to bow down to you to the earth?”
  Genesis 35:19 — Dream interpretation and earlier family circumstance: Jacob's question includes Joseph's mother among those who might bow, while the earlier narrative has reported Rachel's death. Reading both preserves the difficulty rather than inventing an unreported maternal bowing or identifying another woman as the dream's mother.
  Genesis 37:11 — Same-chapter nuanced paternal response: Jacob rebukes the dream report but is then said to keep the saying in mind, giving two different aspects of his response rather than equating his rebuke with the brothers' envy.

Genesis 37:11
And his brothers envied him, but his father kept the saying in mind.
  Acts 7:9 — Later recollection of the brothers' jealousy: Stephen's account identifies the patriarchs' jealousy of Joseph when recounting his sale into Egypt, explicitly recalling the hostility named here before the sale is narrated.
  Luke 2:19 — Family reception of extraordinary reports: Jacob keeps Joseph's saying in mind while Mary later keeps and ponders the matters reported about Jesus; both passages distinguish a parent's retention of an extraordinary report from the reactions of other hearers, without identifying the two events.

Genesis 37:12
And his brothers went to pasture their father’s sheep in Shechem.
  Genesis 33:18-20 — Earlier family setting at Shechem: Jacob's earlier arrival, field purchase and altar are placed in the Shechem setting; the brothers now pasture in Shechem, returning the family narrative to that named locality without identifying their precise grazing plot with the purchased field.
  Genesis 37:17 — Same-chapter reported change of location: The brothers begin pasturing in Shechem, but the man later reports their departure to Dothan, explaining why Joseph must continue beyond the initially named location.

Genesis 37:13
And Israel said to Joseph, “Do not your brothers tend the flock in Shechem? Come, I will send you to them.” And he said to him, “Behold, here am I.”
  Genesis 37:14-16 — Same-chapter commission and ensuing search: Israel sends Joseph to the brothers tending the flock; the next account specifies the welfare report requested and shows Joseph saying that he is looking for his brothers, carrying the commission into the search.

Genesis 37:14
And Israel said to him, “Go and see whether your brothers and the sheep are well, and tell me.” And he sent him from the valley of Hebron, and he came to Shechem.
  Genesis 35:27 — Continuing family location at Hebron: Jacob's arrival at Isaac is placed at Mamre and Hebron; Joseph is now sent from the valley of Hebron, continuing the earlier stated location of the family before his journey to Shechem.
  Genesis 37:16 — Same-chapter assignment and stated search: Israel sends Joseph to find out about the brothers and their sheep; Joseph subsequently tells the man that he seeks his brothers and asks where they pasture, restating the practical object of the assignment.

Genesis 37:15
And a man found him wandering in the plain; and the man asked him, saying, “What are you looking for?”
  Genesis 37:16-17 — Same-chapter question, answer and direction: The man's question about Joseph's search elicits Joseph's request for the brothers' pasture location and then the direction toward Dothan, explaining how the wandering search resumes.

Genesis 37:16
And he said, “I am looking for my brothers; tell me where they pasture the flock.”
  Genesis 37:13-14 — Same-chapter purpose of the search: Joseph's stated search for his brothers and their pasture implements his father's instruction to visit them and report on the welfare of the brothers and flock.
  Genesis 37:17 — Same-chapter request and supplied information: Joseph asks where his brothers pasture; the man answers with their reported move to Dothan and Joseph follows them there, turning the request into a direction and encounter.

Genesis 37:17
And the man said to him, “They have departed from here, for I heard them saying, ‘Let us go to Dothan.’” And Joseph went after his brothers, and found them in Dothan.
  Genesis 37:12-14 — Same-chapter destination changed: The earlier pasture notice and commission name Shechem, whereas Joseph finally finds the brothers in Dothan after hearing of their departure, distinguishing the intended initial destination from the eventual encounter.

Genesis 37:18
And they saw him far off, before he drew near to them, and plotted wickedly to kill him.
  Genesis 27:41 — Earlier fraternal murder intention: Esau earlier intends to kill his brother over the blessing, and Joseph's brothers now plan to kill him after their hostility over favour and dreams; both narratives describe a murder intention within the patriarchal family rather than a completed killing at that point.
  Genesis 50:20 — Later acknowledgment of the evil plan: Joseph later tells his brothers that they planned evil against him while God planned good to preserve lives, providing a retrospective interpretation of the hostile plan introduced here without excusing it.

Genesis 37:19
And they said one to another, “Behold, that dreamer comes.
  Genesis 37:5-9 — Same-chapter dreams behind the taunt: The epithet dreamer points back to the two dream reports and the brothers' hostile response, identifying the earlier words they now use to taunt Joseph.
  Genesis 42:9 — Taunted dreams and later recollection: The brothers identify Joseph by his dreams when planning harm; Joseph later remembers those dreams while the brothers stand before him in Egypt, giving a later narrative response to their dismissive epithet.

Genesis 37:20
“Now therefore come, let us kill him and cast him into one of the pits, and we will say, ‘An evil beast has devoured him’; and we shall see what shall become of his dreams.”
  Genesis 37:33 — Same-chapter planned story and paternal inference: The brothers propose saying that a beast devoured Joseph; after seeing the bloodied tunic Jacob himself concludes that a beast devoured him. The link distinguishes their planned deception from the father's reported belief rather than treating the death claim as the narrator's fact.
  Genesis 50:20 — Hostile purpose and later providential interpretation: The plan to kill Joseph and frustrate his dreams belongs to the evil Joseph later says his brothers intended; his later statement that God planned good supplies a retrospective interpretation of the outcome without making the murderous intention good.
  Genesis 42:6-9 — Challenge to dreams and later encounter: The brothers ask what will become of Joseph's dreams while planning his death; later they bow to him as ruler and he remembers those dreams, providing a narrated reversal of their challenge.

Genesis 37:21
And Reuben heard, and delivered him from their hands, and said, “Let us not take his life.”
  Genesis 42:22 — Reuben's intervention recalled in speech: Reuben later reminds the brothers that he warned them not to wrong the child and says they did not listen, recalling his earlier attempt to prevent Joseph's killing. The later explanation remains Reuben's own speech.
  Genesis 37:29-30 — Same-chapter intervention and frustrated recovery: Reuben intervenes to stop the killing, but his return to the empty pit and distressed question show that his effort did not achieve Joseph's restoration to the family.

Genesis 37:22
And Reuben said to them, “Shed no blood; cast him into this pit in the wilderness, but lay no hand upon him,” that he might deliver him from their hands and restore him to his father.
  Genesis 37:24 — Same-chapter proposal enacted: Reuben proposes putting Joseph in the pit without laying hands on him to kill him; the brothers subsequently cast Joseph into the dry pit, carrying out that immediate part of the proposal.
  Genesis 37:29-30 — Same-chapter rescue intention and absence: The narrator gives Reuben's intention to restore Joseph to his father; Reuben's later return finds the pit empty, preserving the difference between that intention and the outcome.

Genesis 37:23
And when Joseph came to his brothers, they stripped Joseph of the tunic of many colours which was upon him.
  Genesis 37:3 — Same-chapter favoured garment stripped: The tunic stripped from Joseph is the distinctive garment Jacob had made for the son he loved above his other sons, connecting the assault with the earlier sign of paternal favour.
  Genesis 37:31-33 — Same-chapter garment reused as deceptive evidence: After stripping Joseph's tunic, the brothers stain that garment with goat's blood and send it to Jacob, who recognizes it and infers Joseph's death; the same object passes from the attack into the deception.

Genesis 37:24
And they took him and cast him into the pit; and the pit was empty, having no water.
  Genesis 37:22 — Same-chapter proposed confinement enacted: The brothers' casting of Joseph into the pit enacts Reuben's preceding pit proposal; the narrator adds that it is empty and has no water, without saying that Reuben's intended rescue has succeeded.
  Genesis 37:28 — Same-chapter confinement and removal: Joseph is cast into the pit and later drawn up from it before being sold, connecting the initial confinement with the subsequent removal and trafficking rather than with a reported death in the pit.

Genesis 37:25
And they sat down to eat bread; and they lifted up their eyes and saw, and behold, Ishmaelite travellers were coming from Gilead, and their camels were laden with spices and resin and stacte; and they were going to take them down into Egypt.
  Genesis 43:11 — Repeated cargo on journeys toward Egypt: The travellers carry spices, resin and stacte toward Egypt; Jacob later sends his sons with gifts including resin, spices and stacte for the Egyptian ruler who is Joseph. The overlapping supplied cargo accompanies two different journeys in the same family narrative.
  Genesis 39:1 — Journey and later purchase account: The Ishmaelite travellers are headed toward Egypt; the later purchase account says Ishmaelites brought Joseph there and sold him to Potiphar, continuing the journey while leaving the intervening Midianite labels intact.

Genesis 37:26
And Judah said to his brothers, “What profit is there if we kill our brother and conceal his blood?
  Genesis 37:27 — Same-chapter objection and sale proposal: Judah questions the profit of killing and concealing their brother's blood, then proposes selling him instead, supplying the alternative to which his argument leads without presenting the sale as innocent.
  Genesis 44:33 — Judah's contrasting treatment of a brother: Judah here weighs profit in the threat against Joseph; he later offers to become a slave in Benjamin's place, providing a contrasting response by the same brother to a younger brother's threatened loss.

Genesis 37:27
“Come, let us sell him to these Ishmaelites, and do not let our hands be upon him, for he is our brother and our flesh.” And his brothers listened.
  Genesis 44:33-34 — Later reversal in Judah's offer: Judah proposes selling Joseph while appealing to their shared flesh; he later offers himself as a slave so Benjamin can return and their father can be spared grief, contrasting the two offers without calling the earlier sale a rescue accomplished.
  Genesis 45:4-5 — Later fraternal accusation and interpretation: Joseph later tells the brothers that they sold him into Egypt and interprets his arrival there in terms of preservation of life; that speech recalls the family responsibility behind the sale Judah proposes here.

Genesis 37:28
And Midianite merchants passed by; and they drew Joseph up and brought him out of the pit, and sold Joseph to the Ishmaelites for twenty pieces of gold, and brought Joseph down into Egypt.
  Acts 7:9 — Later summary of sale into Egypt: Stephen summarizes Joseph's brothers selling him into Egypt from jealousy, recalling the sale and destination narrated here without reproducing or resolving every merchant label in Genesis.
  Psalms 104:17 — Later recollection of Joseph sold: The psalm names Joseph as the man sent ahead and sold as a slave, supplying a later retrospective of the sale while Genesis here specifies the merchants, Egypt and twenty pieces of gold.
  Genesis 39:1 — Sale and subsequent purchase account: The narrative brings Joseph into Egypt after the sale to Ishmaelites; the later account describes Potiphar buying him from Ishmaelites. This links the stages while preserving the Midianite actors named here and in the chapter's closing notice.
  Genesis 45:4-5 — Joseph's later recollection of the sale: Joseph later identifies himself as the brother they sold into Egypt and then speaks of God's preserving purpose, adding his retrospective interpretation without changing this entry's twenty pieces of gold or its named merchants.

Genesis 37:29
And Reuben returned to the pit, and did not see Joseph in the pit; and he tore his garments.
  Genesis 37:22 — Same-chapter failed restoration plan: Reuben's torn garments on discovering the empty pit follow the narrator's earlier disclosure that he meant to bring Joseph back to their father, explaining his distress through that uncompleted intention.
  Genesis 42:22 — Later recollection of Reuben's warning: Reuben later recalls warning the brothers not to wrong the child; his discovery of the empty pit here belongs to the earlier episode in which that intervention failed to preserve Joseph within the family.

Genesis 37:30
And he returned to his brothers and said, “The child is not here; and I, where shall I now go?”
  Genesis 44:34 — Brothers' contrasting crises over returning: Reuben asks where he can now go after finding Joseph missing, following his stated intention to restore him to their father; Judah later explicitly asks how he can go to his father without Benjamin. Both speeches express a crisis over returning without the younger brother, in distinct circumstances.
  Genesis 42:37 — Later undertaking by Reuben: Reuben's distress over the missing Joseph is followed later by his pledge to bring Benjamin back to Jacob, connecting two episodes in which the same brother confronts responsibility for a younger son's return.

Genesis 37:31
And they took Joseph’s tunic, and slaughtered a young goat, and stained the tunic with the blood.
  Genesis 27:9-16 — Family deception using goats and clothing: Jacob earlier receives Esau's clothing and goat skins as part of the deception of Isaac; his sons now use Joseph's clothing and goat's blood to deceive Jacob. The comparison keeps skins and blood distinct and does not claim a narrated divine punishment.
  Genesis 37:23 — Same-chapter stripped garment repurposed: The garment taken from Joseph is now stained with a slaughtered goat's blood, showing how an object from the assault is prepared for the report to the father.

Genesis 37:32
And they sent the tunic of many colours, and brought it to their father, and said, “We have found this. See whether it is your son’s tunic or not.”
  Genesis 38:25-26 — Recognition request involving identifying objects: The brothers ask Jacob to examine whether the garment is his son's; Tamar later asks Judah to identify the owner of the ring, necklace and staff, and he recognizes them. Both family scenes use identifying possessions, with different speakers and different conclusions.
  Genesis 37:3 — Same-chapter distinctive garment recognized: The tunic brought to Jacob is the many-coloured garment he made for Joseph, explaining why the brothers can present that particular possession as recognizable evidence about his son.

Genesis 37:33
And he recognized it, and said, “It is my son’s tunic; an evil beast has devoured him, a beast has snatched Joseph away.”
  Genesis 44:27-28 — Paternal belief later reported in speech: Judah later recounts Jacob's words about the son said to have been devoured by a beast and not seen again, recalling the belief Jacob forms here. Neither speech changes the narrator's account that Joseph was sold rather than killed by a beast.
  Genesis 38:26 — Recognition with different resulting judgments: Jacob correctly recognizes Joseph's garment but falsely concludes that Joseph is dead; Judah later recognizes his own objects and acknowledges his wrongdoing toward Tamar. The parallel concerns recognition and its resulting judgment without equating the accuracy of the two conclusions.

Genesis 37:34
And Jacob tore his garments, and put sackcloth upon his loins, and mourned his son many days.
  Genesis 45:26-28 — Paternal grief and later renewal: Jacob mourns Joseph as dead; when the brothers later report Joseph alive and he sees the wagons, his spirit is rekindled. The later account supplies the reversal of the grief without making the original death belief true.
  Genesis 37:29 — Same-chapter torn garments and different knowledge: Reuben tears his garments when the pit is empty, while Jacob tears his garments believing Joseph dead; both respond to Joseph's loss from their sight but possess different information about what has happened.

Genesis 37:35
And all his sons and daughters gathered and came to comfort him, but he would not be comforted, saying, “I will go down to my son mourning, into Hades.” And his father wept for him.
  Genesis 42:38 — Repeated paternal grief and Hades language: Jacob later refuses to risk Benjamin and speaks again of his old age being brought with sorrow into Hades, recalling the mourning language he uses after believing Joseph lost. The later statement concerns a feared further loss rather than confirming Joseph's death.
  Genesis 45:26-28 — Unconsoled mourning and later news of life: Jacob refuses comfort over Joseph, but the later report and visible evidence that Joseph lives rekindle his spirit and prompt a wish to see him, reversing his inconsolable grief grounded in the belief that Joseph was dead.

Genesis 37:36
And the Midianites sold Joseph in Egypt to Potiphar, Pharaoh’s eunuch, the chief cook.
  Genesis 39:1 — Repeated purchase notice with different sellers: Both entries name Potiphar as Pharaoh's eunuch and chief cook who acquires Joseph in Egypt; this closing notice names Midianites as sellers whereas the later introduction names Ishmaelites. The supplied seller difference and office wording are retained without harmonization.
  Psalms 104:17 — Later summary of Joseph's enslavement: The psalm recalls Joseph being sold as a slave, while this entry names the Egyptian buyer within the enslavement episode. The comparison does not decide which sale the shorter psalm summary specifically has in view or change Potiphar's supplied title.
  Acts 7:9-10 — Enslavement and later retrospective reversal: Stephen recalls Joseph's sale into Egypt and later rescue and authority before Pharaoh; Genesis closes this episode with his sale to Pharaoh's eunuch, linking the point of enslavement with the later account of his changed position.

Genesis 38:1
And at that time Judah went down from his brothers, and came to a certain Adullamite man whose name was Hirah.
  Genesis 38:12 — Same-chapter explicit person identification: The later journey again names Hirah the Adullamite, now calling him Judah's shepherd, continuing the association introduced when Judah leaves his brothers.
  Genesis 37:26-27 — Narrative sequence: The earlier narrative shows Judah speaking among his brothers about selling Joseph; his departure from his brothers begins a separate Judah episode without supplying an unreported motive or a precise elapsed time.

Genesis 38:2
And Judah saw there a Canaanite man’s daughter named Shua; and he took her, and went in to her.
  Genesis 38:12 — Same-chapter explicit household continuation: The later death notice calls Shua Judah's wife, continuing this marriage account while retaining the supplied name rather than replacing it with another edition's wording.
  1 Chronicles 2:3 — Genealogical comparison: Chronicles recalls Judah's first three sons by a Canaanite woman described as Shua's daughter; the common family account is compared while that description remains distinct from Genesis's naming of Shua.

Genesis 38:3
And she conceived and bore a son, and called his name Er.
  Genesis 46:12 — Genealogical correspondence: The later list names Er among Judah's sons and records his death in Canaan, continuing the family history begun by his birth here.
  1 Chronicles 2:3 — Genealogical correspondence: Chronicles again identifies Er as Judah's son and firstborn, corresponding to the first birth in this household account.

Genesis 38:4
And she conceived again and bore a son, and called his name Onan.
  Genesis 46:12 — Genealogical correspondence: The later migration list names Onan after Er among Judah's sons and records both deaths, preserving the son introduced here.
  1 Chronicles 2:3 — Genealogical correspondence: Chronicles likewise names Onan among the three sons of Judah's Canaanite marriage, corresponding to this second birth.

Genesis 38:5
And she bore another son, and called his name Shelah. And she was in Chasbi when she bore them.
  Genesis 38:11 — Same-chapter narrative continuation: Judah later names Shelah as the surviving son whose growth Tamar is to await, continuing the family role of the third son born here.
  Genesis 46:12 — Genealogical correspondence: The later list includes Shelah among Judah's sons, retaining the family identity without identifying Chasbi with a place from another passage.

Genesis 38:6
And Judah took a wife for Er his firstborn, whose name was Tamar.
  Genesis 38:11 — Same-chapter explicit kinship continuation: The widowhood instruction later calls Tamar Judah's daughter-in-law, continuing the relationship established through her marriage to Er.
  Matthew 1:3 — Genealogical continuation: Matthew later names Tamar as the mother of Judah's sons Perez and Zerah, continuing her family history while keeping that later parentage distinct from her initial marriage to Er.

Genesis 38:7
And Er, Judah’s firstborn, was evil before the Lord, and God put him to death.
  1 Chronicles 2:3 — Explicit narrative recollection: Chronicles expressly repeats that Er, Judah's firstborn, was evil before the Lord and was killed by Him, directly recalling this death notice.
  Genesis 38:10 — Same-chapter narrative parallel: The narrator subsequently also attributes Onan's death to God's judgment of his action, pairing the two brothers' deaths without inventing Er's unspecified wrongdoing.

Genesis 38:8
And Judah said to Onan, “Go in to your brother’s wife, and perform a brother-in-law’s duty to her, and raise up seed to your brother.”
  Deuteronomy 25:5-6 — Later legal parallel: The later law similarly provides for a deceased brother's name through his widow and the surviving brother, corresponding to Judah's instruction without claiming every later legal detail is already stated here.
  Genesis 38:9 — Same-chapter narrative contrast: Onan's stated concern that the seed would not be his and his refusal to give seed to his brother oppose the purpose of Judah's instruction.
  Ruth 4:10 — Family-preservation parallel: Boaz later speaks of raising the dead man's name upon his inheritance through marriage to Ruth, providing another family-preservation purpose while the kinship arrangements remain different.

Genesis 38:9
And Onan knew that the seed would not be his; and whenever he went in to his brother’s wife, he spilled his seed upon the ground, that he might not give seed to his brother.
  Genesis 38:8 — Same-chapter narrative contrast: The narrator expressly connects Onan's conduct with withholding seed from his brother, reversing the purpose Judah had given for the union.
  Genesis 38:10 — Same-chapter narrative consequence: The following judgment is explicitly attached to what Onan did; the link retains this stated context rather than supplying a universal judgment about every different sexual situation.

Genesis 38:10
And it appeared evil before God that he did this, and He put him also to death.
  Genesis 38:7 — Same-chapter narrative parallel: God's putting Onan to death repeats the earlier death of Er, while the narrative gives Onan's action more specifically than Er's wrongdoing.
  Genesis 46:12 — Explicit genealogical recollection: The later family list records that Er and Onan died in Canaan, preserving the two deaths while this chapter supplies their narrated circumstances.

Genesis 38:11
And Judah said to Tamar his daughter-in-law, “Remain a widow in your father’s house until Shelah my son is grown”; for he said, “What if this one also should die, as his brothers?” And Tamar went and lived in her father’s house.
  Genesis 38:14 — Same-chapter narrative contrast: Tamar later sees that Shelah has grown but has not been given to her, contrasting the realized situation with Judah's instruction to wait for his growth.
  Genesis 38:26 — Same-chapter explicit retrospective acknowledgement: Judah later acknowledges that he did not give Tamar to Shelah, recalling the wait for that son begun in this widowhood instruction.

Genesis 38:12
And many days passed, and Shua, Judah’s wife, died; and Judah was comforted, and went up to those who sheared his sheep, he and Hirah his shepherd, the Adullamite, to Timnah.
  Genesis 38:1 — Same-chapter explicit person identification: Hirah is again identified as the Adullamite associated with Judah, with his supplied shepherd designation retained rather than silently changed to another role.
  Genesis 38:13 — Same-chapter explicit reported repetition: The report delivered to Tamar repeats Judah's journey to Timnah for sheep-shearing, transmitting the movement just narrated.

Genesis 38:13
And it was told to Tamar his daughter-in-law, saying, “Behold, your father-in-law goes up to Timnah to shear his sheep.”
  Genesis 38:12 — Same-chapter explicit reported repetition: Tamar's information corresponds to the narrator's preceding account of Judah's sheep-shearing journey to Timnah.
  Genesis 38:14 — Same-chapter narrative response: Tamar's subsequent change of clothes and position beside the Timnah road follow the report of Judah's journey, alongside the narrator's explanation that Shelah was grown but not given to her.

Genesis 38:14
And she took off the garments of her widowhood, and wrapped herself in a veil, and adorned herself, and sat by the gates of Ainan, which is beside the road to Timnah; for she saw that Shelah was grown, but he had not given her to him to be his wife.
  Genesis 38:11 — Same-chapter narrative contrast: Shelah's having grown without being given to Tamar contrasts with Judah's earlier instruction that she remain a widow until he was grown.
  Genesis 38:19 — Same-chapter explicit clothing reversal: Tamar later removes the veil and puts her widowhood garments back on, reversing the change of appearance narrated here.
  Genesis 38:26 — Same-chapter explicit retrospective acknowledgement: Judah's later admission that he did not give Tamar to Shelah corresponds to the reason the narrator gives for her action here.

Genesis 38:15
And Judah saw her, and thought her to be a prostitute; for she had covered her face, and he did not recognize her.
  Genesis 38:14 — Same-chapter narrative explanation: The preceding account of Tamar's veil and roadside position supplies the disguise context for Judah's failure to recognize her and his own assumption about her identity.
  Genesis 38:16 — Same-chapter explicit recognition limitation: The following verse again says Judah did not know she was his daughter-in-law, preserving the distinction between Tamar's actual identity and his assumption.

Genesis 38:16
And he turned aside from the way to her, and said to her, “Allow me to come in to you”; for he did not know that she was his daughter-in-law. And she said, “What will you give me if you come in to me?”
  Genesis 38:15 — Same-chapter narrative explanation: Judah's approach continues his mistaken identification of the veiled woman, rather than the narrator identifying her as an unrelated person.
  Genesis 38:17-18 — Same-chapter narrative response: The promised young goat and requested objects supply the payment and pledge terms in response to Tamar's question about what Judah will give.

Genesis 38:17
And he said, “I will send you a young goat from the flock.” And she said, “If you will give a pledge until you send it.”
  Genesis 38:18 — Same-chapter narrative specification: The next exchange identifies the requested pledge as Judah's ring, necklace and staff, giving particular content to the security requested here.
  Genesis 38:20 — Same-chapter narrative implementation: Judah later sends the promised young goat to recover the pledge, attempting to carry out the exchange proposed here.

Genesis 38:18
And he said, “What pledge shall I give you?” And she said, “Your ring, and your necklace, and the staff in your hand.” And he gave them to her, and went in to her, and she conceived by him.
  Genesis 38:25 — Same-chapter explicit object identification: Tamar later sends the ring, necklace and staff as evidence identifying the man by whom she conceived, directly recalling the objects given as pledge here.
  Genesis 38:26 — Same-chapter narrative recognition: Judah's recognition of the objects and acknowledgement follow from the pledge he gave, connecting his later admission with the narrated encounter.

Genesis 38:19
And she rose and went away, and took off her veil, and put on the garments of her widowhood.
  Genesis 38:14 — Same-chapter explicit clothing reversal: Resuming the widowhood garments reverses Tamar's earlier removal of them and use of a veil, marking the end of the roadside disguise.
  Genesis 38:20-21 — Same-chapter narrative continuation: After Tamar departs and changes her clothing, the shepherd fails to find the woman at the earlier location; the sequence does not identify a new whereabouts for her.

Genesis 38:20
And Judah sent the young goat by the hand of his shepherd, the Adullamite, to recover the pledge from the woman; and he did not find her.
  Genesis 38:17-18 — Same-chapter narrative implementation: The young goat is the promised payment, and the sought pledge consists of the objects Judah left with Tamar in the earlier negotiation.
  Genesis 38:21-22 — Same-chapter narrative elaboration: The local inquiry and the shepherd's report give the details of his failure to find the woman, developing the unsuccessful recovery stated here.

Genesis 38:21
And he asked the men of the place, “Where is the prostitute who was in Ainan by the way?” And they said, “There was no prostitute here.”
  Genesis 38:14 — Same-chapter explicit location correspondence: The inquiry names Ainan beside the road, matching the place where Tamar had sat; it does not turn the men's reply into an independent account of her occupation.
  Genesis 38:22 — Same-chapter explicit reported repetition: The shepherd's later report repeats the local men's statement that there was no prostitute there, retaining the attribution of their words.

Genesis 38:22

  Genesis 38:20 — Same-chapter narrative outcome: The shepherd's report that he did not find her gives the outcome of Judah's attempt to exchange the young goat for the pledge.
  Genesis 38:23 — Same-chapter narrative response: Judah's decision to let the woman retain the objects responds to the reported unsuccessful search and his expressed concern about mockery.

Genesis 38:23

  Genesis 38:17 — Same-chapter narrative retrospective: Judah's reference to having sent the young goat recalls his payment promise, distinguishing that attempt from the pledge still held by the woman.
  Genesis 38:25-26 — Same-chapter narrative development: The objects Judah allows her to keep later become the evidence he recognizes, giving the unrecovered pledge a further role in the story.

Genesis 38:24

  Genesis 38:18 — Same-chapter narrative background: The earlier account attributes Tamar's conception to Judah, supplying the history missing from the accusation reported to him here.
  Genesis 38:26 — Same-chapter narrative reversal: Judah's later acknowledgement that Tamar is more in the right than he is contrasts with his initial punitive order; the order is not presented as an independently stated divine command.

Genesis 38:25

  Genesis 38:18 — Same-chapter explicit object identification: The ring, necklace and staff are the same objects Judah gave as pledge before the conception, grounding Tamar's identification claim in the narrated encounter.
  Genesis 38:26 — Same-chapter narrative response: Judah recognizes the objects and acknowledges his own failure in response to Tamar's request that he identify their owner.

Genesis 38:26

  Genesis 38:11 — Same-chapter explicit retrospective acknowledgement: Judah's admission that he did not give Tamar to Shelah recalls the earlier instruction to wait for that son's growth, without making his comparison a blanket verdict on every act.
  Genesis 38:14 — Same-chapter explicit retrospective acknowledgement: His stated failure corresponds to the narrator's earlier observation that Shelah was grown but Tamar had not been given to him.

Genesis 38:27

  Genesis 38:29-30 — Same-chapter narrative elaboration: The following births identify the two children as Perez and Zerah and distinguish their order, expanding the notice that Tamar carried twins.

Genesis 38:28

  Genesis 38:30 — Same-chapter explicit identifying detail: The scarlet thread is later specified on Zerah's hand, identifying the child whose hand the midwife marked during this birth.
  Genesis 38:29 — Same-chapter narrative reversal: The hand's withdrawal and the other brother's emergence overturn the midwife's expectation about which child would come out first.

Genesis 38:29

  Genesis 46:12 — Genealogical continuation: The later list names Perez among Judah's sons and supplies his sons, continuing the family branch introduced at this birth.
  Matthew 1:3 — Genealogical correspondence: Matthew names Perez and Zerah as Judah's children by Tamar and continues the line through Perez, explicitly corresponding to this birth account.
  Ruth 4:12 — Explicit ancestral recollection: The elders' later blessing expressly recalls Perez whom Tamar bore to Judah, naming the child and parents introduced in this scene.

Genesis 38:30

  Genesis 46:12 — Genealogical correspondence: Zerah appears among Judah's sons in the later family list, corresponding to the second twin identified by the scarlet thread here.
  Matthew 1:3 — Genealogical correspondence: Matthew names Zerah alongside Perez as a son of Judah by Tamar, retaining the parentage and paired brothers of this account.

Genesis 39:1
And Joseph was brought down into Egypt; and Potiphar, Pharaoh’s eunuch, the chief cook, an Egyptian man, bought him from the hands of the Ishmaelites who had brought him down there.
  Genesis 37:36 — Explicit narrative resumption: The earlier notice likewise records Joseph's sale to Potiphar, Pharaoh's eunuch and chief cook; its Midianite seller label is retained beside this passage's Ishmaelite label without inventing a resolution.
  Psalms 104:17 — Explicit retrospective recollection: The Psalm expressly recalls Joseph being sold as a slave, corresponding to his purchase and arrival in Egypt here with the supplied Psalm numbering retained.
  Acts 7:9 — Explicit retrospective recollection: Stephen recalls Joseph's sale into Egypt by the jealous patriarchs, giving a later account of the captivity whose Egyptian household setting is introduced here.

Genesis 39:2
And the Lord was with Joseph, and he was a prosperous man; and he was in the house of his Egyptian master.
  Genesis 39:21-23 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: The later prison account again states that the Lord was with Joseph and made his work prosper, carrying the divine-presence theme through a changed condition.
  Acts 7:9-10 — Explicit retrospective recollection: Stephen likewise says God was with Joseph amid his afflictions and later gave him favour before Pharaoh, recalling divine presence without reducing the story to unbroken comfort.

Genesis 39:3
And his master knew that the Lord was with him, and that whatever he did, the Lord made it prosper in his hands.
  Genesis 39:2 — Same-chapter narrative recognition: The master's recognition of the Lord's presence corresponds to the narrator's preceding statement that the Lord was with Joseph and that he prospered.
  Genesis 39:23 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: The prison conclusion repeats the Lord's presence and prospering what Joseph does, matching the language describing his work in the Egyptian household.

Genesis 39:4
And Joseph found grace before his master, and pleased him; and he set him over his house, and committed all that he had into Joseph’s hand.
  Genesis 39:6 — Same-chapter narrative elaboration: The master's relinquishing concern for the household expands the entrusting of his possessions to Joseph stated here.
  Genesis 39:22 — Same-chapter administrative parallel: The chief jailer later also commits his charge to Joseph, repeating the pattern of delegated responsibility in a different institution.
  Genesis 41:40-41 — Narrative continuation: Pharaoh later places Joseph over his house and Egypt, extending the successive increases of entrusted authority without equating the household master with Pharaoh.

Genesis 39:5

  Genesis 30:27-30 — Household-blessing parallel: Laban and Jacob had spoken of Laban's prosperity through Jacob's presence; this narrator now attributes an Egyptian household's blessing to Joseph, preserving the different speakers and settings.
  Genesis 39:23 — Same-chapter divine-presence parallel: The later prison account likewise attributes Joseph's successful work to the Lord's presence, carrying the narrator's divine-agency theme beyond the blessed household.

Genesis 39:6

  Genesis 39:8-9 — Same-chapter explicit reported correspondence: Joseph's subsequent refusal recalls the master's entrusting everything except his wife, giving Joseph's own account of the trust described by the narrator.
  Genesis 39:7 — Same-chapter narrative sequence: The narrator follows Joseph's physical description with the wife's attention and proposal, without supplying an unreported account of her motives beyond what she says and does.

Genesis 39:7
And after these things his master’s wife cast her eyes upon Joseph, and said, “Lie with me.”
  Genesis 39:10 — Same-chapter explicit repeated solicitation: The later statement describes the woman's continued daily requests and Joseph's refusal, developing the proposition first reported here.
  Genesis 39:12 — Same-chapter explicit repeated solicitation: The same demand to lie with her returns when she seizes his garments, distinguishing her initiative from the accusation she later makes.
  Genesis 39:17 — Same-chapter narrative contradiction: The wife's later accusation attributes the sexual initiative to Joseph, contrasting with the narrator's account of her proposition and his subsequent refusal.

Genesis 39:8
But he would not, and said to his master’s wife, “If my master concerns himself with nothing in his house because of me, and has committed all that he has into my hands,
  Genesis 39:4-6 — Same-chapter explicit reported correspondence: The preceding narrator had described the master's entrusting the household to Joseph, the trust Joseph now invokes in answering the woman.
  Genesis 39:9 — Same-chapter grammatical and narrative continuation: The following verse completes Joseph's explanation by identifying the withheld wife and calling the proposed act evil and a sin before God.

Genesis 39:9
“and nothing in this house is above me, and nothing has been withheld from me except you, because you are his wife, how shall I do this evil thing, and sin before God?”
  Exodus 20:13 — Later legal parallel: The supplied Decalogue forbids adultery at this verse number, corresponding to Joseph's refusal to have relations with his master's wife without asserting that he cites the later written law.
  Genesis 20:6 — Shared sin-against-God language: God's earlier address to Abimelech connects touching another man's wife with sin against Him, corresponding to Joseph's stated moral boundary while the circumstances and speakers differ.
  Genesis 39:10 — Same-chapter narrative implementation: Joseph's continued refusal to sleep with the woman carries out the boundary he states concerning his master's wife and sin before God.

Genesis 39:10
And though she spoke to Joseph day after day, he did not listen to her, to sleep with her and have relations with her.
  Genesis 39:7 — Same-chapter explicit repeated solicitation: The account of daily speech extends the first invitation, preserving Joseph's repeated refusal rather than treating the later accusation as his conduct.
  Genesis 39:12 — Same-chapter narrative development: The attempted seizure follows the repeated refusals, and Joseph again refuses participation by leaving his garments and fleeing.

Genesis 39:11

  Genesis 39:12 — Same-chapter narrative continuation: The seizure and flight occur after Joseph enters to do his work and the narrator notes the absence of the household members.
  Genesis 39:14 — Same-chapter narrative contrast: The woman later calls the household members to hear her account, contrasting their later presence with their absence inside when Joseph entered.

Genesis 39:12

  Genesis 39:13 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: The next verse restates the left garments and flight as what the woman sees, continuing the immediate consequences of Joseph's escape.
  Genesis 39:14-15 — Same-chapter narrative contradiction: The woman later attributes the approach to Joseph and explains his flight by her outcry, whereas the narrator describes her seizure, his abandoned garments and his flight.

Genesis 39:13
And when she saw that he had left his garments in her hands, and had fled and gone outside,
  Genesis 39:12 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: The woman's observation repeats the garments left in her hands and Joseph's flight already narrated, rather than supplying a new encounter.
  Genesis 39:16 — Same-chapter narrative continuation: She later keeps those garments beside her until the master returns, carrying the abandoned clothing into the next accusation scene.

Genesis 39:14
she called those of the household, and said to them, “See, he has brought us a Hebrew servant to mock us. He came in to me, saying, ‘Lie with me’; and I cried with a loud voice.
  Genesis 39:7-12 — Same-chapter narrative contradiction: Her claim that Joseph initiated the sexual approach contrasts with the narrator's account of her demands, his refusal and her seizure of his clothing.

Genesis 39:15

  Genesis 39:12 — Same-chapter reported-event comparison: The narrator likewise records abandoned garments and flight, but does not supply her claimed outcry as the cause; her full account remains an accusation rather than a replacement for the narration.
  Genesis 39:18 — Same-chapter explicit reported repetition: The wife's account to the master repeats her earlier claim about crying out, the garments and Joseph's flight.

Genesis 39:16

Genesis 39:17

Genesis 39:18

  Genesis 39:15 — Same-chapter explicit reported repetition: Her later statement repeats the same claimed outcry, discarded garments and flight from her first report to the household.
  Genesis 39:12 — Same-chapter reported-event comparison: Joseph's actual flight and the clothing in her hands are narrated earlier, but the woman's additional account of his intention and her outcry is not adopted as the narrator's account.

Genesis 39:19

Genesis 39:20

Genesis 39:21

Genesis 39:23

Genesis 40:1

Genesis 40:2

Genesis 40:5

Genesis 40:7

  Genesis 40:6 — Same-chapter narrative response: Joseph's question follows his seeing the officials troubled that morning, preserving the link between the observation and his concern.
  Genesis 40:8 — Same-chapter narrative response: Their reply about the dreams and missing interpreter answers the question about their downcast faces and opens the interpretation exchange.

Genesis 40:8

Genesis 40:9

Genesis 40:10

Genesis 40:11

Genesis 40:12

Genesis 40:18

Genesis 40:20

Genesis 41:1

Genesis 41:2

Genesis 41:3
And seven other cows came up after them from the river, ugly in appearance and lean in flesh, and they grazed beside the cows upon the riverbank.
  Genesis 41:19 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: Pharaoh's retelling again describes the second seven cows as ugly and lean, adding his judgment that he has never seen their like in Egypt.
  Genesis 41:27 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: Joseph explains the later lean cows as seven years of famine, supplying the stated meaning of this second group.

Genesis 41:4
And the seven cows which were ugly and lean in flesh devoured the seven cows which were fair in appearance and choice. And Pharaoh awoke.
  Genesis 41:20 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: Pharaoh's report repeats the lean cows' devouring the fair cows, preserving the reversal at the centre of the first dream.
  Genesis 41:30-31 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: Joseph's account of famine consuming the land and obscuring abundance explains the dream's devouring image without treating the cows as a separate historical event.

Genesis 41:5
And he dreamed a second time, and behold, seven ears of grain came up upon one stalk, choice and good.
  Genesis 41:22 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: Pharaoh again describes seven full, good ears on one stalk, repeating the opening image of his second dream.
  Genesis 41:26 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: Joseph identifies the seven good ears as seven years and joins their meaning to the fair cows, explaining the second image's number.

Genesis 41:6
And seven other ears sprouted after them, thin and blighted by the wind.
  Genesis 41:23 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: The second telling repeats seven thin ears blighted by the wind; no additional wind direction is imported into the supplied wording.
  Genesis 41:27 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: Joseph expressly interprets the thin, wind-blighted ears as seven years of famine, matching their stated number and condition.

Genesis 41:7
And the seven thin ears, blighted by the wind, swallowed the seven choice and full ears. And Pharaoh awoke, and it was a dream.
  Genesis 41:24 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: Pharaoh repeats the thin ears' swallowing the full ears when explaining why he sought an interpretation.
  Genesis 41:30-31 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: The interpretation describes famine following abundance so severely that the earlier plenty is no longer known, corresponding to the dream's swallowing of the good ears.

Genesis 41:8
And morning came, and his soul was troubled; and he sent and called all the interpreters of Egypt and all its wise men. And Pharaoh told them the dream, and there was none to explain it to Pharaoh.
  Genesis 41:15 — Same-chapter explicit narrative recollection: Pharaoh later tells Joseph that no one can interpret his dream, restating the failure of the assembled interpreters and wise men.
  Genesis 40:8 — Narrative parallel: The imprisoned officials likewise lacked an interpreter, and Joseph attributed explanation to God; this supplies the earlier setting for the present interpretive crisis.

Genesis 41:9
And the chief cupbearer spoke to Pharaoh, saying, “I remember my offence today.
  Genesis 40:23 — Narrative reversal: The cupbearer's announced remembering introduces testimony about Joseph after the narrator's earlier statement that he forgot him; the precise offence named in his opening is not newly defined.
  Genesis 40:14 — Narrative response: Joseph had asked the cupbearer to remember him and mention him to Pharaoh; the cupbearer's ensuing speech finally brings Joseph's case before the king.

Genesis 41:10
“Pharaoh was angry with his servants, and put us in custody in the house of the chief cook, me and the chief baker.
  Genesis 40:1-3 — Explicit retrospective account: The cupbearer recalls Pharaoh's anger and the two officials' custody, referring back to the offences and imprisonment previously narrated without inventing the specific offences.
  Genesis 40:7 — Shared custody setting: The earlier dialogue places Pharaoh's officials with Joseph in custody in his master's house, corresponding to the confinement setting the cupbearer now recalls.

Genesis 41:11
“And we dreamed a dream in one night, I and he; each of us saw his own dream.
  Genesis 40:5 — Explicit retrospective account: The cupbearer's statement that both men dreamed their own dreams in one night repeats the narrator's particular account of the two prisoners' visions.
  Genesis 40:9-19 — Narrative retrospective: The earlier vine-and-cup and bread-basket reports supply the two different dreams and their interpretations summarized in the cupbearer's testimony.

Genesis 41:12
“And a young Hebrew servant of the chief cook was with us, and we told him, and he interpreted to us.
  Genesis 40:8 — Explicit retrospective account: Joseph's invitation to tell the dreams precedes the interpretations the cupbearer now attributes to the young Hebrew servant.
  Genesis 40:12-19 — Explicit retrospective account: The earlier interpretations assign three days to the branches and baskets and announce the officials' different outcomes, supplying the content summarized here.

Genesis 41:13
“And as he interpreted to us, so it happened: I was restored to my office, and he was hanged.”
  Genesis 40:21-22 — Explicit retrospective fulfilment: The narrator had already recorded restoration of the cupbearer and hanging of the baker as Joseph interpreted; the surviving official now reports those same outcomes to Pharaoh.
  Genesis 40:13-19 — Prediction and reported fulfilment: Joseph's earlier announcements of restoration and hanging are the predictions to which the cupbearer appeals when describing their fulfilment.

Genesis 41:14
And Pharaoh sent and called Joseph; and they brought him out of the fortress, and shaved him, and changed his garments; and he came to Pharaoh.
  Genesis 40:14 — Narrative fulfilment: Joseph's request to be mentioned to Pharaoh and brought out of the fortress receives its narrative outcome when Pharaoh summons him from confinement.
  Psalms 104:20 — Explicit retrospective account: The psalm's account of a king sending and releasing Joseph recalls the royal summons and release narrated here; the psalm's earlier account names the released man as Joseph.
  Acts 7:10 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen describes God's rescuing Joseph from afflictions and giving him favour before Pharaoh, summarizing the movement from confinement into the king's presence.

Genesis 41:15
And Pharaoh said to Joseph, “I have dreamed a dream, and there is none to interpret it; but I have heard it said concerning you, that when you hear dreams, you can interpret them.”
  Genesis 41:8 — Same-chapter explicit narrative recollection: Pharaoh's statement that no one can interpret the dream recalls the failed assembly of Egypt's interpreters and wise men.
  Genesis 41:12-13 — Same-chapter narrative explanation: The cupbearer's preceding testimony about Joseph's interpretations and their outcomes explains what Pharaoh has heard about his ability.

Genesis 41:16
And Joseph answered Pharaoh, saying, “Without God, no answer bringing deliverance shall be given to Pharaoh.”
  Genesis 40:8 — Shared attribution of interpretation: Joseph had already said that dream explanation comes through God; he again refuses to present Pharaoh's deliverance-giving answer as independent of God.
  Genesis 41:25 — Same-chapter narrative development: Joseph subsequently says God has shown Pharaoh what He will do, developing his initial insistence that an answer bringing deliverance requires God.

Genesis 41:17
And Pharaoh spoke to Joseph, saying, “In my dream I thought I was standing beside the bank of the river.
  Genesis 41:1 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: Pharaoh repeats the riverbank setting of the dream's opening scene, which the narrator had introduced before Joseph's summons.
  Genesis 41:18-20 — Same-chapter dream-scene elaboration: The following cow account supplies what Pharaoh saw from the riverbank; the location belongs to the narrated dream rather than a newly identified historical site.

Genesis 41:18
“And as though from the river there came up seven cows, fair in appearance and choice in flesh, and they grazed among the reeds.
  Genesis 41:2 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: The seven fair, well-fleshed cows and their grazing among reeds repeat the image already given by the narrator.
  Genesis 41:26 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: Joseph later assigns seven years to these seven fair cows, identifying their place in the interpretation of the one dream.

Genesis 41:19
“And behold, seven other cows came up after them from the river, wretched and ugly in appearance and lean in flesh; I have never seen such ugly cows in all the land of Egypt.
  Genesis 41:3 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: Pharaoh again describes the second group of seven cows as ugly and lean, corresponding to the narrator's account of their emergence after the fair cows.
  Genesis 41:27 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: Joseph interprets the seven later lean cows as years of famine, giving the stated significance of the wretched animals Pharaoh describes.

Genesis 41:20
“And the seven ugly and lean cows devoured the first seven cows, which were fair and choice.
  Genesis 41:4 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: The devouring of the fair cows by the lean cows repeats the action in the first telling of the dream.
  Genesis 41:30 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: Joseph's announced famine consuming the land after plenty supplies the explanation of the consuming action in Pharaoh's account.

Genesis 41:21
“And they entered into their bellies, yet it could not be perceived that they had entered into their bellies; and their appearance was ugly as at first. And I awoke, and slept again.
  Genesis 41:31 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: The swallowed cows leave no visible improvement in the lean cows; Joseph's explanation likewise says abundance will not be known because the following famine is so severe.
  Genesis 41:4-5 — Same-chapter expanded narrative retelling: Pharaoh's account expands the transition between waking after the cow dream and dreaming again, adding that the lean cows' appearance remained unchanged.

Genesis 41:22
“And I saw again in my dream, and as though seven ears of grain came up upon one stalk, full and good.
  Genesis 41:5 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: The seven full, good ears growing on one stalk repeat the narrator's opening image of the second dream.
  Genesis 41:26 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: Joseph identifies these good ears as seven years, giving them the same meaning as the seven fair cows.

Genesis 41:23
“And seven other ears sprouted beside them, thin and blighted by the wind.
  Genesis 41:6 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: The thin ears blighted by the wind repeat the condition and number in the earlier dream account, without adding a compass direction.
  Genesis 41:27 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: Joseph expressly explains the seven thin ears as seven years of famine, retaining their role as the counterpart of the lean cows.

Genesis 41:24
“And the seven thin ears, blighted by the wind, swallowed the seven good and full ears. I told the interpreters, but there was none to explain it to me.”
  Genesis 41:7-8 — Same-chapter explicit narrative repetition: Pharaoh repeats both the swallowing of the good ears and the failure to obtain an explanation, bringing together the dream's ending and the earlier interpretive crisis.
  Genesis 41:30-31 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: The following famine will consume the land and obscure the abundance, explaining why the thin ears swallow the full ones in Pharaoh's account.

Genesis 41:25
And Joseph said to Pharaoh, “Pharaoh’s dream is one. God has shown to Pharaoh what He is about to do.
  Genesis 41:26 — Same-chapter explicit interpretive elaboration: Joseph immediately explains the dream's unity by assigning the fair cows and good ears the same seven-year meaning.
  Genesis 41:28 — Same-chapter explicit speech repetition: Joseph repeats that God has shown Pharaoh what He is about to do, emphasizing the divine disclosure already stated here.

Genesis 41:26
“The seven fair cows are seven years, and the seven good ears are seven years. Pharaoh’s dream is one.
  Genesis 41:2-7 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: The seven fair cows and seven good ears in the two dream scenes are the images Joseph now interprets together as one period of seven years.
  Genesis 41:29 — Same-chapter interpretive elaboration: The coming seven years of great abundance specify the positive period represented by the fair cows and good ears.

Genesis 41:27
“And the seven lean cows which came up after them are seven years; and the seven thin ears, blighted by the wind, shall be seven years of famine.
  Genesis 41:3-7 — Same-chapter explicit interpretation: The lean cows and thin wind-blighted ears in the dream narrative are identified as the following seven-year famine period.
  Genesis 41:30 — Same-chapter interpretive elaboration: The following statement describes the famine's seven years and their effect on Egypt, expanding the meaning assigned to the lean cows and thin ears.

Genesis 41:28
“And this is the word which I have spoken to Pharaoh: God has shown to Pharaoh what He is about to do.
  Genesis 41:25 — Same-chapter explicit speech repetition: Joseph returns to his statement that God has shown Pharaoh what He is about to do, framing the interpretation as disclosure from God.
  Genesis 41:54 — Same-chapter prediction and narrative fulfilment: The narrator later says famine began as Joseph had said, supplying the narrated outcome of the future divine action he announces to Pharaoh.

Genesis 41:29
“Behold, seven years of great abundance are coming in all the land of Egypt.
  Genesis 41:47 — Same-chapter prediction and narrative fulfilment: The land's production during seven years of abundance is the narrator's stated outcome of the seven plentiful years Joseph announces.
  Genesis 41:53 — Same-chapter prediction and narrative fulfilment: The later completion of seven years of abundance preserves the number and prosperous period announced in Joseph's interpretation.

Genesis 41:30
“And seven years of famine shall come after them, and they shall forget the plenty in all the land of Egypt; and the famine shall consume the land.
  Genesis 41:54 — Same-chapter prediction and narrative fulfilment: The narrator expressly records the beginning of seven famine years as Joseph had said, matching the period announced after the abundance.
  Genesis 41:31 — Same-chapter interpretive elaboration: Joseph's next statement intensifies the description of famine overcoming plenty by saying the abundance will no longer be known because of its severity.

Genesis 41:31
“And the abundance shall not be known in the land because of the famine which shall follow, for it shall be exceedingly severe.
  Genesis 41:21 — Same-chapter dream-image correspondence: The lean cows' unchanged appearance after swallowing the good cows corresponds to the announced loss of visible abundance under severe famine.
  Genesis 47:13 — Narrative development: The later famine account describes Egypt and Canaan languishing under exceedingly severe famine, developing the severity Joseph predicts without fixing an independent chronology for each shortage.

Genesis 41:32
“And as for Pharaoh’s dream being repeated twice, it is because the word from God shall be true, and God shall hasten to do it.
  Genesis 41:1-7 — Same-chapter explicit retrospective interpretation: The two narrated dream scenes provide the repetition whose stated significance Joseph explains as the truth and haste of God's word.
  Genesis 41:53-54 — Same-chapter prediction and narrative fulfilment: The narrated completion of abundance and beginning of famine provide the eventual fulfilment of the announced sequence; no date calculation is imposed on the statement of divine haste.

Genesis 41:33
“Now therefore seek out a prudent and discerning man, and set him over the land of Egypt.
  Genesis 41:39 — Same-chapter proposal and royal response: Pharaoh applies Joseph's prudent-and-discerning description to Joseph himself, identifying the man he judges suitable for the proposed oversight.
  Genesis 41:41 — Same-chapter proposal and narrative fulfilment: Pharaoh's appointment of Joseph over all Egypt carries out the proposal to set a discerning man over the land.

Genesis 41:34
“And let Pharaoh act, and appoint governors over the land; and let them take a fifth of all the produce of the land of Egypt during the seven years of abundance.
  Genesis 41:48 — Same-chapter proposal and implementation: Joseph's later gathering of food during the abundant years implements the collection aspect of his proposal; that verse does not independently restate the exact fifth-part rate.
  Genesis 47:24-26 — Distinct fifth-part arrangements: A fifth of produce later becomes Pharaoh's continuing land ordinance after the land transactions; the shared fraction is retained while the earlier abundance-year collection and later arrangement remain distinct.

Genesis 41:35
“And let them gather all the food of these seven good years which are coming; and let the grain be gathered under Pharaoh’s hand, and let food be kept in the cities.
  Genesis 41:48 — Same-chapter proposal and narrative fulfilment: The later account explicitly describes Joseph laying up the abundant years' food in cities from their surrounding plains, implementing the storage plan stated here.
  Genesis 41:56 — Same-chapter purpose and narrative outcome: Joseph later opens the granaries and sells grain during famine, showing the famine-time use of the food the proposal directs to be stored.

Genesis 41:36
“And the food shall be kept for the land against the seven years of famine which shall be in the land of Egypt, and the land shall not be destroyed by the famine.”
  Genesis 41:54 — Same-chapter purpose and narrative outcome: Egypt's possession of bread as famine begins supplies the narrated provision corresponding to reserves kept against the coming seven-year shortage.
  Genesis 50:20 — Explicit retrospective interpretation: Joseph later describes God's good purpose as feeding many people, corresponding to the life-preserving aim of the proposed food reserve while remaining his retrospective interpretation.

Genesis 41:37
And the words pleased Pharaoh and all his servants.
  Genesis 41:33-36 — Same-chapter narrative response: The accepted words are Joseph's specific proposal for oversight, collection and food storage against famine, identifying the plan that pleased Pharaoh and his servants.
  Genesis 41:39-41 — Same-chapter narrative development: Pharaoh's commendation and appointment of Joseph develop the favourable response to the proposed famine preparations.

Genesis 41:38
And Pharaoh said to all his servants, “Shall we find such a man as this, who has the Spirit of God in him?”
  Genesis 41:16 — Same-chapter royal response to divine attribution: Pharaoh's question about a man with God's Spirit follows Joseph's insistence that Pharaoh's deliverance-giving answer requires God; the commendation remains Pharaoh's speech.
  Acts 7:10 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen later attributes Joseph's favour and wisdom before Pharaoh to God, recalling the divine wisdom recognized in the royal response.

Genesis 41:39
And Pharaoh said to Joseph, “Since God has shown you all these things, there is no man more prudent and discerning than you.
  Genesis 41:33 — Same-chapter proposal and royal response: Pharaoh repeats the prudent-and-discerning qualities Joseph had requested in an overseer and declares Joseph unsurpassed in those qualities.
