  Acts 7:10 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen's statement that God gave Joseph wisdom and favour before Pharaoh summarizes the explanation of Joseph's suitability expressed in the king's speech.

Genesis 41:40
“You shall be over my house, and all my people shall obey your mouth; only in the throne shall I be above you.”
  Genesis 39:4-6 — Administrative rise parallel: Joseph had earlier been entrusted with his master's house and possessions; Pharaoh now grants a greater household authority, with the different reservations in each setting kept distinct.
  Psalms 104:21 — Explicit retrospective account: The psalm recalls Joseph's appointment as lord of the king's house and ruler of his possessions, corresponding to Pharaoh's household commission.
  Genesis 41:55 — Same-chapter command and narrative implementation: Pharaoh later directs the hungry Egyptians to obey Joseph, implementing the delegation that his people should obey Joseph's mouth.

Genesis 41:41
And Pharaoh said to Joseph, “Behold, I set you today over all the land of Egypt.”
  Genesis 41:33 — Same-chapter proposal and narrative fulfilment: Joseph's appointment over all Egypt supplies the specific overseer and nationwide jurisdiction proposed in the famine plan.
  Acts 7:10 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen expressly recounts Pharaoh's appointment of Joseph as ruler over Egypt and the royal household, recalling this act of promotion.
  Genesis 45:8 — Explicit retrospective interpretation: Joseph later describes God as making him ruler of all Egypt and lord of Pharaoh's house, giving his own providential interpretation of the royal appointment.

Genesis 41:42
And Pharaoh took his ring from his hand and put it upon Joseph’s hand, and clothed him in a robe of fine linen, and put a golden collar about his neck.
  Genesis 41:40-41 — Same-chapter narrative implementation: The ring, fine-linen robe and golden collar accompany the household and nationwide authority Pharaoh has just granted, narrating Joseph's investiture without adding unattested ceremonial meanings.
  Wisdom of Solomon 10:13-14 — Narrative allusion: Wisdom describes a sold righteous man's rise from a pit and bonds to authority over rulers, providing a literary counterpart to Joseph's elevation; the Wisdom passage itself leaves the man unnamed.

Genesis 41:43
And he made him ride in his second chariot, and a herald proclaimed before him; and he set him over all the land of Egypt.
  Genesis 41:41 — Same-chapter narrative implementation: The second chariot, herald and public setting-over-Egypt enact the appointment Pharaoh has just declared; no additional proclamation words are supplied.
  Psalms 104:21-22 — Explicit retrospective account: The psalm recalls Joseph's rule over royal possessions and authority in relation to rulers and elders, summarizing the elevated public standing illustrated here.

Genesis 41:44
And Pharaoh said to Joseph, “I am Pharaoh; without you no one shall lift up his hand in all the land of Egypt.”
  Genesis 41:40 — Same-chapter royal authority qualification: Pharaoh's assertion of his own identity beside Joseph's comprehensive delegated authority corresponds to the earlier reservation that Pharaoh remains above him in the throne.
  Genesis 41:55 — Same-chapter command and narrative implementation: The later instruction that Egyptians go to Joseph and do whatever he says demonstrates the delegated authority Pharaoh declares here.

Genesis 41:45
And Pharaoh called Joseph’s name Psonthomphanech; and he gave him Asenath, the daughter of Potiphera, priest of Heliopolis, to be his wife.
  Genesis 46:20 — Explicit genealogical recollection: The later genealogy repeats Asenath's father Potiphera and his priesthood at Heliopolis when naming her sons by Joseph, identifying the same marriage without changing the Egyptian names.
  Genesis 41:50 — Same-chapter explicit family identification: The birth notice repeats Asenath's parentage and priestly family, carrying forward the wife identified in Pharaoh's marriage arrangement.

Genesis 41:46
And Joseph was thirty years old when he stood before Pharaoh king of Egypt. And Joseph went out from Pharaoh’s presence, and passed through all the land of Egypt.
  Genesis 37:2 — Age and narrative progression: Joseph is introduced at seventeen and now stands before Pharaoh at thirty, marking his progression from the family flock setting to royal service without assigning an undocumented date to each intervening event.
  Genesis 41:41 — Same-chapter appointment and narrative implementation: Joseph's passing through Egypt follows the commission setting him over all the land, connecting his movement with the nationwide office just granted.

Genesis 41:47
And the land brought forth sheaves during the seven years of abundance.
  Genesis 41:29 — Same-chapter prediction and narrative fulfilment: The seven years in which Egypt produces sheaves correspond to the seven years of great abundance announced in Joseph's interpretation.
  Genesis 41:53 — Same-chapter narrative period correspondence: The later closing notice names the completed seven years of abundance, corresponding to the productive period described here.

Genesis 41:48
And he gathered all the food of the seven years in which there was abundance in the land of Egypt, and laid up the food in the cities; the food from the plains around each city he laid up within it.
  Genesis 41:35 — Same-chapter proposal and narrative fulfilment: The storage of each region's food within its city implements the proposed gathering and keeping of grain in the cities under Pharaoh's authority.
  Genesis 41:56 — Same-chapter provision and later use: The famine-time opening of granaries and sale of grain supplies the later use of food laid up during abundance.

Genesis 41:49
And Joseph gathered grain like the sand of the sea, in great abundance, until they could not count it, for it was beyond counting.
  Genesis 41:35 — Same-chapter proposal and narrative implementation: Joseph's uncountable gathering of grain develops the collection proposed for the abundant years; the sea-sand comparison describes grain quantity rather than an independently measured total.
  Genesis 47:14 — Stored supply and famine administration: Joseph's later grain sales yield silver from Egypt and Canaan, continuing the administration of grain introduced by the large stores here without equating stored and sold quantities.

Genesis 41:50
And to Joseph were born two sons before the seven years of famine came, whom Asenath, the daughter of Potiphera, priest of Heliopolis, bore to him.
  Genesis 41:51-52 — Same-chapter explicit family identification: The following naming notices identify the two sons born before famine as Manasseh and Ephraim and preserve their firstborn-and-second order.
  Genesis 46:20 — Explicit genealogical recollection: The later catalogue names Joseph's two sons born in Egypt and repeats Asenath's parentage, corresponding to this birth and family notice.

Genesis 41:51
And Joseph called the name of the firstborn Manasseh, “For God has made me forget all my toil, and all the affairs of my father.”
  Genesis 46:20 — Genealogical correspondence: The later family catalogue names Manasseh among Joseph and Asenath's Egyptian-born sons, corresponding to the firstborn named here.
  Genesis 48:17-18 — Explicit birth-order recollection: Joseph later identifies Manasseh as the firstborn while correcting the placement of Jacob's blessing hand, preserving the birth position given in this naming notice.

Genesis 41:52
And the name of the second he called Ephraim, “For God has increased me in the land of my humiliation.”
  Genesis 46:20 — Genealogical correspondence: The later catalogue names Ephraim as Joseph's son by Asenath, continuing the family branch introduced by this second-son naming statement.
  Genesis 48:14 — Explicit birth-order recollection: Jacob's placing his right hand on Ephraim while the narrator calls him younger recalls the second-born position established here.

Genesis 41:53
And the seven years of abundance which were in the land of Egypt passed away.
  Genesis 41:29 — Same-chapter prediction and narrative fulfilment: The completion of seven abundant years matches the duration and prosperity Joseph had predicted for Egypt.
  Genesis 41:54 — Same-chapter narrative transition: The following seven famine years begin when the seven abundant years pass, preserving the two distinct periods in the announced sequence.

Genesis 41:54
And the seven years of famine began to come, as Joseph had said; and there was famine in all the land, but in all the land of Egypt there was bread.
  Genesis 41:27-30 — Same-chapter prediction and narrative fulfilment: Joseph had announced seven famine years after abundance, and the narrator now expressly says they began as he had said.
  Acts 7:11 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen recalls famine over Egypt and Canaan and the ancestors' lack of provisions, summarizing its wider family impact without erasing Genesis's statement that Egypt initially had bread.
  Genesis 45:6 — Explicit retrospective chronology: Joseph later describes the famine as two years underway with five still remaining, retaining the seven-year duration whose beginning is recorded here.

Genesis 41:55
And all the land of Egypt hungered, and the people cried to Pharaoh for bread. And Pharaoh said to all the Egyptians, “Go to Joseph; do whatever he says to you.”
  Genesis 41:40 — Same-chapter delegated authority in practice: Pharaoh's instruction to follow Joseph's directions applies the earlier command that the people obey Joseph's mouth.
  Genesis 47:15 — Famine-appeal parallel: The Egyptians later again come to Joseph asking for bread after their silver is spent, developing the hunger and demand for provision without making the two notices the same chronological moment.

Genesis 41:56
And the famine was upon the face of all the earth; and Joseph opened all the granaries, and sold to all the Egyptians.
  Genesis 41:35-36 — Same-chapter proposal and narrative implementation: Opening the granaries during famine carries out the proposed preservation of food in cities for the shortage years.
  Genesis 47:14 — Narrative development: The later account describes silver gathered from Egypt and Canaan for grain, expanding the famine-time sales begun here.

Genesis 41:57
And all the countries came into Egypt to buy from Joseph, for the famine prevailed in all the earth.
  Genesis 42:5 — Narrative development: Jacob's sons later arrive among other buyers because famine reaches Canaan, giving a particular family instance of the countries coming to Egypt for grain.
  Acts 7:11-12 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen recalls famine in Egypt and Canaan and Jacob's sending his sons to Egypt for grain, summarizing the movement of buyers that brings Joseph's family into the story.

Genesis 42:1
And Jacob saw that there was grain for sale in Egypt, and said to his sons, “Why are you idle?
  Genesis 41:57 — Narrative continuation: The preceding account of countries coming to Egypt for grain provides the wider supply situation behind Jacob's discovery that grain is available there.
  Acts 7:12 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen expressly recounts Jacob's hearing of grain in Egypt and sending the fathers on their first visit, recalling the information that initiates this journey.

Genesis 42:2
“Behold, I have heard that there is grain in Egypt. Go down there and buy us a little food, that we may live and not die.”
  Genesis 43:2 — Repeated family instruction: After the first purchased grain is consumed, Jacob again asks his sons to buy a little food, repeating the household need behind this initial command.
  Acts 7:12 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen summarizes Jacob's sending the ancestors to Egypt after hearing of grain there, corresponding to this first purchasing commission.

Genesis 42:3
And Joseph’s ten brothers went down to buy grain from Egypt.
  Acts 7:12 — Explicit retrospective account: The first journey to Egypt to buy grain is the visit Stephen attributes to Jacob's sending the ancestors after learning of Egyptian supplies.
  Genesis 42:4 — Same-chapter narrative explanation: The explicit withholding of Benjamin explains his absence from the ten brothers who undertake the purchasing journey.

Genesis 42:4
But he did not send Benjamin, Joseph’s brother, with his brothers; for he said, “What if sickness should befall him?”
  Genesis 46:19 — Explicit family identification: The later list identifies Joseph and Benjamin as Rachel's sons, supplying the full-brother relationship foregrounded when Jacob withholds Benjamin.
  Genesis 42:38 — Same-chapter repeated paternal concern: Jacob later repeats his refusal to send Benjamin and his fear of sickness on the journey, expanding the concern stated before the first departure.

Genesis 42:5
And the sons of Israel came to buy among those who came, for the famine was in the land of Canaan.
  Acts 7:11 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen's account of famine in Egypt and Canaan and the ancestors' lack of provisions corresponds to the Canaanite shortage bringing Israel's sons among the buyers.
  Genesis 41:57 — Narrative development: The sons of Israel are a particular group among the countries previously described as coming into Egypt to buy from Joseph.

Genesis 42:6
And Joseph was ruler of the land; he was the one who sold to all the people of the land. And Joseph’s brothers came and bowed down before him, with their faces to the earth.
  Genesis 37:7-8 — Dream and narrative correspondence: The brothers bow before the ruler who sells them grain, corresponding to the earlier bowing sheaves and their question about Joseph ruling them; the link does not claim every family member has appeared here.
  Genesis 41:41 — Appointment and narrative implementation: Pharaoh's commission over all Egypt explains the nationwide ruling office Joseph exercises when his brothers approach him for grain.
  Acts 7:10 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen expressly recalls Joseph's appointment as ruler over Egypt, the public position through which his family now encounters him.

Genesis 42:7
And Joseph saw his brothers and recognized them, but made himself a stranger to them, and spoke harshly to them, and said to them, “Where have you come from?” And they said, “From the land of Canaan, to buy food.”
  Genesis 42:8 — Same-chapter explicit narrative clarification: The next statement repeats that Joseph recognizes his brothers while they fail to recognize him, clarifying the asymmetry behind his assumed stranger's manner.
  Genesis 42:30 — Same-chapter explicit retrospective account: The brothers later tell Jacob that the lord of the land spoke harshly to them, recalling their encounter while still not identifying him as Joseph.
  Genesis 45:1-4 — Narrative reversal: Joseph later discloses his name and his identity as the brother sold into Egypt, ending the concealment maintained in his harsh first encounter.

Genesis 42:8
And Joseph recognized his brothers, but they did not recognize him.
  Genesis 42:23 — Same-chapter narrative explanation: The brothers' ignorance that Joseph understands them, with an interpreter between them, develops the concealed identity and unequal recognition stated here.
  Genesis 45:3-4 — Narrative reversal: Joseph later names himself and identifies himself as their sold brother, reversing their failure to recognize him during the first visit.

Genesis 42:9
And Joseph remembered the dreams which he had seen, and said to them, “You are spies; you have come to inspect the tracks of the country.”
  Genesis 37:5-10 — Explicit retrospective reference: The earlier sheaf and heavenly-body dreams supply the dreams the narrator now says Joseph remembered; the present scene is not made to enact every detail of both visions at once.
  Genesis 42:14 — Same-chapter explicit speech repetition: Joseph repeats his accusation that the visitors are spies, continuing the testing charge rather than establishing espionage as narrator-verified fact.

Genesis 42:10
And they said, “No, my lord; your servants have come to buy food.
  Genesis 42:3 — Same-chapter narrator and reported purpose: The narrator had stated that the brothers went to buy grain, corresponding to the purchasing purpose they give in answer to Joseph's accusation.
  Genesis 42:31 — Same-chapter explicit retrospective account: The brothers' later report again denies being spies, recalling their defence before the still-unrecognized Joseph.

Genesis 42:11
“We are all sons of one man; we are peaceable men, and your servants are no spies.”
  Genesis 35:22-26 — Genealogical context: The earlier twelve-son family list supplies the shared father and named family background behind the brothers' appeal to being one man's sons.
  Genesis 42:31 — Same-chapter explicit speech recollection: The later report repeats their description as peaceable men and denial of spying, retaining these words as their defence against the accusation.

Genesis 42:12
And he said to them, “No, but you have come to see the tracks of the land.”
  Genesis 42:9 — Same-chapter explicit speech repetition: Joseph's statement about inspecting the tracks of the land repeats the purpose alleged in his initial spying accusation, without establishing that allegation as true.
  Genesis 42:15-16 — Same-chapter accusation and proposed test: Joseph next makes the youngest brother's appearance a test of their account, connecting the repeated accusation with the particular verification he proposes.

Genesis 42:13
And they said, “Your servants are twelve brothers in the land of Canaan; and behold, the youngest is with our father today, and the other is no more.”
  Genesis 35:22-26 — Genealogical context: The earlier list counts Jacob's sons as twelve and names them, giving the family background of the twelve-brother account offered to Joseph.
  Genesis 42:32 — Same-chapter explicit speech recollection: The brothers repeat to Jacob the account of twelve brothers, the absent one and the youngest remaining with their father, preserving their reported perspective on Joseph.

Genesis 42:14
And Joseph said to them, “This is what I said to you, saying, ‘You are spies.’
  Genesis 42:9 — Same-chapter explicit speech repetition: Joseph returns to his earlier spying charge, keeping the accusation within his own dialogue rather than adopting it as a fact about the purchasing journey.
  Genesis 42:16 — Same-chapter accusation and proposed test: His following proposal tests whether their words are true and repeats the spying allegation as the alternative, clarifying the condition he attaches to the charge.

Genesis 42:15
“By this you shall be revealed: by the health of Pharaoh, you shall not go out from here unless your youngest brother comes here.
  Genesis 42:20 — Same-chapter developed testing condition: Joseph again requires the youngest brother and attaches belief in their words to that requirement, preserving the later condition alongside the initial prohibition on departure.
  Genesis 43:3-5 — Explicit retrospective account: Judah later recalls the warning that they cannot see the ruler without their youngest brother, describing the practical return condition arising from Joseph's test without making every reported wording identical.

Genesis 42:16
“Send one of you and bring your brother, but you shall be taken into custody until your words are made clear, whether you speak truth or not; and if not, by the health of Pharaoh, surely you are spies.”
  Genesis 42:19 — Same-chapter changed custody arrangement: The later proposal keeps one brother while the others carry provisions home, differing from the initial plan to send one and hold the others; the two arrangements are not collapsed.
  Genesis 42:20 — Same-chapter developed testing condition: The later demand to bring the youngest brother retains the concern that their words be believed, continuing the verification purpose of the initial proposal.

Genesis 42:17
And he put them in custody for three days.
  Genesis 42:18 — Same-chapter explicit temporal continuation: The following third-day speech supplies the stated point at which Joseph changes the group's custody terms after holding them for three days.
  Genesis 42:24 — Same-chapter narrative development: Simeon's later binding shows the individual detention that remains after the whole group's three-day custody; it does not make every brother remain imprisoned indefinitely.

Genesis 42:18
And on the third day he said to them, “Do this, and you shall live, for I fear God.
  Genesis 39:9 — Shared professed accountability to God: Joseph had earlier refused wrongdoing as sin before God; his present statement that he fears God likewise invokes divine accountability in explaining his conduct.
  Genesis 42:19-20 — Same-chapter speech elaboration: Joseph explains the live-and-do-this instruction through the revised arrangement of one detained brother, food for the household and the required return with the youngest.

Genesis 42:19
“If you are peaceable men, let one of your brothers be held in custody; but you go, and carry away the grain which you have bought for your provision.
  Genesis 42:24 — Same-chapter proposal and narrative implementation: Joseph selects and binds Simeon, implementing the instruction that one brother remain in custody while the others depart with grain.
  Genesis 42:33 — Same-chapter explicit speech recollection: The brothers later report the instruction to leave one brother and carry household provisions home, recalling the revised custody arrangement.

Genesis 42:20
“And bring your youngest brother to me, and your words shall be believed; but if not, you shall die.” And they did so.
  Genesis 43:15 — Condition and narrative fulfilment: The next journey actually includes Benjamin, giving the narrated response to Joseph's demand that the youngest brother be brought to him.
  Genesis 42:34 — Same-chapter explicit speech recollection: The brothers' later report again connects bringing the youngest with recognition of their peaceable purpose, while its promise of release and trade remains part of their fuller reported account.

Genesis 42:21
And they said one to another, “Yes, we are in sin concerning our brother, for we disregarded the affliction of his soul when he begged us, and did not listen to him. Therefore this affliction has come upon us.”
  Genesis 37:23-28 — Explicit retrospective confession: The brothers' confession recalls stripping Joseph, casting him into the pit and selling him; their present speech adds his ignored entreaty, which the earlier scene does not quote.
  Genesis 44:16 — Related reported guilt interpretation: Judah later says God has found the servants' iniquity during the cup crisis, providing another expressed interpretation of guilt without turning either speech into a narrator's declaration of the precise cause of every affliction.

Genesis 42:22
And Reuben answered and said to them, “Did I not speak to you, saying, ‘Do not wrong the child’? And you did not listen to me; and behold, his blood is required.”
  Genesis 37:21-22 — Explicit retrospective reference: Reuben's reminder corresponds to his earlier appeal not to take Joseph's life or shed his blood, while the wording of the two speeches is retained rather than made verbatim identical.
  Genesis 37:29-30 — Narrative background: Reuben's distress on finding Joseph absent from the pit continues his earlier protective concern, supplying narrative background for his present protest against harming the child.

Genesis 42:23
But they did not know that Joseph understood, for the interpreter was between them.
  Genesis 42:21-22 — Same-chapter narrative clarification: The interpreter notice explains why the brothers speak their confession and Reuben's reminder without knowing Joseph understands them; no particular spoken language is newly supplied.
  Genesis 45:1-4 — Narrative reversal: Joseph's later self-disclosure ends the hidden identity maintained while he understands the brothers through the apparently mediated exchange.

Genesis 42:24
And Joseph turned away from them and wept. And he came to them again, and spoke to them, and took Simeon from among them, and bound him before them.
  Genesis 43:30 — Repeated concealed emotion: Joseph later withdraws to weep after seeing Benjamin, paralleling his turning aside here while retaining the different immediate settings of the two emotional scenes.
  Genesis 43:23 — Narrative continuation: The steward later brings Simeon out to the returning brothers, providing the release following his detention here.

Genesis 42:25
And Joseph commanded that their vessels be filled with grain, and that each man’s silver be restored to his sack, and that they be given provisions for the way. And thus it was done to them.
  Genesis 42:35 — Same-chapter command and narrative outcome: The discovery of each man's silver in his sack supplies the outcome of Joseph's instruction to restore their payments, a cause known to the reader before the brothers understand it.
  Genesis 44:1 — Explicit narrative repetition: Joseph later again commands food to be loaded and each man's silver placed at his sack's mouth, repeating a deliberate provision-and-payment arrangement on a separate journey.

Genesis 42:26
And they loaded the grain upon their donkeys, and departed from there.
  Genesis 42:2 — Same-chapter commission and narrative implementation: Their departure with loaded grain carries forward Jacob's instruction to purchase household food in Egypt; their return is not claimed to remove the continuing famine.
  Genesis 43:2 — Narrative continuation: The household later consumes the grain brought from Egypt, providing the destination and use of the load with which the brothers depart here.

Genesis 42:27
And one opened his sack to give fodder to his donkeys where they lodged, and saw his bundle of silver; and it was at the mouth of the sack.
  Genesis 42:25 — Same-chapter narrative explanation: Joseph's preceding command to restore each man's silver supplies the reader's explanation of the silver one brother discovers at the lodging place.
  Genesis 43:21 — Explicit retrospective account: The brothers later describe opening sacks at the lodging place and finding silver, recalling the returned-payment episode without forcing the one-sack and collective reports into an invented sequence.

Genesis 42:28
And he said to his brothers, “My silver is restored to me, and behold, it is in my sack.” And their hearts were astonished, and they were troubled, saying one to another, “What is this that God has done to us?”
  Genesis 42:25 — Same-chapter narrator and reported interpretation: The narrator had disclosed Joseph's restoration order, while the brothers react by asking what God has done; their anxious question is not substituted for the narrated human action.
  Genesis 43:23 — Contrasting reported interpretations: The steward later answers fear with a statement about God-given treasure and received payment, offering reassurance about the sacks while the present brothers voice alarm; neither speech is expanded into an independent transaction reconstruction.

Genesis 42:29
And they came to Jacob their father in the land of Canaan, and told him all that had befallen them, saying,
  Genesis 42:30-34 — Same-chapter narrative elaboration: The following speech gives the harsh encounter, defence and youngest-brother condition that the brothers report upon reaching Jacob.
  Genesis 44:24 — Explicit retrospective account: Judah later recalls going up to their father and telling him the ruler's words, referring back to this return and family report.

Genesis 42:30
“The man, the lord of the land, spoke harshly to us, and put us in custody as spies of the land.
  Genesis 42:7 — Same-chapter explicit narrative recollection: The reported harsh manner corresponds to Joseph's assumed stranger's role and harsh speech during the first encounter.
  Genesis 42:9 — Same-chapter explicit speech recollection: The brothers' statement about treatment as spies recalls Joseph's accusation, retaining the allegation as the ruler's charge rather than a fact about them.

Genesis 42:31
“And we said to him, ‘We are peaceable men; we are no spies.
  Genesis 42:10-11 — Same-chapter explicit speech recollection: The later report repeats their denial of spying and claim to be peaceable men; their earlier defence additionally states the food-purchasing purpose behind that denial.

Genesis 42:32
“‘We are twelve brothers, sons of our father; one is no more, and the youngest is with our father today in the land of Canaan.’
  Genesis 42:13 — Same-chapter explicit speech repetition: The report repeats the twelve-brother family account, including the absent brother and youngest at home, without endorsing their lack of knowledge about Joseph's survival.
  Genesis 35:22-26 — Genealogical context: The earlier account numbers Jacob's sons as twelve and lists them, supplying the actual family background behind the brothers' repeated claim.

Genesis 42:33
“And the man, the lord of the land, said to us, ‘By this I shall know that you are peaceable men: leave one brother here with me, and take the grain which you have bought for your household’s provision, and depart.
  Genesis 42:19 — Same-chapter explicit speech recollection: The reported requirement to leave one brother and carry bought provisions away recalls Joseph's revised custody terms.
  Genesis 42:24 — Same-chapter narrative implementation: Simeon's binding is the narrated detention corresponding to the one-brother arrangement the returning men report to Jacob.

Genesis 42:34
“‘And bring your youngest brother to me, and I shall know that you are no spies, but peaceable men; and I will restore your brother to you, and you may trade in the land.’”
  Genesis 42:20 — Same-chapter explicit speech recollection: The brothers again describe the youngest brother as the condition for believing their words, preserving the distinction between Joseph's recorded warning and their fuller report about release and trade.
  Genesis 43:23 — Condition and narrative outcome: Simeon is brought out when the brothers return with Benjamin, supplying the later release that their reported promise anticipates.

Genesis 42:35
And as they emptied their sacks, each man’s bundle of silver was in his sack; and they and their father saw their bundles of silver, and were afraid.
  Genesis 42:25 — Same-chapter narrative explanation: The silver in every sack results from Joseph's earlier command to restore each man's payment, explaining the discovery that frightens the brothers and Jacob.
  Genesis 43:12 — Narrative response: Jacob later instructs them to return the silver and take an additional payment, responding to the discovery of their original money among the grain.

Genesis 42:36
And Jacob their father said to them, “You have bereaved me of children. Joseph is no more, and Simeon is no more, and you will take Benjamin. All these things have come upon me.”
  Genesis 37:33 — Repeated paternal loss perspective: Jacob had concluded from the bloodied tunic that Joseph was devoured; his present claim that Joseph is no more continues that belief while the narrator has shown Joseph alive.
  Genesis 42:24 — Same-chapter contrasting knowledge and report: The reader has seen Simeon detained by the living Joseph, whereas Jacob speaks of both as lost; his distressed statement is not converted into a narrator's report of their deaths.
  Genesis 43:14 — Developed paternal response: Jacob later prays for the other brother and Benjamin to be released and again speaks of bereavement, developing the concern he expresses here while finally permitting the journey.

Genesis 42:37
And Reuben said to his father, “Slay my two sons if I do not bring him to you. Give him into my hand, and I will bring him back to you.”
  Genesis 37:21-22 — Repeated protective role: Reuben had earlier sought to preserve Joseph and restore him to his father; he now offers to take responsibility for Benjamin's return, with the different terms of his proposal kept visible.
  Genesis 43:8-9 — Contrasting surety proposals: Judah later offers his own continuing accountability for bringing Benjamin back, providing a different guarantee from Reuben's offer involving his two sons without endorsing the proposed killing.

Genesis 42:38
And he said, “My son shall not go down with you, for his brother is dead, and he alone is left; and if he should fall sick on the way which you go, you shall bring down my old age with sorrow into Hades.”
  Genesis 44:27-31 — Explicit retrospective account: Judah later recounts Jacob's two-son grief, feared sickness and sorrowful descent into Hades, repeating the paternal concern that prevents Benjamin's departure here.
  Genesis 37:35 — Repeated grief language: Jacob had earlier spoken of mourning Joseph into Hades; his renewed fear of being brought there in sorrow links Benjamin's possible loss with his earlier bereavement.

Genesis 43:1
And the famine became severe in the land.
  Genesis 41:54-57 — Narrative famine continuation: The earlier account reports the predicted famine and the countries coming to Egypt for grain; the present worsening of famine continues that crisis within Jacob's household story.
  Genesis 12:10 — Patriarchal famine parallel: Abram's earlier journey to Egypt likewise follows famine becoming severe in the land, repeating the narrative circumstance that drives the family toward Egyptian provision.

Genesis 43:2
And when they had finished eating the grain which they brought from Egypt, their father said to them, “Go again and buy us a little food.”
  Genesis 42:1-3 — Repeated household food mission: Jacob's first instruction sent his sons to Egypt to buy food for survival; the renewed command follows the exhaustion of the grain acquired on that journey.
  Genesis 44:25 — Explicit later recollection: Judah later repeats the father's instruction to go again and buy a little food, directly recalling the command that begins this second mission.

Genesis 43:3
And Judah said to him, “The man solemnly warned us, saying, ‘You shall not see my face unless your youngest brother is with you.’
  Genesis 42:15-20 — Reported condition recalled: Joseph's earlier instructions require the youngest brother to be brought for their claims to be believed; Judah now reports that requirement as the condition for another audience.
  Genesis 44:23 — Explicit repeated warning: Judah later tells Joseph the same warning that they cannot see his face unless the youngest brother accompanies them, repeating the condition reported to Israel here.

Genesis 43:4
“If therefore you send our brother with us, we will go down and buy you food.
  Genesis 44:26 — Explicit later recollection: Judah's later appeal repeats that the brothers can go down only if their youngest brother goes with them, recalling the condition he presents to his father here.
  Genesis 43:13-15 — Same-chapter condition and response: The father subsequently agrees to send the brother, and the men take Benjamin to Egypt, supplying the response and action required by Judah's conditional offer.

Genesis 43:5
“But if you do not send our brother with us, we will not go; for the man said to us, ‘You shall not see my face unless your youngest brother is with you.’”
  Genesis 44:26 — Explicit later recollection: The later account repeats the refusal to go without the youngest brother because they cannot see the man's face, preserving the condition that blocks the journey here.
  Genesis 42:20 — Prior instruction and reported consequence: Joseph earlier requires the youngest brother as verification; Judah explains the consequence for the proposed return if Israel refuses to send him.

Genesis 43:6
And Israel said, “Why did you do me harm by telling the man that you had a brother?”
  Genesis 42:13 — Prior family disclosure: The earlier narrated answer names twelve brothers and the youngest remaining with their father, supplying the disclosure about another brother which Israel now questions.
  Genesis 42:36 — Repeated paternal distress: Israel's complaint that the disclosure harms him follows his earlier accusation that the sons have bereaved him and now seek Benjamin, continuing his expressed fear for the family.

Genesis 43:7
And they said, “The man questioned us closely concerning ourselves and our kindred, saying, ‘Is your father still living? Do you have a brother?’ And we told him according to this questioning. Could we know that he would say, ‘Bring your brother’?”
  Genesis 44:19-20 — Reported questioning repeated: Judah later recounts the same inquiry about a father and brother and the family's answer, repeating the brothers' explanation of how the subject arose.
  Genesis 42:13 — Reported account and earlier narration: The earlier scene records the brothers disclosing their father and youngest brother; their present report adds an account of questioning without making the earlier narration quote those questions verbatim.

Genesis 43:8
And Judah said to Israel his father, “Send the lad with me, and we will arise and go, that we may live and not die, both we and you and our households.
  Genesis 42:2 — Repeated survival purpose: The father's first food-buying command aims that they may live and not die; Judah repeats that survival purpose when asking to take Benjamin on the next journey.
  Genesis 44:32-34 — Narrative undertaking developed: Judah's request to take responsibility for the lad develops into his later recollection of the pledge and offer to remain as a slave so the lad can return.

Genesis 43:9
“I will stand as surety for him; require him from my hand. If I do not bring him to you and set him before you, I shall bear the guilt before you all my days.
  Genesis 44:32 — Explicit pledge recalled: Judah later repeats his promise to return the lad and bear lasting guilt if he does not, expressly recalling the surety commitment made to his father here.
  Genesis 42:37 — Contrasting family guarantees: Reuben previously offers his two sons as the guarantee for Benjamin's return; Judah now undertakes personal responsibility and lasting guilt, preserving the different proposed guarantees.

Genesis 43:10
“For if we had not delayed, we should by now have returned twice.”
  Genesis 42:38 — Narrative impasse and reported delay: The father's earlier refusal to send Benjamin supplies the unresolved obstacle behind Judah's complaint about delay; Judah's two possible returns remain a counterfactual claim, not an elapsed-time calculation.

Genesis 43:11
And Israel their father said to them, “If it is so, do this: take some of the fruits of the land in your vessels, and carry down gifts to the man, resin and honey, spices and stacte, terebinth nuts and nuts.
  Genesis 37:25 — Egypt-bound commodities parallel: The earlier travellers carry spices, resin and stacte down to Egypt; these named products recur among the gifts Israel sends there, without making the two cargoes identical.
  Genesis 32:14-21 — Family gift strategy parallel: Jacob earlier sends gifts ahead hoping for Esau's acceptance; Israel now sends gifts to the Egyptian official while seeking favour for his sons, repeating the use of presents in an anxious encounter.

Genesis 43:12
“And take double the silver in your hands; the silver which was returned in your sacks, take back with you; perhaps it was an oversight.
  Genesis 42:27-28 — Earlier returned silver recalled: The lodging-place discovery of returned silver explains why Israel now directs that the original money be carried back together with funds for another purchase.
  Genesis 44:8 — Later appeal to returned payment: The brothers later cite their return of the earlier silver as an argument against the new theft charge, carrying Israel's repayment instruction into their defense.

Genesis 43:13
“And take your brother, and arise and go down to the man.
  Genesis 42:38 — Narrative change of decision: The father who earlier refuses to send Benjamin now commands that the brother be taken, reversing the decision that had prevented the return journey.
  Genesis 43:15 — Same-chapter command enacted: The men take Benjamin with the gifts and silver and travel to Egypt, carrying out the instruction to take the brother and go down.

Genesis 43:14
“And may my God give you grace before the man, and may he release your other brother and Benjamin. As for me, as I am bereaved, I am bereaved.”
  Genesis 43:23 — Same-chapter request and release: Israel asks that the detained brother be released; the steward subsequently brings Simeon out to them, supplying the release sought for that brother.
  Genesis 42:36 — Repeated bereavement language: Israel's acknowledgment of bereavement recalls his earlier complaint that Joseph and Simeon are gone and Benjamin will be taken, preserving the father's expressed uncertainty about his sons.

Genesis 43:15
And the men took these gifts, and took double the silver in their hands, and Benjamin; and they rose and went down into Egypt, and stood before Joseph.
  Genesis 43:11-13 — Same-chapter instructions fulfilled: The departure account names the gifts, double silver and Benjamin, enacting the three preparations Israel has just ordered for the journey.

Genesis 43:16
And Joseph saw them and Benjamin his brother, born of the same mother, and said to the steward of his house, “Bring the men into the house, and slaughter animals and prepare; for the men shall eat bread with me at noon.”
  Genesis 35:24 — Maternal family identification: The family list identifies Joseph and Benjamin as Rachel's sons, supplying the shared maternal relationship explicitly emphasized when Joseph sees Benjamin here.
  Genesis 44:1-2 — Household orders compared: Joseph first directs the steward to prepare a meal and later directs him to arrange the sacks and cup, using the same household intermediary in distinct phases of the encounter.

Genesis 43:17
And the man did as Joseph said, and brought the men into Joseph’s house.
  Genesis 43:16 — Same-chapter order carried out: The steward brings the men into Joseph's house in response to Joseph's instruction, recording the execution of the household reception order.

Genesis 43:18
And the men saw that they were brought into Joseph’s house, and said, “Because of the silver which was returned in our sacks at first we are brought in, that he may falsely accuse us and fall upon us, and take us for slaves, and our donkeys.”
  Genesis 42:25 — Dramatic irony of returned silver: The brothers fear an accusation over the silver, while the earlier narrator states that Joseph ordered its restoration, preserving the gap between their fear and the reader's knowledge.
  Genesis 44:17 — Narrative slavery threat compared: The brothers fear collective enslavement over the silver; Joseph later threatens to retain only the man with the cup, distinguishing the anticipated danger from the later stated terms.

Genesis 43:19
And they drew near to the steward of Joseph’s house, and spoke to him in the gateway of the house,
  Genesis 43:23 — Same-chapter appeal and answer: The approach to the steward leads to his reassurance, payment acknowledgment and release of Simeon, providing his answer to the brothers' concern.

Genesis 43:20
saying, “We beg you, my lord: we came down at first to buy food.
  Genesis 42:3 — Explicit earlier journey recalled: The narrator previously records the ten brothers going down to buy grain; their explanation to the steward identifies that first purchasing journey.

Genesis 43:21
“And when we came to the lodging place and opened our sacks, behold, each man’s silver was in his sack. We have now brought back our silver in its full weight in our hands.
  Genesis 42:27-28 — Discovery accounts compared: The earlier lodging scene records one brother discovering his silver, while this report describes each man's silver at the lodging place; both accounts are retained without making the details identical.
  Genesis 42:35 — Returned-silver accounts compared: The earlier account displays every man's bundle when the sacks are emptied before their father; the present report concerns those returned payments while giving its own lodging-place description.

Genesis 43:22
“And we have brought other silver with us to buy food. We do not know who put the silver into our sacks.”
  Genesis 42:25 — Dramatic irony of unknown agency: The men say they do not know who placed the silver, whereas the narrator has identified Joseph's command to restore it, separating their ignorance from the reported action.
  Genesis 44:1 — Repeated concealed placement: The men's uncertainty about the first returned money precedes Joseph's later order to put each payment in a sack again, repeating an action whose agency is known to the reader.

Genesis 43:23
And he said to them, “May you find favour; do not fear. Your God and the God of your fathers has given you treasures in your sacks; but I have received your good silver in full.” And he brought Simeon out to them,
  Genesis 42:24 — Detention and release: Simeon was previously bound before his brothers; his being brought out to them now ends the detention described in the earlier meeting.
  Genesis 42:25 — Steward's interpretation and narrated action: The steward attributes treasure to their God and acknowledges full payment; the earlier narrator reports Joseph ordering the silver restored, retaining the distinct explanations without inventing a miraculous deposit.

Genesis 43:24
and brought water to wash their feet, and gave fodder to their donkeys.
  Genesis 24:32 — Household hospitality parallel: Abraham's servant receives foot-washing water and fodder for his camels; Joseph's guests likewise receive washing water and feed for their donkeys as household hospitality.
  Genesis 18:4 — Hospitality gesture parallel: Abraham offers water for his visitors' feet, paralleling the washing water provided for Joseph's guests without identifying the hosts or occasions.

Genesis 43:25
And they prepared the gifts until Joseph came at noon, for they heard that he was to dine there.
  Genesis 43:11 — Same-chapter gift preparation: The gifts prepared for Joseph's arrival are the presents Israel directed the sons to carry, connecting their preparation with the father's earlier instruction.
  Genesis 43:16 — Same-chapter meal timing recalled: The guests prepare for Joseph at noon because of the planned meal, recalling Joseph's instruction that they eat with him at that time.

Genesis 43:26
And Joseph came into the house, and they brought him the gifts which were in their hands into the house, and bowed down before him, with their faces to the earth.
  Genesis 37:7 — Dream and later homage: The brothers' bowing before Joseph corresponds to the sheaves bowing to his sheaf in the earlier dream, relating the present gesture to the narrative's stated dream imagery.
  Genesis 42:6-9 — Repeated homage and dream memory: The first visit also includes bowing before Joseph and his recollection of the dreams; the present bowing repeats that gesture on the brothers' return.

Genesis 43:27
And he asked them, “How are you?” And he said to them, “Is your aged father, of whom you spoke, well? Is he still living?”
  Genesis 42:13 — Prior family report recalled: The brothers had earlier spoken of their father in Canaan; Joseph's inquiry now explicitly asks about the aged father of whom they spoke.
  Genesis 45:3 — Repeated paternal inquiry: When Joseph later reveals his identity, he again asks whether his father is living, repeating the concern expressed while his identity remains concealed here.

Genesis 43:28
And they said, “Your servant our father is well; he is still living.” And he said, “Blessed be that man by God.” And they bowed their heads and bowed down before him.
  Genesis 43:27 — Same-chapter question answered: The brothers' statement that their father is well and living directly answers Joseph's preceding inquiry about his welfare and survival.
  Genesis 42:6 — Repeated homage: The brothers' renewed bowing repeats the first visit's prostration before Joseph, continuing the gestures of deference surrounding the family's encounters with him.

Genesis 43:29
And Joseph lifted up his eyes and saw Benjamin his brother, born of the same mother, and said, “Is this your youngest brother, whom you said you would bring to me?” And he said, “May God show mercy to you, my child.”
  Genesis 42:20 — Requested brother presented: Joseph's recognition and question concern the youngest brother whom he previously required them to bring, connecting the person present with the earlier condition.
  Genesis 35:24 — Maternal brotherhood identified: The earlier family summary names Joseph and Benjamin as Rachel's sons, explaining the shared mother emphasized in Joseph's recognition of Benjamin.

Genesis 43:30
And Joseph was troubled, for his inward parts were deeply stirred for his brother, and he sought to weep; and he entered his chamber and wept there.
  Genesis 42:24 — Private weeping repeated: Joseph previously turns away to weep after hearing his brothers; he now withdraws to his chamber to weep over Benjamin, repeating concealed emotion during the meetings.
  Genesis 45:14 — Private and open fraternal tears: Joseph's private tears for Benjamin precede their later mutual weeping upon one another's necks after his identity is revealed.

Genesis 43:31
And he washed his face and went out, and restrained himself, and said, “Set out the bread.”
  Genesis 45:1 — Narrative restraint contrasted: Joseph restrains himself and orders bread set out here, whereas the later disclosure begins when he can no longer bear those standing beside him and sends others away.

Genesis 43:32
And they served him by himself, and them by themselves, and the Egyptians who dined with him by themselves; for the Egyptians could not eat bread with the Hebrews, for it is an abomination to the Egyptians.
  Genesis 46:34 — Egyptian social aversions compared: The meal account names Egyptian aversion to eating with Hebrews; the later settlement advice names aversion to shepherds, retaining these distinct explanations rather than equating their objects.

Genesis 43:33
And they sat before him, the firstborn according to his seniority, and the youngest according to his youth; and the men marvelled one at another.
  Genesis 42:7-8 — Concealed family knowledge context: The brothers marvel at their arrangement by age, while the earlier narration establishes that Joseph recognizes them and they do not recognize him; no method for arranging the seats is supplied.

Genesis 43:34
And portions were brought to them from him; but Benjamin’s portion was five times as great as the portions of them all. And they drank and became drunk with him.
  Genesis 45:22 — Distinctive gift for Benjamin: Benjamin receives an exceptional meal portion here and later exceptional gold and five changes of robes, repeating a distinction in Joseph's distributions to his brothers.

Genesis 44:1
And Joseph commanded the steward of his house, saying, “Fill the men’s sacks with food, as much as they can carry, and put each man’s silver at the mouth of his sack.
  Genesis 42:25 — Concealed silver placement repeated: Joseph again orders each payment put into its owner's sack, repeating the earlier restoration of silver which had already caused fear and questions.
  Genesis 43:12 — Repeated payment placement after attempted repayment: Israel had sent the earlier payments back because of their unexplained return; Joseph's new order repeats the placement of money after that effort to settle the matter.

Genesis 44:2
“And put my silver cup into the sack of the youngest, and the price of his grain.” And it was done according to Joseph’s word, as he had said.
  Genesis 44:12 — Same-chapter placement and discovery: The cup is found in Benjamin's sack after Joseph has explicitly commanded its placement in the youngest brother's sack, establishing a planted object rather than a narrated theft by Benjamin.
  Genesis 44:17 — Same-chapter planted evidence and stated terms: Joseph's later terms retain the man with the cup while allowing the others to leave; the earlier placement connects those terms specifically with the youngest brother's sack.

Genesis 44:3
The morning dawned, and the men were sent away, they and their donkeys.
  Genesis 44:13 — Same-chapter journey reversed: The men initially depart with their donkeys, but after the search they reload and return to the city, reversing the homeward movement begun at dawn.

Genesis 44:4
And when they had gone out of the city, and were not far off, Joseph said to the steward of his house, “Arise, pursue the men, and you shall overtake them, and say to them, ‘Why have you repaid evil for good?
  Genesis 44:6 — Same-chapter instruction delivered: The steward overtakes the men and speaks the words Joseph ordered, carrying the prescribed accusation from Joseph's house to the departing brothers.

Genesis 44:5
“‘Why have you stolen my silver cup? Is not this the cup from which my lord drinks? And with it he indeed practises divination. You have done evil in what you have done.’”
  Genesis 44:2 — Same-chapter accusation and narrated setup: The steward is told to accuse the men of stealing the cup, while the narrator has already attributed its presence to Joseph's instruction, keeping the charge distinct from established events.
  Genesis 44:15 — Same-chapter repeated divination claim: Joseph later repeats the assertion that a man such as himself practises divination, continuing the claim prescribed for the steward without independently demonstrating such a practice.

Genesis 44:6
And he found them, and spoke to them these words.
  Genesis 44:4-5 — Same-chapter message carried out: The steward's encounter fulfils the pursuit order and delivery of the accusation, referring back to the particular words Joseph dictated.

Genesis 44:7
And they said to him, “Why does my lord speak such words? Far be it from your servants to do such a thing.
  Genesis 44:8 — Same-chapter denial supported: The brothers support their denial by appealing to their earlier return of the silver, explaining why they consider the new theft charge implausible.
  Genesis 44:2 — Same-chapter dramatic irony of innocence: The narrator's account of Joseph ordering the cup planted supports the distinction between the brothers' denial and the steward's theft accusation.

Genesis 44:8
“If we brought back to you from the land of Canaan the silver which we found in our sacks, how should we steal silver or gold from your lord’s house?
  Genesis 43:12 — Earlier repayment instruction recalled: The return of the earlier silver follows Israel's instruction to take it back along with new money, supplying the background for the brothers' defense.
  Genesis 43:21-22 — Earlier repayment report recalled: The brothers had told the steward that they returned the full earlier weight and brought fresh purchasing money, the conduct they now invoke against the theft allegation.

Genesis 44:9
“With whomever of your servants the cup is found, let him die; and we also shall be slaves to our lord.”
  Genesis 31:32 — Search declaration compared: Jacob likewise pronounces death if Laban's gods are found with someone, without knowing Rachel stole them; the brothers do not know of the planted cup, so the hidden objects have different origins.
  Genesis 44:10 — Same-chapter proposed penalty narrowed: The steward replaces the offered death and collective slavery with slavery for the single person bearing the cup and clearance for the others.

Genesis 44:10
And he said, “Now let it be as you say: the man with whom the cup is found shall be my slave, but you shall be clear of guilt.”
  Genesis 44:17 — Same-chapter terms repeated: Joseph later states the same limitation: the man with the cup remains as slave while the others return to their father.
  Genesis 44:9 — Same-chapter proposal and restricted answer: The brothers offer death for the bearer and slavery for the group, while the steward's answer narrows the announced consequence to one captive.

Genesis 44:11
And they hurried, and each set down his sack upon the ground, and each opened his sack.
  Genesis 44:12 — Same-chapter search sequence: The opening of the sacks makes way for the ordered search that ends with discovery in the youngest brother's sack.
  Genesis 42:35 — Repeated opening of laden sacks: The earlier emptying of sacks exposes returned silver before their father; the present opening again precedes discovery of an unexpectedly placed object, in a different location and confrontation.

Genesis 44:12
And he searched, beginning with the eldest until he came to the youngest; and he found the cup in Benjamin’s sack.
  Genesis 44:2 — Same-chapter earlier placement explained: The discovery in Benjamin's sack corresponds to Joseph's explicit order to place the cup with the youngest, preventing the discovery itself from being treated as proof of theft.
  Genesis 43:33 — Repeated age-ordered arrangement: The search proceeds from eldest to youngest, repeating the age order emphasized in the preceding meal's seating without explaining how either arrangement was determined.

Genesis 44:13
And they tore their garments, and each loaded his sack upon his donkey, and they returned to the city.
  Genesis 37:29 — Fraternal distress gesture repeated: Reuben tears his garments on discovering Joseph missing from the pit; the brothers now tear theirs when the cup's discovery endangers Benjamin.
  Genesis 37:34 — Family mourning gesture compared: Jacob previously tears his garments when he believes Joseph lost; the sons now use the same distress gesture at the threatened loss of Benjamin, without a death having occurred in this scene.

Genesis 44:14
And Judah and his brothers came to Joseph while he was still there, and fell before him upon the earth.
  Genesis 42:6 — Repeated homage before Joseph: The brothers again fall before Joseph, repeating the first purchasing visit's bowing to the ruler whose identity they do not recognize.
  Genesis 43:26 — Reception and accusation contrasted: Their prostration after the cup search repeats the bowing with gifts at the earlier reception, carrying the same gesture into the changed circumstances of accusation.

Genesis 44:15
And Joseph said to them, “What is this deed that you have done? Do you not know that a man such as I indeed practises divination?”
  Genesis 44:5 — Same-chapter repeated prescribed claim: Joseph's statement about divination repeats the claim which he had instructed the steward to include in the accusation about the silver cup.
  Genesis 44:2 — Same-chapter accusation and known agency: Joseph asks about a deed attributed to the brothers, but the narrator has already recorded his own order to plant the cup, preserving the reader's knowledge alongside his charge.

Genesis 44:16
And Judah said, “What shall we answer to my lord, or what shall we speak, or how shall we be justified? God has found out the iniquity of your servants. Behold, we are household slaves to our lord, both we and he with whom the cup was found.”
  Genesis 42:21-22 — Earlier guilt acknowledged: The brothers earlier connect affliction with their treatment of Joseph; Judah now acknowledges iniquity being exposed, without his unspecific confession establishing an actual theft of the planted cup.
  Genesis 44:9 — Same-chapter collective responsibility offered: Judah again offers the whole group as slaves, echoing their earlier collective offer while omitting the proposed death sentence for the cup's bearer.

Genesis 44:17
And Joseph said, “Far be it from me to do this thing. The man with whom the cup was found shall be my slave; but you go up in safety to your father.”
  Genesis 44:10 — Same-chapter restricted consequence repeated: Joseph's answer repeats the steward's limitation of slavery to the man with the cup and clearance for his companions.
  Genesis 44:33 — Same-chapter alternative proposed: Joseph offers release to the brothers while retaining the bearer; Judah answers by proposing that he remain as slave in the lad's place.

Genesis 44:18
And Judah drew near to him and said, “I beg you, my lord, let your servant speak a word before you, and do not be angry with your servant, for you are next to Pharaoh.
  Genesis 41:40-44 — Authority previously established: Pharaoh had placed Joseph over his house and Egypt with only the throne above him, supplying the narrated authority behind Judah's address that he is next to Pharaoh.

Genesis 44:19
“My lord, you asked your servants, saying, ‘Do you have a father or a brother?’
  Genesis 43:7 — Reported family questioning repeated: The brothers earlier describe questioning about their living father and another brother; Judah now repeats that account directly in his appeal to Joseph.

Genesis 44:20
“And we said to my lord, ‘We have an aged father, and a young son of his old age; and his brother is dead, and he alone is left of his mother, and his father loves him.’
  Genesis 35:24 — Maternal family relationship: The earlier household list identifies Joseph and Benjamin as Rachel's two sons, supplying the maternal relationship behind the claim that only one remains of that mother.
  Genesis 42:38 — Paternal belief reported: The father earlier says Benjamin's brother is dead and he alone remains; Judah repeats that family belief before Joseph, who is alive and hearing the appeal.

Genesis 44:21
“And you said to your servants, ‘Bring him down to me, and I will care for him.’
  Genesis 42:20 — Prior demand recalled: Joseph's earlier command requires them to bring the youngest brother; Judah recounts that demand with a reported assurance of care, preserving his account's additional wording.

Genesis 44:22
“And we said to my lord, ‘The lad cannot leave his father; but if he leaves his father, he will die.’
  Genesis 42:38 — Paternal objection recalled: The father's earlier refusal fears harm on the journey and sorrowful descent to Hades; Judah presents the danger of separation in his report without resolving the pronoun of this death warning beyond the supplied wording.
  Genesis 44:30-31 — Same-chapter appeal expanded: Judah later explains the father's life as bound to the lad's and explicitly fears the father's death if he returns without him, developing the danger stated in the earlier reported exchange.

Genesis 44:23
“And you said to your servants, ‘Unless your youngest brother comes down with you, you shall see my face no more.’
  Genesis 43:3-5 — Explicit warning repeated: Judah previously tells Israel that they cannot see the man's face without the youngest brother; he now repeats that same warning within his appeal to Joseph.

Genesis 44:24
“And when we went up to your servant our father, we told him the words of my lord.
  Genesis 42:29-34 — Earlier return report recalled: The first return account records the brothers telling Jacob what occurred and reporting the demand for the youngest brother, the report Judah now recalls making to their father.

Genesis 44:25
“And our father said to us, ‘Go again, and buy us a little food.’
  Genesis 43:2 — Explicit paternal command recalled: Judah repeats the father's instruction to go again and buy a little food, identifying the command that initiated the second journey.

Genesis 44:26
“And we said, ‘We cannot go down; but if our youngest brother goes down with us, we will go down. For we cannot see the man’s face unless our youngest brother is with us.’
  Genesis 43:4-5 — Explicit response recalled: The earlier exchange states the brothers' conditional willingness to travel with Benjamin and refusal without him, the response Judah now recounts to Joseph.

Genesis 44:27
“And your servant our father said to us, ‘You know that my wife bore me two sons.
  Genesis 35:24 — Maternal pair identified: The family list names Rachel's two sons as Joseph and Benjamin, identifying the two sons within Judah's quotation of his father's words about his wife.

Genesis 44:28
“‘And one went out from me, and you said, “He has been devoured by a beast”; and I have not seen him again to this day.
  Genesis 37:31-35 — Earlier false loss belief recalled: The bloodied tunic leads Jacob to conclude that a beast devoured Joseph and to mourn him; Judah now quotes that continuing belief without making the reported death true.

Genesis 44:29
“‘If therefore you take this one also from my presence, and sickness befalls him on the way, you shall bring down my old age with sorrow into Hades.’
  Genesis 42:38 — Explicit sickness and sorrow warning: The father earlier fears sickness on Benjamin's journey and sorrowful descent of his old age to Hades; Judah repeats that warning with the supplied sickness language intact.
  Genesis 44:31 — Same-chapter feared bereavement repeated: The later part of Judah's plea again fears bringing the father's old age to Hades in anguish, repeating the loss consequence quoted from his father here.

Genesis 44:30
“Now therefore, if I come to your servant our father, and the lad is not with us, since his soul hangs upon the lad’s soul,
  Genesis 44:20 — Same-chapter paternal attachment developed: Judah first says the father loves the youngest son and now describes his soul as hanging upon the lad's soul, intensifying that stated attachment.
  Genesis 44:31 — Same-chapter life-binding image explained: The following statement explicitly predicts the father's death on seeing the lad absent, explaining the life-and-loss concern expressed by the image of their linked souls.

Genesis 44:31
“when he sees that the lad is not with us, he will die; and your servants shall bring down the old age of your servant our father with anguish into Hades.
  Genesis 42:38 — Earlier paternal warning repeated: Judah's predicted descent of the father's old age to Hades in anguish recalls the father's own warning when he initially refused to send Benjamin.
  Genesis 37:35 — Repeated paternal mourning language: Jacob's earlier lament over Joseph also speaks of going mourning into Hades, supplying a previous family expression for the bereavement Judah now fears.

Genesis 44:32
“For your servant became surety for the lad to his father, saying, ‘If I do not bring him to you and set him before you, I shall bear the guilt before my father all my days.’
  Genesis 43:9 — Explicit surety pledge recalled: Judah repeats his personal guarantee to bring the lad back and his promise to bear lifelong guilt before his father, directly recalling the earlier undertaking.

Genesis 44:33
“Now therefore I will remain with you as a servant in the lad’s place, a household slave to my lord; but let the lad go up with his brothers.
  Genesis 43:9 — Personal guarantee developed: The offer to remain as slave gives a concrete proposed means of meeting Judah's earlier personal responsibility for returning Benjamin to his father.
  Genesis 37:26-27 — Judah's contrasting slavery proposals: Judah earlier proposes selling Joseph so the brothers need not kill him; he now offers himself as slave so Benjamin can return, contrasting his two stated proposals without declaring an unspoken inner motive.

Genesis 44:34
“For how shall I go up to my father if the lad is not with us? Let me not see the evils which shall befall my father.”
  Genesis 37:34-35 — Earlier paternal grief recalled: Jacob's prolonged mourning and refusal of comfort over Joseph supply a narrated instance of the paternal suffering Judah now pleads not to witness again.
  Genesis 44:30-31 — Same-chapter final appeal grounded: Judah's final question about returning without the lad rests on his preceding explanation that such a return would bring death and anguish to his father.

Genesis 45:1
And Joseph could no longer bear all those who stood beside him, but said, “Send everyone away from me.” And no one else stood with Joseph when he made himself known to his brothers.
  Genesis 42:7-8 — Concealed identity and disclosure: Joseph previously recognizes the brothers while they do not recognize him; his present dismissal of the attendants and self-disclosure end that earlier concealment.
  Acts 7:13 — Later recollection of disclosure: Stephen expressly recalls Joseph making himself known to his brothers on their second visit, identifying the disclosure narrated here.
  Genesis 44:33-34 — Narrative sequence after Judah's appeal: Judah's offer to remain for Benjamin and his fear for their father immediately precede Joseph's disclosure. The sequence connects the scenes without supplying an unreported explanation of every cause of Joseph's response.

Genesis 45:2
And he lifted up his voice with weeping; and all the Egyptians heard, and it was heard in Pharaoh’s house.
  Genesis 43:30 — Concealed and audible weeping: Joseph earlier withdraws into his chamber to weep over Benjamin; now his weeping is heard by Egyptians and Pharaoh's household, changing the public extent of the emotion.
  Genesis 42:24 — Earlier weeping before the brothers: Joseph previously turns away to weep while concealing his recognition; the present account again records his weeping but describes its sound reaching Pharaoh's house.

Genesis 45:3
And Joseph said to his brothers, “I am Joseph. Is my father still living?” And his brothers could not answer him, for they were troubled.
  Genesis 43:27-28 — Repeated inquiry concerning the father: Joseph earlier asks whether their aged father lives and receives an affirmative answer; he repeats the question while identifying himself, without the narrator explaining why he asks again.
  Genesis 45:15 — Same-chapter inability and restored speech: The brothers cannot answer Joseph at his disclosure; after his kisses and tears the narrator says they speak with him, marking a change within the reunion.

Genesis 45:4
And Joseph said to his brothers, “Draw near to me.” And they drew near. And he said, “I am Joseph your brother, whom you sold into Egypt.
  Genesis 37:28 — Later identification of the sale: Joseph identifies himself as the brother they sold into Egypt; the earlier account narrates his sale through named merchants. The retrospective speech is retained without resolving every earlier actor pronoun or merchant label.
  Acts 7:9 — Later recollection of fraternal sale: Stephen likewise identifies the patriarchs as selling Joseph into Egypt, paralleling Joseph's own accusation concerning the brothers while preserving the later speech setting.

Genesis 45:5
“Now therefore do not be grieved, and do not let it seem hard to you that you sold me here; for God sent me before you to preserve life.
  Genesis 50:20 — Repeated providential interpretation: Joseph later again distinguishes the brothers' evil purpose from God's purpose of feeding many people, developing the preservation-of-life interpretation he gives here without calling the sale itself good.
  Psalms 104:16-17 — Later recollection of sending and sale: The psalm places Joseph's being sent ahead and sold as a slave beside the famine, paralleling Joseph's interpretation of being sent before his family to preserve life.

Genesis 45:6
“For this is the second year of famine upon the land, and there are still five years left in which there shall be neither ploughing nor harvest.
  Genesis 41:29-30 — Famine duration and earlier interpretation: Joseph's earlier interpretation specifies seven years of famine after seven of abundance; his present statement of the second famine year and five remaining years supplies the corresponding stage in his account.
  Genesis 45:11 — Same-chapter remaining famine and provision: Joseph repeats the five remaining famine years when promising to nourish the household, connecting his time statement with the practical reason for moving them.

Genesis 45:7
“For God sent me before you, that a remnant of you might remain upon the earth, and to nourish a great remnant of you.
  Genesis 50:20 — Preservation purpose restated: Joseph later says God planned good so that many people might be fed; that later explanation repeats the life-preserving purpose he now expresses through the language of a surviving remnant.
  Genesis 47:12 — Stated purpose and narrated provision: Joseph interprets his sending as nourishment of the family; the subsequent narrator records his distribution of grain to his father's whole house, supplying a particular act of the promised preservation.

Genesis 45:8
“Now therefore it was not you who sent me here, but God; and He has made me as a father to Pharaoh, and lord of all his house, and ruler of all the land of Egypt.
  Genesis 41:40-41 — Royal appointment and later interpretation: Pharaoh earlier places Joseph over his house and Egypt, supplying the narrated appointment behind Joseph's present description of his authority. His father-to-Pharaoh language remains his own description rather than literal parentage.
  Acts 7:10 — Later recollection of Egyptian authority: Stephen recalls Pharaoh appointing Joseph ruler over Egypt and his household, corresponding to the authority Joseph describes here while giving that later speaker's account of divine favour.
  Psalms 104:20-21 — Later recollection of release and rule: The psalm recounts a king's release of Joseph and appointment over his house and possessions, paralleling the position Joseph invokes in explaining his Egyptian role to the brothers.

Genesis 45:9
“So hurry, and go up to my father, and say to him, ‘Thus says your son Joseph: God has made me lord of all the land of Egypt; come down therefore to me, and do not delay.
  Genesis 45:26 — Same-chapter commissioned and delivered message: Joseph commissions a report of his Egyptian authority and summons his father; the brothers subsequently tell Jacob that Joseph lives and rules Egypt, carrying the essential news home without repeating the commission verbatim.
  Acts 7:14 — Later recollection of the summons: Stephen expressly recounts Joseph summoning Jacob and his relatives, recalling the family invitation Joseph sends through the brothers here.

Genesis 45:10
“‘And you shall live in the land of Goshen of Arabia, and shall be near to me, you and your sons and your sons’ sons, your sheep and your oxen, and all that you have.
  Genesis 46:34 — Repeated proposed settlement: Joseph again names Goshen of Arabia when instructing the brothers for their audience with Pharaoh, repeating the proposed locality and its connection with their livestock household.
  Genesis 47:11 — Invitation and recorded settlement: The invitation names Goshen of Arabia, and the later settlement notice gives the best land in Rameses. The records concern the same family's settlement while their supplied geographic descriptions remain intact without an invented boundary identification.

Genesis 45:11
“‘And I will nourish you there, for there are still five years of famine, lest you be destroyed, you and your sons and all your possessions.’
  Genesis 47:12 — Promise and narrated food provision: Joseph's promise to nourish his father's household is followed by the narrator's report that he measures grain to the family according to each person.
  Genesis 50:21 — Continuing promise to nourish the household: After Jacob's death Joseph again promises to nourish the brothers and their households, continuing the specific provision first promised during the famine invitation.

Genesis 45:12
“Behold, your eyes see, and the eyes of Benjamin my brother, that it is my mouth which speaks to you.
  Genesis 42:23 — Concealment and appeal to direct speech: The brothers earlier do not know Joseph understands them because an interpreter is between them; Joseph now appeals to their sight and his own speaking mouth in asserting his identity, without the text naming the language used here.

Genesis 45:13
“Tell my father therefore of all my glory in Egypt, and of all that you have seen; and hurry and bring my father down here.”
  Genesis 45:26-27 — Same-chapter report requested and supplied: Joseph asks for a report of his Egyptian position and the brothers' observations; they later report his rule, repeat his words and show the wagons, bringing the requested evidence to Jacob.
  Genesis 46:5 — Command to bring the father and ensuing journey: Joseph urges them to bring Jacob down, and the later journey records the sons carrying their father on Joseph's wagons, narrating the family's response to that instruction.

Genesis 45:14
And he fell upon the neck of Benjamin his brother, and wept over him; and Benjamin wept upon his neck.
  Genesis 43:30 — Earlier emotion over Benjamin: Joseph previously withdraws to weep when deeply moved for Benjamin; he now weeps upon Benjamin's neck and receives Benjamin's tears in an open reunion.
  Genesis 33:4 — Fraternal reunion gestures: Esau earlier embraces Jacob and falls upon his neck, and both weep; Joseph now falls upon Benjamin's neck and the brothers weep together, repeating those particular gestures in a distinct fraternal reunion.
  Genesis 45:15 — Same-chapter reunion extended to the brothers: Joseph's mutual weeping with Benjamin is followed by kissing and weeping over all his brothers, extending the reunion beyond the same-mother pair.

Genesis 45:15
And he kissed all his brothers, and wept over them; and afterward his brothers spoke with him.
  Genesis 37:4 — Hostile speech and later restored conversation: The brothers originally cannot speak peaceably to Joseph because of their hatred; after the present kisses and tears they speak with him, giving a particular reversal of their earlier estrangement.
  Genesis 45:3 — Same-chapter speech restored: At Joseph's identification the troubled brothers cannot answer; the reunion concludes with their speaking to him, contrasting the two reported responses.

Genesis 45:16
And the report spread into Pharaoh’s house, saying, “Joseph’s brothers have come.” And Pharaoh and his attendants rejoiced.
  Acts 7:13 — Later recollection of royal awareness: Stephen links Joseph's self-disclosure with his family's becoming known to Pharaoh, recalling the royal household's reception of the news narrated here.
  Genesis 45:2 — Same-chapter reports reaching Pharaoh's house: Joseph's weeping is first heard in Pharaoh's house, and the later report identifies his brothers' arrival and the household's joy. These are distinct notices of the private reunion becoming known outside the room.

Genesis 45:17
And Pharaoh said to Joseph, “Say to your brothers, ‘Do this: load your beasts, and depart to the land of Canaan.
  Genesis 45:25 — Same-chapter commissioned return and arrival: Pharaoh directs the brothers to depart for Canaan; the narrator later records their return from Egypt to Jacob in Canaan, completing that outward stage of the instruction.

Genesis 45:18
“‘And take your father and your possessions, and come to me; and I will give you of all the good things of Egypt, and you shall eat the marrow of the land.’
  Genesis 47:5-6 — Royal invitation and later settlement direction: Pharaoh invites the father and possessions and promises Egypt's good things; his later instruction to settle the family in the best land gives a specific implementation of that welcome.
  Genesis 46:5-7 — Invitation and family migration: The subsequent journey brings Jacob, households and possessions into Egypt, narrating the family's movement in response to the invitation to take the father and possessions there.

Genesis 45:19
“And give this command, that they take wagons from the land of Egypt: ‘Take them for your children and your wives, and bring your father, and come.
  Genesis 45:21 — Same-chapter wagons commanded and supplied: Pharaoh directs that wagons be taken for the family, and Joseph gives wagons according to Pharaoh's words, explicitly carrying out the command.
