  Genesis 46:5 — Command and actual wagon transport: The later migration places Jacob and the households upon the wagons Joseph sent, identifying their use for the family transport requested here.

Genesis 45:20
“‘And do not let your eyes regret your belongings, for all the good things of Egypt shall be yours.’”
  Genesis 45:18 — Same-chapter royal generosity repeated: Pharaoh repeats the promise of Egypt's good things while telling the brothers not to regret their belongings, continuing the material welcome announced in the preceding invitation.
  Genesis 47:6 — Generous offer and allotted land: The assurance about Egypt's good things is followed later by Pharaoh's instruction to settle the father and brothers in the best land, giving the generosity a particular territorial expression.

Genesis 45:21
And the sons of Israel did so; and Joseph gave them wagons according to the words of Pharaoh the king, and gave them provisions for the way.
  Genesis 45:19 — Same-chapter command implemented: The narrator expressly attributes Joseph's wagons to Pharaoh's words, connecting the supplied transport with the preceding royal instruction.
  Genesis 46:5 — Wagons supplied and used: The wagons Joseph gives for the family journey later carry Jacob and the households, tracing the same transport from provision to use.

Genesis 45:22
And to them all he gave two robes each; but to Benjamin he gave three hundred pieces of gold and five changes of robes.
  Genesis 43:34 — Different preferential gifts to Benjamin: Benjamin previously receives a fivefold meal portion and now receives three hundred pieces of gold and five robe changes while the others receive two robes each. Both scenes distinguish Benjamin's gift without making the robe allocation a fivefold ratio.
  Genesis 37:23 — Clothing taken and later given: The brothers earlier strip Joseph of his distinctive garment; Joseph now supplies robes to those brothers, reversing the direction of clothing transfer without claiming that these are the original garment.

Genesis 45:23
And to his father he sent the same, and ten donkeys carrying some of all the good things of Egypt, and ten female mules carrying bread for his father’s journey.
  Genesis 45:21 — Same-chapter provisions extended to the father: The earlier notice gives the brothers provisions for the road; the present notice specifies goods and bread sent for their father's journey, extending the preparations to his travel.
  Genesis 42:2 — Earlier request for food and later supplies: Jacob originally sends his sons to obtain a little food so the family can live; Joseph now sends bread-bearing animals for the father's journey, supplying the father whose need began the Egyptian journeys.

Genesis 45:24
And he sent his brothers away, and they departed; and he said to them, “Do not be angry on the way.”
  Genesis 45:25 — Same-chapter departure and completed return: Joseph dismisses the brothers with an instruction not to be angry on the way; the next notice records their arrival in Canaan, without separately reporting how they observed that instruction during the journey.

Genesis 45:25
And they went up from Egypt, and came into the land of Canaan to Jacob their father.
  Genesis 45:17 — Same-chapter return direction fulfilled: The arrival in Canaan completes the departure Pharaoh had directed, identifying Jacob as the father to whom the brothers return.
  Genesis 42:29 — Repeated return to Jacob in Canaan: The brothers' earlier Egyptian visit also ends with a return to Jacob in Canaan and a report; the present return follows Joseph's disclosure rather than the earlier concealed encounter.

Genesis 45:26
And they told him, saying, “Your son Joseph lives, and he rules all the land of Egypt.” And Jacob’s mind was astonished, for he did not believe them.
  Genesis 37:34-35 — Death belief and news of life: Jacob had mourned Joseph as dead and refused comfort; the report that Joseph lives confronts that earlier belief, explaining the significance of the new message without confirming the supposed death.
  Genesis 41:40-41 — Family report and narrated royal appointment: The brothers' claim that Joseph rules Egypt corresponds to Pharaoh's earlier narrated appointment of him over the house and land, giving narrative background to the authority reported to Jacob.

Genesis 45:27
And they spoke to him all the words which Joseph had said to them. And when he saw the wagons which Joseph had sent to carry him, the spirit of Jacob their father was rekindled.
  Genesis 45:19-21 — Same-chapter wagons and their evidential role: The wagons Jacob sees are the transport Pharaoh commanded and Joseph supplied; the report and visible wagons accompany the rekindling of his spirit.
  Genesis 37:35 — Earlier unconsoled mourning reversed: Jacob's earlier refusal of comfort over Joseph contrasts with the present rekindling of his spirit after the message and wagons, without turning that figurative renewal into a narrated resurrection.

Genesis 45:28
And Israel said, “It is a great thing to me if Joseph my son still lives. I will go and see him before I die.”
  Genesis 46:29-30 — Wish to see Joseph and actual reunion: Jacob declares that he will see Joseph before dying; the subsequent meeting records his seeing Joseph and saying that he may now die because his son is alive.
  Genesis 37:35 — Changed expectation concerning Joseph: Jacob earlier expects to go down mourning to his son in Hades; he now plans to see the living Joseph, marking a specific change from the earlier death belief.

Genesis 46:1
And Israel set out, he and all that he had, and came to the Well of the Oath, and offered a sacrifice to the God of his father Isaac.
  Genesis 26:23-25 — Patriarchal worship at the Well of the Oath: Isaac earlier comes to the Well of the Oath and builds an altar after God's appearance; Jacob now sacrifices there to the God of Isaac, recalling his father's worship at the named locality.
  Genesis 28:10 — Earlier departure from the named place: Jacob's earlier journey toward Harran begins at the Well of the Oath; the present journey toward Egypt again passes through that named place, distinguishing the routes and their settings.

Genesis 46:2
And God spoke to Israel in a vision of the night, saying, “Jacob, Jacob.” And he said, “What is it?”
  Genesis 31:11-13 — Earlier nocturnal address before a journey: Jacob earlier recounts a dream address calling his name and directing his return; God now addresses him in a night vision before the Egyptian journey, again combining personal address with travel guidance.
  Exodus 3:4 — Repeated name and divine-call response: God's double address Jacob, Jacob and Jacob's What is it response have a close narrative parallel in the double call Moses, Moses and the same supplied response, in distinct revelation settings.

Genesis 46:3
And He said, “I am the God of your fathers. Do not fear to go down into Egypt, for I will make you a great nation there.
  Genesis 26:2-3 — Different divine directions during famine: God earlier tells Isaac not to go down into Egypt but to remain where directed; He now tells Jacob not to fear going down. The two instructions retain their different persons and circumstances rather than becoming a universal prohibition of Egypt.
  Deuteronomy 26:5 — Later recollection of becoming a nation in Egypt: The later confession describes the ancestor's going down to Egypt with few people and becoming a great nation there, recalling the journey and national growth promised here.

Genesis 46:4
“And I will go down with you into Egypt, and I will bring you up at last; and Joseph shall lay his hands upon your eyes.”
  Genesis 28:15 — Presence and return promised on journeys: God earlier promises to be with Jacob on every road and bring him back to the land; the Egyptian vision likewise promises divine presence and a bringing up, carrying those themes into another journey.
  Genesis 50:13 — Later carrying of Jacob back to Canaan: The sons later carry Jacob's body from Egypt to the ancestral cave in Canaan, providing a narrated return of Jacob himself to compare with the promised bringing up, without exhausting the promise's possible wider scope.
  Exodus 3:7-10 — Later deliverance of the descendants: God later announces His coming down to deliver Israel from Egypt and bring the people out, providing a later national deliverance to compare with the descent-and-return promise rather than identifying that event with Jacob's burial.

Genesis 46:5

  Genesis 45:19-21 — Previously supplied wagons used: The family uses the wagons Pharaoh commanded and Joseph supplied, connecting the journey's actual transport with the earlier arrangements.
  Acts 7:14-15 — Later summary of the family migration: Stephen recounts Joseph summoning Jacob and relatives and Jacob's going down into Egypt, summarizing the family movement whose transport is described here.

Genesis 46:6

  Genesis 45:18 — Invitation and migration with possessions: Pharaoh's invitation expressly includes the father and possessions; the narrator now records Jacob entering Egypt with what the family acquired in Canaan, supplying the movement of people and property.
  Exodus 1:1 — Later recollection of the household arrival: Exodus opens by recalling the sons entering Egypt with Jacob and each household, continuing the family migration introduced here.

Genesis 46:7
his sons and his sons’ sons with him, his daughters and his sons’ daughters; and all his seed he brought into Egypt.
  Exodus 1:1 — Whole-household migration recalled: The source enumerates sons, grandsons, daughters and granddaughters; Exodus's opening likewise describes each son's whole household arriving with Jacob, retaining the household scope rather than reducing the migration to named adult sons alone.
  Acts 7:14 — Later summary of the relatives summoned: Stephen describes Joseph summoning Jacob and all his relatives, a later summary of the extended family whose generations are explicitly included in the migration notice here.

Genesis 46:8

Genesis 46:10

Genesis 46:11

Genesis 46:12

  Genesis 38:6-10 — Earlier circumstances of the two deaths: The Judah episode narrates Er's and Onan's deaths and their stated circumstances; this genealogy recalls both deaths in Canaan without supplying the earlier account's full explanation.
  Numbers 26:15-17 — Parallel Judah-family record with differences: Numbers repeats Er and Onan dying in Canaan and lists Judah's surviving branches and Perez's sons; its Shelon and Iamun are retained beside Genesis's Shelah and Jemuel, without replacing either supplied family record.

Genesis 46:13

  Numbers 26:19-20 — Issachar family and census compared: The census gives Issachar's Tola, Puah, Jashub and Shimron clans, while Genesis gives Tola, Phua, Jasoub and Zambram as sons. The common ancestral branch supports comparison without silently harmonizing the differing forms.
  1 Chronicles 7:1 — Parallel Issachar-family record: Chronicles gives Issachar four sons as Thola, Phoua, Jasoub and Semeron; Genesis's four-name record retains Tola, Phua, Jasoub and Zambram, making the supplied similarities and differences available for comparison.

Genesis 46:14

Genesis 46:15

Genesis 46:16

Genesis 46:17

Genesis 46:18

Genesis 46:19

Genesis 46:20

And to Manasseh was born a son, whom his Syrian concubine bore to him, Machir; and Machir begot Gilead. And the sons of Ephraim, Manasseh’s brother: Soutalaam and Taam. And the sons of Soutalaam: Edem.
  Genesis 41:50-52 — Earlier births and maternal identity: The earlier account names Asenath, Potiphera and Heliopolis and records Manasseh and Ephraim's births before the famine; this expanded genealogy repeats those immediate relationships and adds further family branches without independently dating every descendant's birth.
  Numbers 26:32-33 — Manasseh branch in a later census: Numbers lists Joseph's Manasseh and Ephraim branches and expressly gives Machir and his son Gilead under Manasseh, corresponding to that part of the expanded family record here.
  Numbers 26:39-40 — Expanded Ephraim branch compared: Numbers supplies Shuthelah, Tanach and Shuthelah's Eden, while Genesis has Soutalaam, Taam and Soutalaam's Edem. The two Ephraim-family records are compared without replacing the supplied names or claiming a new individual-identity verification.
  1 Chronicles 7:14 — Parallel Syrian-concubine and Machir notice: Chronicles likewise places a Syrian concubine in Manasseh's family and names Machir as Galaad's father, while also naming Aseriel. Genesis 46:21

Genesis 46:27

  Deuteronomy 10:22 — Different supplied migration totals: Deuteronomy gives seventy souls for the ancestors' descent into Egypt, whereas Genesis here gives seventy-five. The comparison preserves the difference and does not invent a census adjustment or silently harmonize the totals.

Genesis 46:28
And he sent Judah before him to Joseph, to meet him near Heroopolis in the land of Rameses.
  Genesis 46:29 — Same-chapter advance direction and actual meeting: Judah is sent ahead for the meeting near Heroopolis, and Joseph next goes to meet Israel near Heroopolis, connecting the advance arrangement with the narrated encounter.

Genesis 46:29
And Joseph harnessed his chariots, and went up to meet Israel his father near Heroopolis; and he appeared to him, and fell upon his neck, and wept abundantly.
  Genesis 45:28 — Anticipated and actual reunion: Jacob had declared that he would go and see the living Joseph; Joseph's meeting and embrace of his father narrate the reunion for which that journey began.
  Genesis 45:14 — Reunion gestures with brother and father: Joseph earlier weeps upon Benjamin's neck and now falls upon Israel's neck and weeps, repeating the particular gesture in distinct family reunions.
  Genesis 33:4 — Earlier reunion of Jacob and Esau: Jacob's earlier reunion with Esau includes an embrace, falling upon the neck and weeping; Joseph's embrace and abundant tears with Jacob provide another family reunion using those gestures.

Genesis 46:30

  Genesis 45:28 — Wish to see the living son realized: Jacob had wished to see Joseph before dying; he now says he may die because he has seen Joseph alive, explicitly connecting the reunion with that earlier wish.
  Genesis 47:28 — Readiness-to-die speech and later lifespan notice: Jacob's declaration does not narrate his immediate death; the later account explicitly gives him seventeen years in Egypt, preserving the difference between this emotional speech and his actual remaining life.

Genesis 46:31

Genesis 46:32
“‘And the men are shepherds, for they have been keepers of livestock; and they have brought their livestock and their oxen and all that they have.’
  Genesis 47:3-4 — Prepared description and actual audience: Joseph prepares to describe the family as livestock keepers; the brothers later identify themselves as shepherds and explain their livestock's need for pasture, carrying the occupational account into the royal audience.
  Genesis 37:12 — Earlier work of the brothers: The earlier Joseph narrative places the brothers pasturing their father's sheep in Shechem, providing a particular earlier instance of the occupation Joseph now describes to Pharaoh.

Genesis 46:33

  Genesis 47:3 — Anticipated and actual question: Joseph anticipates Pharaoh asking the brothers their work; Pharaoh later asks that very question, giving the narrated occasion for the prepared response.

Genesis 46:34

  Genesis 47:3-6 — Prepared answer and permitted residence: The brothers later identify their ancestral shepherd occupation, request Goshen and receive permission, connecting Joseph's instructions with the actual audience without attributing an unreported motive to Pharaoh.
  Genesis 43:32 — Different Egyptian exclusion notices: The earlier dining account calls Egyptian eating with Hebrews an abomination, while Joseph here describes Egyptian abhorrence of shepherds. Both describe barriers affecting this family, but the distinct activities and reported settings are not collapsed into one rule.

Genesis 47:1
And Joseph came and told Pharaoh, saying, “My father and my brothers, and their livestock and their oxen and all that they have, have come from the land of Canaan; and behold, they are in the land of Goshen.”
  Genesis 46:31-32 — Prepared report delivered: Joseph now delivers the report he proposed, identifying his father and brothers from Canaan and the livestock they brought, carrying the earlier plan into the audience with Pharaoh.
  Genesis 45:10 — Invited and reported location: Joseph's invitation names Goshen of Arabia for the household; he now reports the family in Goshen, retaining the different supplied scope of the location wording without adding Arabia to this verse's text.

Genesis 47:2
And he took five men from among his brothers, and set them before Pharaoh.
  Genesis 47:3-4 — Same-chapter representatives and their speech: The five selected brothers are presented to Pharaoh, and the following exchange gives the household occupation and residence request they bring before him, without naming which brothers were chosen.
  Genesis 46:33-34 — Preparation and selected audience: Joseph previously prepares the brothers for Pharaoh's occupational question; the selection and presentation of five brothers provide the audience in which that prepared exchange takes place.

Genesis 47:3
And Pharaoh said to Joseph’s brothers, “What is your work?” And they said to Pharaoh, “Your servants are shepherds of sheep, both we and our fathers.”
  Genesis 46:33-34 — Anticipated question and corresponding reply: The actual question about work and answer about ancestral shepherding correspond to Joseph's earlier instructions, preserving the prepared speech and its delivery as separate scenes.
  Genesis 37:12 — Earlier narrated shepherding: The brothers earlier pasture their father's sheep in Shechem, giving narrative background for their present statement that they and their fathers are shepherds.

Genesis 47:4
And they said to Pharaoh, “We have come to live as strangers in the land; for there is no pasture for your servants’ livestock, because the famine has become severe in the land of Canaan. Now therefore your servants will live in the land of Goshen.”
  Genesis 41:54-57 — Earlier famine and Egyptian food supply: The earlier account describes famine outside Egypt and countries coming there for grain; the brothers now explain their particular Canaan pasture shortage and request residence, moving from grain-buying journeys toward settlement.
  Genesis 47:5-6 — Same-chapter residence request and royal permission: The brothers ask to live in Goshen because of the famine, and Pharaoh permits Goshen and the best land, supplying the response to their stated need.

Genesis 47:5
And Pharaoh said to Joseph, “Let them live in the land of Goshen; and if you know that there are able men among them, set them over my livestock.” And Jacob and his sons came into Egypt to Joseph; and Pharaoh king of Egypt heard. And Pharaoh spoke to Joseph, saying, “Your father and your brothers have come to you.
  Genesis 46:34 — Proposed residence and permitted settlement: Joseph's instructions had anticipated living in Goshen; Pharaoh now authorizes that residence. His separate conditional request for able livestock keepers is not turned into an appointment of all five brothers.
  Genesis 45:18 — Royal welcome continued: Pharaoh had invited the father and possessions to Egypt; he now acknowledges the family's arrival and provides residence and a conditional livestock role, continuing that welcome.

Genesis 47:6
“Behold, the land of Egypt is before you; settle your father and your brothers in the best of the land.”
  Genesis 45:18 — Promise of good things and best land: Pharaoh's earlier invitation promises the good things of Egypt; his present instruction specifies the best land for Joseph's father and brothers.
  Genesis 47:11 — Same-chapter settlement command implemented: Joseph later gives the family a possession in the best land as Pharaoh commanded, explicitly implementing the direction stated here.

Genesis 47:7
And Joseph brought in Jacob his father, and set him before Pharaoh; and Jacob blessed Pharaoh.
  Genesis 47:10 — Same-chapter blessing frames the audience: Jacob blesses Pharaoh on being presented and again when leaving, placing blessings at the entrance and exit of their recorded conversation.

Genesis 47:8
And Pharaoh said to Jacob, “How many are the years of the days of your life?”
  Genesis 47:9 — Same-chapter age question answered: Jacob's following answer supplies one hundred and thirty years and his comparison with his fathers, directly answering Pharaoh's question about his lifespan.

Genesis 47:9
And Jacob said to Pharaoh, “The days of the years of my life in which I sojourn are a hundred and thirty years. Few and grievous have been the days of the years of my life, and they have not reached the days of the years of the life of my fathers, in the days when they sojourned.”
  Genesis 25:7-8 — Earlier paternal lifespan for comparison: Abraham's life is recorded as one hundred and seventy-five years; that explicit lifespan supplies one ancestral comparison for Jacob's statement that his own hundred and thirty years have not reached his fathers' years.
  Genesis 35:28-29 — Isaac's lifespan and Jacob's comparison: Isaac's completed life is given as one hundred and eighty years, supplying another particular paternal lifespan behind Jacob's comparison of his present age with his fathers.
  Genesis 47:28 — Same-chapter arrival age and final lifespan: The later notice gives Jacob seventeen years in Egypt and a total life of one hundred and forty-seven years, extending the hundred-and-thirty-year age he reports to Pharaoh rather than treating it as his death age.
  Hebrews 11:13-16 — Later theological reflection on sojourning: Jacob calls his own and his fathers' lives a sojourning; Hebrews later describes the patriarchs as confessing themselves strangers and sojourners and adds its heavenly-homeland interpretation. That later interpretation is distinguished from Jacob's immediate age comparison.

Genesis 47:10
And Jacob blessed Pharaoh, and went out from him.
  Genesis 47:7 — Same-chapter repeated royal blessing: Jacob's blessing on departure repeats the blessing at his presentation, framing the conversation with Pharaoh without claiming that either blessing's words are supplied.

Genesis 47:11
And Joseph settled his father and his brothers, and gave them a possession in the land of Egypt, in the best of the land, in the land of Rameses, as Pharaoh commanded.
  Genesis 47:5-6 — Same-chapter settlement permission carried out: The narrator expressly links Joseph's gift of the best land to Pharaoh's command, connecting the family's Rameses possession with the preceding royal settlement instructions.
  Genesis 46:28 — Earlier naming of the Rameses region: The approach to Egypt already names Heroopolis in the land of Rameses; the settlement notice again names the land of Rameses, retaining the distinction between the named meeting place and the wider regional description.

Genesis 47:12
And Joseph measured out grain to his father and to his brothers and to all his father’s house, according to each person.
  Genesis 45:11 — Promised nourishment supplied: Joseph had promised to nourish Jacob's household through the remaining famine; the narrator now records grain measured to the father, brothers and whole house according to each person.
  Genesis 50:21 — Provision continued after Jacob's death: Joseph later again promises to nourish the brothers and their households, continuing the family-care commitment manifested in this grain distribution.

Genesis 47:13
And there was no grain in all the land, for the famine had become exceedingly severe; and the land of Egypt and the land of Canaan languished because of the famine.
  Genesis 41:30-31 — Severe famine anticipated: The earlier interpretation predicts a famine so severe that the abundance will be forgotten; this later notice describes Egypt and Canaan languishing under the famine, corresponding to that stated severity.
  Acts 7:11 — Later recollection of widespread famine: Stephen likewise describes famine and great affliction in both Egypt and Canaan, recalling the two lands whose suffering the narrator names here.

Genesis 47:14
And Joseph gathered all the silver found in the land of Egypt and in the land of Canaan for the grain which they bought, and he measured it out to them; and Joseph brought all the silver into Pharaoh’s house.
  Genesis 41:56-57 — Grain sales and collected payment: The earlier account describes Joseph selling grain to Egyptians and other countries; the present account specifies the silver collected from Egypt and Canaan and brought into Pharaoh's house.
  Genesis 47:15 — Same-chapter collected and exhausted silver: Joseph gathers the silver paid for grain, and the next notice says the silver is spent and records the Egyptians' renewed bread request, tracing the change in available payment.

Genesis 47:15
And all the silver in the land of Egypt and in the land of Canaan was spent. And all the Egyptians came to Joseph, saying, “Give us bread; why should we die before you? For our silver is spent.”
  Genesis 41:55 — Repeated Egyptian request for bread: Egyptians earlier cry to Pharaoh for bread and are directed to Joseph; they now come directly to Joseph with the added explanation that their silver is spent.
  Genesis 47:16 — Same-chapter request and barter proposal: The Egyptians ask for bread after their silver is spent; Joseph responds by proposing livestock in exchange, giving the specific answer to their payment shortage.

Genesis 47:16
And Joseph said to them, “Bring your livestock, and I will give you bread in exchange for your livestock, if the silver is spent.”
  Genesis 47:17 — Same-chapter barter proposal implemented: The next notice records horses, sheep, oxen and donkeys being brought and bread being supplied in exchange, carrying out Joseph's livestock proposal.

Genesis 47:17
And they brought their livestock to Joseph; and Joseph gave them bread in exchange for their horses and sheep and oxen and donkeys, and nourished them with bread in exchange for all their livestock that year.
  Genesis 47:16 — Same-chapter exchange terms carried out: The narrator's livestock-for-bread transaction implements Joseph's preceding instruction to bring livestock once the silver is exhausted.
  Genesis 47:18 — Same-chapter transaction and later acknowledgment: The people later acknowledge that livestock and possessions have passed to Joseph, corresponding to the preceding year's narrated livestock exchange while remaining their reported financial summary.

Genesis 47:18
And that year ended, and they came to him in the second year, and said to him, “Let us not be destroyed before our lord. For if our silver is spent, and our possessions and livestock have passed to you, our lord, there remains nothing to us before our lord except our own bodies and our land.
  Genesis 47:14-17 — Same-chapter retrospective financial summary: The people's account of spent silver and transferred livestock recalls the previously narrated grain payments and barter, explaining their statement that only their bodies and land remain.
  Genesis 47:19 — Same-chapter depleted resources and proposed terms: The statement that only bodies and land remain leads into the people's request to exchange themselves and their land for bread and seed, specifying the remedy they propose.

Genesis 47:19
“Therefore, lest we die before you and the land become desolate, buy us and our land in exchange for bread, and we and our land shall be Pharaoh’s slaves. Give us seed, that we may sow and live and not die, and the land shall not be desolate.”
  Genesis 47:20-21 — Same-chapter proposed sale and narrated result: The people's proposal that Joseph buy them and their land is followed by the narrator's land purchase for Pharaoh and explicit enslavement of the people. Their desperate request is not rewritten as a universal moral approval of the resulting system.
  Genesis 47:23 — Same-chapter request for seed answered: Joseph later states that he has bought the people and land for Pharaoh and gives seed to sow, answering the request for seed alongside the proposed transfer.

Genesis 47:20
And Joseph bought all the land of the Egyptians for Pharaoh; for the Egyptians sold their land to Pharaoh, because the famine prevailed over them. And the land became Pharaoh’s.
  Genesis 47:22 — Same-chapter general purchase and explicit exception: The narrator follows the purchase of all Egyptian land with an explicit exception for the priests' land, qualifying the general statement rather than erasing the exception.
  Genesis 47:26 — Same-chapter land regime and continuing ordinance: The later ordinance gives Pharaoh a fifth and again distinguishes the priests' land as not Pharaoh's, carrying the purchase-and-exception account into the description of the continuing land arrangement.

Genesis 47:21
And he enslaved the people to him as slaves, from one end of the borders of Egypt to the other.
  Genesis 47:19 — Same-chapter proposed and narrated enslavement: The people had proposed becoming Pharaoh's slaves, and the narrator now expressly says they were enslaved as slaves. The supplied action is retained rather than replaced with a different edition's population-transfer wording.
  Genesis 47:23 — Same-chapter narrator and Joseph on the purchase: Joseph's subsequent statement that he bought the people and their land for Pharaoh corresponds to the narrator's enslavement notice, retaining the distinction between the narrated action and his speech.

Genesis 47:22
The land of the priests was the only land Joseph did not buy; for Pharaoh gave an allowance to the priests, and they ate of the allowance which Pharaoh gave them. Therefore they did not sell their land.
  Genesis 47:26 — Same-chapter priestly-land exception repeated: The concluding ordinance again says the priests' land was not Pharaoh's, repeating the exception whose allowance and lack of sale the narrator explains here.

Genesis 47:23
And Joseph said to all the Egyptians, “Behold, I have bought you and your land today for Pharaoh. Take seed for yourselves, and sow the land.
  Genesis 47:19 — Same-chapter seed supplied after the request: The people had asked for seed so the land would not become desolate; Joseph now supplies seed and directs sowing after declaring the purchase, answering that part of their request.
  Genesis 47:24 — Same-chapter sowing and subsequent crop terms: Joseph's seed-and-sowing instruction is immediately followed by the fifth owed to Pharaoh and the four parts retained for seed and household food, specifying the terms for the resulting produce.

Genesis 47:24
“And of its produce you shall give a fifth part to Pharaoh; but the four parts shall be yours, for seed for the land, and for food for you and for all who are in your houses.”
  Genesis 47:26 — Same-chapter crop terms and ordinance: The narrative concludes by establishing the fifth to Pharaoh as an ordinance, repeating the crop share Joseph specifies here and adding the continuing priestly-land exception.
  Genesis 41:34 — Distinct fifth-share arrangements: Joseph earlier proposes taking a fifth during the seven years of abundance; here he requires a fifth of produce after the purchase of land and people. The shared fraction is compared while the temporary collection and later land terms remain distinct arrangements.

Genesis 47:25
And they said, “You have saved us; we have found grace before our lord, and we shall be Pharaoh’s slaves.”
  Genesis 47:19 — Same-chapter request and people's evaluation: The people had asked to live by exchanging themselves and land for bread; they now say Joseph saved them and accept slavery to Pharaoh. Their gratitude remains their stated evaluation rather than an independent universal endorsement by the narrator.
  Genesis 47:21 — Same-chapter reported acceptance and narrated status: The people's statement that they will be Pharaoh's slaves corresponds to the narrator's preceding enslavement notice, keeping their own words distinct from the account of the action.

Genesis 47:26
And Joseph established this for them as an ordinance concerning the land of Egypt to this day, to give a fifth to Pharaoh, except only the land of the priests, which was not Pharaoh’s.
  Genesis 47:22 — Same-chapter priestly exception preserved: The continuing ordinance repeats that the priests' land was not Pharaoh's, recalling the earlier explanation that their allowance prevented the need to sell it.
  Genesis 47:24 — Same-chapter specified share codified: The fifth named in the ordinance is the share Joseph requires from the crop, while the earlier speech also specifies the four parts retained by the cultivators for seed and food.

Genesis 47:27
And Israel lived in the land of Egypt, in the land of Goshen; and they obtained possessions in it, and increased and multiplied exceedingly.
  Genesis 46:3 — Promised and narrated growth in Egypt: God promises to make Jacob a great nation in Egypt; the settlement notice subsequently reports Israel increasing and multiplying there, giving a narrated development consistent with that promise.
  Exodus 1:7 — Continued multiplication in Egypt: Exodus's opening likewise reports Israel increasing, multiplying and becoming exceedingly strong, carrying the Egyptian growth described here into the next book.
  Psalms 104:23-24 — Later recollection of Egyptian sojourn and increase: The psalm recounts Israel entering Egypt, Jacob sojourning there and God's greatly increasing His people, recalling the settlement-and-growth sequence summarized here.

Genesis 47:28

  Genesis 47:9 — Same-chapter present age and completed life: Jacob reports one hundred and thirty years to Pharaoh; the later notice gives seventeen years in Egypt and one hundred and forty-seven in all, joining the two stated age markers.
  Genesis 49:33 — Lifespan notice and subsequent death: The age summary precedes the closing family instructions and the later narrator's report of Jacob's death, keeping the supplied lifespan connected with its ensuing death account rather than an earlier emotional readiness to die.

Genesis 47:29

  Genesis 24:2-4 — Earlier oath gesture in a different commission: Abraham asks his servant to place a hand under his thigh for a marriage-related oath; Jacob uses the same stated gesture for a burial request, retaining the different persons and purposes.
  Genesis 49:29-32 — Burial request later specified: Jacob later names the ancestral cave in Canaan and those buried there, giving the particular family burial place behind his present request not to be buried in Egypt.

Genesis 47:30

Genesis 47:31
And he said, “Swear to me.” And he swore to him. And Israel bowed in worship upon the top of his staff.

The matching worship wording is retained without collapsing the surrounding scenes into one event or replacing staff with bed.

“There they buried Abraham and Sarah his wife; there they buried Isaac and Rebekah his wife; and there I buried Leah,
  Genesis 23:19 — Explicit earlier burial recalled: The earlier narrator records Abraham burying Sarah in the double cave opposite Mamre, directly supporting the Sarah burial recalled in Jacob's list.
  Genesis 25:9-10 — Explicit earlier burial recalled: Abraham's sons bury him in the double cave, and the earlier summary names Abraham and Sarah there, directly supporting that part of Jacob's family burial recollection.
  Genesis 35:29 — Earlier paternal death and burial: The earlier notice records Esau and Jacob burying Isaac, supplying his burial event while not independently locating Rebekah's or Leah's graves outside Jacob's present statement.

Genesis 49:32
“in the possession of the field and the cave within it, acquired from the sons of Heth.”
  Genesis 23:17-20 — Explicit property recollection: The earlier account confirms the field and cave as Abraham's burial possession from the sons of Heth, closely corresponding to this closing description of the property.
  Genesis 50:13 — Later burial implementation: The sons' later burial uses the cave bought from Ephron opposite Mamre, continuing the identified burial property rather than an unreported second ancestral site.

Genesis 49:33
And Jacob ceased charging his sons; and he drew up his feet upon the bed, and died, and was added to his people.
  Genesis 50:1 — Immediate narrative continuation: Joseph's mourning over his father's face follows Jacob's death, giving the son's immediate response to the death just narrated.
  Genesis 35:29 — Generational death-notice parallel: Isaac's earlier death notice also describes dying and being added to his family before burial by his sons, providing a paternal-generation counterpart without adding a new doctrine about the phrase.

Genesis 50:1
And Joseph fell upon his father’s face, and wept over him, and kissed him.
  Genesis 49:33 — Immediate narrative response: Joseph's weeping and kiss respond to the preceding account of Jacob's death after his final instructions.
  Genesis 46:29-30 — Family-encounter contrast: Joseph had earlier fallen on his living father's neck and wept at their reunion; he now falls upon his father's face in mourning, preserving the differing gestures and circumstances.

Genesis 50:2
And Joseph commanded his servants the embalmers to prepare his father for burial; and the embalmers prepared Israel for burial.
  Genesis 50:3 — Same-chapter burial preparation elaborated: The following forty-day notice supplies the narrated duration for the embalmers' preparation, distinct from the seventy days of Egyptian mourning.
  Genesis 50:26 — Same-chapter later burial-rites counterpart: Joseph's own later burial rites and coffin in Egypt provide a second burial-preparation notice, without claiming that the later verse independently describes the embalmers' exact procedure.

Genesis 50:3
And they fulfilled forty days for him, for this is how the days of burial preparation are reckoned; and Egypt mourned him seventy days.
  Genesis 50:4 — Same-chapter mourning sequence: Joseph approaches Pharaoh's rulers after the mourning days have passed, continuing the chronology announced by the Egyptian mourning notice.
  Genesis 50:10 — Same-chapter distinct mourning stages: The later seven-day mourning at Atad belongs to the burial journey, distinct from the forty-day preparation and seventy-day Egyptian mourning; no new combined total is asserted.

Genesis 50:4
And when the days of mourning had passed, Joseph spoke to Pharaoh’s rulers, saying, “If I have found grace in your sight, speak concerning me in Pharaoh’s ears, saying,
  Genesis 50:5 — Same-chapter message content: The next verse supplies the oath-based burial request Joseph asks Pharaoh's rulers to transmit.
  Genesis 50:6 — Same-chapter request and royal response: Pharaoh's permission answers the message presented through his rulers, advancing Joseph's request to make the burial journey.

Genesis 50:5
“‘My father made me swear, saying, “In the tomb which I dug for myself in the land of Canaan, there you shall bury me.” Now therefore I will go up and bury my father, and return.’”
  Genesis 47:29-31 — Earlier oath recalled: The earlier exchange records Jacob's request for burial outside Egypt with his fathers and Joseph's oath, supplying the oath background while the present report retains its dug-tomb wording.
  Genesis 49:29-32 — Earlier burial instructions: Jacob's final instructions specify the Canaanite family cave bought by Abraham, corresponding to the requested Canaan burial without erasing the report's distinct description of the tomb.

Genesis 50:6
And Pharaoh said, “Go up and bury your father, as he made you swear.”
  Genesis 50:5 — Same-chapter request accepted: The permission to bury his father follows Joseph's request based on the paternal oath and promised return.
  Genesis 50:7 — Same-chapter permission enacted: Joseph's departure with the burial company carries out Pharaoh's authorization to go and bury his father.

Genesis 50:7
And Joseph went up to bury his father; and with him went up all Pharaoh’s servants, and the elders of his house, and all the elders of the land of Egypt,
  Genesis 50:8-9 — Same-chapter procession expanded: The following clauses add the family, chariots and horsemen to the Egyptian officials already named, completing the description of the great burial company.
  Genesis 50:14 — Same-chapter journey and return: The later return notice includes Joseph, his brothers and the others who went for the burial, closing the journey begun here.

Genesis 50:8
and all Joseph’s household, and his brothers, and all his father’s house; but they left their kindred and their sheep and their oxen in the land of Goshen.
  Genesis 47:27 — Earlier household residence: Israel's household had settled and obtained possessions in Goshen, supplying the residence where the present account says kindred and livestock remain.
  Genesis 50:14 — Same-chapter household journey and return: The brothers who accompany the burial later return to Egypt with Joseph, while the current clause separately records those and the livestock left in Goshen without specifying an unreported roster.

Genesis 50:9
And chariots and horsemen also went up with him, and the company was exceedingly great.
  Genesis 50:7-8 — Same-chapter procession elaboration: The chariots and horsemen enlarge the company of officials and family described in the preceding clauses, giving the particular context of the great company here.

Genesis 50:10
And they came to the threshing floor of Atad, which is beyond the Jordan; and they lamented him with a great and exceedingly grievous lamentation, and he mourned his father seven days.
  Genesis 50:11 — Same-chapter mourning and naming response: The inhabitants' observation and the name Mourning of Egypt follow the grief at Atad, giving the narrated reason for the locality's name.
  Genesis 50:3 — Same-chapter distinct mourning stages: The seven days at the threshing floor are a later journey-stage observance, compared with but not substituted for the earlier Egyptian mourning period.

Genesis 50:11
And the inhabitants of the land of Canaan saw the mourning at the threshing floor of Atad, and said, “This is a great mourning for the Egyptians.” Therefore its name was called Mourning of Egypt, which is beyond the Jordan.
  Genesis 50:10 — Same-chapter place-name explanation: The preceding great lamentation at Atad supplies the event the inhabitants observe when the place receives its supplied mourning name.

Genesis 50:12
And his sons did thus to him, and buried him there.
  Genesis 49:29-32 — Earlier burial charge implemented: Jacob's sons act after his instructions specifying the ancestral cave and burial possession, giving the charge behind their action.
  Genesis 50:13 — Same-chapter burial location specified: The following verse explicitly locates the burial in Canaan's double cave opposite Mamre, clarifying the destination rather than inventing a separate burial at the threshing floor.

Genesis 50:13
And his sons carried him up into the land of Canaan, and buried him in the double cave, the cave which Abraham bought for a burial possession from Ephron the Hittite, opposite Mamre.
  Genesis 23:17-20 — Explicit property and burial correspondence: The original field-and-cave transaction identifies the Ephron property opposite Mamre as Abraham's burial possession, corresponding to the cave used for Jacob here.
  Genesis 49:29-32 — Explicit burial fulfilment: The sons bury Jacob at the named ancestral cave he had required, carrying out the location and family-burial instructions.

Genesis 50:14
And Joseph returned into Egypt, he and his brothers and those who had gone up to bury his father.
  Genesis 50:5 — Same-chapter promised return completed: Joseph's return to Egypt after the burial carries out the return promised in his request to Pharaoh.
  Genesis 47:27 — Continuing household residence: The household's earlier settlement in Egypt supplies the continuing residence to which Joseph and the brothers return after the temporary burial journey.

Genesis 50:15
And Joseph’s brothers saw that their father was dead, and said, “What if Joseph should bear a grudge against us, and fully repay us all the evils which we did to him?”
  Genesis 45:4-5 — Earlier reconciliation and continued fear: Joseph had already acknowledged the sale and reassured the brothers through a preserving-life explanation, providing the earlier family encounter alongside their renewed fear after Jacob's death.
  Genesis 50:19-21 — Same-chapter fear answered: Joseph's repeated Do not fear and promise to nourish the households answer the brothers' stated fear of repayment, without making their fear a report that he had planned revenge.

Genesis 50:16
And they came to Joseph, saying, “Your father gave a charge by oath before he died, saying,
  Genesis 50:15 — Same-chapter narrative sequence: The reported paternal charge follows the brothers' fear after the father's death; it remains their report rather than an independently narrated earlier conversation with Jacob.
  Genesis 50:17 — Same-chapter reported charge continued: The following quotation supplies the forgiveness request said to come from their father, continuing the report introduced here without independently verifying the claimed oath.

Genesis 50:17
“‘This is what you shall say to Joseph: “Forgive them their iniquity and their sin, for they did evil to you.”’ And now, pardon the iniquity of the servants of your father’s God.” And Joseph wept as they spoke to him.
  Genesis 45:14-15 — Earlier family weeping: Joseph had already wept during the reunion with Benjamin and the other brothers, providing a prior family weeping scene without declaring the circumstances or spoken requests identical.
  Genesis 50:21 — Same-chapter petition and reassurance: Joseph's comfort and pledge to nourish the households follow the petition for pardon, recording his response without adding an otherwise unquoted formula.

Genesis 50:18
And they came to him, and said, “Behold, we are your household slaves.”
  Genesis 37:27-28 — Narrative status reversal: The brothers had proposed selling Joseph and he was sold toward Egypt; they now offer themselves as his household slaves, reversing the expressed relation without inventing a bowing action absent from this supplied verse.
  Genesis 50:21 — Same-chapter offer and response: Joseph answers the family's fear with a promise to nourish them, rather than the narrator recording an acceptance of their offer as an imposed new enslavement.

Genesis 50:19
And Joseph said to them, “Do not fear, for I belong to God.
  Genesis 50:21 — Same-chapter reassurance repeated: Joseph repeats Do not fear and adds his promise of provision, developing the reassurance that begins here with I belong to God.
  Genesis 45:5 — Earlier reassurance parallel: Joseph had previously told the brothers not to grieve over the sale and invoked God's preserving action; the present reassurance continues that response while retaining its distinct I belong to God wording.

Genesis 50:20
“You planned evil against me, but God planned good concerning me, that it might come to pass as it is today, that many people might be fed.”
  Genesis 45:5-8 — Earlier providential interpretation: Joseph's earlier explanation likewise attributes his Egyptian position to God's sending for preservation and nourishment, corresponding to his distinction here between the brothers' evil plan and God's good purpose.
  Genesis 37:18-20 — Earlier hostile plan recalled: The narrator had explicitly described the brothers' wicked plan to kill Joseph, supplying particular earlier evidence for the evil intention Joseph now names without calling that intention good.
  Psalms 104:16-17 — Later providential recollection: The Psalm places the famine beside God's sending Joseph ahead through a sale into slavery, providing another account of provision through the same history without erasing the human wrongdoing in Genesis.

Genesis 50:21
And he said to them, “Do not fear; I will nourish you and your households.” And he comforted them, and spoke to their hearts.
  Genesis 45:10-11 — Earlier household-provision promise: Joseph had already promised to nourish his father's family in Goshen during the famine, supplying the earlier practical pledge that corresponds to his renewed assurance here.
  Genesis 50:19 — Same-chapter reassurance developed: The repeated Do not fear continues Joseph's initial answer and joins it to food provision and comfort, retaining the supplied I belong to God response rather than another edition's question.

Genesis 50:22
And Joseph lived in Egypt, he and his brothers and all his father’s household; and Joseph lived a hundred and ten years.
  Genesis 50:26 — Same-chapter lifespan correspondence: The closing death notice repeats Joseph's hundred-and-ten-year lifespan, matching the age given while describing his continuing Egyptian residence.
  Exodus 1:5-6 — Later household-generation continuation: Exodus likewise locates Joseph in Egypt before recalling his death and that of his brothers' generation, continuing the family setting without changing its supplied census wording.

Genesis 50:23
And Joseph saw Ephraim’s children to the third generation; and the sons of Machir, Manasseh’s son, were born upon Joseph’s thighs.
  Genesis 48:11 — Generational descendants parallel: Jacob earlier credits God with showing him Joseph's offspring; Joseph now sees later descendants within those sons' families, extending the generational theme while keeping the different people and generations distinct.
  Joshua 17:1 — Later family-branch correspondence: Joshua identifies Machir as Manasseh's firstborn and father of Gilead in the tribal inheritance account, extending the named Machir branch without treating every later tribal action as an independently recorded act of Joseph's grandson.

Genesis 50:24
And Joseph spoke to his brothers, saying, “I am dying; but God shall surely visit you, and bring you up from this land to the land which God swore to our fathers Abraham and Isaac and Jacob.”
  Exodus 3:16-17 — Later visitation-and-return promise: God's later message to Israel's elders joins sure visitation in Egypt with bringing them to the promised land, closely corresponding to Joseph's assurance while naming the ancestral God.
  Hebrews 11:22 — Later faith interpretation: Hebrews explicitly characterizes dying Joseph's mention of Israel's departure and instructions about his bones as faith, interpreting the expectation voiced here and in the following oath.

Genesis 50:25
And Joseph made the sons of Israel swear, saying, “In the visitation with which God shall visit you, you shall carry up my bones from here with you.”
  Exodus 13:19 — Explicit oath implementation: Moses later takes Joseph's bones and the narrator repeats the visitation-and-bones oath, directly recalling and carrying out Joseph's instruction.
  Joshua 24:32 — Later bones-transfer and burial recollection: Joshua recalls Israel bringing Joseph's bones from Egypt and burying them in the Shechem portion, continuing the carried-up bones instruction into the later burial account.

Genesis 50:26
And Joseph died, being a hundred and ten years old; and they performed his burial rites, and laid him in a coffin in Egypt.
  Genesis 50:22 — Same-chapter lifespan correspondence: The age at Joseph's death repeats the hundred and ten years already stated, closing his life narrative while his coffin remains in Egypt.
  Exodus 1:6 — Explicit later death recollection: Exodus expressly recalls Joseph's death along with his brothers and their generation, continuing from this closing Egyptian burial notice into the next book's family history.
